j(\alpha)\neq\alpha
, if it exists. The existence of a measurable cardinal is equivalent to the existence of a nontrivial elementary embedding
j\colon V\to M
, where
M
is an inner model of
V
.

The following large cardinal assumption, due to Woodin, will be crucially used in Chapter
8
.

Definition 2.9.1
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Card}
be uncountable. We say that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds if there exists a nontrivial elementary embedding
j\colon L(V_{\lambda+1})\to L(V_{\lambda+1})
such that
\operatorname{crt}(j)<\lambda
.
The assertion that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds for some
\lambda\in\mathrm{Card}
is abbreviated by
\mathsf{I0}
.

The axiom
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
is at the top of the large cardinal hierarchy. It implies that
\lambda
has countable cofinality: indeed, if
\lambda_{0}=\operatorname{crt}(j)
and
\lambda_{n+1}=j(\lambda_{n})
, then
\lambda=\sup_{n\in\omega}\lambda_{n}
. Moreover, each
\lambda_{n}
is
m
-huge for every
m\in\omega
, and in particular it is a measurable cardinal. Notably, if
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds then
V_{\lambda+1}
cannot be well-ordered in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
, therefore
L(V_{\lambda+1})\models\neg{\sf AC}
; however,
L(V_{\lambda+1})\models{\sf DC}_{\lambda}
. For more informations on
\mathsf{I0}
, see
[
8
]
and
[
9
]
.

Chapter 3
\lambda
-Polish spaces

From this point onward, as declared we will tacitly work in our base theory

{\sf ZF}+{\sf AC}_{\lambda}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}).

However, some of the results in this chapter can be proved in even weaker theories, so we sometimes briefly discuss the exact amount of choice needed for the specific statement at hand.

3.1.
Definition and examples

The following definition naturally generalizes that of a Polish space, which corresponds to the case
\lambda=\omega
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
\lambda
be an infinite cardinal. A topological space
X
is
\lambda
-Polish
if it is completely metrizable and
\operatorname{wt}(X)\leq\lambda
.

When dealing with a
\lambda
-Polish space, we will often implicitly assume that it comes with a corresponding well-ordered basis of size at most
\lambda
; the terminology “basic open set”, if not specified otherwise, tacitly refers to sets in such a basis. Recall also that the condition
\operatorname{wt}(X)\leq\lambda
is equivalent to
\operatorname{dens}(X)\leq\lambda
because
X
is metrizable.

Example 3.1.2
.

The following are examples of
\lambda
-Polish spaces.

(a)

Any (classical) Polish space—although in this context they somewhat trivialize if
2^{\aleph_{0}}\leq\lambda
(this will often be the case).

(b)

Any discrete space
X
of weight at most
\lambda
. Notice that in this case
X
is well-orderable and
|X|\leq\lambda
.

(c)

The space

B(\lambda)={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}

and, if
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
and
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda_{i})_{i\in\omega}
is cofinal in
\lambda
, the space

C(\lambda)={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(\vec{\lambda})}=\prod_{i\in\omega}\lambda_{i},

where both
B(\lambda)
and
C(\lambda)
are endowed with the product of the discrete topologies on
\lambda
and
\lambda_{i}
or, equivalently, with the (relativization of the) bounded topology
\tau_{b}
on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}
. Such spaces were studied in
[
47
]
, where in particular it is shown that
B(\lambda)\approx C(\lambda)
. The latter result is obtained in
[
47
]
through a topological characterization of
B(\lambda)
. We will give a direct proof of (a generalization of) this fact in Theorem
3.2.2
, and also provide several alternative topological characterizations of
B(\lambda)
when
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
in Theorems
3.3.4
and
3.4.2
.

(d)

The space

{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}

of all binary
\lambda
-sequences equipped with the bounded topology is called generalized Cantor space. It is a
\lambda
-Polish space if and only if
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
and
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
or, equivalently, if
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
and
\lambda
is strong limit (see
[
4
, Proposition 3.12]
); this is precisely condition
(2)
from Section
1.2
. Notice also that the space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
, which at first glance might be proposed as a generalization of the Baire space, has weight
\lambda^{<\lambda}
, and thus it becomes
\lambda
-Polish if and only if
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
and
\lambda^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Since the latter condition is equivalent to “
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and
\lambda
is regular”, this happens only when
\lambda=\omega
, in which case we are back to the usual Baire space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
.

(e)

Let
\lambda
be a limit cardinal.
Then the Woodin topology on
V_{\lambda+1}
, which was introduced in
[
50
, p. 298]
, is the topology generated by the basic open sets

O_{(a,\alpha)}=\{A\subseteq V_{\lambda}\mid A\cap V_{\alpha}=a\}

with
\alpha<\lambda
and
a\subseteq V_{\alpha}
. Such basis has cardinality

|V_{\lambda}|
. Assume that
V_{\lambda}
is well-orderable, so that
|V_{\lambda}|
is a cardinal. Then
|V_{\lambda}|
is strong
limit because
\lambda
is limit, and therefore this is the least possible cardinality for a basis of the Woodin topology by Fact
2.2.1
. Therefore
the weight of
V_{\lambda+1}
is
|V_{\lambda}|
. If

{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
, then we can define a complete metric on
V_{\lambda+1}
by
fixing a strictly increasing sequence
(\lambda_{i})_{i\in\omega}
cofinal in
\lambda

and setting, for all distinct
x,y\in V_{\lambda+1}
,
d(x,y)=2^{-i}
if and only if
i\in\omega
is
smallest such that
x\cap V_{\lambda_{i}}\neq y\cap V_{\lambda_{i}}
. Conversely, if

V_{\lambda+1}
is first-countable, then
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
. Therefore

V_{\lambda+1}
is (completely) metrizable if and only if
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
, and in such case

V_{\lambda+1}
endowed with the Woodin topology is a
|V_{\lambda}|
-Polish space.
Thus
V_{\lambda+1}
is a
\lambda
-Polish space whenever
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
and
|V_{\lambda}|=\lambda
. Notice that the latter condition is equivalent to
\beth_{\lambda}=\lambda
, that is, condition
(3)
from Section
1.2
.

(f)

If
X
is
\lambda
-Polish, then the hyperspace
K(X)
of compact subsets of
X
endowed with the Vietoris topology is still
\lambda
-Polish (see
[
28
, Section 4.F]
).

(g)

All Banach spaces with density character
\lambda
, viewed as topological spaces, are
\lambda
-Polish. This includes e.g. the Banach space
c_{0}^{\lambda}
consisting of those sequences
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{\lambda}
such that for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
one has
|x_{\alpha}|<\varepsilon
for all but finitely many
\alpha<\lambda
, equipped with the pointwise operations and the sup norm.

The class of
\lambda
-Polish spaces is clearly closed under
G_{\delta}
subspaces,
1
1

1

Indeed, by
[
28
, Theorem 3.11]
a subspace of a
\lambda
-Polish space
X
is
\lambda
-Polish if and only if it is
G_{\delta}
in
X
.

sums
(i.e. disjoint unions) of size at most
\lambda
, and countable products. However, if
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
such class is not closed under
products of size
\kappa>\omega
, independently of the chosen support for the product topology. Indeed, let
(Y_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\kappa}
be a family of discrete spaces of size
\lambda
. Consider the product

Y=\prod_{\alpha<\kappa}Y_{\alpha}
equipped with the
<\mu
-supported product topology,
where
\mu
is an infinite cardinal such that
\mu\leq\kappa
. If
\mu>\omega
, then the weight of
Y
, if defined,
would be at least
\lambda^{\omega}=\lambda^{{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)}>\lambda
; if instead

\mu=\omega
, then
Y
would be non-first-countable, and hence not metrizable. In both cases,
the product
Y
is not
\lambda
-Polish. (In contrast, we anticipate that a strictly related kind of spaces, namely the standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces, are closed under products of size at most
\lambda
if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
— see Section
5.4
, and in particular Proposition
5.4.7
.)

Extension theorems like the ones by Tietze
[
28
, Theorem 1.3]
, Kuratowski
[
28
, Theorem 3.8]
, and Lavrentiev
[
28
, Theorem 3.9]
are in particular true for
\lambda
-Polish spaces, as well as Urysohn’s lemma
[
28
, Theorem 1.2]
. Moreover, every
\lambda
-Polish space is Baire by Baire’s category theorem
[
28
, Theorem 8.4]
. There are a number of consequence of this that remains true in our generalized setup, e.g. the topological
0
-
1
law
[
28
, Theorem 8.46]
. However, the results that can be proved using category arguments are sparse in the realm of
\lambda
-Polish spaces with
\lambda>\omega
: for example, the natural notion of “Borelness” in this context is, arguably,
\lambda
-Borelness (see Section
4
), but there are very simple
\lambda
-Borel subsets of
B(\lambda)
that do not have the Baire property.

Example 3.1.3
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{\aleph_{0}}\leq\lambda
. Let
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}
be a flip set, that is, a set such that for every
x,y\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}
, if
\exists!n\in\omega\,(x(n)\neq y(n))
then
x\in A\iff y\notin A
— such a set can be constructed recursively using any well-order of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}
, which exists by our assumption on
\lambda
. Let
f\colon\lambda\to 2
be the map such that
f(\alpha)=0
if and only if
\alpha
is even, i.e. it is of the form
\gamma+k
with
\gamma=0
or
\gamma
limit, and
k\in\omega
even, and let
g\colon B(\lambda)\to{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}
be defined using
f
coordinatewise. Finally, let
B=g^{-1}(A)
. The set
A
is the union of
2^{\aleph_{0}}
-many singletons, and hence
B
is a union of at most
\lambda
-many closed sets because
g
is continuous and
2^{\aleph_{0}}\leq\lambda
: this shows that
B
is
\lambda
-Borel, and in fact it belongs to the second level of the
\lambda
-Borel hierarchy (see Section
4.2
).
Notice that the set
B
is neither meager nor comeager in
\boldsymbol{N}_{s}
, for any
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
.
Indeed, let
\phi\colon\lambda\to\lambda
be the bijection swapping every even ordinal with its successor. Then the map
h\colon\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\to\boldsymbol{N}_{s}
defined by letting
h(x)(\operatorname{lh}(s))=\phi(x(\operatorname{lh}(s)))
and
h(x)(n)=x(n)
for every
n\neq\operatorname{lh}(s)

is a homeomorphism of
\boldsymbol{N}_{s}
into itself such that
h(B\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s})=\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\setminus B
. If
B
where either meager or comeager in
\boldsymbol{N}_{s}
, then we would obtain that
\boldsymbol{N}_{s}
is meager in itself, against the fact that it is a clopen subspace of
B(\lambda)
, and hence a
\lambda
-Polish space. Therefore
B
cannot have the Baire property because of
[
28
, Proposition 8.26]
.

In view of this,
one may be tempted to generalize the notion of meagerness by
considering
\nu
-meager sets with
\nu>\omega
, where
a subset of a
\lambda
-Polish space is
\nu
-meager if it can be written as a
\nu
-sized union of nowhere dense sets. Setting
\nu=\lambda
, one would then get that all
\lambda
-Borel sets have the
\lambda
-Baire property, i.e. they differ from an open set by a
\lambda
-meager set. However such a notion would be useless because e.g.
B(\lambda)
is already
\omega_{1}
-meager, hence all of its subsets trivially have the
\nu
-Baire property for any
\nu>\omega
. To see this, for
\alpha<\omega_{1}
set
U_{\alpha}=\{x\in B(\lambda)\mid x(n)=\alpha\text{ for some }n\in\omega\}
and observe that all the sets
U_{\alpha}
are open dense, yet
\bigcap_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}U_{\alpha}=\emptyset
.

Notice also that in the context of metrizable spaces with a well-orderable basis, complete metrizability is equivalent
to the space being strong Choquet. This follows from
[
28
, Section 8.E]
once we observe that separability can be dropped because
[
28
, Lemma 8.20]
directly follows from paracompactness.
Moreover, if
X
has a basis
\mathcal{B}
of size at most
\lambda
and
f\colon X\to Y
is an open continuous surjection, then
\{f(U)\mid U\in\mathcal{B}\}
is a basis for
Y
witnessing
\operatorname{wt}(Y)\leq\lambda
.
It readily follows that Sierpiński’s theorem on open continuous images of Polish spaces can be generalized as follows.

Proposition 3.1.4
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space and
Y
be a metrizable space.
If there is a continuous open surjection of
X
onto
Y
, then
Y
is
\lambda
-Polish as well.

We finally mention that by another result of Sierpiński
[
46
]
, if

2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
then there is a (non necessarily unique) universal

\lambda
-Polish space, that is, a
\lambda
-Polish space
\mathfrak{U}_{\lambda}
in which every

\lambda
-Polish space can be isometrically embedded (as a closed set). However,
\mathfrak{U}_{\lambda}
is not necessarily a higher analogue of the Urysohn space, in the sense that it cannot be

\lambda
-homogeneous
2
2

2

A space
X
is called
\lambda
-homogenous if every
partial
isometry of
X
of size less than
\lambda
can be extended to an auto-isometry of the whole space.

unless
\lambda
is regular (see
[
27
]
).

3.2.
Basic properties

Unless otherwise stated, from now on we let
\lambda>\omega
be a limit cardinal such that
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
. We also fix once and for all a strictly increasing sequence
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda_{i})_{i\in\omega}
of cardinals cofinal in
\lambda
. When
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, so that we have enough choice below
\lambda
to ensure that
\kappa^{+}
is regular for every
\kappa<\lambda
by Fact
2.4.7
(i)
, we further convene that each
\lambda_{i}
is regular.

In particular, for the sake of definiteness we let
C(\lambda)
be the space defined as in Example
3.1.2
(c)
using such cardinals
\lambda_{i}
(the choice of the sequence
\vec{\lambda}
is irrelevant by Remark
3.2.3
below).

The interested reader might appreciate that all results in this section can be proved in
{\sf ZF}
alone. For example,
in the next lemma no choice is actually required because
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
and all its subsets can be canonically well-ordered using Gödel’s enumeration.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
be a pruned tree. Then
[T]\approx B(\lambda)
if and only if

(3.1)

\forall s\in T\,\big(|\{t\in T\mid t\supseteq s\}|\geq\lambda\big).

Proof.

In
B(\lambda)
, every nonempty open set has weight
\lambda
. If (
3.1
) fails as witnessed by some
s\in T
, then the nonempty basic open set
\boldsymbol{N}_{s}([T])
of
[T]
would have weight smaller than
\lambda
, hence
[T]\not\approx B(\lambda)
.

For the other direction, first notice that
condition (
3.1
) is equivalent to

(3.2)

\forall s\in T\,\forall\nu<\lambda\,\exists n>\operatorname{lh}(s)\,\big(|\{t\in\mathrm{Lev}_{n}(T)\mid t\supseteq s\}|\geq\nu\big).

Indeed, if (
3.2
) fails then there are
s\in T
and
\nu<\lambda
such that
|\{t\in\mathrm{Lev}_{n}(T)\mid t\supseteq s\}|<\nu
for all
n>\operatorname{lh}(s)
: this implies that
|\{t\in T\mid t\supseteq s\}|\leq\nu\times\omega<\lambda
, contradicting (
3.1
). The other implication is obvious, as (
3.2
) implies that
|\{t\in T\mid t\supseteq s\}|\geq\nu
for all
\nu<\lambda
.

So let us assume that (
3.2
) holds and prove that
[T]\approx{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}=B(\lambda)
.
Say that
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
is a partition of
t\in T
if the elements of
T^{\prime}
properly extend
t
, are pairwise incomparable, and for all
t\subseteq x\in[T]
there is
t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}
such that
t^{\prime}\subseteq x
. (This is equivalent to requiring that
\{\boldsymbol{N}_{t^{\prime}}([T])\mid t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}\}
is a clopen partition of
\boldsymbol{N}_{t}([T])
, whence the name).

Claim 3.2.1.1
.

For every
t\in T
there is a
canonical
partition of
t
of size
\lambda
.

Proof of the claim.

Fix
t\in T
. Apply (
3.2
) with
s=t
and
\nu=\omega
, and let
n>\operatorname{lh}(t)
be smallest
such that the set
\{t^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Lev}_{n}(T)\mid{t^{\prime}\supseteq t}\}
is infinite. Let
(t_{i})_{i<\alpha}
be the enumeration of such set according to Gödel’s ordering, for the appropriate
\alpha\geq\omega
. For each
i<\omega
, apply
again (
3.2
) with
s=t_{i}
and
\nu=\lambda_{i}
and let

m_{i}>\operatorname{lh}(t_{i})=n
be smallest such that the set
T_{i}=\{t^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Lev}_{m_{i}}(T)\mid t^{\prime}\supseteq t_{i}\}
has size
greater than
\lambda_{i}
. Then

T^{\prime}=\{t_{i}\mid\omega\leq i<\alpha\}\cup\bigcup\nolimits_{i<\omega}T_{i}

is a partition of
t
of size
\lambda
.
∎

Define
\varphi\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\to T
by induction on
\operatorname{lh}(s)
as follows. Set
\varphi(\emptyset)=\emptyset
. Given any
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
, by the claim we have a canonical partition
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
of
\varphi(s)
of size
\lambda
. Enumerate without repetitions (following Gödel’s ordering again)
T^{\prime}
as
(t_{\beta})_{\beta<\lambda}
, and then set
\varphi(s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta)=t_{\beta}
for all
\beta<\lambda
. It is clear
\varphi
is monotone and such that
\operatorname{lh}(\varphi(s))\geq\operatorname{lh}(s)
for all
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
, hence the map

f_{\varphi}\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}\to[T]\colon x\mapsto\bigcup\nolimits_{i<\omega}\varphi(x\restriction i)

is well-defined. By construction,
f_{\varphi}
is a bijection. Moreover, it is a homeomorphism because
f(\boldsymbol{N}_{s})=\boldsymbol{N}_{\varphi(s)}([T])
for all
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
and

\{\boldsymbol{N}_{\varphi(s)}([T])\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
is, by construction, a basis for the topology of
[T]
.
∎

The next theorem directly shows that, under the appropriate hypothesis on
\lambda
, all spaces in items
(c)
–
(e)
of Example
3.1.2
are homeomorphic. Part
(ii)
of Theorem
3.2.2
has been implicitly considered in
[
10
, Section 7]
, although in a slightly different setup.

Theorem 3.2.2
.

(i)

For any sequence
\vec{\lambda}^{\prime}=(\lambda^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\omega}
of cardinals cofinal in
\lambda
,

B(\lambda)\approx{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(\vec{\lambda}^{\prime})}=\prod_{i\in\omega}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}.

In particular,
B(\lambda)\approx C(\lambda)
.

(ii)

If
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, we further have

{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\approx B(\lambda)\approx C(\lambda).

(iii)

If moreover
|V_{\lambda}|=\lambda
, then

V_{\lambda+1}\approx{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\approx B(\lambda)\approx C(\lambda).

Notice that the conditions on
\lambda
in the various items are exactly the ones needed to guarantee that the mentioned spaces are
\lambda
-Polish: so one can briefly say that the spaces
B(\lambda)
,
C(\lambda)
,
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
and
V_{\lambda+1}
are all homeomorphic whenever they are
\lambda
-Polish.

Proof.

(i)

Recall from Section
2.3
that
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(\vec{\lambda}^{\prime})}=[{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\vec{\lambda}^{\prime})}]
.
Since
T={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\vec{\lambda}^{\prime})}
clearly satisfies (
3.1
) (because the
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
are cofinal in
\lambda
), the result follows from Lemma
3.2.1
.

(ii)

The space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
is obviously homeomorphic to
\prod_{i<\omega}{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda_{i}}{2}
where each
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda_{i}}{2}
is endowed with the discrete topology. Thus
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\approx\prod_{i\in\omega}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
where
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}=|{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda_{i}}{2}|<\lambda
(the last inequality follows from
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
). Since
\lambda_{i}<2^{\lambda_{i}}=\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
the
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
are cofinal in
\lambda
, thus the result follows from part
(i)
.

(iii)

For each
i\in\omega
, let
V^{-}_{\lambda_{i}+1}=\{A\setminus V_{\lambda_{i-1}}\mid A\subseteq V_{\lambda_{i}}\}=\mathscr{P}(V_{\lambda_{i}}\setminus V_{\lambda_{i-1}})
and
V_{\lambda_{-1}}=\emptyset
. We are going to prove that
V_{\lambda+1}\approx\prod_{i\in\omega}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
where
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}=|V^{-}_{\lambda_{i}+1}|
; the
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
’s are clearly cofinal in
\lambda
because so are the cardinals
\lambda_{i}
. By part
(i)
, it will then follow that
V_{\lambda+1}\approx C(\lambda)\approx B(\lambda)
, hence also
V_{\lambda+1}\approx{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
by part
(ii)
(which can be applied here because
|V_{\lambda}|=\lambda
implies
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
when
\lambda
is limit).

For each
i\in\omega
, fix a bijection
s_{i}\colon\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\to V^{-}_{\lambda_{i}+1}
. Then
s\colon\prod_{i\in\omega}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\to V_{\lambda+1}
, defined by
s(x)=\bigcup_{i\in\omega}s_{i}(x(i))
for all
x\in\prod_{i\in\omega}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
, is a homeomorphism, since
\{O_{(a,\lambda_{i})}\mid i\in\omega\wedge a\subseteq V_{\lambda_{i}}\}
is a basis for the Woodin topology on
V_{\lambda+1}
.
∎

Remark 3.2.3
.

By Theorem
3.2.2
(i)
, the choice of the sequence
(\lambda_{i})_{i\in\omega}
in the definition of
C(\lambda)
is irrelevant and can be changed when needed (provided that we always work with a sequence cofinal in
\lambda
). In particular, the chosen sequence needs not to be increasing.

Recall that a subset
C\subseteq X
of a topological space
X
is a retract of
X
if there is a continuous function
f\colon X\to C
such that
f(x)=x
for all
x\in C
(in particular,
f
is surjective).
By
[
28
, Proposition 2.8]
we get
3
3

3

As formulated in
[
28
]
, the proof requires a bit of dependent choices when dealing with trees on arbitrary sets
A
. In contrast, we only need to work with trees on the well-ordered set
\lambda
, thus in our case the argument clearly works in
{\sf ZF}
.

Proposition 3.2.4
.

Every nonempty closed subset of
B(\lambda)
is a retract of the entire space.

The next proposition shows in particular that
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}=B(\lambda)\approx C(\lambda)
is surjectively universal for
\lambda
-Polish spaces. It is already stated and proved (in a slightly more precise form, which is not relevant here) in
[
47
, Section 3.3]
.

Proposition 3.2.5
.

Let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish. Then there is a closed set
F\subseteq B(\lambda)
and a continuous bijection
f\colon F\to X
. If moreover
X\neq\emptyset
, then there is a continuous surjection
g\colon B(\lambda)\to X
.

One way to prove Proposition
3.2.5
is to adapt
the proof of
[
28
, Theorem 7.9]
and build an appropriate
\lambda
-scheme on
X
, still requiring the sets in the scheme to be
F_{\sigma}
. This is an illuminating example of the fact that not all classical proofs must be blindly generalized by replacing
\omega
with
\lambda
everywhere: indeed, in this case this would lead us to build a scheme consisting of sets which are union of
\lambda
-many closed sets, a move that unfortunately would not make the argument go through. Because of this subtlety (and for the sake of completeness) we feel that it is fair towards the reader to provide the details of the proof. Moreover, since the argument in
[
28
, Theorem 7.9]
requires a form of dependent choice, which is not necessarily available in our setup, we slightly refine the argument and show that we do not need any choice principle at all.

Proof.

(Compare with
[
28
, Theorem 7.9]
.)
Fix a compatible complete metric
d
on
X
with
d\leq 1
.
By Fact
2.5.1
it is enough to build a
\lambda
-scheme
\{B_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
on
X
such that for all
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
and
\beta,\beta^{\prime}<\lambda

(1)

B_{\emptyset}=X
and
B_{s}=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}
;

(2)

B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}\cap B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta^{\prime}}=\emptyset
if
\beta\neq\beta^{\prime}
;

(3)

\mathrm{cl}(B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})\subseteq B_{s}
;

(4)

\mathrm{diam}(B_{s})\leq 2^{-\operatorname{lh}(s)}
.

We will additionally construct
for each
B_{s}
a family
(C^{s}_{n})_{n\in\omega}
of closed sets such that
B_{s}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}C^{s}_{n}
, so that
B_{s}
is in particular an
F_{\sigma}
set.

The construction is by recursion on
\mathrm{lh}(s)
. Set
B_{\emptyset}=X
and
C^{\emptyset}_{n}=X
for all
n\in\omega
. Now assume that
B_{s}
and
(C^{s}_{n})_{n\in\omega}
have been constructed as above.
Cover
B_{s}
with basic open sets
D_{\alpha}
,
\alpha<\lambda
, with diameter at most
2^{-(\operatorname{lh}(s)+1)}
. Consider the sets

F_{\alpha,n}=\left(D_{\alpha}\setminus\bigcup\nolimits_{\beta<\alpha}D_{\beta}\right)\cap\left(C^{s}_{n}\setminus\bigcup\nolimits_{m<n}C^{s}_{m}\right).

for
\alpha<\lambda
and
n\in\omega
.
Clearly,
\mathrm{cl}(F_{\alpha,n})\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(C^{s}_{n})=C^{s}_{n}\subseteq B_{s}
and
\mathrm{diam}(F_{\alpha,n})\leq\mathrm{diam}(D_{\alpha})\leq 2^{-(\operatorname{lh}(s)+1)}
. Finally,
\bigcup_{\alpha<\beta,n\in\omega}F_{\alpha,n}=B_{s}
because both families
\{C^{s}_{n}\mid n\in\omega\}
and
\{D_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
cover
B_{s}
. Thus it is enough to let
(B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})_{\beta<\nu}
be any enumeration without repetitions of those sets
F_{\alpha,n}
which are nonempty (where
1\leq\nu\leq\lambda
is the cardinality of such family), and

B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\gamma}=\emptyset
for
\nu\leq\gamma<\lambda
if any such
\gamma
exists.
Since by construction each
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}
is a Boolean combination of open sets, by Fact
2.2.4
we can canonically write it as a countable union
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}C^{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}_{n}
of closed sets
C^{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}_{n}
; for
\nu\leq\gamma<\lambda
, we just set
C^{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\gamma}_{n}=\emptyset
for all
n\in\omega
.

The additional part follows from Proposition
3.2.4
.
∎

Remark 3.2.6
.

Let
f
and
F
be as in the proof of Proposition
3.2.5
. Then
f(\boldsymbol{N}_{s}(F))=B_{s}
, hence the image of any basic open set through
f
is an
F_{\sigma}
subset of
X
.

If we drop injectivity, we can require that the continuous surjection
g\colon B(\lambda)\to X
from the above proposition is also open (Corollary
3.2.9
). The easy and short proof of this fact is the natural adaptation of the classical one: our interest in it is that it yields to the technical fact Proposition
3.2.8
that will frequently be used later. Let us first isolate the following notion.

Definition 3.2.7
.

A
tree-basis
for a
\lambda
-Polish space
X
is a family
\mathcal{B}=\{U_{s}\mid s\in T\}
of subsets of
X
indexed by some
\omega
-tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
(called
index tree
of
\mathcal{B}
) such that for all
s\in T
and
\alpha<\lambda
with
s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\alpha\in T

(a)

U_{s}
is a nonempty open set;

(b)

\mathrm{cl}(U_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\alpha})\subseteq U_{s}
;

(c)

U_{\emptyset}=X
and
U_{s}=\bigcup\{U_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}\mid s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta\in T\}
;

(d)

\mathrm{diam}(U_{s})\leq 2^{-\operatorname{lh}(s)}
(where the diameter is computed with respect to any complete metric
d\leq 1
on
X
fixed in advance).

The name tree-basis comes from the fact that every family
\mathcal{B}=\{U_{s}\mid s\in T\}
satisfying conditions
(c)
–
(d)
above is automatically a basis
4
4

4

Given any open ball
B
centered in
x\in X
with radius
2^{-k}
for some
k\in\omega
, by condition
(d)
we have
x\in U_{s}\subseteq B
for any
s\in\mathrm{Lev}_{k+1}(T)
such that
x\in U_{s}
; such an
s
exists because by condition
(c)
, we have
X=\bigcup\{U_{s}\mid s\in\mathrm{Lev}_{n}(T)\}
for every
n\in\omega
.

for the the topology of
X
, whose inclusion relation is “controlled” by its index tree
T
. A tree-basis
\mathcal{B}
clearly constitutes a
\lambda
-scheme on
X
whose induced function is denoted by
f_{\mathcal{B}}
. By Fact
2.5.1
, conditions
(a)
–
(d)
ensure that this is always a continuous open surjection from the closed set
[T]\subseteq B(\lambda)
onto
X
.

Proposition 3.2.8
.

Every nonempty
\lambda
-Polish space
X
admits a tree-basis with index tree the full
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Fix a compatible complete metric
d
on
X
with
d\leq 1
, and a basis for
X
of size at most
\lambda
.
We construct the tree-basis

\mathcal{B}=\{U_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
for
X

by recursion on
\operatorname{lh}(s)
, starting with
U_{\emptyset}=X
. Assuming that
U_{s}
has been defined, let
(U_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})_{\beta<\lambda}
be an enumeration (possibly with repetitions) of all nonempty basic open sets having diameter at most
2^{-(\operatorname{lh}(s)+1)}
and closure contained in
U_{s}
. It is then clear that conditions
(a)
–
(d)
from Definition
3.2.7
are satisfied.
∎

Corollary 3.2.9
.

For every nonempty
\lambda
-Polish space
X
there is a continuous open surjection
g\colon B(\lambda)\to X
.

A partial converse to Corollary
3.2.9
is given by Proposition
3.1.4
. Combining these two results we get a characterization of
\lambda
-Polish spaces depending just on their prototype
B(\lambda)
.

Corollary 3.2.10
.

Let
X
be a nonempty metrizable space.
Then
X
is
\lambda
-Polish
if and only if there is a continuous open surjection
g\colon B(\lambda)\to X
.

Along the same lines, we prove the following result, which will be used in the sequel.
It might be a known fact, at least in the case
\lambda=\omega
, but we could not trace it back in the literature. Also, it does not seem to directly follow from either Proposition
3.2.5
and Corollary
3.2.9
, or their proof.

Proposition 3.2.11
.

Let
\lambda
be a limit cardinal with countable cofinality, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then there is a closed
F\subseteq B(\lambda)
and a continuous surjection
f\colon F\to X
such that
f^{-1}(K)
is compact for every compact
K\subseteq X
.

Proof.

For
n\geq 1
we recursively build canonical open covers
\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
of
X
such that:

(1)

|\mathcal{U}^{(n)}|\leq\lambda
;

(2)

\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
is locally finite;

(3)

U\neq\emptyset
and
\mathrm{diam}(U)\leq 2^{-n}
for every
U\in\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
(where the diameter is computed with respect to any compatible complete metric on
X
fixed in advance);

(4)

for each
U\in\mathcal{U}^{(n+1)}
there is
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
such that
\mathrm{cl}(U)\subseteq U^{\prime}
.

Indeed, at step
n\geq 1
it is enough to consider the family
\mathcal{V}

consisting of all basic open sets
V\subseteq X
such that
\mathrm{diam}(V)\leq 2^{-n}

and
\mathrm{cl}(V)\subseteq U
for some
U\in\mathcal{U}^{(n-1)}
(where

\mathcal{U}^{(0)}=\{X\}
), which clearly is an open cover of
X
, and then refine it in the canonical way
to the desired
\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
using paracompactness and the fact that
\operatorname{wt}(X)\leq\lambda
(see the discussion at the end of Section
2.2
).

Now we label the elements of such open covers with sequences from
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
in order to get a family
\{B_{s}\mid s\in T\}
with
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
a tree satisfying the following conditions:

(a)

B_{\emptyset}=X
;

(b)

if
n\geq 1
, the map
s\mapsto B_{s}
is a bijection between
\mathrm{Lev}_{n}(T)
and
\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
, so that in particular
\mathrm{diam}(B_{s})\leq 2^{-\operatorname{lh}(s)}
;

(c)

\mathrm{cl}(B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\alpha})\subseteq B_{s}
whenever
s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\alpha\in T
.

This can be done recursively on
n=\operatorname{lh}(s)
for
s\in T
by letting e.g.
\{B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\alpha}\mid\alpha<I\}
(for the appropriate
I\leq\lambda
) be an enumeration without repetitions of those
U\in\mathcal{U}^{(n+1)}
such that
\mathrm{cl}(U)\subseteq B_{s}
but
\mathrm{cl}(U)\not\subseteq B_{t}
for all
t\in\mathrm{Lev}_{n}(T)
with
t<_{\mathrm{lex}}s
, where
<_{\mathrm{lex}}
is the strict part of the lexicographical order. (This procedures also determines which
s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\alpha
will be added to
T
; it does not require choice because each
\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
is well-orderable by construction.)

Extend the above family to a
\lambda
-scheme on
X
by setting
B_{s}=\emptyset
for all
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\setminus T
, and let
f
be the induced continuous function. Since for all relevant
s
and
\beta
we have
\mathrm{cl}(B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})\subseteq B_{s}
and
B_{s}\neq\emptyset\iff s\in T
, the domain of
f
will be the closed set
F=[T]
. We claim that
f
is as required.

To check surjectivity, fix any
x\in X
and let
T_{x}=\{t\in T\mid x\in B_{t}\}
, which is clearly a subtree of
T
. The levels
\mathrm{Lev}_{n}(T_{x})
of
T_{x}
are finite. Indeed, given
n\geq 1
fix an open neighborhood
W_{x}
of
x
such that
W_{x}
intersects only finitely many elements of
\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
: since
t\in\mathrm{Lev}_{n}(T_{x})
implies
B_{t}\cap W_{x}\neq\emptyset
, the finiteness of
\mathrm{Lev}_{n}(T_{x})
follows from condition
(b)
. Moreover, each
\mathrm{Lev}_{n}(T_{x})
is nonempty, as
\{B_{t}\mid t\in\mathrm{Lev}_{n}(T)\}=\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
covers
X
. Thus by König’s lemma
5
5

5

Again, no choice is needed for this because
T_{x}\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
can be canonically well-ordered using Gödel’s enumeration of finite sequences over
\lambda
.

there is
y\in[T_{x}]\subseteq[T]
. Since
x\in B_{y\restriction n}
for all
n\in\omega
, it follows that
f(y)=x
, as desired.

Finally, let
K\subseteq X
be compact. The set
f^{-1}(K)
is closed in
F
and thus in
B(\lambda)
.
To show that
f^{-1}(K)
is compact, it is enough to show that for each
n\geq 1
there is a finite set
A^{(n)}_{K}\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{\lambda}
such that
f^{-1}(K)\subseteq\bigcup_{t\in A^{(n)}_{K}}\boldsymbol{N}_{t}
. Indeed, after replacing each
A^{(n)}_{K}
with its subset

\left\{t\in A^{(n)}_{K}\mid t\restriction m\in A^{(m)}_{K}\text{ for every }1\leq m\leq n\right\},

we can assume without loss of generality that
T_{K}=\{\emptyset\}\cup\bigcup_{n\geq 1}A^{(n)}_{K}
is an
\omega
-tree, which is finitely branching by the finiteness of
A^{(n)}_{K}
. Therefore
f^{-1}(K)
, being a closed subset of the compact set
[T_{K}]
, is compact itself.

Let us now construct the required set
A^{(n)}_{K}
, for any fixed
n\geq 1
.
For each
x\in K
let
W_{x}
be the basic open set with smallest index such that
x\in W_{x}
and
W_{x}
intersects only finitely many elements of
\mathcal{U}^{(n)}
. By condition
(b)
, this means that there are just finitely many
t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{\lambda}
such that
B_{t}\cap W_{x}\neq\emptyset
. Since the sets
W_{x}
cover
K
and the latter is compact, there are finitely many
x_{0},\dotsc,x_{\ell}\in K
such that
K\subseteq\bigcup_{m\leq\ell}W_{x_{m}}
: this implies that the set
A^{(n)}_{K}=\{t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{\lambda}\mid B_{t}\cap K\neq\emptyset\}\subseteq\{t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{\lambda}\mid B_{t}\cap\bigcup_{m\leq\ell}W_{x_{m}}\neq\emptyset\}
is finite. To show that
f^{-1}(K)\subseteq\bigcup_{t\in A^{(n)}_{K}}\boldsymbol{N}_{t}
, just notice that if
y\in F
is such that
f(y)\in K
, then
f(y)
witnesses
B_{y\restriction n}\cap K\neq\emptyset
, hence
y\restriction n\in A^{(n)}_{K}
.
∎

3.3.
Lebesgue covering dimension
0

In the literature on general topology, there are a few alternative definitions of
“(Lebesgue) covering dimension
0
”, but all of them are equivalent for metrizable
spaces. We will indeed restrict to such spaces and adopt the following one, which is sometimes referred to as “ultraparacompactness”.

Definition 3.3.1
.

A metrizable space
X
has (
Lebesgue
)
covering dimension
0
, in symbols
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
, if every open covering of
X
can be refined to a clopen partition
6
6

6

A family
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathscr{P}(X)\setminus\{\emptyset\}
is a partition of
X
if
\bigcup\mathcal{P}=X
and
A\cap B=\emptyset
for all distinct
A,B\in\mathcal{P}
; it is a clopen partition if consists of clopen (equivalently, open) subsets of
X
.

of
X
.

Recall that the Lebesgue covering dimension
\mathrm{dim}(X)
, the large inductive dimension
\mathrm{Ind}(X)
, and the small inductive dimension
\mathrm{ind}(X)
all coincide for
separable
metrizable spaces (actually, being normal and second-countable suffices). For arbitrary metrizable spaces
X
this is no longer true. The condition

\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
is always equivalent to
\mathrm{Ind}(X)=0
, and it also implies
\mathrm{ind}(X)=0
(i.e.
X
admits a clopen basis).
7
7

7

More generally, for a metrizable space
X
it holds
\mathrm{ind}(X)\leq\mathrm{Ind}(X)=\mathrm{dim}(X)
.

Nevertheless, in the non-separable context there are complete metric spaces with a clopen basis which have Lebesgue covering dimension greater than
0
(see
[
43
]
): thus we are taking the strongest among the possible topological definitions of zero-dimensionality, a clarification that by the above discussion is not necessary in the classical case
\lambda=\omega
.

Suppose that
X
is a metrizable space such that
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
.
If
X
has a well-orderable basis, any clopen partition
\mathcal{U}
of
X
must be of size at most
\operatorname{wt}(X)
. Indeed, if
\mathcal{B}=\{U_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\operatorname{wt}(X)\}
is a basis for
X
, then the map sending each
U\in\mathcal{U}
into the smallest
\alpha<\operatorname{wt}(X)
such that
\emptyset\neq U_{\alpha}\subseteq U
is a well-defined injection from
\mathcal{U}
to
\operatorname{wt}(X)
.
We will also frequently use the obvious fact that if
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
and
C\subseteq X
is closed, then
\mathrm{dim}(C)=0
. (Recall however that the closedness condition on
C
can actually be dropped: this follows from
[
11
, Corollary 3.1.20]
and the fact that metric spaces are perfectly normal.)

Spaces of sequences of the form
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{\lambda}
(with
\mu,\lambda
arbitrary infinite cardinals) always have Lebesgue covering dimension
0
when equipped with the bounded topology. Indeed, if
\mathcal{U}
is an open covering of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{\lambda}
let

A=\{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\mu}{\lambda}\mid\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\subseteq U\text{ for some }U\in\mathcal{U}\}

and

A^{\prime}=\{s\in A\mid\forall\beta<\operatorname{lh}(s)\,(s\restriction\beta\notin A\}.

Then
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=\{\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\mid s\in A^{\prime}\}
is a clopen partition of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{\lambda}
refining
\mathcal{U}
. In particular,

\mathrm{dim}(B(\lambda))=\mathrm{dim}(C(\lambda))=\mathrm{dim}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})=0.

Proposition 3.3.2
.

If
X
is a
\lambda
-Polish space, then
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
if and only if
X\approx C
for some closed
C\subseteq B(\lambda)
.

We follow the natural generalization of the proof sketched in
[
28
, Theorem 7.8]
. However, it must be noticed that the construction of the desired
\lambda
-scheme from
\mathrm{ind}(X)=0
alone would require the class of basic clopen sets to be closed under intersections of arbitrarily large size below
\lambda
. This is plainly false for nontrivial metrizable
spaces, as closure under infinite intersections (that is,
\omega_{1}
-additivity) already implies that the space is discrete. Our detailed argument below makes evident where the stronger hypothesis
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
is needed for non-separable spaces, and shows that again one does not need an axiom of dependent choices to carry out the recursive constructions.

Proof.

(Compare with
[
28
, Theorem 7.8]
.)
For the nontrivial direction, it is enough to fix a compatible complete metric
d\leq 1
on
X
and build a
\lambda
-scheme
\{B_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
on
X
such that for all
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
and distinct
\beta,\beta^{\prime}<\lambda

(1)

B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}\cap B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta^{\prime}}=\emptyset
;

(2)

B_{\emptyset}=X
and
B_{s}=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}
;

(3)

B_{s}
is clopen and
\mathrm{diam}(B_{s})\leq 2^{-\operatorname{lh}(s)}
.

(Notice that the last condition, together with the definition of
\lambda
-scheme, already gives that
\mathrm{cl}(B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})\subseteq B_{s}
.)

Given
n>0
, let
\mathcal{U}_{n}
be the open cover of
X
consisting of all basic open sets with diameter at most
2^{-n}
. Since
\operatorname{wt}(X)\leq\lambda
and
\dim(X)=0
, for
each
n>0
we can fix
8
8

8

Here we are using
{\sf AC}_{\lambda}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
, but actually
{\sf AC}_{\omega}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
would suffice. The same holds for the proof of Theorem
3.3.4
, where we use the clopen partitions
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{n}
again.

a well-orderable clopen partition
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{n}=\{V^{(n)}_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\nu_{n}\}
(for some
\nu_{n}\leq\lambda
) which refines
\mathcal{U}_{n}
.

The construction of the sets
B_{s}
is then by recursion on
\mathrm{lh}(s)
, starting with
B_{\emptyset}=X
. Suppose that
B_{s}
satisfies the above conditions.
Then it is enough to let
(B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})_{\beta<\nu}
be an enumeration without repetitions, for the appropriate
\nu
, of the nonempty clopen sets of the form
B_{s}\cap V^{(\operatorname{lh}(s)+1)}_{\alpha}
with
\alpha<\nu_{\operatorname{lh}(s)+1}
, and
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\gamma}=\emptyset
for
\nu\leq\gamma<\lambda
if there is any such
\gamma
.
∎

This in particular entails
[
47
, Theorem 2]
, i.e. that every
G_{\delta}
of
B(\lambda)
is homeomorphic to a closed subspace of
B(\lambda)
. Moreover, combining the previous results with Proposition
3.2.4
we also get the following result, stated (in a more precise form) as Theorem 3 and Theorem
3^{\prime}
in
[
47
]
.

Corollary 3.3.3
.

If
X
is
\lambda
-Polish and
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
, then every nonempty closed subset of
X
is a retract of the entire space.

The following theorem gives a characterization of
B(\lambda)={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}
. By Theorem
3.2.2
, it also characterizes
C(\lambda)
, the generalized Cantor space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
(if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
), and Woodin’s
V_{\lambda+1}
(if
|V_{\lambda}|=\lambda
).
Notice also that the characterization using condition
(2d)
naturally generalizes the Alexandrov-Urysohn characterization of the classical Baire space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
, see e.g.
[
28
, Theorem 7.7]
. (Recall that by definition
\omega
-Lindelöf is just compactness.) The characterization using
(2b)
(which actually works for arbitrary infinite cardinals
\lambda
and not just for the case
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
) is essentially due to Stone, see
[
47
, Theorem 1]
.

Theorem 3.3.4
.

The space
B(\lambda)
is the unique
\lambda
-Polish space
X
such that:

(1)

\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
;

(2)

X
is everywhere of weight
\lambda
, i.e. every (cl)open subspace of
X
has weight
\lambda
.

In
(2)
we can of course replace “weight” with “density character”.
Moreover, such condition can be replaced by any of the following:

(2a)

Every (cl)open subset of
X
has discrete subsets of size
\nu
, for all
\nu<\lambda
.

(2b)

Each (cl)open subset of
X
has a discrete subset of size
\lambda
.

(2c)

For every
\nu<\lambda
, all
\nu
-Lindelöf subsets of
X
have empty interior.

(2d)

Every
\lambda
-Lindelöf subset of
X
has empty interior.

(2e)

\operatorname{wt}(X)=\lambda
and

X
is strongly homogeneous (or
h
-homogeneous), that is, every nonempty clopen subset of
X
is homeomorphic to
X
.

If
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible,then one can further replace
(2)
with any of the following conditions:

(2f)

Every (cl)open subsets of
X
has cardinality at least
\lambda
.

(2g)

Every (cl)open subsets of
X
has cardinality greater than
\lambda
.

(2h)

Every (cl)open subsets of
X
has cardinality
\lambda^{\omega}
.

(2i)

B(\lambda)
can be embedded as a clopen set into any (cl)open subset of
X
.

Finally, if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
(and thus, in particular,
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible), then in the above statements we can replace all occurrences of
B(\lambda)
and
\lambda^{\omega}
with
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
and
2^{\lambda}
, respectively.

Proof.

It is clear that
B(\lambda)
has properties
(1)
–
(2)
, as well as all the variants
(2a)
–
(2i)
of
(2)

(for
(2g)
we are using the fact that
\lambda^{\omega}=\lambda^{{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)}>\lambda
).

To prove the first characterization, by Lemma
3.2.1

it is enough to show that if
X
is a
\lambda
-Polish space satisfying conditions
(1)
–
(2)
, then there is a pruned tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
satisfying (
3.1
) and such that
X\approx[T]
. To this aim, we fix a compatible complete metric
d\leq 1
on
X
and build a
\lambda
-scheme
\{B_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
on
X
such that for all
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
and distinct
\beta,\beta^{\prime}<\lambda

(1)

B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}\cap B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta^{\prime}}=\emptyset
;

(2)

B_{\emptyset}=X
and
B_{s}=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}
;

(3)

B_{s}
is clopen and
\mathrm{diam}(B_{s})\leq 2^{-\operatorname{lh}(s)}
;

(4)

if
B_{s}\neq\emptyset
, then
|\{\beta<\lambda\mid B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}\neq\emptyset\}|\geq\lambda_{\operatorname{lh}(s)}
.

(The latter condition is to ensure that the tree
T=\{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\mid B_{s}\neq\emptyset\}
satisfies condition (
3.1
), as required.)

As usual, the construction is by recursion on
\mathrm{lh}(s)
.
Set
B_{\emptyset}=X
and
let
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{n}=\{V^{(n)}_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\nu_{n}\}
be as in the proof of Proposition
3.3.2
. Next assume that
B_{s}
satisfies all the above conditions. We can assume that
B_{s}\neq\emptyset
(otherwise we just set
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}=\emptyset
for every
\beta<\lambda
). For each
n>\operatorname{lh}(s)
let
\mathcal{P}_{n}
be the (well-orderable) clopen partition of
B_{s}
given by the nonempty sets of the form
B_{s}\cap V^{(n)}_{\alpha}
with
\alpha<\nu_{n}
. Then we must have
|\mathcal{P}_{n}|\geq\lambda_{\mathrm{lh}(s)}
for some
n>\mathrm{lh}(s)
: otherwise the family
\mathcal{B}=\bigcup_{n>\mathrm{lh}(s)}\mathcal{P}_{n}
would have size ta most
\lambda_{\mathrm{lh}(s)}\times\omega=\lambda_{\mathrm{lh}(s)}
, and since
\mathcal{B}
is a basis for
B_{s}
this would imply
\operatorname{wt}(B_{s})<\lambda
, a contradiction. Let
n>\mathrm{lh}(s)
be smallest such that
|\mathcal{P}_{n}|\geq\lambda_{\mathrm{lh}(s)}
and let
(B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})_{\beta<|\mathcal{P}_{n}|}
be an enumeration without repetitions of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}=\emptyset
for
|\mathcal{P}_{n}|\leq\beta<\lambda
: the
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}
are clearly as required.

We now come to the alternative characterizations. It is enough to show that for any
\lambda
-Polish space
X
, condition
(2)
is implied by any of its variants. In particular, since
(2b)

\Rightarrow

(2a)
,

(2d)

\Rightarrow

(2c)
,

(2i)

\Rightarrow

(2h)
(when
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible),

(2h)

\Rightarrow

(2g)
, and
(2g)

\Rightarrow

(2f)
, it is enough to consider the case of
(2a)
,
(2c)
,
(2e)
, and
(2f)
.

For the first one, observe that any discrete set in a topological space with a well-orderable basis is well-orderable itself and has size at most the weight of the space (this gives the contrapositive of
(2a)

\Rightarrow

(2)
because
\lambda
is limit).
For the second one, notice that if a topological space has weight
\nu<\lambda
, then it is trivially
\nu^{+}
-Lindelöf: applied to (cl)open subsets of
X
and using the fact that
\nu^{+}<\lambda
because
\lambda
is limit, this gives the contrapositive of
(2c)

\Rightarrow

(2)
.
The implication
(2e)

\Rightarrow

(2)
is straightforward.
Finally, the contrapositive of
(2f)

\Rightarrow

(2)
follows from Lemma
2.4.4
.
∎

3.4.
\lambda
-Perfect spaces

The following definition generalizes the notion of isolated point and perfect space to arbitrary cardinals
\lambda
. As usual, setting
\lambda=\omega
one recovers (up to equivalence) the classical definitions.

Definition 3.4.1
.

Let
X
be a topological space and
\lambda
be an infinite cardinal. A point
x\in X
is
\lambda
-isolated
in
X
if there is an open neighborhood
U
of
x
such that
|U|<\lambda
. The space
X
is
\lambda
-perfect
if it has no
\lambda
-isolated points. A subspace
P\subseteq X
is
\lambda
-perfect

(
in
X
) if it is closed and
\lambda
-perfect as a subspace.

Since the above definition crucially refers to the cardinality of subsets of
X
, in view of Lemma
2.4.4
it becomes more meaningful when
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible and
X
is metrizable. Moreover, in such a situation
a point in
X
is
\lambda
-isolated if and only if it has an open neighborhood of weight smaller than
\lambda
(by Lemma
2.4.4
again). Thus for such a
\lambda
, a metrizable space
X
with a well-orderable basis is
\lambda
-perfect if and only if it is everywhere of weight at least
\lambda
.

This immediately provides a further variant of the characterizations in Theorem
3.3.4
. It is reminiscent of Brouwer’s characterization of the Cantor space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}
(see e.g.
[
28
, Theorem 7.4]
), but notably dropping any compactness condition.

Theorem 3.4.2
.

Assume that
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible.
The space
B(\lambda)
is the unique nonempty
\lambda
-perfect
\lambda
-Polish space
X
with
\dim(X)=0
. If
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, the same applies to the generalized Cantor space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
.

We now consider
\lambda
-Polish spaces of arbitrary (Lebesgue covering) dimension, and study when
B(\lambda)
can be (topologically) embedded into them.
Two of the well-known embedding theorems for classical Polish spaces
X
are the following:

(1)

C(\omega)\approx{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}
embeds into
X
(necessarily as a closed set, by compactness of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}
) if and only if
|X|>\omega
(see
[
28
, Corollary 6.5]
);

(2)

B(\omega)={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
embeds into
X
as a closed set if and only if
X
is not
\sigma
-compact, i.e. it cannot be written as a countable union of compact sets (see
[
28
, Theorem 7.10]
).

Notice that both statements are of the form: a certain canonical space can be embedded
into
X
(as a closed set) if and only if
X
is not too small. In our generalized setting

\lambda>\omega
, the two statements tend to collapse into a unique one. Indeed,
on the one hand
B(\lambda)\approx C(\lambda)
by
Theorem
3.2.2
; on the other hand, any set of size at most

\lambda
is trivially
\lambda
-compact (that is, a union of

\lambda
-many compact sets), and the converse holds as well
if
2^{\aleph_{0}}\leq\lambda
because compact metrizable spaces have size at most
2^{\aleph_{0}}
. Things would not change if instead of considering compactness we consider
\lambda
-Lindelöfness. It will follow from our results (Corollary
3.4.11
) that if
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible, then any
\lambda
-Lindelöf
\lambda
-Polish space is well-orderable and has size at most
\lambda
. (The same applies of course to spaces which are
\lambda
-sized unions of closed, or even just
G_{\delta}
,
\lambda
-Lindelöf subsets of a
\lambda
-Polish space.)

Together with Corollary
2.4.5
,
the above discussion
entails that
the following would be the common analogue of both statements
(1)
and
(2)
when

\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible and
X
is
\lambda
-Polish:

B(\lambda)
embeds into
X
(as a closed set) if and only if
|X|>\lambda
.

This will be proved in Corollary
3.4.8
(recall that in our setup
\lambda^{\omega}=\lambda^{{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)}>\lambda
). The nontrivial direction of that result follows from the more technical Theorem
3.4.3
below, which holds without any further assumption on
\lambda
: its essence is that
B(\lambda)
embeds into any
\lambda
-Polish space which is everywhere large enough.

Theorem 3.4.3
.

Let
X
be a completely metrizable space which is everywhere of density character
\lambda
. Then
B(\lambda)
embeds into
X
as a closed set.

In particular, the theorem applies to all
\lambda
-Polish spaces satisfying condition
(2)
of Theorem
3.3.4
.

Proof.

Let
D
be a well-orderable dense subset of
X
.
By Lemma
3.2.1
it is enough to show that
there is a pruned tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
satisfying (
3.1
) and such that
[T]
embeds into
X
as a closed set. To this aim, we fix a compatible complete metric
d\leq 1
on
X
and build a
\lambda
-scheme
\{B_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
on
X
together with a family
\{r_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\setminus\{\emptyset\}\}
of positive reals such that for all
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
and distinct
\beta,\beta^{\prime}<\lambda
:

(1)

B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}\cap B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta^{\prime}}=\emptyset
;

(2)

B_{\emptyset}=X
,
B_{s}
is open, and
\mathrm{diam}(B_{s})\leq 2^{-\operatorname{lh}(s)}
;

(3)

\mathrm{cl}(B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})\subseteq B_{s}
;

(4)

the distance between
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}
and
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta^{\prime}}
is at least
r_{s}
(if they are both nonempty);

(5)

if
B_{s}\neq\emptyset
, then
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
\beta<\lambda_{\operatorname{lh}(s)}
.

The latter condition is to ensure that the tree
T=\{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\mid B_{s}\neq\emptyset\}
is as required (indeed, we get
[T]=C(\lambda)
), while condition
(4)
, together with
(3)
, ensures that the range of the embedding induced by the
\lambda
-scheme is closed.

As usual, the construction of the
\lambda
-scheme (and of the reals
r_{s}
) is by recursion on
\operatorname{lh}(s)
, starting with
B_{\emptyset}=X
.
Assume that
B_{s}
satisfies all the above conditions. If
B_{s}=\emptyset
it is enough to set
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}=\emptyset
for all
\beta<\lambda
(and
r_{s}>0
be arbitrary), hence we can assume
B_{s}\neq\emptyset
. For
\lambda_{\operatorname{lh}(s)}\leq\beta<\lambda
, set
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}=\emptyset
. Then apply Lemma
2.4.1
with
Y=B_{s}
and
\nu=\lambda_{\operatorname{lh}(s)}
and enumerate the resulting
r
-spaced set as
(x_{\beta})_{\beta<\lambda_{\operatorname{lh}(s)}}
: by Remark
2.4.2
such a set and the real
r
can be canonically extracted from the well-orderable set
D\cap B_{s}
, which is clearly dense in
B_{s}
. Set
r_{s}=r/3
and, for each
\beta<\lambda_{\operatorname{lh}(s)}
, set
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}=B_{d}(x_{\beta},2^{-n})
where
0\neq n\in\omega
is smallest such
2^{-n}\leq\min\{2^{-(\operatorname{lh}(s)+2)},r_{s}\}
and
B_{d}(x_{\beta},2^{-(n-1)})\subseteq B_{s}
.
∎

In view of the fact that, when
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible,
\lambda
-perfectness amounts to require that the
\lambda
-Polish space be everywhere of weight
\lambda
, we get:

Corollary 3.4.4
.

Assume that
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible. Then
B(\lambda)
can be embedded as a closed set into any nonempty
\lambda
-perfect
\lambda
-Polish space.

Theorem 3.4.5
.

Let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish. Then
X
can be written as a disjoint union
P\cup C
, where
P
is
\lambda
-perfect and
C
is a well-orderable open set of size at most
\lambda
. If
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible, then such a decomposition is unique and the set
P
is called
\lambda
-perfect kernel
of
X
.

Although the proof follows the classical argument (see e.g.
[
28
, Theorem 6.4]
), we report it here for the sake of completeness and to make it evident where
\omega
-inaccessibility is needed in the second part.

Proof.

Let
C
be the union of all basic open sets
U
such that
|U|\leq\lambda
and set
P=X\setminus C
. Then
C
is open and
|C|\leq\lambda
by Fact
2.4.7
(ii)
. It follows that
P
is closed and also
\lambda
-perfect: if
U\cap P
is a nonempty (relative) basic open subset of
P
with
|U\cap P|<\lambda
, then
|U|\leq\lambda
because
U\setminus P\subseteq C
, hence we would have
U\subseteq C
, in contradiction with
P\cap U\neq\emptyset
.

Assume now that
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible and let
X=P^{\prime}\cup C^{\prime}
with
9
9

9

Interestingly, we do not need to assume
P^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime}=\emptyset
. Indeed, it is a corollary of the proof that if
P^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}
are as required, then they must necessarily be disjoint.

P^{\prime}
a
\lambda
-perfect set and
C^{\prime}
open such that
|C^{\prime}|\leq\lambda
.
Clearly
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
by definition of
C
. Let
x\in P^{\prime}
be arbitrary, and
assume towards a contradiction that
x\in C
. Then by definition of
C
there would be some basic open neighborhood
U
of
x
such that
|U|\leq\lambda
. On the other hand,
the space
Y=U\cap P^{\prime}
is
\lambda
-Polish because it is a
G_{\delta}
-subset of
X
(Fact
2.2.4
). Moreover it is
\lambda
-perfect because it is relatively open in the
\lambda
-perfect space
P^{\prime}
, and it is nonempty because
x\in Y
. Hence the space
B(\lambda)
embeds into
Y
by Corollary
3.4.4
. Since
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
we then have
|U|\geq|U\cap P^{\prime}|\geq|B(\lambda)|=\lambda^{\omega}>\lambda
, contradicting the choice of
U
. Thus
x\in P=X\setminus C
, and since
x
was an arbitrary element of
P^{\prime}
, this shows
P^{\prime}\subseteq P
. Since
X=P^{\prime}\cup C^{\prime}
, it then follows
P^{\prime}=P
and
C^{\prime}=C
, as desired.
∎

Combining this with Theorem
3.4.2
, we get the following curious phenomenon.

Corollary 3.4.6
.

Assume that
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible and that
X
is a
\lambda
-Polish space with
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
. Then the
\lambda
-perfect kernel of
X
, if nonempty, is homeomorphic to
B(\lambda)
.

This is in strong contrast with the situation in the classical setup
\lambda=\omega
, where we can find many different kinds of perfect kernels of zero-dimensional Polish spaces.

Remark 3.4.7
.

As for the classical case
\lambda=\omega
, one can obtain the decomposition
P\cup C
of
X
as in Theorem
3.4.5
via (generalized) Cantor-Bendixson derivatives. More precisely, given a
\lambda
-Polish space
Y
let

Y^{\prime}=\{y\in Y\mid y\text{ is not $\lambda$-isolated in }Y\}

be the
\lambda
-Cantor-Bendixson derivative of
Y
, and notice that
Y^{\prime}
is closed in
Y
and
|Y\setminus Y^{\prime}|\leq\lambda
. This operation can be iteratively applied to our
\lambda
-Polish space
X
as follows (the definition is by recursion on all ordinals):

\displaystyle X^{(0)}

\displaystyle=X

\displaystyle X^{(\alpha+1)}

\displaystyle=(X^{(\alpha)})^{\prime}

\displaystyle X^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{\beta<\gamma}X^{(\beta)}\qquad\text{if $\gamma$ is limit}.

Since
X
has weight at most
\lambda
and all the sets
X^{(\beta)}
are closed, there is
\alpha<\lambda^{+}
such that
X^{(\alpha)}=X^{(\alpha+1)}
, that is
X^{(\alpha)}
is
\lambda
-perfect. It then follows that
P=X^{(\alpha)}
is the
\lambda
-perfect kernel of
X
(whenever this terminology makes sense, i.e. if
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible).

Arguing as in the classical case, Corollary
3.4.4
and Theorem
3.4.5
yields to the following dichotomy.

Corollary 3.4.8
.

Assume that
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible
and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then either
X
is well-orderable and
|X|\leq\lambda
, or else
B(\lambda)
embeds into
X
as a closed set (and hence
|X|=\lambda^{\omega}
).

By Theorem
3.2.2
we also get the following analogue of
[
28
, Corollary 6.5]
.

Corollary 3.4.9
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda

and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then either
X
is well-orderable and
|X|\leq\lambda
, or else
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
embeds into
X
as a closed set (and thus
|X|=2^{\lambda}
).

Remark 3.4.10
.

It is not possible to extend Corollaries
3.4.4
and
3.4.8
to cardinals
\lambda
of countable cofinality which are not
\omega
-inaccessible, even when restricting to
\lambda
-Polish spaces of maximal weight
\lambda
. Indeed, if
\lambda
is not
\omega
-inaccessible then by definition there is
\kappa<\lambda
such that
\kappa^{\omega}\not<\lambda
. Letting
X
be the sum of
\lambda
-many copies of
B(\kappa)
we get a
\lambda
-Polish space of weight
\lambda
and cardinality greater than
\lambda
which is
\lambda
-perfect, yet
B(\lambda)
cannot embed into it. Indeed, on the one hand every nonempty open subset of
X
has size at least
\kappa^{\omega}
, so no point of
X
is
\lambda
-isolated by the choice of
\kappa
. On the other hand, since
\operatorname{wt}(B(\kappa))=\kappa<\lambda

the preimage of each copy of
B(\kappa)
under a topological embedding should have weight at most
\kappa
, but this is impossible because every nonempty open subset of
B(\lambda)
has weight
\lambda
. A similar observation applies to Corollary
3.4.9
: if
\lambda
is singular but not strong limit, then
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
has weight
2^{<\lambda}>\lambda
and thus cannot be embedded into any
\lambda
-Polish space.

We conclude this section by proving the announced results on
\lambda
-Lindelöf
\lambda
-Polish spaces.

Corollary 3.4.11
.

Let
\lambda
be
\omega
-inaccessible. If a
\lambda
-Polish space
X
is
\lambda
-Lindelöf, then
X
is well-orderable and
|X|\leq\lambda
.

Proof.

If not, by Corollary
3.4.8
the space
B(\lambda)
would embed as a closed set in
X
and would thus be
\lambda
-Lindelöf as well, which is obviously false.
∎

3.5.
\lambda
-Polish spaces for other cofinalities

So far, in this chapter on
\lambda
-Polish spaces we just considered the case when
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
because that is the main focus of the present paper. However, most of the results can in fact be proved for arbitrary uncountable cardinals
\lambda
, and actually some of the proofs even simplify when
\lambda
has uncountable cofinality. In what follows we thus assume that
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)>\omega
(which in particular implies
\lambda>\omega
) and discuss which results from Sections
3.1
–
3.4

still hold in this other setup. For the sake of brevity we just sketch the part of the proofs that need to be modified, leaving to the interested reader to fill in all the details.

First of all, notice that in the new setup
C(\lambda)
is undefined while
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
and
\mathrm{V}_{\lambda+1}
are not
\lambda
-Polish because they are not even first-countable, hence there cannot be any analogue of Theorem
3.2.2
. However, the more technical Lemma
3.2.1
continue to be true: this is just for the records, as we do not have any use for this fact at the moment. Propositions
3.2.4
,
3.2.5
, and
3.2.8
, Corollaries
3.2.9
and
3.2.10
, and Proposition
3.2.11
holds as well (the proofs are identical).

We next move to
\lambda
-Polish spaces
X
with
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
. Both Proposition
3.3.2
and Corollary
3.3.3
clearly remain true (their proofs go unchanged). Also the characterizations of
B(\lambda)
in Theorem
3.3.4
work in the more general setting, with the following exceptions and restrictions:

•

conditions
(2a)
and
(2c)
can be included only when
\lambda
is limit;
10
10

10

Notice however that when
\lambda
is not limit, we can still directly prove
(2b)

\Rightarrow

(2)
and
(2d)

\Rightarrow

(2)
, by-passing the missing conditions
(2a)
and
(2c)
.

•

condition
(2g)
must be removed: if
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible of uncountable cofinality, then
\lambda^{\omega}=\lambda
and thus
|B(\lambda)|=\lambda
(in a sense this indicates that such setting is less natural when studying spaces of the form
B(\lambda)
, see below for a more thorough discussion on this issue);

•

the additional part concerning
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
must be removed, as such space is not even
\lambda
-Polish.

Some parts of the proof of Theorem
3.3.4
need to be adapted as well. In particular, in the proof of the main characterization (i.e. the one involving condition
(2)
), we now have to
11
11

11

Any space of the form
\prod_{i\in\omega}\lambda_{i}
has weight
\sup_{i\in\omega}\lambda_{i}
. If
\lambda_{i}<\lambda
for all but finitely many
i\in\omega
and
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)>\omega
, then such a space cannot be homeomorphic to
B(\lambda)
because it would contain clopen sets of weight smaller than
\lambda
. If instead
\lambda_{i}=\lambda
for infinitely many
i\in\omega
, then
\prod_{i\in\omega}\lambda_{i}
would be homeomorphic to
B(\lambda)
but then in the proof it would no longer be useful to consider such a space instead of directly working with
B(\lambda)
.

directly require that
B_{s}\neq\emptyset

for all
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
(this forces the resulting tree
T
to be the full
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
, whence the induced map is a homeomorphism between
B(\lambda)
itself and
X
). This is possible because, in the notation of the proof of Theorem
3.3.4
, we can now infer that
|\mathcal{P}_{n}|=\lambda
for some
n\geq\operatorname{lh}(s)
, as otherwise the basis
\mathcal{B}=\bigcup_{n\geq\operatorname{lh}(s)}\mathcal{P}_{n}
would be such that
|\mathcal{B}|=\sup_{n\geq\operatorname{lh}(s)}|\mathcal{P}_{n}|<\lambda
(here is where we use the fact that we are now assuming
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)>\omega
).

Finally, let us consider
\lambda
-perfect
\lambda
-Polish spaces.
Theorem
3.4.2
obviously remains true.
Since now
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)>\omega
, one can also improve the final part of Lemma
2.4.1
by further requiring that
|A|=\lambda
.
Indeed, assume
\operatorname{dens}(X)=\lambda
and define the sets
B_{n}
as in the proof of Lemma
2.4.1
: if
|B_{n}|<\lambda
for all
n\in\omega
, then the dense set
B=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}B_{n}
would have size
\sup_{n\in\omega}|B_{n}|<\lambda=\operatorname{dens}(X)
, a contradiction.
This allows us to reprove Theorem
3.4.3
in our new setup, and thus also Corollary
3.4.4
. Formally, the decomposition Theorem
3.4.5
holds as well, except for the fact that if
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible the decomposition might not be unique:
12
12

12

Notice however that the uniqueness part is not needed for the other results.

for a counterexample, let
X
be any
\lambda
-Polish space and observe that we can equivalently take
P=\emptyset
(this works because
|X|=\lambda^{\omega}=\lambda
by Fact
2.4.3
and the fact that
\lambda
is an
\omega
-inaccessible cardinal with uncountable cofinality) or
P=X
. These observations trivialize the subsequent Corollaries
3.4.8
and
3.4.9
as stated. (Notice also that the second alternative in the latter is out of question because
2^{\lambda}>\lambda
while, as argued, all
\lambda
-Polish spaces have size at most
\lambda
if
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible and has uncountable cofinality.) However, some of their technical variants can be nontrivial also when
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)>\omega
. For example,
using the Cantor-Bendixson derivation argument one can restate
Corollary
3.4.8
as: For each
\lambda
-Polish space
X
, either
X=\bigcup_{\alpha<\rho}V_{\alpha}
where
\rho<\lambda^{+}
and
(V_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\rho}
is an increasing sequence of open sets such that
|V_{\alpha+1}\setminus V_{\alpha}|<\lambda
for all
\alpha<\rho
, or else
B(\lambda)
embeds into
X
as a closed set.

The above discussion hints to the fact that, although some of our results trivialize in the
uncountable cofinality case, it makes sense to consider and study the class of

\lambda
-Polish spaces also when
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)>\omega
. However, it is then
unreasonable to proceed with the development of a
generalized
descriptive set
theory for such spaces, as we plan to do in the next chapters. This is due to the fact that, as already mentioned, all
\lambda
-Polish spaces have size at most

\lambda^{\omega}
by Fact
2.4.3
. Thus if
\lambda^{\omega}=\lambda
, which is always the case if
e.g.
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)>\omega
and we work in a model of
{\sf GCH}
, or if
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)>\omega
and
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible, then all

\lambda
-Polish spaces have size at most
\lambda
.
This immediately trivializes the notion of
\lambda^{+}
-Borel
set that will be introduced at the very beginning of Chapter
4
, as well as all subsequent definability
notions. In contrast, notice that it makes perfectly sense to develop
classical

descriptive set theory (that is, the theory of classical (
\omega_{1}
-)Borel sets, (
\omega
-)analytic
sets, and so on) for
\lambda
-Polish spaces, independently of
\lambda
(see
e.g.
[
47
,
48
]
).

Despite the above observation, it is interesting to notice that
\kappa
-Polish spaces for
\kappa\geq\lambda
will indeed play a role in the definition of
\kappa
-Souslin subsets of
\lambda
-Polish spaces. This is a notion which is relevant to our study of definable subsets of
\lambda
-Polish spaces when
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
(Section
5.7
).

Chapter 4
\lambda^{(+)}
-Borel sets

4.1.
Definitions and basic facts

The following definition naturally generalizes to an arbitrary
\nu
the usual notion of a Borel set (which corresponds to the case
\nu=\omega
).

Definition 4.1.1
.

Let
X,Y
be topological spaces and
\nu
be an infinite cardinal. A set
B\subseteq X
is
\nu^{+}
-Borel
if it belongs to the
\nu^{+}
-algebra generated by the open sets of
X
. The collection of
\nu^{+}
-Borel subsets of
X
is denoted by
\nu^{+}\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
.

A function
f\colon X\to Y
is
\nu^{+}
-Borel
(
measurable
) if the preimage of any open (equivalently,
\nu^{+}
-Borel) subset of
Y
is
\nu^{+}
-Borel in
X
.
A function
f\colon X\to Y
is a
\nu^{+}
-Borel isomorphism
if it is bijective and both
f
and
f^{-1}
are
\nu^{+}
-Borel; when such an
f
exists, we say that
X
and
Y
are
\nu^{+}
-Borel isomorphic
.
Finally, a
\nu^{+}
-Borel embedding
is a function
f\colon X\to Y
which is a
\nu^{+}
-Borel isomorphism on its image.

Thus
\omega_{1}\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
coincides with the collection of all (classical) Borel sets, i.e. it is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the topology of
X
. We will of course be mostly interested in the case
\nu=\lambda
. Since in our setup
\lambda
is singular, the collection of
\lambda^{+}
-Borel subsets of
X
may equivalently be described as the smallest
\lambda
-algebra containing all open sets. For this reason, we drop the “
+
” from the above terminology and notation and just speak e.g. of
\lambda
-Borel sets and functions. Accordingly, we denote by
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
the collection of all
\lambda
-Borel subsets of
X
, dropping the reference to
X
when there is no danger of confusion, and writing instead
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)
when it is necessary to specify the topology
\tau
on
X
we are referring to. Notice also that if
Y\subseteq X
then
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(Y)=\{B\cap Y\mid B\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)\}
.

Remark 4.1.2
.

When
X={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
and
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, the bounded topology and the product topology on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
generate the same

\lambda^{+}
-Borel sets. In fact, the bounded topology
\tau_{b}
is finer than the product topology. Conversely, every
\tau_{b}
-basic open set
\boldsymbol{N}_{s}
is closed in the product topology, thus the result easily follows once we impose that there are only
\lambda
-many
\tau_{b}
-basic open sets, namely,
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.

Arguing as in the classical case (see
[
28
, Proposition 12.4]
), one easily gets:

Proposition 4.1.3
.

Let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish and
Y
be a metrizable
1
1

1

Actually, it is enough that
Y
be a
T_{0}
-space.

space of weight at most
\lambda
. If
f\colon X\to Y
is
\lambda
-Borel, then its graph

\mathrm{graph}(f)=\{(x,y)\in X\times Y\mid f(x)=y\}

is
\lambda
-Borel in
X\times Y
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{B}=\{U_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
be a basis for
Y
, and notice that for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y

f(x)=y\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\forall\alpha<\lambda\,(y\in U_{\alpha}\iff f(x)\in U_{\alpha}).\qed

If
Y
is
\lambda
-Polish
the converse of the above theorem holds as well, as we will show in Theorem
5.3.6
.

4.2.
The
\lambda
-Borel hierarchy

In the rest of this chapter, we will frequently use the fact that we are assuming
{\sf AC}_{\lambda}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
in the background. For example, when arguing that the length of the
\lambda
-Borel hierarchy is (at most)
\lambda^{+}
we need that
\lambda^{+}
be regular,
and under
{\sf AC}_{\lambda}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
this is true by Fact
2.4.7
.
Assume from now on that
X
is metrizable.

The
\lambda^{+}
-algebra
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
can be naturally stratified as follows. Define by recursion on the ordinal
\xi\geq 1

\begin{cases}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(X)=\text{open sets}&\\
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)=\left\{\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}A_{\alpha}\,\,\big|\,\,X\setminus A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)\text{ for some }\xi^{\prime}<\xi\right\},&\text{if }\xi>1\end{cases}

and then set

\displaystyle\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)

\displaystyle=\{X\setminus A\mid A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)\}

\displaystyle\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X)

\displaystyle=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)\cap\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X).

Arguing as in the classical case, it is not hard to see that for all
1\leq\xi<\xi^{\prime}

(4.1)

\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X),\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X),\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X),

and that since
\lambda^{+}
is regular

\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)=\bigcup_{1\leq\xi<\lambda^{+}}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)=\bigcup_{1\leq\xi<\lambda^{+}}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)=\bigcup_{1\leq\xi<\lambda^{+}}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X).

(Here we use that closed sets are
G_{\delta}
by Fact
2.2.4
, and hence elements of
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(X)
.) One can also easily check by induction on
\xi\geq 1
that
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
if and only if
A=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}B_{\alpha}
with
B_{\alpha}\in\bigcup_{\xi^{\prime}<\xi}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)
and, dually,
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
if and only if
A=\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}B_{\alpha}
with
B_{\alpha}\in\bigcup_{\xi^{\prime}<\xi}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)
. If
\xi=\xi^{\prime}+1
, this further reduces to the fact that
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
if and only if
A=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}B_{\alpha}
with
B_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)
, and analogously for
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
. Arguing again by induction on
\xi\geq 1
, it is also easy to verify that all the above mentioned classes of sets are closed under finite unions, finite intersections, continuous preimages, and moreover:
2
2

2

Here we are again heavily relying on the fact that we assumed
{\sf AC}_{\lambda}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
, together with the fact that for each
\xi^{\prime}\geq 1
there are canonical surjections from
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
onto the classes
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)
and
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)
, and thus also onto the classes
\bigcup_{\xi^{\prime}<\xi}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)
and
\bigcup_{\xi^{\prime}<\xi}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)
for all
1\leq\xi<\lambda^{+}
. Altogether, this ensures that e.g. a
\lambda
-sized union of sets which are
\lambda
-sized unions of element of one of those classes is still a
\lambda
-sized union of elements of the same class.

•

\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
is closed
under well-ordered unions of length at most
\lambda
;

•

\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
is closed under well-ordered intersections of length at most
\lambda
;

•

\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
is closed under complements (i.e. it is a Boolean algebra).

Unlike the regular case,
however, the above classes are not closed under longer unions and intersections different from those specified in the above list. For example, if
X
is not discrete, then already taking countable intersections of open sets may result in proper closed (or even more complicated) sets because
X
, being metrizable, is first-countable. (See
[
2
]
for more details.) This is partially balanced by the following technical proposition, which is specific to uncountable singular cardinals.

Proposition 4.2.1
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Let
X
be a metrizable space and
1\leq\xi<\lambda^{+}
.

(i)

If
\xi>1
is a successor ordinal, then every
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
can be written as
A=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}A_{n}
with
A_{n}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
for all
n\in\omega
, and dually for sets in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
.

(ii)

If
\xi
is limit, then every
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
can be written as
A=\bigcup_{\alpha<{\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)}A_{\alpha}
with
A_{\alpha}\in\bigcup_{\xi^{\prime}<\xi}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
for all
\alpha<{\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)
, and dually for sets in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
(notice that
{\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)<\lambda
since
\lambda
is singular).

(iii)

If
\nu<\lambda
and
A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
for
\alpha<\nu
, then
\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi+1}(X)
. Dually, the union of less than
\lambda
-many sets in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
belongs to
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi+1}(X)
.

If moreover
X
is
\lambda
-Polish and
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
, then part
(i)
holds also with
\xi=1
.

Proof.

By induction on
\xi
. If
\xi=1
we just have to prove
(iii)
. (The case of
(i)
when
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
will be treated separately at the end of this proof.) Since each
A_{\alpha}
is open and
X
is metrizable, we can canonically write
A_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}B_{\alpha}^{n}
with
B_{\alpha}^{n}
closed by Fact
2.2.4
. Then since

\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}A_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\nu}{\omega}}\,\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}B^{s(\alpha)}_{\alpha},

we get that such set is a union of
\omega^{\nu}
-many closed sets. Since
\lambda
is strong limit when
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, we have
\omega^{\nu}<\lambda
and thus
\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(X)
. Since
\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(X)
by definition, it follows
\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
, as required.

Now let
\xi>1
and assume that
(i)
–
(iii)
holds for all
\xi^{\prime}<\xi
. We first prove
(i)
, so assume that
\xi=\xi^{\prime}+1
is a successor ordinal. If
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
, then there are
B_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)
such that
A=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}B_{\alpha}
. For
n\in\omega
, set
A_{n}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda_{n}}B_{\alpha}
, so that
A=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}A_{n}
. By
(iii)
applied to
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)
, we get that
A_{n}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}+1}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
and we are done.

The case of
(ii)
when
\xi
is limit is even simpler. Let
(\xi_{\alpha})_{\alpha<{\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)}
be a strictly increasing sequence cofinal in
\xi
. Given
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
, let
B_{\beta}\in\bigcup_{\xi^{\prime}<\xi}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)
be such that
A=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}B_{\beta}
. Set

A_{\alpha}=\bigcup\{B_{\beta}\mid B_{\beta}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi_{\alpha}}(X)\}.

Then
A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi_{\alpha}+1}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi_{\alpha}+2}(X)\subseteq\bigcup_{\xi^{\prime}<\xi}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X)
and
A=\bigcup_{\alpha<{\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)}A_{\alpha}
, as desired.

As for
(iii)
, since if all
A_{\alpha}
are in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
then

\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi+1}(X)
by definition, it is enough to prove that
\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi+1}(X)
. For all
\alpha<\nu
, apply
(i)
or
(ii)
(depending on whether
\xi
is a successor or a limit ordinal) to get
B^{\beta}_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
such that
A_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\beta<\mu}B^{\beta}_{\alpha}
, where
\mu=\omega
if
\xi
is successor and
\mu={\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)
otherwise. (This can be done because at this point we already proved
(i)
and
(ii)
for level
\xi
and we assumed
{\sf AC}_{\lambda}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
.)
