Notice that since
\lambda
is singular, in all cases
\mu<\lambda
. Then we again have

\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}A_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\nu}{\mu}}\,\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}B^{s(\alpha)}_{\alpha}.

Since
\mu,\nu<\lambda
we have
\mu^{\nu}<\lambda
because
\lambda
is strong limit. It then easily follows that since
\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}B^{s(\alpha)}_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
by definition, we have
\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi+1}(X)
, as desired.

Finally, we consider
(i)
for
\xi=1
under the extra assumption that
X
is a
\lambda
-Polish space with
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
. By Proposition
3.3.2
, we can assume that
X
is a closed subset of
B(\lambda)
. Then every open
A\subseteq X
can be written as
A=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}A_{n}
with

A_{n}=\bigcup\{\boldsymbol{N}_{s}(X)\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{\lambda}\wedge\boldsymbol{N}_{s}(X)\subseteq A\},

which is clearly a clopen subset of
X
because
X\setminus A_{n}=\bigcup\{\boldsymbol{N}_{s}(X)\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{\lambda}\wedge\boldsymbol{N}_{s}(X)\nsubseteq A\}
.
∎

Obviously, if
|X|\leq\lambda
then any of its subsets is trivially in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
. To see that when
|X|\nleq\lambda
the hierarchy does not collapse before
\lambda^{+}
(i.e. that all inclusions in equation (
4.1
) are proper) we use universal sets just as in the classical case. However, the situation here is slightly more delicate because the natural space of codes for sets in one of the given
\lambda
-Borel classes is the generalized Cantor space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, hence we need to assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
in order to have that such a coding space is
\lambda
-Polish itself. Granting this, one can repeat
mutatis mutandis
the arguments in
[
28
, Chapter 22]
and show that if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
then

(1)

each
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
has a
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
-universal set, that is a set
U\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\times X)
such that
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)=\{U_{y}\mid y\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\}
for
U_{y}=\{x\in X\mid(y,x)\in U\}
, and similarly for
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
;

(2)

in contrast, there is no
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
-universal set for
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
(this follows from the fact that
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}
is closed under continuous preimages and complements).

Using also Corollary
3.4.9
, one finally obtains

Theorem 4.2.2
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and that
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space with
|X|>\lambda
. Then
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)\neq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
for each
1\leq\xi<\lambda^{+}
. Therefore for all
1\leq\xi<\xi^{\prime}<\lambda^{+}

\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X)\subsetneq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)\subsetneq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X),

and similarly for
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
.

Remark 4.2.3
.

In particular, the above non-collapsing theorem applies to the space
X={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\approx B(\lambda)\approx C(\lambda)
.
In
[
4
]
it is shown that such result holds unconditionally for the space
X={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, even if the existence of universal sets is not granted when
2^{<\lambda}>\lambda
(and thus a different argument is required). However, notice that if
2^{<\lambda}>\lambda
, then
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
is not
\lambda
-Polish because of its weight, so in that case one is actually working with a space which does not fit the framework of the present paper.

Another consequence of the existence, under
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, of universal sets
3
3

3

To be precise, the existence of universal sets grants
|\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)|\leq 2^{\lambda}
. For the lower bound, observe that
|X|\geq\lambda
is equivalent to
\operatorname{dens}(X)=\lambda
by Lemma
2.4.4
. Then, a nontrivial argument involving Lemma
2.4.1
shows that
|\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)|\geq 2^{\lambda}
. Notice that if instead
|X|<\lambda
, then
|\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)|\leq|\mathscr{P}(X)|<\lambda
by
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.

is e.g. that if
X
is a
\lambda
-Polish space such that
|X|\geq\lambda
, then for all
1\leq\xi<\lambda^{+}

|\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)|=|\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)|=|\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)|=2^{\lambda}.

The same applies to
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
if
\xi>1
.

We conclude this section by observing that when
2^{\aleph_{0}}\leq\lambda
, the classical Borel subsets of

\lambda
-Polish spaces appear at the smallest possible level in the
\lambda
-Borel hierarchy:
this shows that there is a huge difference between our approach and the one considered e.g. in
[
47
,
16
,
48
,
19
,
20
]
.

Proposition 4.2.4
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space.

(i)

If
\nu
is an infinite cardinal such that
2^{\nu}\leq\lambda
, then
\nu^{+}\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
.

(ii)

If
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and
|X|>\lambda
, then
\nu^{+}\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)\subsetneq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
for all infinite
\nu<\lambda
.

Proof.

(i)

By assumption,
2^{\nu}
is a cardinal not grater than
\lambda
: to simplify the notation, set
\mu=2^{\nu}
. Consider the collection
\mathcal{A}_{\nu}
of all subsets of
X
which can be written both as a
\mu
-sized intersection of open set and as a
\mu
-sized union of closed sets, so that clearly
\mathcal{A}_{\nu}\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
. The class
\mathcal{A}_{\nu}
contains all open sets by Fact
2.2.4
, and is closed under complements by definition. We claim that
\mathcal{A}_{\nu}
is also closed under
\nu
-sized intersections, so that
\nu^{+}\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\nu}\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
, as required. Let
(B_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\nu}
be a family of sets in
\mathcal{A}_{\nu}
so that
B_{\alpha}=\bigcap_{\beta<\mu}U_{\alpha,\beta}=\bigcup_{\beta<\mu}C_{\alpha,\beta}
with all the sets
U_{\alpha,\beta}
open and all the sets
C_{\alpha,\beta}
closed. Then
\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}B_{\alpha}=\bigcap_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\nu\times\mu}U_{\alpha,\beta}
and the latter can be viewed as a well-ordered intersection of open sets of length
\mu
because
\nu\leq\mu
. Moreover

\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}B_{\alpha}=\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}\bigcup_{\beta<\mu}C_{\alpha,\beta}=\bigcup_{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\nu}{\mu}}\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}C_{\alpha,s(\alpha)}.

Since
\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}C_{\alpha,s(\alpha)}
is closed and
|{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\nu}{\mu}|=2^{\nu}=\mu
because
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\nu}{({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\nu}{2})}
is obviously in one-to-one correspondence with
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\nu}{2}
, this shows that
\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}B_{\alpha}
is a
\mu
-sized union of closed sets as well.

(ii)

For each
\nu<\lambda
we have
\nu^{+}\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
by part
(i)
and
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
Assume towards a contradiction that
\nu^{+}\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
. Let
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(X)\setminus\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
: such a set exists by Theorem
4.2.2
. Let
(C_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
be closed sets such that
A=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}C_{\alpha}
. Then by Proposition
4.2.1
(iii)
we have
A_{n}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda_{n}}C_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)=\nu^{+}\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
for each
n\in\omega
. But then
A=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}A_{n}\in\nu^{+}\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
by closure of
\nu^{+}\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
under unions of size
\nu
and
\nu\geq\omega
, a contradiction.
∎

In particular, if
\lambda\geq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
then the collection of classical (i.e.
\omega_{1}
-)Borel subsets of
X
is contained in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
, and if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
then the inclusion is proper, i.e. there are already
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(X)
-sets which are not Borel in the classical sense.

4.3.
Changes of topology

In this section we address the problem of whether in our generalized context we have an
analogue of the powerful technique of changes of topology developed
e.g. in
[
28
, Chapter 13]
. This would amount to
prove that for every
\lambda
-Borel subset
A
of a
\lambda
-Polish space
X

there is another
\lambda
-Polish topology on
X
which refines the original one,
yields the same
\lambda
-Borel sets, and turns
A
into a clopen set. Interestingly
enough, the argument from
[
28
]
cannot be adapted to our context
because we miss the analogue of
[
28
, Lemma 13.3]
, whose proof requires that a product of
\lambda
-many
\lambda
-Polish spaces be
\lambda
-Polish as well: as already observed, this is
plainly false if
\lambda>\omega={\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)
, where we even miss closure under
uncountable products. To overcome this obstacle, we basically reverse the classical approach and first prove the technical Theorem
4.3.1
, from which we then derive a number of standard consequences including the result on changes of topology (Corollary
4.3.6
). It might be worth noticing that this alternative approach actually works quite uniformly and independently of
\lambda
and its cofinality: with small and straightforward modifications, it can be successfully used in the case
\lambda=\omega
too, but also in the case of a regular
\lambda
(see
[
3
, Section 4]
) and of a singular
\lambda
with uncountable cofinality (see
[
1
, Section 6]
), provided as usual that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.

We will need the following functions.
4
4

4

The functions
\pi_{\alpha}
should already be used in the (omitted) proof of the existence of universal sets for
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\alpha}(X)
— see
[
28
, Theorem 22.3]
, where the analogues of such functions are denoted by
y\mapsto(y)_{n}
.

Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon\lambda\times\lambda\to\lambda
be the usual Gödel pairing function, and for each
\alpha
consider the continuous and open surjection

\pi_{\alpha}\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\to{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}

defined by setting for
\beta<\lambda

(4.2)

\pi_{\alpha}(x)(\beta)=x(\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle).

Similarly, given
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{2}
we let
\pi_{\alpha}(s)\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{2}
be defined by setting
\pi_{\alpha}(s)(\beta)=s(\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle)
for all
\beta
such that
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle<\operatorname{lh}(s)
. (The fact that
\pi_{\alpha}(s)
is defined on an initial segment of
\lambda
follows from the fact that Gödel’s pairing function is monotone in the second coordinate.)

Theorem 4.3.1
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then for every
B\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
there is a closed set
F\subseteq B(\lambda)
and a continuous
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism
f\colon F\to X
such that
f^{-1}(B)
is clopen relatively to
F
.

Noteworthy, the following proof ultimately relies on the fact that if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda>\omega={\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)
then
B(\lambda)\approx{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
(Theorem
3.2.2
(ii)
).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the class of all
B\subseteq X
satisfying the conclusion of the theorem. Since it is clearly closed under complements, it is enough to show that it contains all closed sets and that it is closed under intersections of size
\lambda
.

Assume
B\subseteq X
is closed, so that both
B
and
X\setminus B
are
\lambda
-Polish. By Proposition
3.2.5
there are closed
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq B(\lambda)
and continuous bijections
f_{0}\colon F_{0}\to B
and
f_{1}\colon F_{1}\to X\setminus B
.

Since
B(\lambda)
is strongly homogeneous, we can assume without loss of generality that
F_{i}\subseteq\boldsymbol{N}_{\langle i\rangle}
: we claim that the closed set
F=F_{0}\cup F_{1}
and the continuous bijection
f=f_{0}\cup f_{1}

witness
B\in\mathcal{C}
.
Indeed, we only need to check that
f
has a
\lambda
-Borel inverse: this follows from the fact that, by Remark
3.2.6
,
f_{i}(U)\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}(X)
for each relatively open
U\subseteq F_{i}
.

Assume now that
B_{\alpha}
,
\alpha<\lambda
, are subsets of
X
for which there are
F_{\alpha}
and
f_{\alpha}
witnessing
B_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}
, and consider
B=\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}B_{\alpha}
. Since
B(\lambda)\approx{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
by Theorem
3.2.2
, without loss of generality we may assume that
F_{\alpha}
is a closed subset of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
. Consider the closed set

F^{\prime}=\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}\pi_{\alpha}^{-1}(F_{\alpha}),

and its closed subset

F^{\prime\prime}=\{x\in F^{\prime}\mid(f_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\alpha})(x)=(f_{\alpha^{\prime}}\circ\pi_{\alpha^{\prime}})(x)\text{ for all }\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}<\lambda\}.

The function
h\colon F^{\prime\prime}\to X\colon x\mapsto(f_{0}\circ\pi_{0})(x)
is clearly continuous and surjective. To check that it is injective, pick distinct
x,x^{\prime}\in F^{\prime\prime}
. Then there is
\alpha<\lambda
such that
\pi_{\alpha}(x)\neq\pi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})
, hence
(f_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\alpha})(x)\neq(f_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\alpha})(x^{\prime})
because
f_{\alpha}
is injective. By definition of
F^{\prime\prime}
, it then follows that

h(x)=(f_{0}\circ\pi_{0})(x)=(f_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\alpha})(x)\neq(f_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\alpha})(x^{\prime})=(f_{0}\circ\pi_{0})(x^{\prime})=h(x^{\prime}).

Moreover, for every
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{2}
we have
h(\boldsymbol{N}_{s}(F^{\prime\prime}))=\bigcap_{\alpha<\kappa}f_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{N}_{\pi_{\alpha}(s)}(F_{\alpha}))
,
hence
h
has
\lambda
-Borel inverse because by assumption all the maps
f_{\alpha}
have
\lambda
-Borel inverses.

Now notice that

h^{-1}(B_{\alpha})=F^{\prime\prime}\cap(f_{0}\circ\pi_{0})^{-1}(B_{\alpha})=F^{\prime\prime}\cap(f_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\alpha})^{-1}(B_{\alpha})

is clopen in
F^{\prime\prime}
, thus

h^{-1}(B)=h^{-1}\left(\bigcap\nolimits_{\alpha<\lambda}B_{\alpha}\right)=\bigcap\nolimits_{\alpha<\lambda}h^{-1}(B_{\alpha})

is closed in
F^{\prime\prime}
. Since
F^{\prime\prime}
is
\lambda
-Polish, so are
h^{-1}(B)
and
F^{\prime\prime}\setminus h^{-1}(B)
. Arguing as above, there are closed
G_{0},G_{1}\subseteq B(\lambda)
with
G_{i}\subseteq\boldsymbol{N}_{\langle i\rangle}
(
i\in\{0,1\}
) and continuous
\lambda
-Borel isomorphisms
g_{0}\colon G_{0}\to B
and
g_{1}\colon G_{1}\to X\setminus B
. Therefore the closed set
G=G_{0}\cup G_{1}
and the continuous bijection
g=h\circ(g_{0}\cup g_{1})
, which still has
\lambda
-Borel inverse, witness that
B=\bigcap\nolimits_{\alpha<\lambda}B_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}
, as desired.
∎

Notice that combining Theorem
4.3.1
with the argument in the second part of its proof (namely, the one leading to the construction of the closed set
F^{\prime\prime}
and the continuous bijection
h
), we easily obtain the following generalization of it.

Corollary 4.3.2
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then for every family
(B_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
of
\lambda
-Borel subsets of
X
there is a closed set
F\subseteq B(\lambda)
and a continuous
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism
f\colon F\to X
such that
f^{-1}(B_{\alpha})
is clopen relatively to
F
for every
\alpha<\lambda
.

Of course one can then derive all standard consequences of these facts. For example, the following corollary significantly extends both
[
47
, Theorem 4]
and the corollary in
[
47
, Section 3.6]
from (classical) Borel sets of a
\lambda
-Polish space to arbitrary
\lambda
-Borel sets, of course under our extra assumptions on
\lambda
.

Corollary 4.3.3
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then for every
B\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
there is a closed set
G\subseteq B(\lambda)
and a continuous bijection
g\colon G\to B
(with
\lambda
-Borel inverse).
If moreover
B\neq\emptyset
, then there is a continuous surjection
h\colon B(\lambda)\to B
.

Proof.

Let
F\subseteq B(\lambda)
and
f\colon F\to X
be as in Theorem
4.3.1
, and then set
G=f^{-1}(B)
and
g=f\restriction G
.
∎

Corollary 4.3.4
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then for every
B\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
there is a closed set
H\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
such that for all
x\in X

x\in B\iff\exists y\,(x,y)\in H\iff\exists!y\,(x,y)\in H.

Proof.

Given
G
and
g
as in Corollary
4.3.3
, set
H=(\mathrm{graph}(g))^{-1}
. The set
H
is closed because
G
is closed in
B(\lambda)
and
g\colon G\to X
is a continuous function, thus
\mathrm{graph}(g)
is closed relatively to
G\times X\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{1}(B(\lambda)\times X)
.
∎

Corollary 4.3.5
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space and
B\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
. Then either
B
is well-orderable and
|B|\leq\lambda
, or else there is a continuous
\lambda
-Borel embedding
h
from
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
into
B
with
\lambda
-Borel range (thus, in particular,
|B|=2^{\lambda}
).

Proof.

Let
F\subseteq B(\lambda)
and
f\colon F\to X
be as in Theorem
4.3.1
.
Since
f^{-1}(B)
is clopen in
F
and thus closed in
B(\lambda)
, it is a
\lambda
-Polish space. By Corollary
3.4.9
,
either
f^{-1}(B)
is well-orderable and
|f^{-1}(B)|\leq\lambda
, in which case
|B|=|f^{-1}(B)|\leq\lambda
too, or else
there is an embedding
g\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\to f^{-1}(B)
with closed range: the composition
h=f\circ g
is then as desired.
∎

The above result will be substantially improved in Corollary
7.1.2
by showing that
h
can be taken to be a
topological
embedding with a
closed-in-
X
range.

We finally come to the desired result on changes of topology. Applying Corollary
4.3.2
and pushing forward the topology on
F
along the
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism
f
, we obtain that, as in the classical case, one can refine a
\lambda
-Polish topology in order to make clopen sets that were just
\lambda
-Borel, without changing the overall
\lambda
-Borel structure of the space.

Corollary 4.3.6
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X=(X,\tau)
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then for every family
(B_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
of
\lambda
-Borel subsets of
X
there is a
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau^{\prime}
on
X
refining
\tau
such that
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau^{\prime})=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)
and
B_{\alpha}
is
\tau^{\prime}
-clopen for every
\alpha<\lambda
. Moreover, such a
\tau^{\prime}
can be taken so that
\mathrm{dim}(X,\tau^{\prime})=0
.

As for
\lambda
-Borel functions, applying Corollary
4.3.6
to the preimages of the basic open sets of
Y
we get:

Corollary 4.3.7
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Let
(X,\tau)
be
\lambda
-Polish and
Y
be a topological space of weight at most
\lambda
. For every
\lambda
-Borel function
f\colon(X,\tau)\to Y
there is a
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau^{\prime}
on
X
refining
\tau
such that
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau^{\prime})=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)
and
f\colon(X,\tau^{\prime})\to Y
is continuous. Moreover, such a
\tau^{\prime}
can be taken so that
\mathrm{dim}(X,\tau^{\prime})=0
.

Corollary 4.3.8
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and let
(X,\tau)
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. For every
\lambda
-Borel function
f\colon(X,\tau)\to(X,\tau)
there is a
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau^{\prime}
on
X
refining
\tau
such that
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau^{\prime})=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)
and
f\colon(X,\tau^{\prime})\to(X,\tau^{\prime})
is continuous. Moreover, such a
\tau^{\prime}
can be taken so that
\mathrm{dim}(X,\tau^{\prime})=0
.

Proof.

Iteratively apply Corollary
4.3.7
to get a chain of
\lambda
-Polish topologies
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\omega}
such that
\tau_{0}=\tau
,
\tau_{n+1}\supseteq\tau_{n}
,
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau_{n+1})=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau_{n})
, and
f\colon(X,\tau_{n+1})\to(X,\tau_{n})
is continuous. Since we are dealing with just countably many
\lambda
-Polish topologies, the proof of
[
28
, Lemma 13.3]
shows that the topology
\tau^{\prime}
generated by
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}\tau_{n}
is as required.
∎

Similarly, with a back and forth argument one easily obtains:

Corollary 4.3.9
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and let
(X,\tau_{X})
and
(Y,\tau_{Y})
be
\lambda
-Polish spaces. For every
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism
f\colon(X,\tau_{X})\to(Y,\tau_{Y})
there are
\lambda
-Polish topologies
\tau^{\prime}_{X}
and
\tau^{\prime}_{Y}
on
X
and
Y
, respectively, which refine the original topologies, generate the same
\lambda
-Borel sets, and such that
f\colon(X,\tau^{\prime}_{X})\to(Y,\tau^{\prime}_{Y})
is a homeomorphism. Moreover, such
\tau^{\prime}_{X}
and
\tau^{\prime}_{Y}
can be taken so that
\mathrm{dim}(X,\tau^{\prime}_{X})=\mathrm{dim}(Y,\tau^{\prime}_{Y})=0
. Finally, if
(X,\tau_{X})=(Y,\tau_{Y})
we can have
\tau^{\prime}_{X}=\tau^{\prime}_{Y}
.

Clearly, one can also (simultaneously) work with any
\lambda
-sized family of
\lambda
-Borel functions.

4.4.
Structural properties

We now turn to structural properties. It is convenient to introduce a general notion of
\lambda
-pointclass.

Definition 4.4.1
.

A
boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
is an operator
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
assigning to each
\lambda
-Polish space
X
a collection of subsets
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)\subseteq\mathscr{P}(X)
which is closed under continuous preimages, that is, if
f\colon X\to Y
is continuous and
B\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(Y)
then
f^{-1}(B)\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
. The dual
\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is the boldface
\lambda
-pointclass defined by
\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(X)=\{X\setminus A\mid A\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)\}
. The ambiguous
\lambda
-pointclass
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
associated to
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is defined by
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(X)=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)\cap\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(X)
.

Examples of boldface
\lambda
-pointclasses are the classes
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
, for an arbitrary
1\leq\xi<\lambda^{+}
. In this case,
\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
, and
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
.

We list here a number of important structural properties (compare them with those defined in
[
28
, Section 22.C]
).

Definition 4.4.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
be a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass.

(a)

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
has the
separation property
if for every
\lambda
-Polish space
X
and
A,B\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
with
A\cap B=\emptyset
, there is
C\in\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(X)
separating
A
from
B
, i.e. such that
A\subseteq C
and
C\cap B=\emptyset
.

(b)

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
has the
\lambda
-generalized separation property
if for every
\lambda
-Polish space
X
and any sequence of sets
A_{\alpha}\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
,
\alpha<\lambda
, with
\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}A_{\alpha}=\emptyset
there is a sequence of sets
B_{\alpha}\in\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(X)
with
A_{\alpha}\subseteq B_{\alpha}
and
\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}B_{\alpha}=\emptyset
.

(c)

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
has the
reduction property
if for every
\lambda
-Polish space
X
and for any
A,B\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
there are disjoint sets
A^{*},B^{*}\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
such that
A^{*}\subseteq A
,
B^{*}\subseteq B
, and
A^{*}\cup B^{*}=A\cup B
. (If this happens, we say that the pair
(A^{*},B^{*})
reduces the pair
(A,B)
.)

(d)

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
has the
\lambda
-generalized reduction property
if for every
\lambda
-Polish space
X
and any sequence of sets
A_{\alpha}\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
,
\alpha<\lambda
, there is a sequence of pairwise disjoint sets
A^{*}_{\alpha}\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
with
A^{*}_{\alpha}\subseteq A_{\alpha}
and
\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}A^{*}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}A_{\alpha}
.

(e)

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
has the
ordinal
\lambda
-uniformization property
if for every
\lambda
-Polish space
X
and for any
R\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X\times\lambda)
(where
X\times\lambda
is endowed with the product of the topology of
X
and the discrete topology on
\lambda
) there is a uniformization of
R
in
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
, that is, a set
R^{*}\subseteq R
such that
R^{*}\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X\times\lambda)
and
R^{*}
is the graph of a function with domain the projection
p(R)=\{x\in X\mid\exists\alpha<\lambda\,(x,\alpha)\in R\}
of
R
on its first coordinate.

We first prove the following Proposition
4.4.4
, which is a higher analogue of
[
28
, Theorem 22.16]
. The natural generalization of the original argument would require that the classes
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
are closed under intersections of length smaller than
\lambda
, which is false when
\lambda
is singular. To overcome this difficulty, we will use a nontrivial adaptation of that proof which heavily relies on our Proposition
4.2.1
.

Definition 4.4.3
.

A boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is called
\lambda
-reasonable
if the following property holds:

Suppose that
I
is a set of cardinality at most
\lambda
and
X
is
\lambda
-Polish. Let
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
be a sequence of subsets of
X
. Then
A_{i}\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
for all
i\in I
if and only if

A=\{(x,i)\in X\times I\mid x\in A_{i}\}\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X\times I),

where
X\times I
is endowed with the product of the topology of
X
and the discrete topology on
I
.

Notice that if a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\supseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{1}
is closed under unions of length
\lambda
, then
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is
\lambda
-reasonable. Moreover, if
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is
\lambda
-reasonable then so are
\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
because

(X\times I)\setminus\{(x,i)\in X\times I\mid x\in A_{i}\}=\{(x,i)\in X\times I\mid x\in X\setminus A_{i}\}.

It follows that all of
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}
,
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}
, and
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}
are
\lambda
-reasonable.
5
5

5

Examples of boldface
\lambda
-pointclasses which are not
\lambda
-reasonable are the ones defined by setting
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X)\cup\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
for any
1\leq\xi<\lambda^{+}
, or even just
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)=\{\emptyset,X\}
.

Proposition 4.4.4
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. For every
1<\xi<\lambda^{+}
, the boldface

\lambda
-pointclass
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}
has the ordinal
\lambda
-uniformization property. The same is true for
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{1}
if we restrict the attention to
\lambda
-Polish spaces
X
with
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
.

Proof.

We first consider the case where
\xi=\xi^{\prime}+1
is a successor ordinal. Let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish and
R\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X\times\lambda)
. By definition, there are
R_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X\times\lambda)
such that
R=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}R_{\alpha}
. Set

Q=\{(x,\gamma,\alpha)\in X\times\lambda\times\lambda\mid(x,\gamma)\in R_{\alpha}\}

and notice that
Q\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X\times\lambda\times\lambda)
because
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}
is
\lambda
-reasonable. Order
\lambda\times\lambda
by setting
(\gamma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\prec(\gamma,\alpha)
if and only if
\langle\gamma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\rangle<\langle\gamma,\alpha\rangle
, where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is again the Gödel pairing function. Define then
Q^{*}\subseteq X\times\lambda\times\lambda
by setting

(x,\gamma,\alpha)\in Q^{*}\iff(x,\gamma,\alpha)\in Q\wedge\forall(\gamma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\prec(\gamma,\alpha)\,[(x,\gamma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\notin Q].

Since there are less than
\lambda
-many pairs
(\gamma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\prec(\gamma,\alpha)
and the intersection of fewer than
\lambda
-many sets from
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi^{\prime}}(X\times\lambda\times\lambda)
is in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X\times\lambda\times\lambda)
by Proposition
4.2.1
(iii)
, it follows that
Q^{*}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X\times\lambda\times\lambda)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X\times\lambda\times\lambda)
. Thus the set
R^{*}\subseteq X\times\lambda
defined by

(x,\gamma)\in R^{*}\iff\exists\alpha<\lambda\,[(x,\gamma,\alpha)\in Q^{*}]

is in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X\times\lambda)
, and it clearly uniformizes
R
.

If
\xi
is limit, we use instead Proposition
4.2.1
(ii)
. Given
R\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}(X\times\lambda)
, let
(R_{\alpha})_{\alpha<{\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)}
and
s\colon{\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)\to\xi
be such that
R_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{s(\alpha)}(X\times\lambda)
and
R=\bigcup_{\alpha<{\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)}R_{\alpha}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
s
is non-decreasing. For
\alpha<{\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)
, let
Q^{\alpha}\subseteq X\times\lambda\times\lambda
be defined by

Q^{\alpha}=\{(x,\gamma,\alpha^{\prime})\in X\times\lambda\times\lambda\mid\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha\wedge(x,\gamma)\in R_{\alpha^{\prime}}\},

and set

Q=\{(x,\gamma,\alpha)\in X\times\lambda\times\lambda\mid(x,\gamma)\in R_{\alpha}\}=\bigcup_{\alpha<{\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)}Q^{\alpha}.

Since
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{s(\alpha^{\prime})}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{s(\alpha)}(X)
for
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
because

s
is non-decreasing, and since the boldface
\lambda
-pointclasses
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{s(\alpha)}
and
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}
are
\lambda
-reasonable, each set
Q^{\alpha}
is
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{s(\alpha)}
, while
Q
is
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}
. Define
Q^{*}\subseteq X\times\lambda\times\lambda
by

\displaystyle(x,\gamma,\alpha)\in Q^{*}\iff(x,\gamma,\alpha)\in Q

\displaystyle\wedge\forall\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha\,\forall\gamma^{\prime}<\lambda\,[(x,\gamma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\notin Q^{\alpha}]

\displaystyle\wedge\forall\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma\,[(x,\gamma^{\prime},\alpha)\notin Q^{\alpha}].

Since for a fixed pair
(\gamma,\alpha)
the part after the first conjunction in the right term defines a set in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{s(\alpha)+1}(X)
and
s(\alpha)+1<\xi
because
\xi
is limit, by

\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{s(\alpha)+1}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X)
and the

\lambda
-reasonability of
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}
we easily get
Q^{*}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{\xi}(X\times\lambda\times\lambda)
. As before, it follows that the set
R^{*}
consisting of those
(x,\gamma)\in X\times\lambda
such that
(x,\gamma,\alpha)\in Q^{*}
for some
\alpha<{\operatorname{cf}}(\xi)
is the desired uniformization of
R
in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}
.

Assume now that
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
and
\xi=1
. None of the two previous arguments can work in this case because
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{1}
is in general not closed under infinite intersections, so a further refinement is in order. By Proposition
3.3.2
, without loss of generality we may assume that
X\subseteq B(\lambda)
. A set
A\subseteq X
is
n
-clopen if there is
S\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{\lambda}
such that
A=\bigcup\{\boldsymbol{N}_{s}(X)\mid s\in S\}
. It is easy to check that
n
-clopen sets are closed under complements and
arbitrary
unions and intersections. Moreover, if
m\leq n
then every
m
-clopen set is also
n
-clopen. Let now
R\subseteq X\times\lambda
be open. For each
\gamma<\lambda
and
n\in\omega
, the set

R^{\gamma}_{n}=\bigcup\{\boldsymbol{N}_{s}(X)\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{\lambda}\wedge\boldsymbol{N}_{s}(X)\times\{\gamma\}\subseteq R\}

is
n
-clopen, so that the set
R_{n}=\bigcup_{\gamma<\lambda}R^{\gamma}_{n}
is
n
-clopen as well. By the closure properties of
n
-closed sets, also the sets
Q^{\gamma}_{n}\subseteq X
defined by

x\in Q^{\gamma}_{n}\iff x\in R^{\gamma}_{n}\wedge\forall m<n\,(x\notin R_{m})\wedge\forall\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma\,(x\notin R^{\gamma^{\prime}}_{n})

are
n
-clopen, hence the set

\displaystyle Q^{*}

\displaystyle=\{(x,\gamma,n)\in X\times\lambda\times\omega\mid x\in Q^{\gamma}_{n}\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\omega\\
\gamma<\lambda\end{subarray}}Q^{\gamma}_{n}\times\{\gamma\}\times\{n\}

is open. Then, the open set
R^{*}\subseteq X\times\lambda
defined by

(x,\gamma)\in R^{*}\iff\exists n\in\omega\,(x,\gamma,n)\in Q^{*}

unifomizes
R
.
∎

The following is the analogue of
[
28
, Proposition 22.15]
. We omit the proof since it is (almost) identical to the original one.

Proposition 4.4.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
be a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass.

(i)

If
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
has the reduction property, then
\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
has the separation property.

(ii)

If
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is closed under unions of length
\lambda
and has the
\lambda
-generalized reduction property, then
\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
has the
\lambda
-generalized separation property.

(iii)

If
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is
\lambda
-reasonable, then
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
has the
\lambda
-generalized reduction property if and only if
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
has the ordinal
\lambda
-uniformization property.

(iv)

Assume that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
admits a
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
-universal set, that is, a set
U\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
such that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})=\{U_{y}\mid y\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\}
.
Then
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
cannot have both the reduction and the separation properties.

Finally, using the fact that the boldface
\lambda
-pointclasses
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}
are
\lambda
-reasonable and admit
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
-universal sets if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, we can combine Propositions
4.4.4
and
4.4.5
to obtain:

Theorem 4.4.6
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and
1<\xi<\lambda^{+}
. Then
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{\xi}
has the ordinal
\lambda
-uniformization property and the (
\lambda
-generalized) reduction property, but it does not have the separation property. The class
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{\xi}
has instead the (
\lambda
-generalized) separation property but not the reduction property. If we restrict the attention to
\lambda
-Polish spaces
X
with
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
, the same holds for
\xi=1
.

4.5.
Failure of
\lambda
-Borel determinacy

An important tool in classical descriptive set theory is constituted by infinite, two-players, zero-sum, perfect information games of length
\omega
, also called Gale-Stewart games. These are games of the form
G^{\omega}_{X}(A)
, where
X
is a nonempty set and
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{X}
. Two players
\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{II}
alternatively pick elements
x_{0}
,
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
, …from
X
for
\omega
-many turns, so that in the end of the run a sequence
(x_{i})_{i\in\omega}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{X}
has been played;
\mathbf{I}
wins if
(x_{i})_{i\in\omega}\in A
, otherwise
\mathbf{II}
wins. Winning strategies for either
\mathbf{I}
or
\mathbf{II}
are defined in the obvious way, and a set
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{X}
is said
determined
if one (and only one) of the players has a winning strategy in the corresponding game
G^{\omega}_{X}(A)
. Notice that if we equip
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{X}
with the product of the discrete topology on
X
, we can consider the topological complexity of the payoff set
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{X}
.

One of the earliest determinacy results is the following Gale-Stewart theorem (see e.g.
[
28
, Theorem 20.1]
).

Proposition 4.5.1
.

Let
X
be a well-orderable set. If
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{X}
is closed or open, then
G^{\omega}_{X}(A)
is determined.

The hypothesis that
X
is well-orderable cannot be removed — the assertion that
G^{\omega}_{X}(A)
is determined
for every
X
and every closed set
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{X}
is equivalent to the full
{\sf AC}
.
However, in the choiceless setting one can work with “non-deterministic” analogues of strategies, called quasistrategies (see
[
28
, Section 20.B]
for the definition and more details). Using this notion, Proposition
4.5.1
can be restated for all sets
X
as follows:

Proposition 4.5.2
.

Let
X
be any set. If
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{X}
is closed or open, then
G^{\omega}_{X}(A)
is
quasidetermined
i.e. one (and only one) of the players has a winning quasistrategy.

It is easy to see that when
X
is well-ordered by
\precsim
, then from any winning quasistrategy
\sigma
for one of the players in the game
G^{\omega}_{X}(A)
one can canonically extract a winning strategy by picking the
\precsim
-least move compatible with
\sigma
in each round. (Actually, for this argument to work it is enough that the set of
all moves that can be made by the given player
can be well-ordered; this is relevant for the “games with rules” briefly discussed at the end of this section.)
This simple observation shows that Proposition
4.5.2
implies Proposition
4.5.1
.

The use of games in classical descriptive set theory relies on the fact that one can sometimes formalize a given problem via a suitable game of the form
G^{\omega}_{X}(A)
, for some
X
and
A
: when such a game is determined, one then obtain useful information on (if not the solution to) the given problem. For example, the regularity of Borel subsets of the classical Baire space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
in terms of Baire category, Lebesgue measurability, perfect set property, and so on, can be deduced via games from Borel determinacy, a theorem proved by Martin (see e.g.
[
28
, Theorem 20.5]
) which greatly extend Proposition
4.5.1
when
X
is a countable set.

Theorem 4.5.3
.

If
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
is (
\omega_{1}
-)Borel, then
G^{\omega}_{\omega}(A)
is determined.

It is also worth noticing that if
\mathbb{R}
is well-orderable, then there are sets
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}
which are not determined.

Moving to our generalized context, there are at least two possible directions one might take: considering games
6
6

6

Usually, in games longer than
\omega
one stipulates that at limit levels it is
\mathbf{I}
who has to play next. However, different choices make no difference for what concerns Proposition
4.5.4
.

G^{\mu}_{X}(A)
of length
\mu>\omega
or, in view of Proposition
4.5.1
, considering games of the form
G^{\omega}_{\nu}(A)
with
\nu>\omega
. In the first case, it is natural to endow the space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{X}
with the bounded topology. However, in this setup there is no hope to get analogues of Proposition
4.5.1
and Theorem
4.5.3
as soon as
\mathbb{R}
is well-orderable.

Proposition 4.5.4

(Folklore)
.

Let
\mu>\omega
and equip
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{X}
with the bounded topology. Assume that
\mathbb{R}
is well-orderable and that
X
has at least two elements. Then there is a clopen set
C\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{X}
such that
G^{\mu}_{X}(C)
is not determined.

Proof.

Let
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}
be such that
G^{\omega}_{2}(A)
is not determined.
Fix distinct
x_{0},x_{1}\in X
. By identifying
i\in\{0,1\}
with the corresponding
x_{i}
, without loss of generality we can assume that
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{X}
Set
C=\bigcup_{s\in S}\boldsymbol{N}_{s}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{X})
where
S
is the union of
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{X}
with all finite sequences
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{X}
such that
s\notin{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{2}
and the smallest
i<\operatorname{lh}(s)
such that
s(i)\notin\{0,1\}
is odd. It is straightforward to check that
G^{\mu}_{X}(C)
is equivalent to
G^{\omega}_{2}(A)
, that is, a player has a winning strategy in the first game if and only if (s)he has a winning strategy in the latter one. Thus
G^{\mu}_{X}(C)
is not determined by the choice of
A
. Moreover,
C
is clearly open with respect to the bounded topology, but it is also closed because
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{X}\setminus C=\bigcup_{t\in T}\boldsymbol{N}_{t}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{X})
where
T
is the union of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}\setminus A
with all finite sequences
t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{X}
such that
t\notin{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{2}
and the smallest
i<\operatorname{lh}(t)
such that
t(i)\notin\{0,1\}
is even.
∎

Remark 4.5.5
.

One might argue that the problem arises because we considered the bounded topology on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{X}
. But even when considering the (
<\omega
-supported) product topology on such a space, the set
C
constructed in the proof of Proposition
4.5.4
is not very complicated. If e.g.
\nu\geq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
, then
C
is in the class
\nu\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}
relatively to product topology
\tau_{p}
, that is,
C
is both a
\nu
-sized union of
\tau_{p}
-closed sets and a
\nu
-sized intersection of
\tau_{p}
-open sets.

The second option seems more promising, as Proposition
4.5.1
applies: even when
\nu>\omega
, the game
G^{\omega}_{\nu}(A)
is determined whenever
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\nu}
is closed or open. However, if
\mathbb{R}
is well-orderable we cannot go much further.

Proposition 4.5.6
.

Let
\nu\in\mathrm{Card}
be such that
2^{\aleph_{0}}\leq\nu
. Then there is a
\nu\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\nu})
set
D
such that
G^{\omega}_{\nu}(D)
is not determined.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
4.5.4
.
Let
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
be such that
G^{\omega}_{\omega}(A)
is not determined. Let
D
be the set
A\cup\bigcup_{s\in S}\boldsymbol{N}_{s}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\nu})
where
S\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\nu}
consists of those
s\notin{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\omega}
such that the smallest
i<\operatorname{lh}(s)
such that
s(i)\geq\omega
is odd. Then
G^{\omega}_{\nu}(D)
is equivalent to
G^{\omega}_{\omega}(A)
, hence it is not determined. Moreover
D
is the union of at most
2^{\aleph_{0}}
-many singletons (the elements of
A
) with an open set, thus
D\in\nu\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\nu})
because
\nu\geq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
. Similarly, the complement of
D
is again a union of at most
\nu
-many singletons (the elements in
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}\setminus A
) with the open set
\bigcup_{t\in T}\boldsymbol{N}_{t}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\nu})
, where
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\nu}
consists of those
t\notin{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\omega}
such that the smallest
i<\operatorname{lh}(t)
such that
t(i)\geq\omega
is even. Thus
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\nu}\setminus D\in\nu\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}_{2}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\nu})
as well, hence
D\in\nu\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\nu})
.
∎

Stepping back to our uncountable
\lambda
, the previous results show that we cannot hope for any form of “
\lambda
-Borel determinacy” for subsets of
B(\lambda)
or
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
. More in detail, when
A\subseteq B(\lambda)
the Gale-Stewart Theorem (Proposition
4.5.1
) for games of the form
G^{\omega}_{\lambda}(A)
gives us determinacy for closed sets, but Proposition
4.5.6
already gives us
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(B(\lambda))
-sets which are not determined. If we instead consider the generalized Cantor space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
and assume
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
(which is the condition turning
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
into a
\lambda
-Polish space), then by Proposition
4.5.4
there are even clopen games
G^{\lambda}_{2}(A)
which are not determined.

To consider spaces like
C(\lambda)
or
V_{\lambda+1}
, we need to extend our definition of games by adding extra rules in the form of a tree of “legal positions”. This means that besides the length
\mu
of the game and the set
X
of all possible moves, we also fix a
<\mu
-closed
7
7

7

A
\mu
-tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\mu}{X}
is
<\mu
-closed if for every limit ordinal
\alpha<\mu
and every
t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\alpha}{X}
, if
t\restriction\beta\in T
for every
\beta<\alpha
then
t\in T
.

\mu
-tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\mu}{X}
and require the players to ensure that every partial play in a given run of the game, which is an element of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\mu}{X}
, belongs to
T
— the first player that breaks this prescription loses immediately. Given a payoff set
A\subseteq[T]
, we then obtain the game “with rules”
G^{\mu}_{X}(T,A)
. (This is just a jargon to make the game look anthropomorphic: every game of this form can be turned into a game of the form
G^{\mu}_{X}(A^{\prime})
, where
A^{\prime}
is obtained from
A
by adding a suitable open set to it.)
For example, all the games in Section
7
, if properly formalized, are of this sort.

This formalism allows us to consider natural games on
C(\lambda)
and
V_{\lambda+1}
too. For example, if
A\subseteq C(\lambda)
, then we can consider the game on
\lambda
of length
\omega
in which we require that each move
x_{i}
belongs to
\lambda_{i}
, for every
i\in\omega
; in order words, we consider the game
G^{\omega}_{\lambda}(T,A)
where
T={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\vec{\lambda})}
is the
\omega
-tree on
\lambda
such that
[T]=C(\lambda)
.
Then Proposition
4.5.6
holds with the same proof, and if
2^{\aleph_{0}}\leq\lambda
(in particular: if
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible) there are
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(C(\lambda))
-subsets of
C(\lambda)
which are not determined, while all closed subsets of
C(\lambda)
are determined by Proposition
4.5.1
.

Under the assumption
\beth_{\lambda}=\lambda
, given
A\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
we can think of two possible games with payoff set
A
. The first one is the game of length
\lambda
in which we require that the
\alpha
-th move
x_{\alpha}
is a subset of
V_{\alpha+1}\setminus V_{\alpha}
; then player
\mathbf{I}
wins if and only if
\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}x_{\alpha}\in A
. In this case the proof of Proposition
4.5.4
yields that there are non-determined clopen games. As an alternative, one could consider the game of length
\omega
in which the
i
-th move
x_{i}
is a subset of
V_{\lambda_{i}}\setminus V_{\lambda_{i-1}}
(where we set
V_{\lambda_{-1}}=\emptyset
), and player
\mathbf{I}
wins if and only if
\bigcup_{i\in\omega}x_{i}\in A
. In this case,
all closed sets
A\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
are determined by Proposition
4.5.1
, but
there are
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(V_{\lambda+1})
-subsets of
V_{\lambda+1}
which are not determined by Proposition
4.5.6
.

The latter suggests an alternative game for subsets of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, under the assumption
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Consider the game of length
\omega
in which the
i
-th move is an element of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda_{i}}{2}
, and player
\mathbf{I}
wins if and only if the concatenation of those
\omega
-many moves belongs to
A
. Then we have closed determinacy (Proposition
4.5.1
), but also
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{0}_{2}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
-sets which are not determined (Proposition
4.5.6
).

Summing up, the above discussion shows that in the generalized context there is no natural analogue of Theorem
4.5.3
, irrespective of the chosen space and of the game on it. For this reason, results asserting that certain games are determined, like Corollary
7.4.13
, become informative.

Chapter 5
\lambda
-analytic and
\lambda
-projective sets

5.1.
Definition and examples

The following is the natural generalization of
[
28
, Definition 14.1]
to arbitrary infinite cardinals. By its equivalent reformulation from Proposition
5.1.2
(4)
, when
X
is a (classical) Polish space it also coincides with the notion of
\lambda
-Souslin sets (see e.g.
[
28
, Section 31.B]
).

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. A set
A\subseteq X
is
\lambda
-analytic
if it is
a continuous image of some
\lambda
-Polish space
Y
.

The collection of all
\lambda
-analytic subsets of
X
is denoted by
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
.
As usual, when
X
is clear from the context we remove it from the notation above and we sometime say that “
A
is
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}
” instead of “
A
is
\lambda
-analytic”. We also set

\displaystyle\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(X)

\displaystyle=\{A\subseteq X\mid X\setminus A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)\}

\displaystyle\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)

\displaystyle=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)\cap\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(X).

Sets in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(X)
are called
\lambda
-coanalytic
, while sets in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)
are called
\lambda
-bianalytic
.

It is straightforward to check that if
Y\subseteq X
are both
\lambda
-Polish, then

\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(Y)=\{A\cap Y\mid A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)\},

and similarly for the classes
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(Y)
and
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(Y)
.

By Corollary
4.3.3
, if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
then

(5.1)

\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X),

hence
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)
because
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
is closed under complements.

This enables us to prove several characterizations of
\lambda
-analytic sets. The proof is basically identical to the classical case
\lambda=\omega
(using of course the results proved so far, including e.g. Proposition
3.2.5
and Corollaries
4.3.6
and
4.3.7
), so it will be omitted. In what follows we denote by
p(F)
the projection on the first-coordinate of the set
F\subseteq X\times Y
.

Proposition 5.1.2
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. For any
\emptyset\neq A\subseteq X
the following are equivalent:

(1)

A
is
\lambda
-analytic;

(2)

A
is a continuous image of a closed (or even just
G_{\delta}
) subset of some
\lambda
-Polish space
Y
;

(3)

A
is a continuous image of
B(\lambda)
(equivalently, of
C(\lambda)
), or even just of a closed or
G_{\delta}
subset of it;

(4)

A=p(F)
for some closed
F\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
;

(5)

A=p(G)
for some
G\subseteq X\times Y
with
Y
a
\lambda
-Polish space and
G
a
G_{\delta}
set.

If
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
we can further add

(6)

A
is a continuous image of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
;

(7)

A=p(B)
for some
\lambda
-Borel
B\subseteq X\times Y
, where
Y
is any
\lambda
-Polish space;

(8)

A
is a continuous image of a
\lambda
-Borel subset of some Polish space
Y
;

(9)

A
is a
\lambda
-Borel image of a
\lambda
-Borel subset of some Polish space
Y
.

The various equivalent definitions of
\lambda
-analytic sets from Proposition
5.1.2
allow us to obtain more information on them. For example,
by
(9)
we have that if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then the notion of
\lambda
-analytic set depends just on the
\lambda
-Borel structure of the space
X
. Indeed, if
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
are different
\lambda
-Polish topologies on
X
such that
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau^{\prime})
and
A\subseteq X
, then
A
is
\lambda
-analytic with respect to
\tau
if and only if it is analytic with respect to
\tau^{\prime}
. The same holds for
\lambda
-coanalytic and
\lambda
-bianalytic sets.
Moreover, by
(4)
a set
A\subseteq B(\lambda)
is
\lambda
-analytic if and only if there is an
\omega
-tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\lambda\times\lambda)}
on
\lambda\times\lambda
such that
1
1

1

Formally, we should have written
A=p([T])
instead of
A=p[T]
. However, when dealing with projections of the body of a tree
T
, we prefer to systematically omit the parentheses around
[T]
in order to simplify the notation.

(5.2)

A=p[T].

Clearly, if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
we can also replace
B(\lambda)
with
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
and notice that
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
is
\lambda
-analytic if and only if there is a
\lambda
-tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(2\times 2)}
such that
A=p[T]
. Similar considerations hold for subsets of (at most countable) products of copies of
B(\lambda)
or
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
. Equation (
5.2
) provides a sort of “normal form” representation of
\lambda
-analytic sets and will often be our working definition.

Remark 5.1.3
.

Inspired by the usual definition given in the regular case (see e.g.
[
4
,
13
,
39
,
33
,
22
,
12
,
32
,
23
,
6
]
and the references contained therein), one might be tempted to define
\lambda
-analytic subsets of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
as the sets of the form
p[T]
for
T
a subtree of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(2\times\lambda)}
or, equivalently, as the projections of closed subsets of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
. However, besides the fact that if
\lambda
is singular the space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
arguably fall in a different setup because it has weight greater than
\lambda
, one can also prove that the class obtained in this way trivializes.
Indeed, when
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda>{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
there is
a
continuous
surjection from any
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{\lambda}
with
\mu>\omega
onto any nonempty
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, so that all subsets of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
are projections of closed subsetes of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
. (Indeed, by Theorem
3.2.2
we can work with nonempty sets
A\subseteq B(\lambda)
. Fix any
y_{0}\in A
and define
f\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mu}{\lambda}\to B(\lambda)
by
f(x)=x\restriction\omega
if
x\restriction\omega\in A
and
f(x)=y_{0}
otherwise. Then
f
is clearly continuous and onto
A
.)
All of this adds evidence to the fact that, in the general case, the “right” analogue of the Baire space is
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)}{\lambda}
. Notice that when
\lambda
has countable cofinality this results in the space
B(\lambda)={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}
we are currently using, while when
\lambda
is regular it is the usual space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
considered in the existing literature.

Another consequence of the reformulation
(4)
from Proposition
5.1.2

is that if

2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and
|X|>\lambda
, then the inclusion in (
5.1
) is proper, that is, there are
\lambda
-analytic subsets of
X
which are not
\lambda
-Borel: this follows from the existence of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
-universal sets for
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
, which can be obtained as in the classical case as a suitable projection of a universal set for closed subsets of
X\times B(\lambda)
. This also implies that for most
\lambda
-Polish spaces
X
there are sets
C\subseteq X
which are complete for
\lambda
-analytic sets, i.e. such that for every
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(B(\lambda))
there is a continuous function
f\colon B(\lambda)\to X
such that
f^{-1}(C)=A
.

An example of such a complete
\lambda
-analytic set is the collection of codes for ill-founded
\omega
-trees on
\lambda
, which is defined as follows.
Fix a bijection

(5.3)

\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\to\lambda

such that for all
\alpha<\lambda
there is
n\in\omega
for which
\operatorname{lh}(\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}^{-1}(\beta))\leq n
for all
\beta<\alpha
. Using
\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}
, every set
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
can be coded into an element
x_{A}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
by setting
x_{A}(\alpha)=1\iff\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}^{-1}(\alpha)\in A
. Vice versa, every
x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
codes the set
T_{x}=\{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\mid x(\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}(s))=1\}
. Let

(5.4)

\mathrm{Tr}_{\lambda}=\{x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\mid T_{x}\text{ is a tree}\}.

It is not hard to check that
\mathrm{Tr}
is closed in
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, hence it is
\lambda
-Polish whenever
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
Consider the set

\mathrm{WF}_{\lambda}=\{x\in\mathrm{Tr}\mid[T_{x}]=\emptyset\}

of (codes for) well-founded
\omega
-trees on
\lambda
, and let
\mathrm{IF}_{\lambda}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\lambda}\setminus\mathrm{WF}_{\lambda}
be the set of (codes for) ill-founded
\omega
-trees.

Proposition 5.1.4
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
The set
\mathrm{IF}_{\lambda}
is complete for
\lambda
-analytic sets.

Proof.

To see that
\mathrm{IF}_{\lambda}
is
\lambda
-analytic, consider the set
F\subseteq\mathrm{Tr}_{\lambda}\times B(\lambda)
consisting of those pair
(x,y)
such that for all
n<m
we have
\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}^{-1}(y(n))\subsetneq\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}^{-1}(y(m))
and
x(y(n))=x(y(m))=1
. Then
F
is closed and
\mathrm{IF}_{\lambda}=p(F)
.

Given
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(B(\lambda))
, let
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\lambda\times\lambda)}
be a tree such that
A=p[T]
, so that

x\in A
if and only if the corresponding section tree
T(x)
is ill-founded. Let
f\colon B(\lambda)\to\mathrm{Tr}_{\lambda}
be the function sending
x
to the (unique) code for
T(x)
. By the choice of
\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}
, the map
f
is continuous. Indeed, let
x\in B(\lambda)
and consider the basic neighborhood
\boldsymbol{N}_{f(x)\restriction\alpha}
of
f(x)
, for any
\alpha<\lambda
. Let
n\in\omega
be such that
\operatorname{lh}(\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}^{-1}(\beta))\leq n
for all
\beta<\alpha
: then
f(\boldsymbol{N}_{x\restriction n})\subseteq\boldsymbol{N}_{f(x)\restriction\alpha}
. Finally,
f^{-1}(\mathrm{IF}_{\lambda})=A
by the choice of
T
.
∎

As in the classical case, this allows us to move from
\omega
-trees to linear orders. Identify
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
with
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
using the Gödel pairing function
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon\lambda\times\lambda\to\lambda
. Given
x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
and
\alpha,\beta<\lambda
, set
\alpha\preceq_{x}\beta\iff x(\alpha,\beta)=1
. It is not hard to check that

\mathrm{LO}_{\lambda}=\{x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}\mid{\preceq_{x}}\text{ is a linear order on }\lambda\}

of (codes for) linear orders on
\lambda
is closed in
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
, and thus it is
\lambda
-Polish if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Let

\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}=\{x\in\mathrm{LO}_{\lambda}\mid{\preceq_{x}}\text{ is well-founded}\},

and
\mathrm{NWO}_{\lambda}=\mathrm{LO}_{\lambda}\setminus\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
. Then
\mathrm{NWO}_{\lambda}
is clearly
\lambda
-analytic, hence
\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
is
\lambda
-coanalytic. Consider the map sending each
x\in\mathrm{Tr}_{\lambda}
into the linear ordering
\trianglelefteq_{x}
on
\lambda
defined by setting
\alpha\trianglelefteq_{x}\beta
if and only if
x(\alpha)=x(\beta)=1
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}^{-1}(\alpha)\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}^{-1}(\beta)
(where
\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}
is the usual Kleene-Brouwer ordering), or
x(\alpha)=1
and
x(\beta)=0
, or
x(\alpha)=x(\beta)=0
and
\alpha\leq\beta
. Such map is continuous and
T_{x}\in\mathrm{WF}_{\lambda}\iff{\trianglelefteq_{x}}\in\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
. It follows that
[
28
, Theorem 27.12]
generalizes to:

Proposition 5.1.5
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
Then
\mathrm{NWO}_{\lambda}
is a complete
\lambda
-analytic set.

Remark 5.1.6
.

Propositions
5.1.4
and
5.1.5
work precisely because we assumed
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
. If
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)>\omega
, then the coding spaces
\mathrm{Tr}_{\lambda}
and
\mathrm{LO}_{\lambda}
can still be defined, and they turn out to be closed subsets of the generalized Cantor space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
. But the sets
\mathrm{IF}_{\lambda}
and
\mathrm{NWO}_{\lambda}
would then become open subsets of such spaces.
Indeed, let
x\in\mathrm{IF}_{\lambda}
, and let
(\alpha_{n})_{n\in\omega}
be a sequence of ordinals smaller than
\lambda
such that
(\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}^{-1}(\alpha)n))_{n\in\omega}
is an infinite branch through
T_{x}
. By
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)>\omega
, we have that
\alpha=\sup_{n\in\omega}\alpha_{n}<\lambda
: therefore,
\boldsymbol{N}_{x\restriction\alpha}\cap\mathrm{Tr}_{\lambda}
is a basic neighborhood of
x
contained in
\mathrm{IF}_{\lambda}
, as desired.
A similar argument shows that
\mathrm{NWO}_{\lambda}
is open in
\mathrm{LO}_{\lambda}
.

5.2.
Basic properties

We now check some standard closure properties of
\lambda
-analytic sets.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

(i)

If
X
is
\lambda
-Polish, then
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
is closed under well-ordered unions of length at most
\lambda
and countable intersections. If we further assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
is also closed under well-ordered intersections of length at most
\lambda
.

(ii)

If
X
and
Y
are
\lambda
-Polish spaces,
f\colon X\to Y
is
\lambda
-Borel,
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
, and
B\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(Y)
, then both
f^{-1}(B)\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
and
f(A)\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(Y)
.

Proof.

For the first part, let
A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
and
f_{\alpha}\colon Y_{\alpha}\to X

be witness of this. Using
(3)
of Proposition
5.1.2
we can e.g. assume that
Y_{\alpha}=B(\lambda)
for all
\alpha<\lambda
. Let
Y
be the sum of the spaces
Y_{\alpha}
, which is thus a
\lambda
-Polish space, and let
f=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}f_{\alpha}
: then
f\colon Y\to X
is continuous and
f(Y)=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}A_{\alpha}
, hence
\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
.

As for countable intersections, let
f_{n}\colon Y_{n}\to X
be witnesses of
A_{n}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
with e.g.
Y_{n}=B(\lambda)
for all
n\in\omega
.
Let

Y=\left\{(x_{n})_{n\in\omega}\in\prod\nolimits_{n\in\omega}Y_{n}\mid f_{n}(x_{n})=f_{m}(x_{m})\text{ for all }n,m\in\omega\right\}.

Since
\prod_{n\in\omega}Y_{n}
is
\lambda
-Polish and
Y
is closed in it, it is
\lambda
-Polish as well and
f\colon Y\to X\colon(x_{n})_{n\in\omega}\mapsto f_{0}(x_{0})
is a continuous function witnessing
\bigcap_{n\in\omega}A_{n}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
.

Now let
A_{\alpha}
,
Y_{\alpha}
and
f_{\alpha}
be as in the first paragraph, with all the sets
A_{\alpha}
nonempty (this is not restrictive, as otherwise
\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}A_{\alpha}=\emptyset
would trivially be
\lambda
-analytic). By the equivalent definition
(6)
in Proposition
5.1.2
, if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
we can assume that
Y_{\alpha}={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
for all
\alpha<\lambda
. Let

Y=\{x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\mid(f_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\alpha})(x)=(f_{\beta}\circ\pi_{\beta})(x)\text{ for all }\alpha,\beta<\lambda\},

where the maps
\pi_{\alpha}
are defined as in (
4.2
).
Since all the functions
f_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\alpha}
are continuous, the set
Y
is closed, and hence
\lambda
-Polish. Moreover,
f=(f_{0}\circ\pi_{0})\restriction Y
is continuous and witnesses
\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
.

The second part of the proposition can easily be proved as in the classical case (using the first part), see e.g.
[
28
, Proposition 14.4]
.
∎

Corollary 5.2.2
.

The boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
is closed under well-ordered intersections of length at most
\lambda
, countable unions, and
\lambda
-Borel preimages. If we further assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
is also closed under well-ordered unions of length at most
\lambda
.

The boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}
is closed under countable unions, countable intersections, complements, and
\lambda
-Borel preimages. If
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}
is also closed under well-ordered unions and intersections of length at most
\lambda
, i.e. each
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)
is a
\lambda^{+}
-algebra on
X
.

The normal form for
\lambda
-analytic sets
A\subseteq B(\lambda)
from equation (
5.2
) allows us to prove a higher version of Lusin-Sierpiński theorem
[
28
, Theorem 25.16]
stating that
A
can be expressed as both a
\lambda^{+}
-long union or a
\lambda^{+}
-long intersection of
\lambda
-Borel sets.

Theorem 5.2.3
.

If
A\subseteq B(\lambda)
is
\lambda
-analytic, then
A=\bigcup_{\xi<\lambda^{+}}A_{\xi}=\bigcap_{\xi<\lambda^{+}}B_{\xi}
with
A_{\xi},B_{\xi}\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}
, and the same is true for
\lambda
-coanalytic sets.

Sketch of the proof.

(See
[
28
, Theorem 25.16]
for more details.)
Let
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\lambda\times\lambda)}
be a tree such that
A=p[T]
. For
\xi<\lambda^{+}
and
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
set

C^{\xi}_{s}=\{x\in B(\lambda)\mid\rho_{T(x)}(s)\leq\xi\}.

Arguing by induction on
\xi<\lambda^{+}
, one easily sees that each
C^{\xi}_{s}
is
\lambda
-Borel. Indeed,
C^{0}_{s}=\{x\in B(\lambda)\mid\forall\alpha<\lambda\,(x\restriction(\operatorname{lh}(s)+1),s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\alpha)\notin T\}
is closed, and if
\xi>0
then
C^{\xi}_{s}=\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}\bigcup_{\xi^{\prime}<\xi}C^{\xi^{\prime}}_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\alpha}
.

Then
A=\bigcap_{\xi<\lambda^{+}}B_{\xi}
with
B_{\xi}=B(\lambda)\setminus C^{\xi}_{\emptyset}
. On the other hand,
A=\bigcup_{\xi<\lambda^{+}}A_{\xi}
with

\displaystyle A_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\{x\in B(\lambda)\mid[T(x)]\neq\emptyset\wedge\rho(T(x))\leq\xi\}

\displaystyle=\{x\in B(\lambda)\mid\rho_{T(x)}(\emptyset)>\xi\wedge\forall s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\,(\rho_{T(x)}(s)\neq\xi)\}

\displaystyle=\Big\{x\in B(\lambda)\mid x\notin C^{\xi}_{\emptyset}\wedge\forall s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\,\Big(x\in C^{\xi}_{s}\Rightarrow x\in\bigcup\nolimits_{\xi^{\prime}<\xi}C^{\xi^{\prime}}_{s}\Big)\Big\}.\qed

As a consequence, using Corollary
4.3.5
we also get information on the cardinality of
\lambda
-coanalytic sets (under a small amount of additional choice
2
2

2

The choiceless version of Corollary
5.2.4
reads as follows: Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. For every
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(X)
, either
A
can be written as a
\lambda^{+}
-sized union of well-orderable sets of size at most
\lambda
, or there is a continuous injection from
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
into
A
(and thus
|A|=2^{\lambda}
).
).

Corollary 5.2.4

(
{\sf AC}_{\lambda^{+}}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
)
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. For every
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(B(\lambda))
, either
A
is well-orderable and
|A|\leq\lambda^{+}
or there is a continuous injection from
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
into
A
(and thus
|A|=2^{\lambda}
).

Of course a similar result holds for
\lambda
-analytic sets, but for such sets we will actually prove a better result in Corollary
7.1.2
.

In view of Theorem
5.2.1
(ii)
and Theorem
5.3.10
, if
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible then both Theorem
5.2.3
and Corollary
5.2.4
can be extended to arbitrary
\lambda
-Polish spaces.

5.3.
Lusin separation theorem and Souslin’s theorem

Replacing
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
and Borel sets with, respectively,
B(\lambda)
and
\lambda
-Borel sets in the proof of
[
28
, Theorem 14.7]
we get the analogue of the Lusin separation theorem.

Theorem 5.3.1
.

Let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish and
A,B\subseteq X
be disjoint
\lambda
-analytic sets. Then there is a
\lambda
-Borel set
C\subseteq X
separating
A
from
B
, i.e. such that
A\subseteq C
and
C\cap B=\emptyset
.

Remark 5.3.2
.

If one follows the “constructive proof” of
[
38
, Theorem 2E.1]
instead of the proof of
[
28
, Theorem 14.7]
, then one can actually exhibit a somewhat canonical
\lambda
-Borel set separating any two disjoint
\lambda
-analytic subsets of
B(\lambda)
. Moreover, this can be extended to subsets of an arbitrary
\lambda
-Polish space using Proposition
3.2.5
and Remark
3.2.6
in the obvious way. The possibility of finding a canonical witness to Theorem
5.3.1
might be useful in choiceless settings.

Remark 5.3.3
.

Since
\lambda
-analytic subsets of a
\lambda
-Polish space are closed under finite intersections, the notion of
\lambda
-analicity can be relativized to
\lambda
-analytic spaces. More precisely, if
Y\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
then
A=A^{\prime}\cap Y
for some
A^{\prime}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
if and only if
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
(and thus
A
is a continuous image of a
\lambda
-Polish space itself). This allows us to transfer Theorem
5.3.1
to the broader setup of
\lambda
-analytic spaces
, that is, of those spaces which are homeomorphic to a
\lambda
-analytic subset of a
\lambda
-Polish space. The same applies to most of the results related to separation theorems for
\lambda
-analytic sets.

Since in Proposition
5.2.1
(i)
we proved that
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}
is closed under well-ordered unions of length at most
\lambda
, we also get:

Corollary 5.3.4
.

Let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish and
(A_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
be a sequence of pairwise disjoint
\lambda
-analytic subsets of
X
. Then there are pairwise disjoint
\lambda
-Borel sets
B_{\alpha}
with
B_{\alpha}\supseteq A_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\lambda
.

Finally, taking
B=X\setminus A
in Theorem
5.3.1
when
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)
, we get the analogue of Souslin’s theorem.

Theorem 5.3.5
.

Assume
3
3

3

Interestingly enough, the condition
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
is required to prove
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)
, while the reverse inclusion
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
holds unconditionally.

that

2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish. Then
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)
.

Arguing as in the classical case, we can then apply this result to get a converse to Proposition
4.1.3
.

Theorem 5.3.6
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
Let
X,Y
be
\lambda
-Polish spaces, and let
f\colon X\to Y
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

f
is
\lambda
-Borel;

(2)

\mathrm{graph}(f)\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X\times Y)
.

In particular, if
f
is a
\lambda
-Borel bijection, then
f
is a
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism (i.e.
f^{-1}
is
\lambda
-Borel as well).

If we assume
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then we can add

(3)

\mathrm{graph}(f)\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X\times Y)
.

to the above list of equivalent conditions.

Remark 5.3.7
.

It is easy to see that Theorem
5.3.6
holds also for functions
f\colon A\to B
with
A\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
and
B\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(Y)
.

Further consequence that can be proved
mutatis mutandis
as in the classical case
\lambda=\omega
are the following ones.

Theorem 5.3.8
.

Let
X,Y
be
\lambda
-Polish spaces and
f\colon X\to Y
be
\lambda
-Borel. If
A\subseteq X
is
\lambda
-Borel and
f\restriction A
is injective, then
f(A)
is
\lambda
-Borel and
f\restriction A
is a
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism between
A
and
f(A)
.

Together with Corollary
4.3.3
, this gives:

Corollary 5.3.9
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and
let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish. A set
A\subseteq X
is
\lambda
-Borel if and only if it is a continuous (equivalently,
\lambda
-Borel) injective image of a closed subset of
B(\lambda)
.

Using again Proposition
3.2.5
and Remark
3.2.6
, Corollary
3.4.8
, and the
\lambda
-Borel version of the usual Cantor-Schröder-Bernstein argument granted by Theorem
5.3.8
, we finally get the following isomorphism theorem.

Theorem 5.3.10
.

Assume that
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible. If
X
is a
\lambda
-Polish space with
|X|>\lambda
, then
X
is
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic to
B(\lambda)
. Moreover, if
X,Y
are
\lambda
-Polish spaces, then
X
and
Y
are
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic if and only if
|X|=|Y|
.

Together with the fact that by Corollary
3.4.8
either
X
is well-orderable and
|X|\leq\lambda
, or else
|X|=|B(\lambda)|=\lambda^{\omega}
, this completely classifies
\lambda
-Polish spaces up to
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism when
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible. Moreover, it allows us to rewrite Corollary
5.3.9
as follows.

Corollary 5.3.11
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and
let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish. A set
A\subseteq X
is
\lambda
-Borel if and only if either
|A|\leq\lambda
or else
A
is a
\lambda
-Borel injective image of the whole
B(\lambda)
.

5.4.
Standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces

5.4.1.
\lambda
-measurable spaces and functions

A
\lambda
-measurable space
is a pair
X=(X,\mathcal{B})
with
\mathcal{B}
a
\lambda^{+}
-algebra on
X
.
A function
f
between two
\lambda
-measurable spaces
(X,\mathcal{B})
and
(Y,\mathcal{C})
is
\lambda
-measurable
if
f^{-1}(C)\in\mathcal{B}
for all
C\in\mathcal{C}
.
Two
\lambda
-measurable spaces
(X,\mathcal{B})
and
(Y,\mathcal{C})
are
isomorphic
if there is a bijection
f\colon X\to Y
such that both
f
and
f^{-1}
are
\lambda
-measurable, that is,
B\in\mathcal{B}
if and only if
f(B)\in\mathcal{C}
for all
B\subseteq X
.

Definition 5.4.1
.

A
\lambda
-measurable space
X=(X,\mathcal{B})
is called
\lambda
-Borel space
if
\mathcal{B}
is
\lambda
-generated and separates points, i.e. for all distinct
x,y\in X
there is
B\in\mathcal{B}
such that
x\in B
while
y\notin B
.

If
(X,\mathcal{B})
is a
\lambda
-Borel space, any
Y\subseteq X
can be canonically equipped with the
\lambda^{+}
-algebra
\mathcal{B}\restriction Y=\{B\cap Y\mid B\in\mathcal{B}\}
, which turns it into a
\lambda
-Borel space as well.
Moreover,
every metrizable space
X
of weight at most
\lambda
can be canonically turned into a
\lambda
-Borel space by setting
\mathcal{B}=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
. For this reason, if
(X,\mathcal{B})
is an arbitrary
\lambda
-Borel space, the elements of
\mathcal{B}
are called
\lambda
-Borel sets of
X
. Similarly,
\lambda
-measurable functions between
\lambda
-Borel spaces are called
\lambda
-Borel (measurable). The notions of
\lambda
-Borel embedding and
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism are defined accordingly. Notice that all the terminology and notation is consistent with the one introduced at the beginning of Section
4
.

Naturally adapting the proof of
[
28
, Proposition 12.1]
and recalling that if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
then the product topology and the bounded topology on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
generate the same
\lambda
-Borel sets, one easily obtain the following characterization of
\lambda
-Borel spaces.

Proposition 5.4.2
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and let
(X,\mathcal{B})
be a
\lambda
-measurable space. The following are equivalent:

(1)

(X,\mathcal{B})
is a
\lambda
-Borel space;

(2)

\mathcal{B}=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)
for some metrizable topology
\tau
on
X
of weight at most
\lambda
;

(3)

(X,\mathcal{B})
is isomorphic to
(Z,\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(Z))
for some
Z\subseteq Y
with
Y
a
\lambda
-Polish space;

(4)

(X,\mathcal{B})
is isomorphic to
(Z,\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(Z))
for some
Z\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
.

(5)

(X,\mathcal{B})
is isomorphic to
(Z,\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(Z))
for some subspace
Z
of the topological space
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2},\tau_{p})
, where
\tau_{p}
is the product topology.

Similarly, adapting the classical argument one obtains the following analogue of
[
28
, Theorem 12.2]
, originally due to Kuratowski.

Theorem 5.4.3
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-measurable space and
Y
be
\lambda
-Polish. If
Z\subseteq X
and
f\colon Z\to Y
is
\lambda
-measurable, then there is a

\lambda
-measurable function
\hat{f}\colon X\to Y
extending
f
.

It follows that any partial
\lambda
-Borel function between
\lambda
-Polish spaces can be extended to a total
\lambda
-Borel function.

Definition 5.4.4
.

A
\lambda
-measurable space
X=(X,\mathcal{B})
is a
standard
\lambda
-Borel space
if there is a
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau
on
X
such that
\mathcal{B}=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)
.

With an abuse of terminology, we will also say that a topological space
(X,\tau)
is (standard)
\lambda
-Borel if so is the
\lambda
-measurable space
(X,\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau))
, even if
\tau
itself was not
\lambda
-Polish.

Example 5.4.5
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and equip
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
with the
product
topology
\tau_{p}
. The space
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda},\tau_{p})
is not
\lambda
-Polish because it is not even first-countable (hence neither metrizable), yet its weight is
\lambda
and, being Hausdorff,
\tau_{p}
separates points. It follows that
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda},\mathcal{B})
, where
\mathcal{B}=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda},\tau_{p})
, is a
\lambda
-Borel space: we claim that it is standard. Indeed, equip
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
with the product topology. Then the map
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}\to{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
sending
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
to the characteristic function of its graph is a topological embedding, and one can easily compute that its range is
\lambda
-Borel. Identifying
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
with
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
in the obvious way,
we get that
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda},\mathcal{B})
is
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic to a
\lambda
-Borel subset of

({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2},\tau_{p})
, thus it is standard
\lambda
-Borel because the product and the bounded topology on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
give rise to the same
\lambda
-Borel sets under
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
,
and each
\lambda
-Borel subset of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
can be equipped with a
\lambda
-Polish topology generating its
\lambda
-Borel structure by Corollary
4.3.6
.
(More generally, see condition
(5)
in the ensuing Proposition
5.4.6
).

In contrast, if we equip
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
with the bounded topology
\tau_{b}
, we get a space which is completely metrizable but has weight
\lambda^{{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)}>\lambda
, hence it is not
\lambda
-Polish again. But this time one can actually check that
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda},\tau_{b})
is not even (standard)
\lambda
-Borel. Indeed, any
\lambda
-generated
\lambda^{+}
-algebra has size at most
2^{\lambda}
, while there are
2^{(\lambda^{{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)})}=2^{2^{\lambda}}
distinct
\tau_{b}
-open sets, hence
|\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda},\tau_{b})|=2^{2^{\lambda}}>2^{\lambda}
.

The only nontrivial implications in the next result are
(3)

\Rightarrow

(2)
and
(1)

\Rightarrow

(4)
: the former follows from Corollary
4.3.6
, while the latter follows from Proposition
5.4.2
and Theorem
5.3.8
.

Proposition 5.4.6
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Borel space. The following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is a standard
\lambda
-Borel space;

(2)

X
is
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic to some
\lambda
-Polish space
Y
(we can additionally require
\mathrm{dim}(Y)=0
);

(3)

X
is
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic to a
\lambda
-Borel subset of some
\lambda
-Polish space
Y
(with
