\mathrm{dim}(Y)=0
, if needed);

(4)

X
is
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic to a
\lambda
-Borel subset of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
;

(5)

X
is
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic to a
\lambda
-Borel subset of
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2},\tau_{p})
.

Propositions
5.4.2
and
5.4.6
in particular imply that (standard)
\lambda
-Borel spaces are closed under (
\lambda
-Borel) subspaces,
but one can show that they are also closed under products of size
\lambda
. Recall that the product of
\lambda
-many
\lambda
-measurable spaces
(X_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i})
is the
\lambda
-measurable space
\big(\prod_{i<\lambda}X_{i},\mathcal{B}_{\Pi}\big)
, where
\mathcal{B}_{\Pi}
is the
\lambda^{+}
-algebra on the Cartesian product
\prod_{i<\lambda}X_{i}
generated by the direct product
4
4

4

Recall that the direct product of the
\lambda^{+}
-algebras
\mathcal{B}_{i}
is the collection of the sets of the form
\prod_{i<\lambda}A_{i}\subseteq\prod_{i<\lambda}X_{i}
where
A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{i}
for all
i<\lambda
. It is a
\lambda^{+}
-complete Boolean algebra if equipped with the
pointwise operations
, but it is not closed under unions, intersections, and complements relatively to the ambient space
\prod_{i<\lambda}X_{i}
.

of the
\lambda^{+}
-algebras
\mathcal{B}_{i}
. Notice that if each
\mathcal{B}_{i}
is generated by a corresponding topology
\tau_{i}
on
X
, then the resulting
\mathcal{B}_{\Pi}
on
X
coincides with the
\lambda^{+}
-algebra generated by some/any product topology
5
5

5

Here we can equivalently take any
\mu\leq\lambda
and consider the
<\mu
-supported product topology. Even considering the box topology would give rise to the same
\lambda^{+}
-algebra.

of the
\tau_{i}
; notice however that even if all the
\tau_{i}
are
\lambda
-Polish, such a product needs not to be
\lambda
-Polish.

Proposition 5.4.7
.

Assume
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Suppose that
(X_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i})
are (standard)
\lambda
-Borel spaces for
i<\lambda
. Then the product
\lambda
-measurable space
\big(\prod_{i<\lambda}X_{i},\mathcal{B}_{\Pi}\big)
is (standard)
\lambda
-Borel.

Proof.

By
(5)
of Proposition
5.4.2
, each
(X_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i})
is
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic to a subspace
A_{i}
of
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2},\tau_{p})
via some
f_{i}
. Thus the product
\prod_{i<\lambda}f_{i}
of such maps witness that
\big(\prod_{i<\lambda}X_{i},\mathcal{B}_{\Pi}\big)
is
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic to
\prod_{i<\lambda}A_{i}\subseteq\prod_{i<\lambda}{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})}
, where the latter is equipped with the induced product
\lambda
-Borel structure. However, such
\lambda^{+}
-algebra is induced by the
<\omega
-supported product of
\tau_{p}
on each copy of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, which turns
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})}
into a topological space homeomorphic to
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2},\tau_{p})
. Thus
\big(\prod_{i<\lambda}X_{i},\mathcal{B}_{\Pi}\big)
is
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic to a subspace of
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2},\tau_{p})
, and hence we are done by
(5)
of Proposition
5.4.2
.

For the case of standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces, it is enough to use Proposition
5.4.6
instead of Proposition
5.4.2
and notice that if all the sets
A_{i}
were in
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2},\tau_{p})
, then
\prod_{i<\lambda}A_{i}
is by definition in the product of
\lambda
-many copies of
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2},\tau_{p})
.
∎

By Proposition
5.4.6
, if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
then Corollary
3.4.8
and Theorem
5.3.10
extend to standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces as well (including
\lambda
-Borel subsets of
\lambda
-Polish spaces).

Theorem 5.4.8
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. If
X
is a standard
\lambda
-Borel space,
then either
X
is well-orderable and
|X|\leq\lambda
, or else

X
is
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic to
B(\lambda)
(and thus has size
\lambda^{\omega}=2^{\lambda}
). Moreover, if
X,Y
are standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces, then
X
and
Y
are
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic if and only if
|X|=|Y|
.

In particular we get:

Corollary 5.4.9
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
B\subseteq X
is
\lambda
-Borel, then
|B|>\lambda
if and only if
B
is
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic to
B(\lambda)
.

This result is similar in spirit to
[
47
, Theorem 13]
, but it is different: on the one hand we have the additional assumption
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda>{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
and we reach the weaker conclusion that the spaces be
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic (rather than (
\omega_{1}
-)Borel isomorphic); but on the other hand we can apply it to arbitrary
\lambda
-Borel sets and not just (classical) Borel sets.

Proposition
5.4.6
allows us to transfer many notion and results from the realm of
\lambda
-Polish spaces to that of standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces in a natural way. For example a subset of a standard
\lambda
-Borel space
(X,\mathcal{B})
is called
\lambda
-analytic
if and only if it is
\lambda
-analytic with respect to some
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau
on
X
for which
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)=\mathcal{B}
: when
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, the definition depends only on the
\lambda
-Borel structure of
X
and not on the chosen
\tau
.
Equivalently, a nonempty
A\subseteq X
is
\lambda
-analytic if and only if it is the image of
B(\lambda)
through a
\lambda
-Borel measurable function.
It readily follows that all separation theorems seen so far, together with their consequences, hold in this broader context as well. For example:

•

a subset of a standard
\lambda
-Borel set is
\lambda
-Borel if and only if it is
\lambda
-bianalytic,

•

a function between standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces
X
and
Y
is
\lambda
-Borel if and only it its graph is
\lambda
-Borel in
X\times Y
,

and so on.

5.4.2.
The Effros
\lambda
-Borel space

In the classical setting, a widely considered standard Borel space is the so-called Effros Borel space. This can be straightforwardly generalized as follows. Given a topological space, endow the collection
F(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{1}(X)
of all closed subsets of
X
with the

\lambda
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{F}(X)
generated by the sets of the form

(5.5)

B_{U}(X)=\{F\in F(X)\mid F\cap U\neq\emptyset\},

where
U
varies over open subsets of
X
. Moreover, if
X
has weight at most
\lambda
, then in (
5.5
) it is enough to consider the open sets
U
in a fixed
\lambda
-sized basis. This shows that under such hypothesis
\mathcal{B}_{F}(X)
is
\lambda
-generated. Moreover, it separate points: indeed, if
F,F^{\prime}\in F(X)
are distinct with
F\nsubseteq F^{\prime}
, then
B_{X\setminus F^{\prime}}(X)
contains
F
but not
F^{\prime}
. This shows that
(F(X),\mathcal{B}_{F}(X))
is a
\lambda
-Borel space, that in analogy with the classical case, will be called
Effros
\lambda
-Borel space
(of
X
) and denoted by
F(X)
.

The next result is the analogue of
[
28
, Theorem 12.6]
. However, the proof is necessarily different, as spaces
X
such that
|X|\nleq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
cannot have a (metrizable) compactification.
We provide an alternative argument, which incidentally works also in the classical case

\lambda=\omega
.

Theorem 5.4.10
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. If
X
is a
\lambda
-Polish space, then its Effros
\lambda
-Borel space
F(X)
is standard.

Proof.

We first prove the result when
X=B(\lambda)
, following
[
28
, Exercise 12.10]
. Recall from
[
28
, Proposition 2.4]
that the map
F\mapsto T_{F}=\{x\restriction n\mid x\in F\wedge n\in\omega\}
is a bijection between
F(B(\lambda))
and pruned
\omega
-trees
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
.
Recall also the
\lambda
-Polish space
\mathrm{Tr}_{\lambda}
of (codes for)
\omega
-trees on
\lambda
from equation (
5.4
), and the bijection
\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\to\lambda
from equation (
5.3
) used to define it.
Consider the subspace
\mathrm{PTr}_{\lambda}
of (codes for) pruned
\omega
-trees: it is easy to see that
\mathrm{PTr}_{\lambda}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{2}(\mathrm{Tr}_{\lambda})
, so it is a
\lambda
-Borel subset of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
. We claim that the map
F\mapsto T_{F}
is a
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism between
F(B(\lambda))
and
\mathrm{PTr}_{\lambda}
, thus witnessing that
F(B(\lambda))
is standard by conditions
(4)
of Proposition
5.4.6
. It is clear that for every
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
,

F\in B_{\boldsymbol{N}_{s}}(B(\lambda))\iff s\in T_{F}\iff x_{T_{F}}(\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}(s))=1.

Since the sets of the form
\{x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\mid x(\alpha)=i\}
with
\alpha<\lambda
and
i\in\{0,1\}
constitute a subbasis for the product topology on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, and since the product and the bounded topology on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
generate the same
\lambda
-Borel structure because we assumed
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, it easily follows that
F\mapsto T_{F}
is a
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism.

We now move to an arbitrary
\lambda
-Polish space
X
, which can be assumed to be nonempty because otherwise
F(X)=\{\emptyset\}
would trivially be
\lambda
-Polish. By Corollary
3.2.9
, there is a continuous open surjection
g\colon B(\lambda)\to X
. We claim that the map

(5.6)

G\colon F(X)\to F(B(\lambda)),\quad F\mapsto g^{-1}(F)

is a
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism between
F(X)
and a
\lambda
-Borel subset of
F(B(\lambda))
, which implies that
F(X)
is standard by Proposition
5.4.6
again.

First observe that
G
is injective: if
F_{0},F_{1}\in F(X)
are distinct and
x\in F_{i}\setminus F_{1-i}
, then every
y\in g^{-1}(x)
, which exists because
g
is surjective, will belong to
G(F_{i})
but not to
G(F_{1-i})
, witnessing that they are different.

We next show that the range of
G
is
\lambda
-Borel.
Given
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
, let
V_{s}=g^{-1}(g(\boldsymbol{N}_{s}))
, which is open by the choice of
g
. We claim that the range of
G
consists of those
F^{\prime}\in F(B(\lambda))
such that

(5.7)

\forall s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\,(F^{\prime}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\neq\emptyset\iff F^{\prime}\cap V_{s}\neq\emptyset),

which proves that it is
\lambda
-Borel with respect to the Effros
\lambda
-Borel structure of
B(\lambda)
.
Let
F^{\prime}
be in the range of
G
, i.e.
F^{\prime}=g^{-1}(F)
for some
F\in F(X)
. Then by surjectivity of
g
we have, for every
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
:

g^{-1}(F)\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\neq\emptyset\iff F\cap g(\boldsymbol{N}_{s})\neq\emptyset\iff g^{-1}(F)\cap V_{s}\neq\emptyset.

Thus equation (
5.7
) holds for such an
F^{\prime}
.
Conversely, assume that
F^{\prime}
is not in the range of
G
. Since
g
is open and surjective,
if
g^{-1}(A)
is closed than so is
A
, because
X\setminus A=g(B(\lambda)\setminus g^{-1}(A))
. Applying this with
A=g(F^{\prime})
, one easily gets
that
F^{\prime\prime}=g^{-1}(g(F^{\prime}))\supsetneq F^{\prime}
— the inclusion must be proper because otherwise
g(F^{\prime})
would be closed and
F=F^{\prime\prime}=G(g(F^{\prime}))
would be in the range of
G
, against the choice of
F^{\prime}
. Pick
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
such that
F^{\prime\prime}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\neq\emptyset
but
F^{\prime}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}=\emptyset
. By definition of
V_{s}
, we have
F^{\prime}\cap V_{s}\neq\emptyset
because

F^{\prime}\cap V_{s}\neq\emptyset\iff g(F^{\prime})\cap g(\boldsymbol{N}_{s})\neq\emptyset\iff F^{\prime\prime}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\neq\emptyset.

Hence
s
witnesses that (
5.7
) is not satisfied by
F^{\prime}
, as desired.

Finally, we address
\lambda
-measurability of the map
G
and of its inverse, starting from the latter. If
U\subseteq X
is open, then by surjectivity of
g
we have that for every
F\in F(X)

F\cap U\neq\emptyset\iff g^{-1}(F)\cap g^{-1}(U)\neq\emptyset,

so that the
G
-image of the basic
\lambda
-Borel set
B_{U}(X)
is the (trace on the range of
G
of the) basic
\lambda
-Borel set
B_{g^{-1}(U)}(B(\lambda))
. Conversely, fix any
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
and
F\in F(X)
. Since the set
g^{-1}(F)
, being in the range of
G
, satisfies (
5.7
), by surjectivity of
g
we have that

g^{-1}(F)\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\neq\emptyset\iff g^{-1}(F)\cap V_{s}\neq\emptyset\iff F\cap g(\boldsymbol{N}_{s})\neq\emptyset.

Since
g(\boldsymbol{N}_{s})
is open in
X
, this shows that the
G
-preimage of
B_{\boldsymbol{N}_{s}}(B(\lambda))
is
B_{g(\boldsymbol{N}_{s})}(X)
.
Thus
G
and
G^{-1}
are both
\lambda
-Borel measurable, and
G
is a
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism between
F(X)
and the range of
G
, which is a
\lambda
-Borel subset of
F(B(\lambda))
.
∎

Example 5.4.11
.

Consider again a universal
\lambda
-Polish space
\mathfrak{U}_{\lambda}
(see the end of Section
3.1
). The space
F(\mathfrak{U}_{\lambda})
can be equipped with its Effros
\lambda
-Borel structure, which is standard by Theorem
5.4.10
; thus
F(\mathfrak{U}_{\lambda})
can be construed as the standard
\lambda
-Borel space
6
6

6

A different way of forming a space of (codes for)
\lambda
-Polish spaces was presented in
[
4
,
40
]
.

of all
\lambda
-Polish spaces. Similarly, by Proposition
3.3.2
the Effros
\lambda
-Borel space
F(B(\lambda))
can be viewed as the standard
\lambda
-Borel space of all
\lambda
-Polish spaces with
\mathrm{dim}(X)=0
.

Theorem
5.4.10
unlocks a number of interesting and useful facts, whose proof goes essentially unchanged. For example,
we have the following analogue of the Kuratowski-Ryll-Nardzewski’s Selection Theorem for
F(X)
.

Theorem 5.4.12
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then there is a sequence
(\sigma^{X}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
of
\lambda
-Borel functions
\sigma^{X}_{\alpha}\colon F(X)\to X
such that for every nonempty
F\in F(X)
the set
\{\sigma^{X}_{\alpha}(F)\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
is dense in
F
.

We present a slight variation of the classical proof which relies on the constructions carried out in the proof of Theorem
5.4.10
.

Proof.

(Compare with
[
28
, Theorem 12.13]
.)
Consider first the case
X=B(\lambda)
. Fix any
y_{0}\in B(\lambda)
, and consider the map
\sigma\colon F(B(\lambda))\to B(\lambda)
defined by
\sigma(\emptyset)=y_{0}
and

\sigma(F)=\text{ the leftmost branch of }T_{F}

if
F\neq\emptyset
. It is easy to see that
\sigma
is
\lambda
-Borel, and that
\sigma(F)\in F
when
F\neq\emptyset
. Next fix a bijection
f\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\to\lambda
. For each
\alpha<\lambda
, the map from
F(X)
into itself sending
F
to
F\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{f^{-1}(\alpha)}
is
\lambda
-Borel because
\boldsymbol{N}_{f^{-1}(\alpha)}
is clopen. The maps
\sigma^{B(\lambda)}_{\alpha}\colon F(B(\lambda))\to B(\lambda)
can then be defined by setting
\sigma^{B(\lambda)}_{\alpha}(F)=\sigma(F\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{f^{-1}(\alpha)})
if
F\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{f^{-1}(\alpha)}\neq\emptyset
, and
\sigma^{B(\lambda)}_{\alpha}(F)=\sigma(F)
otherwise.

We now move to an arbitrary
\lambda
-Polish space
X
. Without loss of generality we may assume
X\neq\emptyset
. Let
G\colon F(X)\to F(B(\lambda))
be the map defined in (
5.6
), where
g\colon B(\lambda)\to X
is an open continuous surjection and
G(F)=g^{-1}(F)
for every
F\in F(X)
. Then each
\sigma^{X}_{\alpha}=g\circ\sigma^{B(\lambda)}_{\alpha}\circ G
is clearly
\lambda
-Borel. Fix
\emptyset\neq F\in F(X)
and
U\subseteq X
open such that
F\cap U\neq\emptyset
. Then
G(F)\cap g^{-1}(U)\neq\emptyset
as well because
g
is surjective. Let
\alpha<\lambda
be such that
\sigma_{\alpha}^{B(\lambda)}(G(F))\in G(F)\cap g^{-1}(U)
:
then
\sigma^{X}_{\alpha}(F)\in F\cap U
. This shows that
\{\sigma^{X}_{\alpha}(F)\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
is dense in
F
, hence we are done.
∎

Recall that given an equivalence relation
E
on
X
, a
selector
for
E
is a map
s\colon X\to X
such that
s(x)\mathrel{E}x
and
s(x)=s(y)
whenever
x\mathrel{E}y
, while a
transversal
for
E
is a set
T\subseteq X
that meets every equivalence class in exactly one point. Notice that if
s
is a selector for
E
, then
T=\{x\in X\mid s(x)=x\}
is a transversal for
X
; conversely, if
T
is a transversal for
E
then the map
s
sending
x
to the unique
y\in T
with
y\mathrel{E}x
is a selector for
X
. These observations work in the definable context as well. By the previous arguments, if
X
is a standard
\lambda
-Borel space then every
\lambda
-Borel sector
s
for
E
yields a
\lambda
-Borel transversal
T
for
E
, while if
E
is
\lambda
-Borel (as a subset of
X^{2}
) then every
\lambda
-Borel transversal
T
for
E
yields the
\lambda
-Borel selector
s
for
E
defined by setting
s(x)=y
if and only if
x\mathrel{E}y
and
y\in T
.

For a set
A\subseteq X
, the (
E
-
)
saturation

[A]_{E}
of
A
is defined by

[A]_{E}=\{x\in X\mid\exists y\in A\,(x\mathrel{E}y)\}.

The following is a direct consequence of Theorem
5.4.12
and is obtained by adapting the proof of
[
28
, Theorem 12.16]
in the obvious way.

Theorem 5.4.13
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
,
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space, and
E
be an equivalence relation on
X
such that every equivalence class is closed and the saturation of any open set is
\lambda
-Borel. Then
E
admits a
\lambda
-Borel selector, and thus also a Borel transversal.

Sketch of the proof.

(See
[
28
, Theorem 12.16]
for more details.)
By our assumptions, the map
X\to F(X)
sending
x
to
[x]_{E}
is well-defined and
\lambda
-Borel, hence the map
s\colon X\to X
defined by
s(x)=\sigma_{0}^{X}([x]_{E})
, where
\sigma_{0}^{X}
is as in Theorem
5.4.12
, is a
\lambda
-Borel selector.
∎

The condition that the saturation of open sets is
\lambda
-Borel can easily be replaced by the condition that the saturation of closed sets is
\lambda
-Borel. An important special case of
Theorem
5.4.13
is the following (see
[
28
, Theorem 12.17]
). A
\lambda
-Polish group
is a topological group whose topology is
\lambda
-Polish.

Theorem 5.4.14
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
,
G
be a
\lambda
-Polish group, and
H\subseteq G
be a closed subgroup. Then there is a
\lambda
-Borel selector for the equivalence relation whose classes are the (left) cosets of
H
. In particular, there is a
\lambda
-Borel set meeting every (left) coset in exactly one point.

Proof.

Theorem
5.4.13
can be applied because every (left) coset
gH
is closed and the saturation of an open set
U
is the open set
UH=\bigcup_{h\in H}Uh
.
∎

The following is a standard application of Theorem
5.4.14
: we report its short proof for the reader’s convenience.

Corollary 5.4.15
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
G
and
H
be
\lambda
-Polish groups. If
\varphi\colon G\to H
is a continuous homomorphism, then
\varphi(G)
is
\lambda
-Borel in
H
.

Proof.

Apply Theorem
5.4.14
to get a
\lambda
-Borel set
T
meeting every (left) coset of the the closed subgroup
\varphi^{-1}(\mathrm{id}_{H})
of
G
in exactly one point. Then
\varphi\restriction T
is injective, hence
\varphi(T)
is
\lambda
-Borel by Theorem
5.3.8
. Moreover
\varphi(T)=\varphi(G)
, hence the result follows.
∎

From Theorem
5.4.14
we also obtain the following (weak) analogue of Miller’s theorem
[
28
, Theorem 15.14]
.

Theorem 5.4.16
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
,
G
be a
\lambda
-Polish group, and
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
(g,x)\mapsto g\cdot x
is a continuous action of
G
on
X
, then for every
x\in X
its orbit
\{g\cdot x\mid g\in G\}
is
\lambda
-Borel.

Sketch of the proof.

(See
[
28
, Theorem 15.14]
for more details.)
Since the action is continuous, the stabilizer
G_{x}=\{g\in G\mid g\cdot x=x\}
of each point
x\in X
is a closed subgroup of
G
. Let
T_{x}
be a
\lambda
-Borel set meeting every left coset of
G_{x}
in exactly one point (Theorem
5.4.14
). The map
g\mapsto g\cdot x
turns out to be a
\lambda
-Borel bijection between
T_{x}
and the orbit
\{g\cdot x\mid g\in G\}
, hence the latter is
\lambda
-Borel by Theorem
5.3.8
.
∎

5.5.
Other separation theorems for
\lambda
-analytic sets

Some of the results of this section are stated for
\lambda
-Polish spaces in order to minimize the cardinal arithmetic assumptions on
\lambda
.
However, if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
they can be extended to standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces as well, whenever this makes sense.

5.5.1.
Invariant Souslin’s theorem

We begin with the invariant version of Theorem
5.3.1
.
An equivalence relation
E
on a
\lambda
-Polish (or even just standard
\lambda
-Borel) space
X
is called
\lambda
-analytic if
E\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X\times X)
; similarly,
E
is
\lambda
-Borel if
E\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X\times X)
. A set
A\subseteq X
is
E
-invariant
if

A=[A]_{E}
. Notice that if both
E
and
A
are
\lambda
-analytic, then so is
[A]_{E}
.

Theorem 5.5.1
.

Let
E
be a
\lambda
-analytic equivalence relation on a
\lambda
-Polish space
X
. If
A,B\subseteq X
be disjoint
\lambda
-analytic
E
-invariant sets, then there is an
E
-invariant
\lambda
-Borel set
C\subseteq X
separating
A
from
B
.

The following argument is pretty standard, and is reported only for the reader’s convenience.

Proof.

Recursively
7
7

7

The proof seems to require an axiom of dependent choices, namely
{\sf DC}_{\omega}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
, to be carried out. However, once a canonical representation witnessing
E\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X^{2})
is fixed, the sets
A_{i}
and
C_{i}
can all be determined in a canonical way by Remark
5.3.2
.

define the sequences
(A_{i})_{i\in\omega}
and
(C_{i})_{i\in\omega}
by letting
A_{0}=A
,
A_{i+1}=[C_{i}]_{E}
, and
C_{i}
be any
\lambda
-Borel set separating the disjoint
\lambda
-analytic sets
A_{i}
and
B
(such
C_{i}
exists by Theorem
5.3.1
). Then let
C=\bigcup_{i\in\omega}C_{i}=\bigcup_{i\in\omega}A_{i}
.
∎

5.5.2.
Novikov’s separation theorem

The following separation theorem is the analogue of
[
28
, Theorem 28.5]
, due to Novikov;
its proof is a fairly nontrivial adptation of the original argument.

Theorem 5.5.2
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
The class
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}
has the generalized separation property, that is: For every
\lambda
-Polish space
X
and every sequence
(A_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
of
\lambda
-analytic subsets of
X
, if
\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}A_{\alpha}=\emptyset
then there is a sequence
(B_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
of
\lambda
-Borel
8
8

8

Recall that when
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
, then
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
by the generalized Souslin’s Theorem
5.3.5
.

sets such that
B_{\alpha}\supseteq A_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\lambda
and
\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}B_{\alpha}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

We can assume without loss of generality that
A_{\alpha}\neq\emptyset
for all
\alpha<\lambda
. Thus by
(3)
of Proposition
5.1.2
there are continuous surjections
f_{\alpha}\colon C(\lambda)\to A_{\alpha}
: for each
\alpha<\lambda
and
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\vec{\lambda})}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}\big(\prod_{i<n}\lambda_{i}\big)
, set
P^{(\alpha)}_{s}=f_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{N}_{s}(C(\lambda)))
and notice that each
P^{(\alpha)}_{s}
is a
\lambda
-analytic set.
A sequence
c=(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
is called a configuration of level
n\in\omega
if
s_{\alpha}\in\prod_{i<n}\lambda_{i}
for
\alpha<\lambda_{n-1}
and
s_{\alpha}=\emptyset
otherwise, where by convention
\lambda_{-1}=0
. In particular, there is a unique configuration
c_{0}
of level
0
, namely the one with
s_{\alpha}=\emptyset
for all
\alpha<\lambda
. A configuration
c^{\prime}=(s^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
of level
n+1
extends a configuration
c=(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
of level
n
, in symbols
c^{\prime}\supseteq c
, if
s_{\alpha}\subseteq s^{\prime}_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\lambda
. Since we assumed
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, the set
\mathscr{C}_{n}
of configurations of level
n
is canonically well-orderable, and in fact there are less than
\lambda
-many configurations of level
n+1
extending a given configuration
c
of level
n
. Finally, say that a configuration
c=(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
(of any level) is bad if the conclusion of the theorem fails for the family
\big(P^{(\alpha)}_{s_{\alpha}}\big)_{\alpha<\lambda}
, that is, there is no sequence
(B_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
of
\lambda
-Borel sets such that
B_{\alpha}\supseteq P^{(\alpha)}_{s_{\alpha}}
and
\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}B_{\alpha}=\emptyset
. (Notice that
\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}P^{(\alpha)}_{s_{\alpha}}=\emptyset
always holds because
P^{(\alpha)}_{s_{\alpha}}\subseteq A_{\alpha}
for every
\alpha<\lambda
.)

Claim 5.5.2.1
.

Fix any
n\in\omega
. Every bad configuration
c=(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
can be extended to a bad configuration
c^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{n+1}
.

Proof of the claim.

Suppose not, and for every
c\subseteq c^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{n+1}
, let
\big(B^{(c^{\prime})}_{\alpha}\big)_{\alpha<\lambda}
be a family of
\lambda
-Borel sets witnessing that
c^{\prime}
is not bad. For every
\alpha<\lambda_{n}
and
t\in\prod_{i<n+1}\lambda_{i}
such that
s_{\alpha}\subseteq t
, let

D_{\alpha,t}=\bigcap\Big\{B^{(c^{\prime})}_{\alpha}\mid c^{\prime}=(s^{\prime}_{\gamma})_{\gamma<\lambda}\in\mathscr{C}_{n+1}\wedge c^{\prime}\supseteq c\wedge s^{\prime}_{\alpha}=t\Big\}

and

D_{\alpha}=\bigcup\bigg\{D_{\alpha,t}\mid s_{\alpha}\subseteq t\in\prod_{i<n+1}\lambda_{i}\bigg\}.

If instead
\lambda_{n}\leq\alpha<\lambda
, set

D_{\alpha}=\bigcap\{B^{(c^{\prime})}_{\alpha}\mid c^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{n+1}\wedge c^{\prime}\supseteq c\}.

Since
c
has less than
\lambda
-many extensions in
\mathscr{C}_{n+1}
, it follows that all the sets
D_{\alpha,t}
and
D_{\alpha}
are
\lambda
-Borel. Moreover, if
\alpha<\lambda_{n}
then
D_{\alpha,t}\supseteq P^{(\alpha)}_{t}
, thus

D_{\alpha}=\bigcup\bigg\{D_{\alpha,t}\mid s_{\alpha}\subseteq t\in\prod_{i<n+1}\lambda_{i}\bigg\}\supseteq\bigcup\bigg\{P^{(\alpha)}_{t}\mid s_{\alpha}\subseteq t\in\prod_{i<n+1}\lambda_{i}\bigg\}=P^{(\alpha)}_{s_{\alpha}}

for all
\alpha<\lambda_{n}
.
Similarly,
D_{\alpha}\supseteq P^{(\alpha)}_{s_{\alpha}}
for every
\lambda_{n}\leq\alpha<\lambda
because if
c^{\prime}=(c^{\prime}_{\gamma})_{\gamma<\lambda}\in\mathscr{C}_{n+1}
then
s^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\emptyset=s_{\alpha}
.
We claim that
\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}D_{\alpha}=\emptyset
, contradicting the assumption that
c
was bad. Indeed, if
x\in\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}D_{\alpha}
, then for every
\alpha<\lambda_{n}
there is
t_{\alpha}\supseteq s_{\alpha}
with
t_{\alpha}\in\prod_{i<n+1}\lambda_{i}
such that
x\in D_{\alpha,t_{\alpha}}
. Consider the configuration
c^{\prime}=(s^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}\in\mathscr{C}_{n+1}
with
s^{\prime}_{\alpha}=t_{\alpha}
if
\alpha<\lambda_{n}
and
s^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\emptyset
otherwise: it clearly extends
c
, and is such that
x\in D_{\alpha,t_{\alpha}}\subseteq B^{(c^{\prime})}_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\lambda_{n}
, and also
x\in D_{\alpha}\subseteq B^{(c^{\prime})}_{\alpha}
for all
\lambda_{n}\leq\alpha<\lambda
.
Thus
x\in\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}B^{(c^{\prime})}_{\alpha}
, against the choice of the sequence
(B^{(c^{\prime})}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
.
∎

Towards a contradiction, assume that the conclusion of the theorem fails or, equivalently, that the (unique) configuration
c_{0}
of level
0
is bad.
Recursively apply the claim to find
9
9

9

Since each set of configurations
\mathscr{C}_{n}
is canonically well-orderable, at each step we can just take the configuration with the desired properties which appears first with respect to such well-ordering. This shows that no choice is needed here.

a sequence
(c_{n})_{n\in\omega}
of configurations
c_{n}=(s^{n}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
such that
c_{n}
is of level
n
,
c_{n}
is bad, and
c_{n+1}\supseteq c_{n}
, for every
n\in\omega
. For each
\alpha<\lambda
set
x_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}s^{n}_{\alpha}\in C(\lambda)
and
p_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha}(x_{\alpha})
. Since
p_{\alpha}\in A_{\alpha}
and
\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}A_{\alpha}=\emptyset
, there are
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}<\lambda
such that
p_{\alpha_{0}}\neq p_{\alpha_{1}}
: let
U_{0},U_{1}\subseteq X
be open neighborhoods of
p_{\alpha_{0}}
and
p_{\alpha_{1}}
, respectively, such that
U_{0}\cap U_{1}=\emptyset
.
By continuity of the maps
f_{\alpha_{i}}
, there is
m\in\omega
such that
\lambda_{m-1}>\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}
and
f_{\alpha_{i}}(\boldsymbol{N}_{x_{\alpha_{i}}\restriction m}(C(\lambda)))=P^{(\alpha_{i})}_{x_{\alpha_{i}}\restriction m}\subseteq U_{i}
. It follows that the sequence
(B_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
with
B_{\alpha_{i}}=U_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
and
B_{\beta}=X
for all
\beta
different from
\alpha_{0}
and
\alpha_{1}
witnesses that
c_{m}
is not bad, a contradiction.
∎

Theorem
5.5.2
can be reformulated in many different ways. For example, dualizing the statement and recursively disjointifying the resulting
\lambda
-Borel sets we get a weaker form of the
\lambda
-generalized reduction property for
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
.

Corollary 5.5.3
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
(C_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
is a sequence of
\lambda
-coanalytic sets covering
X
, then it can be refined to a
\lambda
-Borel
weak
partition of
X
, that is, there are pairwise disjoint (possibly empty)
\lambda
-Borel sets
B_{\alpha}
,
\alpha<\lambda
, such that
\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}B_{\alpha}=X
and
B_{\alpha}\subseteq C_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\lambda
.

Clearly, Corollary
5.5.3
in turn implies Theorem
5.5.2
via dualization again, so it is equivalent to it. Moreover, from Corollary
5.5.3
one can derive with a standard argument the following result. It is a weaker form of the ordinal
\lambda
-uniformization property for
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
, and can trivially be extended to
\lambda
-coanalytic subsets of
X\times\lambda
whose projection on the first coordinate is
\lambda
-analytic (and thus
\lambda
-Borel).

Corollary 5.5.4
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
C\subseteq X\times\lambda
(where
\lambda
is endowed with the discrete topology) is
\lambda
-coanalytic and such that for all
x\in X
there is
\alpha<\lambda
for which
(x,\alpha)\in C
, then there is a
\lambda
-Borel function
f\colon X\to\lambda
such that
(x,f(x))\in C
for all
x\in X
.

Proof.

For each
\alpha<\lambda
, let
C_{\alpha}=\{x\in X\mid(x,\alpha)\in C\}
. Apply Corollary
5.5.3
and then set
f(x)=\alpha
if and only if
x\in B_{\alpha}
.
∎

Since
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
is clearly a
\lambda
-reasonable (in the sense of Definition
4.4.3
) boldface
\lambda
-pointclass, one can easily verify that in turn Corollary
5.5.4
implies Corollary
5.5.3
, hence it is equivalent to Theorem
5.5.2
again.

As observed, all results seen so far in this subsection remain true if we work with standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces rather than
\lambda
-Polish spaces, as we are assuming
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
anyway. Thus
combining Corollary
5.5.3
with (the analogue for standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces of) Theorem
5.3.1
, one can prove an analogue of
[
28
, Theorem 28.7]
in our new setup. For technical reasons (see the proof of Theorem
6.1.14
), our version is slightly stronger than the original formulation, which corresponds to the special case where
\mathcal{U}
is a basis for the whole topology of
Y
of size at most
\lambda
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a collection of open subsets of a topological space
X=(X,\tau)
. A
basis for
\mathcal{V}
is a collection
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\tau
such that each
V\in\mathcal{V}
is a union of elements of
\mathcal{U}
. In particular,
\mathcal{U}
is a basis for
X
if and only if it is a basis for
\mathcal{V}=\tau
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{U}
is a basis for
\mathcal{V}
,
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{U}
, then
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is a basis for
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.

Theorem 5.5.5
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X,Y
be
\lambda
-Polish spaces. Let
A\subseteq X\times Y
be a
\lambda
-Borel set such that every vertical section

A_{x}=\{y\in Y\mid(x,y)\in A\}

is open. If
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
is a basis for
\mathcal{V}=\{A_{x}\mid x\in X\}
, then

A
can be written as a union of rectangles

A=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}(B_{\alpha}\times U_{\alpha})

with
B_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
for all
\alpha<\lambda
. In particular, the result applies when
\mathcal{U}
is a basis for
Y
.

Proof.

(Compare with
[
28
, Theorem 28.7]
.)
By the choice of
\mathcal{U}
,
A=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}(X_{\alpha}\times U_{\alpha})
where
X_{\alpha}=\{x\in X\mid U_{\alpha}\subseteq A_{x}\}
. Since
X_{\alpha}
is
\lambda
-coanalytic, so is each
Z_{\alpha}=X_{\alpha}\times U_{\alpha}
. Applying (the analogue for standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces of) Corollary
5.5.3
to the covering of the standard
\lambda
-Borel space
A
constituted by the sets
Z_{\alpha}
, we get a family of
\lambda
-Borel sets
A_{\alpha}\subseteq Z_{\alpha}
such that
A=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}A_{\alpha}
. Let
S_{\alpha}
be the projection on
X
of
A_{\alpha}
, so that
S_{\alpha}\subseteq X_{\alpha}
. Since
S_{\alpha}
is
\lambda
-analytic and
X_{\alpha}
is
\lambda
-coanalytic, by Theorem
5.3.1
there is a
\lambda
-Borel set
B_{\alpha}
such that
S_{\alpha}\subseteq B_{\alpha}\subseteq X_{\alpha}
. Since
A_{\alpha}\subseteq B_{\alpha}\times U_{\alpha}\subseteq Z_{\alpha}
, the sets
B_{\alpha}
are as required.
∎

Applying Corollary
4.3.6
to the sets
B_{\alpha}
from Theorem
5.5.5
after considering as
\mathcal{U}
any basis for
Y
of size at most
\lambda
, one easily gets:

Corollary 5.5.6
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X,Y
be
\lambda
-Polish spacs, with
\tau
the topology of
X
. Let
A\subseteq X\times Y
be a
\lambda
-Borel set such that all its vertical sections
A_{x}
are open (respectively, closed). Then there is a
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau^{\prime}\supseteq\tau
such that
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau^{\prime})=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)
and
A
is open (respectively, closed) as a subset of
(X,\tau^{\prime})\times Y
. Moreover, we can further require
\mathrm{dim}(X,\tau^{\prime})=0
.

An important application of Corollary
5.5.6
is the following.

Lemma 5.5.7
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X=(X,\tau)
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
E
is a
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relation on
X
all of whose equivalence classes are closed, then there is a
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau^{\prime}\supseteq\tau
such that
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau^{\prime})=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)
and
E
is closed as a subset of
(X,\tau^{\prime})\times(X,\tau^{\prime})
.

Corollary 5.5.8
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
E
be a
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relation on a standard
\lambda
-Borel space
(X,\mathcal{B})
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

there is a
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau
on
X
such that
\mathcal{B}=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)
and
E
is
\tau\times\tau
-closed;

(2)

there is a
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau
on
X
such that
\mathcal{B}=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X,\tau)
and each
E
-equivalence class is
\tau
-closed.

Equivalence relations satisfying condition
(1)
in Corollary
5.5.8
are called
potentially closed
.
Thus Corollary
5.5.8
says that a
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relation on a standard
\lambda
-Borel space is potentially closed if and only if all its equivalence classes are “
simultaneously
potentially closed”.

If a potentially closed equivalence relation
E
is induced by an action of a
\lambda
-Polish group
G
such that the action is continuous when
X
is equipped with a topology
\tau
as in
(2)
of Corollary
5.5.8
, then
E
is
smooth
, i.e. there is a
\lambda
-Borel function
f\colon X\to Y
with
Y
standard
\lambda
-Borel such that for all
x_{0},x_{1}\in X

x_{0}\mathrel{E}x_{1}\iff f(x_{0})=f(x_{1}).

This follows from the next, more general result.

Proposition 5.5.9
.

Let
\lambda^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Let
E
be an equivalence relation on
X
induced by a continuous action
(g,x)\mapsto g\cdot x
a
\lambda
-Polish group
G
, that is,
x\mathrel{E}y\iff\exists g\in G\,(g\cdot x=y)
for every
x,y\in X
. If all
E
-equivalence classes are
G_{\delta}
, then
E
is smooth. Furthermore, if all
E
-equivalence classes are closed, then
E
admits a
\lambda
-Borel selector (equivalently: a
\lambda
-Borel transversal).

Proof.

Set
Y=F(X)
and consider the map
f
sending
x
to
\mathrm{cl}([x]_{E})
. Then
f
is
\lambda
-Borel because for every open
U\subseteq X
and every
x\in X
,

\mathrm{cl}([x]_{E})\cap U\neq\emptyset\iff[x]_{E}\cap U\neq\emptyset\iff\exists\alpha<\lambda\,(g_{\alpha}\cdot x\in U),

where
\{g_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
is any dense subset of
G
fixed in advance. Obviously, if
x_{0}\mathrel{E}x_{1}
then
[x_{0}]_{E}=[x_{1}]_{E}
, and hence
f(x_{0})=f(y_{1})
. Vice versa, if
\mathrm{cl}([x_{0}]_{E})=\mathrm{cl}([x_{1}]_{E})
then both
[x_{0}]_{E}
and
[x_{1}]_{E}
are dense
G_{\delta}
subsets of the
\lambda
-Polish space
\mathrm{cl}([x_{0}]_{E})
, hence they must intersect by the Baire category theorem for complete metric spaces, and therefore
x_{0}\mathrel{E}x_{1}
.

Assume now that all
E
-equivalence classes are closed. The fact that
E
is a induced by a continuous action of a
\lambda
-Polish group implies that the saturation of any open subset of
X
is still open, hence we are done by Theorem
5.4.13
.
∎

Conversely, an arbitrary smooth equivalence relation
E
on a standard
\lambda
-Borel space
X
is necessarily potentially closed.
Indeed, let
f\colon X\to Y
witness that
E
is smooth, and let
\tau_{X}
and
\tau_{Y}
be
\lambda
-Polish topology on
X
and
Y
, respectively, generating their
\lambda
-Borel structure. Apply Corollary
4.3.7
to refine
\tau_{X}
to a
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau^{\prime}_{X}
still generating the same
\lambda
-Borel structure on
X
but turning
f
into a continuous function. Then
E
is
\tau^{\prime}_{X}
-closed, and so are all its equivalence classes.

5.5.3.
Monotone and positive
\lambda
-Borel sets

A set
A\subseteq\mathscr{P}(\lambda)
is
monotone
if it is upward closed with respect to inclusion, i.e. if
y\in A
whenever
x\subseteq y\subseteq\lambda
for some
x\in A
. Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and identify
\mathscr{P}(\lambda)
with
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
through characteristic functions, so that
\mathscr{P}(\lambda)
is
\lambda
-Polish as well. Consider the induced
\lambda
-Borel structure
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(\mathscr{P}(\lambda))
on
\mathscr{P}(\lambda)
. It is not hard to see that
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(\mathscr{P}(\lambda))
is obtained by closing under unions and intersections of size
\lambda
the sets of the form

\displaystyle U_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\{x\subseteq\lambda\mid\alpha\in x\}

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\{x\subseteq\lambda\mid\alpha\notin x\},

for
\alpha<\lambda
. Indeed, through the above mentioned identification the sets
U_{\alpha}
and
\hat{U}_{\alpha}
correspond to the subbasis of the product topology on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, which generates the same
\lambda
-Borel sets as the bounded topology because we are assuming
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Notice also that since on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
the product topology is coarser than the bounded topology, the sets
U_{\alpha}
and
\hat{U}_{\alpha}
are open subsets of
\mathscr{P}(\lambda)
.

The
\lambda
-Borel subsets of
\mathscr{P}(\lambda)
obtained from the sets
U_{\alpha}
using only
\lambda
-sized unions and intersections are called
positive

\lambda
-Borel sets. (The name comes from the fact that the variable “
x
” is used only positively in their definitions.) The following result generalizes
[
28
, Theorem 28.11]
.

Theorem 5.5.10
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
For every
\lambda
-Borel set
A\subseteq\mathscr{P}(\lambda)
,
A
is monotone if and only if
A
is positive.

One direction is obvious: since the sets
U_{\alpha}
are monotone, and monotone sets are closed under (arbitrary) intersections and unions, all positive sets are trivially monotone.
As in the countable case, the other direction is instead derived by a stronger separation theorem, whose classical version
[
28
, Theorem 28.12]
is due to Dyck.

Theorem 5.5.11
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Let
A,B\subseteq\mathscr{P}(\lambda)
be disjoint
\lambda
-analytic sets with
A
monotone. Then there is a positive
\lambda
-Borel set
C\subseteq\mathscr{P}(\lambda)
separating
A
from
B
.

Proof.

(Compare with
[
28
, Theorem 28.12]
.)
Let
f,g\colon B(\lambda)\to\mathscr{P}(\lambda)
be continuous functions such that
f(B(\lambda))=A
and
g(B(\lambda))=B
. For each
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
, let
P_{s}=f(\boldsymbol{N}_{s})
e
Q_{s}=g(\boldsymbol{N}_{s})
. Call a pair
(s,t)\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
bad if
P_{s}
cannot be separated from
Q_{t}
by a positive
\lambda
-Borel set. Notice that if
(s,t)
is bad then there are
\alpha,\beta\in\lambda
such that
(s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\alpha,t{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta)
is bad as well: indeed, if
R_{\alpha,\beta}
is a positive
\lambda
-Borel set separating
P_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\alpha}
from
Q_{t{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}
, then
\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}\bigcap_{\beta<\lambda}R_{\alpha,\beta}
is a positive
\lambda
-Borel set separating
P_{s}
from
Q_{t}
.

Assume towards a contradiction that the conclusion of the theorem is false, i.e. that
(\emptyset,\emptyset)
is bad. Recursively construct
x,y\in B(\lambda)
such that
(x\restriction n,y\restriction n)
is bad for all
n\in\alpha
. Since
f(x)\in A
and
g(y)\in B
, and thus
g(y)\notin A
because
A\cap B=\emptyset
, it follows from the monotonicity of
A
that
f(x)\not\subseteq g(y)
. Let
\alpha<\lambda
be such that
\alpha\in f(x)\setminus g(y)
, so that
f(x)\in U_{\alpha}
and
g(y)\in\hat{U}_{\alpha}
. Since
U_{\alpha},\hat{U}_{\alpha}
are open and the maps
f
and
g
are continuous, there is
k\in\omega
large enough so that
f(\boldsymbol{N}_{x\restriction k})=P_{x\restriction k}\subseteq U_{\alpha}
and
g(\boldsymbol{N}_{y\restriction k})=Q_{y\restriction k}\subseteq\hat{U}_{\alpha}
. Then
U_{\alpha}
is a positive
\lambda
-Borel (in fact, open) set separating
P_{x\restriction k}
from
Q_{y\restriction k}
, contradicting the fact that
(x\restriction k,y\restriction k)
was bad.
∎

5.6.
The
\lambda
-projective hierarchy

In this short section we introduce all relevant definitions and basic facts concerning the

\lambda
-projective hierarchy. We omit all proofs because they follow the ideas used in the classical case, possibly with the modifications and clarifications pointed out in Sections
5.1
and
5.2
.
Also, we stick to the realm of
\lambda
-Polish spaces to avoid further assumptions on
\lambda
, but when
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
everything can be transferred to arbitrary standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces by Proposition
5.4.6
.

Definition 5.6.1
.

For
n\geq 1
,
recursively define the
\lambda
-pointclass
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n+1}
as follows.
Let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish and
A\subseteq X
. Then
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n+1}(X)
if and only if there is some
\lambda
-Polish space
Y
and a continuous function
f\colon Y\to X
such that
A=f(B)
for some
B\subseteq Y
with
Y\setminus B\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(Y)
.

We also let
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n+1}
and
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{n+1}
be the dual and the ambiguous
\lambda
-pointclasses, respectively, associated to
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n+1}
, that is:
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n+1}(X)=\{X\setminus A\mid A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n+1}(X)\}
and
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{n+1}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n+1}(X)\cap\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n+1}(X)
.

Since
\emptyset\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(Y)
for every
\lambda
-Polish
Y
, we trivially have
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}(X)
, hence arguing by induction we get that for every
1\leq n<m<\omega

\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(X),\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{m}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{m}(X),\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{m}(X).

In particular,

\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(X)=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}(X)=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{n}(X).

If we further assume
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then each class
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(X)
and
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}(X)
, for any
n\geq 1
, has a
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
-universal set, hence if
|X|>\lambda
the previous inclusions are proper.

Definition 5.6.2
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. A set
A\subseteq X
is
\lambda
-projective
if
A\in\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(X)
.

Arguing as in the first part of the proof of Proposition
5.2.1
, one can easily show by induction on
n\geq 1
that all the classes
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}(X)
,
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}(X)
, and
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{n}(X)
are closed under finite unions and intersections. Moreover they contain all closed sets, hence using the standard argument one sees that they are closed under continuous preimages, i.e. they are all boldface
\lambda
-pointclasses. Using these easy facts, as in the classical case one can then provide a number of equivalent reformulation of the class
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n+1}
from Definition
5.6.1
.

Proposition 5.6.3
.

Let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish and
A\subseteq X
. For each
n\geq 1
the following are equivalent:

(1)

A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n+1}(X)
, i.e.
A
is a continuous image of some
B\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}(Y)
for some
\lambda
-Polish space
Y
;

(2)

A
is a continuous image of some
B\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}(B(\lambda))
(or, equivalently,
B\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}(C(\lambda))
);

(3)

A=p(C)
with
C\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}(X\times B(\lambda))
;

(4)

A=p(C)
with
C\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}(X\times Y)
for some
\lambda
-Polish space
Y
.

If
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, we can further replace
B(\lambda)
with
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
in
(2)
and
(3)
, or replace continuous functions with
\lambda
-Borel functions in
(1)
and
(2)
.

Finally, the arguments used in Proposition
5.2.1
allow one to prove analogous closure properties for all levels of the
\lambda
-projective hierarchy (again by induction on
n\geq 1
).

Proposition 5.6.4
.

Let
n\geq 1
.

(i)

The boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}
is closed under well-ordered unions of length at most
\lambda
, countable intersections, and
\lambda
-Borel images and preimages. If
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then it is also closed under well-ordered intersections of length at most
\lambda
.

(ii)

The boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}
is closed under well-ordered intersections of length at most
\lambda
, countable unions, and
\lambda
-Borel preimages. If
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then it is also closed under well-ordered unions of length at most
\lambda
.

(iii)

The boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{n}
is closed under countable unions, countable intersections, complements, and
\lambda
-Borel preimages. If
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then it is also closed under well-ordered unions and intersections of length at most
\lambda
, i.e. each
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{n}(X)
is a
\lambda^{+}
-algebra on
X
.

Notice that the non-collapse of the
\lambda
-projective hierarchy on
\lambda
-Polish spaces larger than
\lambda
under the assumption
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
implies the failure of the missing closure properties, namely:

\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n}
and
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}
are not closed under complements, and

\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{n}
and
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{n}
are not closed under continuous (hence
\lambda
-Borel) images.

5.7.
\kappa
-Souslin sets

Definition 5.7.1
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space and
\kappa\geq\lambda
be a cardinal. A set
A\subseteq X
is
\kappa
-Souslin
if it is a continuous image of some
\kappa
-Polish space
Y
.

Thus
\lambda
-Souslin sets are precisely the
\lambda
-analytic set. Moreover, if
\lambda\leq\kappa\leq\kappa^{\prime}
then all
\kappa
-Souslin sets are also
\kappa^{\prime}
-Souslin. Thus, in particular, the collection of
\kappa
-Souslin sets always contains all
\lambda
-Borel sets if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.

Using the results from Section
3.5
, it is not hard to see that the notion of a
\kappa
-Souslin set can be reformulated in several different ways (compare the next proposition with Proposition
5.1.2
).

Proposition 5.7.2
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space and
\kappa\geq\lambda
be a cardinal. For any
\emptyset\neq A\subseteq X
the following are equivalent:

(1)

A
is
\kappa
-Souslin;

(2)

A
is a continuous image of a closed (or even just
G_{\delta}
) subset of some
\kappa
-Polish space
Y
;

(3)

A
is a continuous image of
B(\kappa)={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\kappa}
, or even just of a closed or
G_{\delta}
subset of it;

(4)

A=p(F)
for some closed
F\subseteq X\times B(\kappa)
;

(5)

A=p(G)
for some
G\subseteq X\times Y
with
Y
a
\kappa
-Polish space and
G
a
G_{\delta}
set.

The reformulation
(4)
from Proposition
5.7.2
allows one to find a canonical representation of
\kappa
-analytic subsets of
B(\lambda)
which is analogous to the one in equation (
5.2
), namely:
A\subseteq B(\lambda)
is
\kappa
-Souslin if and only if there is an
\omega
-tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\lambda\times\kappa)}
on
\lambda\times\kappa
such that

(5.8)

A=p[T].

Arguing as in Proposition
5.2.1
and recalling that
\lambda
-Borel sets are
\kappa
-Souslin if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, one easily gets the following closure properties. (The extra choice assumptions are needed to collect continuous functions
f\colon B(\kappa)\to X
witnessing that the sets we are dealing with are
\kappa
-Souslin.)

Proposition 5.7.3
.

Let
\kappa\geq\lambda
.

(i)

Let
\omega\leq\mu\leq\kappa
and
assume
{\sf AC}_{\mu}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\kappa}{2})
. Then the collection of
\kappa
-Souslin sets is closed under
well-ordered unions of length at most
\mu
and under countable intersection.

(ii)

The collection of
\kappa
-Souslin sets is closed under continuous images and preimages; in particular, it is a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass.
If
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then it is also closed under
\lambda
-Borel images and preimages.

Finally, using the obvious modifications of the proofs of Theorems
5.3.1
and
5.3.5
(see also
[
38
, Theorems 2E.1 and 2E.2]
), one easily gets:

Proposition 5.7.4
.

Let
\kappa\geq\lambda
,
X
be
\lambda
-Polish, and
A,B\subseteq X
be disjoint
\kappa
-Souslin sets. Then there is a
\kappa^{+}
-Borel set
C\subseteq X
separating
A
from
B
.

In particular, if
A\subseteq X
is such that both
A
and
X\setminus A
are
\kappa
-Souslin, then
A
is
\kappa^{+}
-Borel.

Assuming a bit more choice than usual, one can prove that all sets in the first levels of the
\lambda
-projective hierarchy are
\lambda^{+}
-Souslin, and sometimes even
\lambda^{++}
-Borel. This generalizes a theorem from the classical setup due to Shoenfield (see
[
38
, Theorem 2D.3]
).

Theorem 5.7.5

(
{\sf AC}_{\lambda^{+}}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
)
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then every
\lambda
-coanalytic subset of
X
, and thus also every set in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}(X)
, is
\lambda^{+}
-Souslin.

Proof.

Let
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(X)
. By Theorem
5.2.3
there are
\lambda
-Borel sets
A_{\xi}
,
\xi<\lambda^{+}
, such that
A=\bigcup_{\xi<\lambda^{+}}A_{\xi}
, and without loss of generality they might be assumed to be nonempty.
Since
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)\subseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
, for each
\xi<\lambda^{+}
there is a continuous map
f_{\xi}\colon B(\lambda)\to X
with range
A_{\xi}
. Consider the closed set
C\subseteq B(\lambda^{+})
defined by

C=\{x\in B(\lambda^{+})\mid\forall n>0\,(x(n)<\lambda)\},

so that each
x\in C
is of the form
\xi{}^{\smallfrown}{}x^{-}
for some
\xi\in\lambda^{+}
and
x^{-}\in B(\lambda)
. Then the map
f\colon C\to X
defined by
f(x)=f_{x(0)}(x^{-})
is continuous and such that
f(C)=\bigcup_{\xi<\lambda^{+}}A_{\xi}=A
. Therefore, using
(3)
of Proposition
5.7.2
we get that the set
A
is
\lambda^{+}
-Souslin.

Since
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}
sets are continuous images of
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
sets, they are
\lambda^{+}
-Souslin as well by Proposition
5.7.3
(ii)
.
∎

Corollary 5.7.6

(
{\sf AC}_{\lambda^{+}}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
)
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then every set in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{2}(X)
is
\lambda^{++}
-Borel.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.7.5
and Proposition
5.7.4
.
∎

5.8.
Some observations on absoluteness

Our proof of Theorem
5.7.5
is somewhat unsatisfactory, as it would be desirable to have a “definable” representation of
\lambda
-coanalytic sets as
\lambda^{+}
-Souslin sets to be able to deal with (the higher analogues of) scales and Shoenfield’s absoluteness. In contrast, our proof heavily relies on the possibility of choosing
\lambda^{+}
-many representations for certain analytic sets: this is in general not possible in the definable context. One of the most common definable Souslin representation of coanalytic sets is called Shoenfield tree, and in the following we see how the theory of Shoenfield trees and Shoenfield’s absoluteness generalize in our setting. All the notations have been introduced in Section
2.3
. As (
5.2
) is the fundamental starting point of such analysis, we will work only on (at most countable copies of)
B(\lambda)
. The same analysis can be carried on
mutatis mutandis
for
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
.

Definition 5.8.1
.

Let
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(B(\lambda))
, and let
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\lambda\times\lambda)}
be a tree such that
B(\lambda)\setminus A=p[T]
, as granted by (
5.2
). For any
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
, let
R(s)=\{t\in T(s)\mid\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}(t)\in\lambda_{\operatorname{lh}(s)}\}
. The
Shoenfield tree
of
A
with respect to
T
is the

\omega
-tree
S
on
\lambda\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}
consisting of all
(s,u)\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\lambda\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})})}
such that for every
i\leq j<\operatorname{lh}(s)

(a)

u(i)\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda_{i}}{(\lambda^{+})}
;

(b)

u(i)\subseteq u(j)
;

(c)

the restriction of
u(i)\circ\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}
to
R(s\restriction i)
is an order-preserving map between
(R(s\restriction i),\leq_{\mathrm{KB}})
and
(\lambda^{+},{\leq})
.

If
A
is a
\lambda
-coanalytic subset of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{B(\lambda)}
for some
n\leq\omega
, the Shoenfield tree
S
on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}
is defined in the expected way.

The idea is the same as the classical Shoenfield tree, that can be found e.g. in
[
25
, Chapter 25]
: the left side of
S
is building some
x\in B(\lambda)
, while the right side of
S
is trying to build level-by-level an order-preserving function from
(T(x),{\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}})
to
(\lambda^{+},{\leq})
. In this way, for any
x\in B(\lambda)
we have that if there is
g
such that
(x,g)\in[S]
, then
g
witnesses that
T(x)
is well-founded (so that
x\in A
), while if there is no such
g
, then
T(x)
is ill-founded (i.e.
x\notin A
).

There is a substantial difference with the classical case
\lambda=\omega
, though: a branch of the original Shoenfield tree still provides
x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
and an order-preserving
g\colon T(x)\to\omega_{1}
(where
T(x)
is endowed with
\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}
), but since
T(x)\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\omega}
, we can code
g
with some
g^{\prime}\colon\omega\to\omega_{1}
, and therefore the resulting Shoenfield tree can be construed as a tree on
\omega\times\omega_{1}
. In the generalize case, instead, the witness
g
is a function from
\omega
to
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}
, and in general it is no longer possible to code such an object as an element of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(\lambda^{+})}
(or, even worse, as an element of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\nu}
with
\nu\in\mathrm{Card}
, in case
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}
is not well-orderable); in other words, we cannot define
S
directly on products of ordinals. For this reason, it is worth checking whether the original proof still holds in the generalized context.

Proposition 5.8.2
.

Let
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(B(\lambda))
, and let
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\lambda\times\lambda)}
be such that
B(\lambda)\setminus A=p[T]
. Let
S
be the Shoenfield tree of
A
with respect to
T
. Then
A=p[S]
.

Proof.

Let
x\in A
. Then, the section tree
T(x)
is well-founded, and this implies that the restriction of
\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}
to
T(x)
is a well-ordering of length less than
\lambda^{+}
. Let
\rho\colon T(x)\to\lambda^{+}
be the rank function
\rho_{<_{\mathrm{KB}\restriction T(x)}}
associated to (the strict part of)
\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}\restriction T(x)
. For every
i\in\omega
,
\rho_{i}=\rho\restriction T(x\restriction i)
is an order-preserving mpap between
(T(x\restriction i),\leq_{\mathrm{KB}})
and
(\lambda^{+},{\leq})
.
Let

w(i)=\rho_{i}\circ\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}^{-1}\colon\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}(T(x\upharpoonright i))\to\lambda^{+}.

and let
u(i)\colon\lambda_{i}\to\lambda^{+}
be defined by
u(i)(\alpha)=w(i)(\alpha)
if
\alpha<\lambda_{i}
is such that
\alpha\in\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}(T(x\restriction i))
, and
U(i)(\alpha)=0
otherwise.
Then
u(i)\circ\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}
coincides with
\rho_{i}
on
R(x\restriction i)\subseteq T(x\restriction i)
, and it is therefore order-preserving. Let
u=(u(i))_{i\in\omega}
. Then

(x,u)
is a branch of
S
witnessing
x\in p[S]
.

Vice versa, let
x\in p[S]
, and let
u=(u(i))_{i\in\omega}
be such that
(x,u)\in[S]
. Since
u(i)\subseteq u(j)
for every
i<j
, then
\bar{u}=\bigcup_{i\in\omega}u(i)
is a function from
\bigcup_{i\in\omega}\lambda_{i}=\lambda
to
\lambda^{+}
, and, by construction, the restriction of
\bar{u}\circ\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}
to
T(x)
is an order-preserving map between
(T(x),\leq_{\mathrm{KB}})
and
(\lambda^{+},{\leq})
. Therefore
T(x)
is well-founded and
x\in A
.
∎

Proposition
5.8.2
easily extends to the case where
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{B(\lambda)})
, with
n
finite or countable.

If
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}
is well-orderable, then in the construction of the Shoenfield tree we can substitute the elements of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}
that appear in the tree with an ordinal, and therefore recover
\kappa
-Souslinity for a suitable cardinal
\kappa\geq\lambda^{+}
.

Corollary 5.8.3
.

Suppose that
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}=\kappa
for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{Card}
. Then every
\lambda
-coanalytic set in
B(\lambda)
is
\kappa
-Souslin.

In particular, if
|{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}|=\lambda^{+}
(that holds e.g. under
{\sf GCH}
), then we recover Theorem
5.7.5
. However, notice that this fails to be a “definable” result too, as it uses a bijection from
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}
to
\lambda^{+}
.

The Shoenfield tree was introduced to prove Shoenfield absoluteness (see e.g.,
[
25
, Theorem 25.20]
), and we can exploit Proposition
5.8.2
to prove a similar result.
Recall that, given
M\subseteq N
sets or classes and
\upvarphi(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
a formula in the language of set theory, possibly with parameters in
M
, we say that
\upvarphi
is
absolute between
M
and
N
if and only if for any
a_{1},\dotsc,a_{n}\in M
,

M\models\upvarphi[a_{1}/v_{1},\dots,a_{n}/v_{n}]\quad\iff\quad N\models\upvarphi[a_{1}/v_{1},\dots,a_{n}/v_{n}].

We say that
\upvarphi
is
upward absolute
if the direction from left to right in the displayed equivalence holds, and that
\upvarphi
is
downward absolute
if the direction from right to left holds.
If
N=V
, we do not mention
N
.

For example, the definition of
B(\lambda)
as in Definition
3.1.2
(c)
is absolute for all models of large enough fragments of
{\sf ZF}
that contain
\lambda
, where with the phrase “models of large enough fragments of
{\sf ZF}
” we mean that we need that the model satisfies enough axioms so that ordinals are absolute and
B(\lambda)
can be defined from
\lambda
. In the following, with an abuse of terminology, we will simply say “models of
{\sf ZF}
” to mean “models of large enough fragments of
{\sf ZF}
”, and the exact fragments of
{\sf ZF}
to be considered will be clear from the context.

In descriptive set theory it is common to talk about absoluteness of definable sets, via their definition. If
A
is a
\lambda
-projective subset of
B(\lambda)
, then by definition
A
is constructed via simpler sets. For example, if
A
is
\lambda
-coanalytic, then it is the complement of the projection of a closed subset of
B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda)
. The given construction of
A
yields a formula
\upvarphi(v)
in the language of set theory, possibly with parameters in
B(\lambda)
, such that
A=\{x\in B(\lambda)\mid V\models\upvarphi[x/v]\}
. If
M
is a model for which
B(\lambda)
is absolute, that contains the relevant parameters, and such that
\upvarphi(v)
is absolute for
M
, then
\{x\in M\mid M\models\upvarphi[x/v]\}=A\cap M
: when this happens, with some abuse of terminology we will say that
A
is absolute for
M
.
10
10

10

The abuse comes from the fact that
A
can be defined using different formulas and different parameters, and therefore
A
can be absolute for
M
according to one definition but not accoding to some other one.

By equation (
5.2
), if
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(B(\lambda))
then there exists an
\omega
-tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\lambda\times\lambda)}
such that
A=p[T]
, namely, such that
x\in A
if and only if
T(x)
is ill-founded, for every
x\in B(\lambda)
. This is a definition for
A
that is absolute for any model of
{\sf ZF}
that contains
\lambda
and
T
. The notion of complement is also absolute for such a model, and therefore also
\lambda
-conalytic subsets of
B(\lambda)
are absolute for models of
{\sf ZF}
that contain
\lambda
and the relevant tree
T
in their definition as a complement of a
\lambda
-analytic set.

Proposition 5.8.4
.

Let
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}(B(\lambda))
, let
C\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda))
be such that
A=p(C)
, and let
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\lambda^{3})}
be an
\omega
-tree such that
C=(B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda))\setminus p[T]
. Then
A
is upward absolute for all models of
{\sf ZF}
that contain
\lambda
and
T
.

Proof.

Let
\upvarphi(u,v,\lambda,T)
be the first-order formula in the language of set theory formalizing the assertion “
u\in B(\lambda)\wedge v\in B(\lambda)\wedge T(u,v)
is well-founded”, so that for every
x,y\in B(\lambda)
we have that

\upvarphi[x/u,y/v,\lambda,T]
holds (in
V
) if and only if
(x,y)\in C
. Then the formula
\exists v\,\upvarphi(u,v,\lambda,T)
is a definition of
A
witnessing that it is upward absolute for all models of
{\sf ZF}
containing
\lambda
and
T
.
∎

The previous proof shows why
A
is possibly not downward absolute: if
x\in A\cap M
, then it is possible that any
y\in B(\lambda)
witnessing “
x\in A
” in
V
, i.e. such that
V\models\upvarphi[x/u,y/y,\lambda,T]
, does not belong to
M
. However, if
M
contains all ingredients needed for the construction of the Shoenfield tree, we recover also downward absoluteness.

Proposition 5.8.5
.

Let
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}(B(\lambda))
, let
C\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda))
be such that
A=p(C)
, and let
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\lambda^{3})}
be an
\omega
-tree such that
C=(B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda))\setminus p[T]
.
Then
A
is absolute for all models of
{\sf ZF}
that contain
\lambda
,
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda_{i})_{i\in\omega}
,
T
, and
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}
.

Proof.

Let
M
be a model of
{\sf ZF}
such that
\lambda,\vec{\lambda},T,{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}\in M
. Since
M
contains
\lambda
, then
M
contains also
\sigma_{\mathrm{Tr}}
. Reading the definition of the Shoenfield tree
S
of
C
with respect to
T
, it is clear that
S\in M
: since
T\in M
, then
T(s)\in M
for any
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
, and since
\vec{\lambda}\in M
, then
R(s)\in M
: this proves that the definition of
S
is absolute for
M
. Since
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}\in M
, then also
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})})}\in M
, and therefore
S\subseteq M
, and in fact
S\in M
.

By Proposition
5.8.2
, for every
x,y\in B(\lambda)
we have that
(x,y)\in C
if and only if
S(x,y)
is ill-founded, and therefore
x\in A
if and only if
S(x)
is ill-founded.
11
11

11

Notice that since
C\subseteq B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda)
, its Shoenfield tree
S
is an
\omega
-tree on
\lambda\times\lambda\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(\lambda^{+})}
, so it makes sense to consider both its section trees
S(x)
obtained by fixing only its first coordinate
x
, and its section trees
S(x,y)
obtained by fixing its first two coordinates
x
and
y
.

Since
S\in M
and well-foundedness is absolute for
M
, this yields absoluteness of
A
, as desired.
∎

Note that if
\lambda=\omega
, then any model
M
of
{\sf ZF}
that contains
\omega_{1}
also contains
\omega
,
\vec{\omega}
(which in this case can be taken as the sequence of all natural numbers), and
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\omega_{1}}
, and this explains why the hypotheses of the usual Shoenfield’s Absoluteness Theorem are much weaker than those used in its counterpart for
\lambda>\omega
, i.e. in Proposition
5.8.5
. Indeed, in the classical case
\lambda=\omega
it is enough to prove that
S
is absolute for
M
(and not necessarily an element of
M
), and therefore we can even drop the assumption
\omega_{1}\in M
, and reach the usual formulation of the original Shoenfield’s Absoluteness Theorem.

We also remark that Proposition
5.8.5
is not meant to be optimal: for instance, in
[
30
, Theorem 1.4]
there is an example of absoluteness of a certain type of
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{2}(V_{\lambda+1})
sets, assuming very large cardinals, for an inner model that does not satisfy the hypothesis of Proposition
5.8.5
.

Chapter 6
Uniformization results and
\lambda
-coanalytic ranks

6.1.
Uniformization for
\lambda
-analytic and
\lambda
-Borel sets

Recall that a
uniformization
of
A\subseteq X\times Y
is a set
A^{*}\subseteq A
such that for all
x\in p(A)
there is exactly one
y\in Y
with
(x,y)\in A^{*}
.
The existence of a uniformization of
A\subseteq X\times Y
is indeed equivalent to the existence of a
uniformizing function
for
A
, that is, a function
f\colon p(A)\to Y
such that
f(x)
belongs to the vertical section
A_{x}
of
A
at
x
, for all
x\in p(A)
.
Indeed,
A^{*}
is a uniformization of
A
if and only if
A^{*}
is the graph of a uniformizing function for
A
.
Notice also that if
X
and
Y
are standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces and
A^{*}\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X\times Y)
, then
p(A)
is
\lambda
-Borel as well by Theorem
5.3.8
(here the key fact is that the projection function
p
is continuous,
p(A^{*})=p(A)
, and
p\restriction A^{*}
is injective because
A^{*}
is a uniformization of
A
). By Theorem
5.3.6
and Proposition
5.4.6
, under
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
we have that the set
A
has a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization if and only if
p(A)
is
\lambda
-Borel and there is a
\lambda
-Borel uniformizing function
f\colon p(A)\to Y
.

In Section
4.4
we already proved that if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
,
X
is a
\lambda
-Polish space, and
Y
is any
\lambda
-Borel space of size at most
\lambda
, then every
\lambda
-Borel subset of
X\times Y
has a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization — this is basically the ordinal
\lambda
-uniformization property, once we notice that without loss of generality the space
Y
can be given the discrete topology. This implies that under the same assumption on
\lambda
, if
X,Y
are standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces and
A\subseteq X\times Y
is a
\lambda
-Borel set whose projection on the second coordinate has size
1
1

1

By Proposition
5.1.2
, such projection is a
\lambda
-analytic subset of
Y
. It will follow from Corollary
7.1.2
that either it is well-orderable and of size at most
\lambda
, or else it has size
2^{\lambda}
.

at most
\lambda
, then
A
has a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization.

In this section we prove some other uniformization results for
\lambda
-Borel sets, mirroring corresponding classical facts. All proofs share the fact that, thanks to the results in Section
3.2
, one can without loss of generality work with (often closed) subsets of
X\times B(\lambda)
— see in particular Theorems
6.1.4
,
6.1.7
, and
6.1.14
.
As usual, to minimize the requirements on
\lambda
some of the results are stated for
\lambda
-Polish spaces only, but they work for arbitrary standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces (whenever this makes sense) as soon as we assume
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.

6.1.1.
Jankov-von Neumann uniformization

The first and easiest uniformization result is the analogue of the Jankov-von Neumann uniformization theorem
[
28
, Theorem 18.1]
, and actually it applies to arbitrary
\lambda
-analytic sets. Given a standard
\lambda
-Borel space
X
, let
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})(X)
be the
\lambda
-algebra generated by the
\lambda
-analytic subsets of
X
. If
Y
is a
\lambda
-Borel space, we say that a function
f\colon X\to Y
is
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
-measurable
if
f^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})(X)
for every
B\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(Y)
. Since the projection of a
\lambda
-analytic set is still
\lambda
-analytic, and hence in
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
, the proof of Proposition
4.1.3
shows that if a
\lambda
-analytic set
P\subseteq X\times Y
admits a
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
-measurable uniformizing function, then
P
has also a
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
uniformization.

Theorem 6.1.1
.

Let
X,Y
be
\lambda
-Polish spaces. Then for every
\lambda
-analytic
P\subseteq X\times Y
there is a
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
-measurable uniformizing function for
P
. In particular,
P
has a
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
uniformization.

Sketch of the proof.

(See
[
28
, Theorem 18.1]
for more details.)
We can assume
P\neq\emptyset
, as otherwise there is nothing to prove.
Using
(3)
of Proposition
5.1.2
,
let
h\colon B(\lambda)\to X\times Y
be continuous and such that
h(B(\lambda))=P
. Let also
g\colon F\to X
be a continuous bijection with
F\subseteq B(\lambda)
closed (Proposition
3.2.5
). By Remark
3.2.6
we can assume that
g^{-1}
is
\lambda
-Borel measurable. Let
P^{\prime}\subseteq B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda)
be the closed set defined by

(x,y)\in P^{\prime}\iff x\in F\wedge p(h(y))=g(x),

where as usual
p\colon X\times Y\to X
is the projection on the first coordinate. If
f^{\prime}\colon p(P^{\prime})\to B(\lambda)
is a
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
-measurable uniformizing function for
P^{\prime}
, then the function
f\colon p(P)\to Y
sending
x\in p(P)
to the projection on
Y
of
h(f^{\prime}(g^{-1}(x)))
is the desired
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
-measurable uniformizing function for
P
.

The previous paragraph shows that without loss of generality we can assume that
X=Y=B(\lambda)
and that
P\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{1}(X\times Y)
. Let
T
be an
\omega
-tree on
\lambda\times\lambda
such that
P=[T]
. Then the map sending each
x\in p(P)
to the leftmost branch of the section tree
T(x)
is a
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
-measurable uniformizing function for
P
.
∎

Theorem
6.1.1
cannot be improved: there are even closed sets
P\subseteq X\times Y
such that
p(P)=X
, yet there is no
\lambda
-Borel uniformization of
P
(see Example
6.1.3
).

Corollary 6.1.2
.

Let
X,Y
be
\lambda
-Polish spaces. Every
\lambda
-Borel function
f\colon X\to Y
admits a
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
-measurable right-inverse, that is, a
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
-measurable map
g\colon f(X)\to X
such that
(f\circ g)(y)=y
for all
y\in f(X)
.

6.1.2.
A general criterion

We now move to

\lambda
-Borel uniformizations of
\lambda
-Borel sets. This is a highly nontrivial task, because as in the classical case there are even very simple
\lambda
-Borel sets which do not admit a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization.

Example 6.1.3
.

If
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then there is a closed
F\subseteq B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda)
such that
p(F)=B(\lambda)
but there is no
\lambda
-Borel uniformization for
F
. Indeed, let
C_{0},C_{1}\subseteq B(\lambda)
be disjoint
\lambda
-coanalytic sets that cannot be separated by a
\lambda
-Borel set (the existence of such sets is granted by Theorem
6.2.5
, which will be proved later on).
For
i\in\{0,1\}
let
F_{i}\subseteq B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda)
be closed such that
X\setminus C_{i}=p(F_{i})
, and let
F=F_{0}\cup F_{1}
. It is not hard to check that
F
is as required because if
f
were a
\lambda
-Borel uniformizing function for
F
, then the
\lambda
-Borel set
f^{-1}(F_{1})
would separate
C_{0}
from
C_{1}
.

We
first establish a quite general criterion along the lines of Theorem
4.3.1
for the existence of a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization of a given
\lambda
-Borel set. It works also in the classical case
\lambda=\omega
and in that setup it might be folklore, although we could not trace it back in the literature.
If
B
is a subset of a topological space
X
, we say that
B
has an isolated point if there is
x\in B
which is isolated with respect to the relative topology on
B
inherited from
X
.

Theorem 6.1.4
.

Let
X,Y
be
\lambda
-Polish spaces, and let
A\subseteq X\times Y
be a nonempty
\lambda
-Borel set. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

A
has a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization;

(2)

there is a continuous
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism
h\colon H\to X\times Y
with
H\subseteq B(\lambda)
closed such that the set

F=\{(x,z)\in X\times B(\lambda)\mid z\in H\wedge h(z)\in A\wedge p(h(z))=x\}

is closed and all its nonempty vertical sections
F_{x}
have an isolated point;

(3)

there is a closed set
F\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
and a continuous
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism
f\colon F\to A
such that
p(w)=p(f(w))
for all
w\in F
and all nonempty vertical sections
F_{x}
of
F
have an isolated point;

(4)

there is a continuous surjection
g\colon B(\lambda)\to Y
and a closed set
F\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
such that
(\operatorname{id}_{X}\times\,g)(F)=A
and all nonempty vertical sections
F_{x}
of
F
have an isolated point;

(5)

the topology of
X\times Y
can be refined to a
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau^{\prime}
generating the same
\lambda
-Borel sets such that
A
is
\tau^{\prime}
-closed and all its nonempty vertical sections
A_{x}
, when identified with the subspace
\{x\}\times A_{x}
of
(X\times Y,\tau^{\prime})
, have an isolated point.

Before proving Theorem
6.1.4
, we need a technical lemma. By adapting in the obvious way the proof of
[
28
, Theorem 18.11]
, one obtains a generalization of Luzin’s theorem on the set of unicity of a Borel set.

Lemma 6.1.5
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, let
X,Y
be standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces, and let
R\subseteq X\times Y
be
\lambda
-Borel. Then the set

\{x\in X\mid\exists!y\,(x,y)\in R\}

is
\lambda
-coanalytic.

Combining this with Corollary
5.5.3
, we obtain the following technical uniformization result, which will be subsumed by Theorem
6.1.4
once the proof of the latter will be complete.

Proposition 6.1.6
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, let
X,Y
be
\lambda
-Polish spaces, and let

A\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X\times Y)
.
Suppose that there is a family
\{U_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
of
\lambda
-Borel subsets of
X\times Y
such that for every
x\in p(A)
there is
\alpha<\lambda
such that
U_{\alpha}\cap(\{x\}\times A_{x})
is a singleton.
Then there is a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization
A^{*}
of
A
.

Proof.

For each
\alpha<\lambda
, consider the set
R_{\alpha}=A\cap U_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X\times Y,\tau)
.
By Lemma
6.1.5
, the set
C_{\alpha}=\{x\in X\mid\exists!y\,(x,y)\in R_{\alpha}\}
is
\lambda
-coanalytic. By our hypotheses, we have
p(A)=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}C_{\alpha}
, hence
p(A)
is
\lambda
-coanalytic by Corollary
5.2.2
; since obviously
p(A)\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
, then
p(A)
is
\lambda
-Borel by Theorem
5.3.5
. It follows that
(C_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
is a covering of the standard
\lambda
-Borel space
p(A)
with
\lambda
-coanalytic sets. By Corollary
5.5.3
we can refine such a covering to a weak partition of
p(A)
in
\lambda
-Borel sets
(B_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
such that
B_{\alpha}\subseteq C_{\alpha}
. Let
A_{\alpha}=\{(x,y)\in R_{\alpha}\mid x\in B_{\alpha}\}=R_{\alpha}\cap(B_{\alpha}\times Y)\subseteq A
, so that
A_{\alpha}\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X\times Y,\tau)
. Since
B_{\alpha}\subseteq C_{\alpha}
, for each
x\in B_{\alpha}
there is a unique
y\in Y
such that
(x,y)\in A_{\alpha}
: it follows that
A^{*}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}A_{\alpha}
is a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization of
A
, as required.
∎

An important special case in which Proposition
6.1.6
can be applied is when each nonempty vertical section
A_{x}
has an isolated point (with respect to the topology of
Y
).
We are now ready to prove our characterization of the existence of
\lambda
-Borel uniformizations of a given
\lambda
-Borel set.

Proof of Theorem
6.1.4
.

(1)

\Rightarrow

(2)
.
Let
A^{*}
be a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization of
A
. By applying Corollary
4.3.2
to the family of
\lambda
-Borel sets
\{A^{*},A\setminus A^{*}\}
, there is a closed set
H\subseteq B(\lambda)
and a continuous
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism
h\colon H\to X\times Y
such that
h^{-1}(A^{*})
and
h^{-1}(A\setminus A^{*})
are both clopen in
H
, and hence closed in
B(\lambda)
. Since
h(z)\in A
if and only if
z\in h^{-1}(A^{*})\cup h^{-1}(A\setminus A^{*})
, it follows that the set
F
in condition
(2)
is closed. Given any
x\in p(F)=p(A)
, let
y\in Y
be the unique point such that
(x,y)\in A^{*}
. Let also
z\in H
be the unique point such that
h(z)=(x,y)
. Then
z\in F_{x}\subseteq H
. Since
h^{-1}(A^{*})
is open relatively to
H
and since for all
z^{\prime}\in F_{x}\setminus\{z\}
we have
h(z^{\prime})\notin A^{*}
(because
A^{*}
was a uniformization of
A
), we have that
h^{-1}(A^{*})\cap F_{x}
is a relatively open set isolating
z
in
F_{x}
.

(2)

\Rightarrow

(3)
.
Let
h\colon H\to X\times Y
and
F
be as in
(2)
. Let
f\colon F\to A
be defined by
f(x,z)=h(z)
for every
(x,z)\in F
. Using also Theorem
5.3.6
, it is easy to verify that
f
is as required.

(2)

\Rightarrow

(4)
.
Let
h\colon H\to X\times Y
and
F
be as in
(2)
. Let
r\colon B(\lambda)\to H
be a retraction onto
H
and
p_{2}\colon X\times Y\to Y
be the projection on the second coordinate. Then
g=p_{2}\circ h\circ r
and the same
F
witness
(4)
.

(2)

\Rightarrow

(5)
.
Let
h\colon H\to X\times Y
and
F
be as in
(2)
. Set also

F^{\prime}=\{(x,z)\in X\times B(\lambda)\mid z\in H\wedge p(h(z))=x\}.

Then
F^{\prime}
is closed,
F\subseteq F^{\prime}
, and the map
