f^{\prime}\colon F^{\prime}\to X\times Y
sending
(x,z)\in F^{\prime}
to
h(z)
is a continuous
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism such that for all
x\in p(F)=p(A)
it holds
f^{\prime}(\{x\}\times F_{x})=\{x\}\times A_{x}
, so that in particular
f^{\prime}(F)=A
. It easily follows that the push forward
\tau^{\prime}
of the topology of
F^{\prime}
along
f^{\prime}
is as desired.

We are left with proving that each of
(3)
,
(4)
, and
(5)
implies
(1)
.
The implication
(5)

\Rightarrow

(1)
directly follows from Proposition
6.1.6
,
once we let
\{U_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
be any
\lambda
-sized basis for
\tau^{\prime}
.
For the remaining implications
(3)

\Rightarrow

(1)
and
(4)

\Rightarrow

(1)
we first apply Proposition
6.1.6
to the set
F\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
with
\{U_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
any
\lambda
-sized basis for the product topology on
X\times B(\lambda)
to obtain a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization of
F^{*}
. Since both the restriction of the function
f
from
(3)
and the restriction of the function
\operatorname{id}_{X}\times\,g
from
(4)
to the
\lambda
-Borel set
F^{*}
are necessarily injective
\lambda
-Borel functions, it follows from Theorem
5.3.8
that their range
A^{*}\subseteq A
is
\lambda
-Borel, and by construction it is a uniformization of
A
.
∎

6.1.3.
\lambda
-Borel uniformizations for sets with countable sections

We next move to
\lambda
-Borel sets
A\subseteq X\times Y
all of whose vertical sections
A_{x}
are countable. The following result naturally generalizes the Luzin-Novikov’s theorem (see
[
28
, Theorem 18.10]
). Our proof is necessarily different from that of
[
28
, Theorem 18.10]
because, as already observed, we lack a Baire category notion matching well with that of
\lambda
-Borel sets. Also, notice that part
(iii)
is obviously implied by part
(iv)
, but we do not know how to obtain the latter without first proving the former.

Theorem 6.1.7
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Let
X
be a standard
\lambda
-Borel space, and let
Y
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Let
A\subseteq X\times Y
be a
\lambda
-Borel set all of whose vertical sections are countable.

(i)

There is a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization of
A
, hence in particular
p(A)
is
\lambda
-Borel.

(ii)

The map from
p(A)
to
F(Y)
sending
x\in p(A)
to
\mathrm{cl}(A_{x})
is
\lambda
-Borel.

(iii)

There is a sequence
(\varsigma^{A}_{n})_{n\in\omega}
of
\lambda
-Borel functions
\varsigma^{A}_{n}\colon p(A)\to Y
such that for all
x\in p(A)
the set
\{\varsigma^{A}_{n}(x)\mid n\in\omega\}
is dense in
A_{x}
.

(iv)

There is a sequence
(\varrho^{A}_{n})_{n\in\omega}
of
\lambda
-Borel functions
\varrho^{A}_{n}\colon p(A)\to Y
such that
A_{x}=\{\varrho^{A}_{n}(x)\mid n\in\omega\}
for all
x\in p(A)
.

(v)

The set
A
can be written as
A=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}P_{n}
, where the sets
P_{n}
are pairwise disjoint
\lambda
-Borel graphs of partial (necessarily
\lambda
-Borel) functions.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
X
is
\lambda
-Polish too.

(i)

It is enought to show that
(5)
of Theorem
6.1.4
is satisfied.
Apply Corollary
4.3.6
within the
\lambda
-Polish space
X\times Y
to turn
A
into a
\tau^{\prime}
-closed set, for a suitable
\lambda
-Polish topology
\tau^{\prime}
. Each nonempty vertical section
A_{x}
, when equipped with the topology induced by
\tau^{\prime}
via the identification of
A_{x}
with
\{x\}\times A_{x}
, is a countable (classical!) Polish spaces because
\{x\}\times A_{x}
is
\tau^{\prime}
-closed, and hence it surely has isolated points.

(ii)

For any open
U\subseteq Y
and
x\in X
we have

\mathrm{cl}(A_{x})\cap U\neq\emptyset\iff A_{x}\cap U\neq\emptyset\iff x\in p(A\cap(X\times U)).

Since
A\cap(X\times U)
is a
\lambda
-Borel set with countable vertical sections, it follows that
p(A\cap(X\times U))
is
\lambda
-Borel by part
(i)
, hence we are done.

(iii)

We first observe that it is enough to consider the case where
A
is a (closed) subset of
X\times B(\lambda)
.
Since we already proved
(i)
, we can use
(3)
of Theorem
6.1.4
to get a closed set
F\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
and a continuous
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism
f\colon F\to A
such that
p(w)=p(f(w))
for every
w\in F
, so that in particular
p(F)=p(A)
and
F
has countable vertical sections too. If
(\varsigma^{F}_{n})_{n\in\omega}
are as in
(iii)
with respect to the set
F
, then it is enough to let
\varsigma^{A}_{n}(x)=p_{2}(f(x,\varsigma^{F}_{n}(x)))
,
where
p_{2}
is again the projection on the second coordinate, to get the desired result.

So without loss of generality we may assume that
Y=B(\lambda)
and
A\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
be closed.
Fix any bijection
\sigma\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\to\lambda
and for each infinite
\beta<\omega_{1}
fix a strict well-ordering
<_{\beta}
of
\omega
of order type
\beta
.
Let
G\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
be the set of pairs
(x,z)\in X\times B(\lambda)
such that
x\in p(A)
,

\forall n,m\in\omega\,(n<_{\beta}m\iff z(n)<z(m))

for some (necessarily unique)
\beta<\omega_{1}
, and for all
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}

A_{x}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\neq\emptyset\iff\exists n\in\omega\,(z(n)=\sigma(s)).

Clearly
G
is a
\lambda
-Borel set by parts
(i)
and
(ii)
and
\omega_{1}\leq\lambda
(notice that
A_{x}=\mathrm{cl}(A_{x})
since
A
is closed). Moreover,
since each
A_{x}
is countable then for every
x\in p(A)
there are exactly
\aleph_{0}
-many
\alpha<\lambda
such that
A_{x}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{\sigma^{-1}(\alpha)}\neq\emptyset
, hence the set of such ordinals
\alpha
will be well-ordered by the ordering of
\lambda
in order type
\beta
for a unique
\omega\leq\beta<\omega_{1}
. It follows that there is a unique
z\in B(\lambda)
such that
(x,z)\in G
. Thus
G
is the graph of a
\lambda
-Borel function
g\colon p(A)\to B(\lambda)
.
For each
\alpha<\lambda
let
f_{\alpha}\colon p(A\cap(X\times\boldsymbol{N}_{\sigma^{-1}(\alpha)}))\to B(\lambda)
be a
\lambda
-Borel uniformizing function for the
\lambda
-Borel set
A\cap(X\times\boldsymbol{N}_{\sigma^{-1}(\alpha)})
, which exists by part
(i)
because
A\cap(X\times\boldsymbol{N}_{\sigma^{-1}(\alpha)})\subseteq A
is
\lambda
-Borel and has countable vertical sections. Define
\varsigma^{A}_{n}
by setting
\varsigma_{n}^{A}(x)=f_{g(x)(n)}(x)
for each
x\in p(A)
.
By definition of
G
, for each
x\in p(A)
the ordinal
\alpha=g(x)(n)
is such that
A_{x}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{\sigma^{-1}(\alpha)}\neq\emptyset
, thus
f_{\alpha}
is defined on
x
and
\varsigma^{A}_{n}(x)=f_{\alpha}(x)\in A_{x}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{\sigma^{-1}(\alpha)}
. This shows in particular that each map
\varsigma^{A}_{n}
is defined on the entire
p(A)
. To see that it is
\lambda
-Borel, it is enough to observe that for every
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
and
x\in p(A)
we have
\varsigma^{A}_{n}(x)\in\boldsymbol{N}_{s}
if and only if
\exists\alpha<\lambda\,(g(x)(n)=\alpha\wedge f_{\alpha}(x)\in\boldsymbol{N}_{s})
, and that the latter condition is
\lambda
-Borel because the functions
g
and
f_{\alpha}
are all
\lambda
-Borel. Finally, to show that
\{\varsigma^{A}_{n}(x)\mid n\in\omega\}
is dense in
A_{x}
notice that if
A_{x}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\neq\emptyset
, then by construction there is
n\in\omega
such that
g(x)(n)=\sigma(s)
, and hence
\varsigma^{A}_{n}(x)\in A_{x}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}
.

(iv)

Arguing as in the first paragraph of the proof of
(iii)
,
without loss of generality we can again assume that
A
be closed.
(Actually, having that all vertical sections
A_{x}
are closed would be enough for the ensuing argument.)
Let
(\varsigma^{A}_{n})_{n\in\omega}
be as in part
(iii)
, and fix a bijection
h\colon 2^{\aleph_{0}}\to{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
.
Given
\alpha<2^{\aleph_{0}}
let
\vartheta^{A}_{\alpha}\colon p(A)\to Y
be defined by setting for
x\in p(A)

\vartheta^{A}_{\alpha}(x)=\begin{cases}\lim_{n\to\infty}\varsigma^{A}_{h(\alpha)(n)}(x)&\text{if }(\varsigma^{A}_{h(\alpha)(n)}(x))_{n\in\omega}\text{ is a Cauchy sequence}\\
\varsigma^{A}_{0}(x)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Then each
\vartheta^{A}_{\alpha}
is
\lambda
-Borel and for each
x\in p(A)

A_{x}=\{\vartheta^{A}_{\alpha}(x)\mid\alpha<2^{\aleph_{0}}\}

because
A_{x}
is closed and
\{\varsigma^{A}_{n}(x)\mid n\in\omega\}
is dense in it.
Now for every
e\colon\omega\to 2^{\aleph_{0}}
let
X_{e}
be the set of those
x\in p(A)
such that for every
\alpha<2^{\aleph_{0}}
there is
n\in\omega
such that
\vartheta^{A}_{\alpha}(x)=\vartheta^{A}_{e(n)}(x)
. Then
X_{e}
is
\lambda
-Borel

because
2^{\aleph_{0}}<\lambda
, all the maps
\vartheta^{A}_{\alpha}
are
\lambda
-Borel, and
p(A)
is
\lambda
-Borel by part
(i)
. Moreover,
p(A)=\bigcup\{X_{e}\mid e\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(2^{\aleph_{0}})}\}
because each
A_{x}
is countable, and for
x\in X_{e}
we have
A_{x}=\{\vartheta^{A}_{e(n)}(x)\mid n\in\omega\}
. Since there are less than
\lambda
-many functions
e\colon\omega\to 2^{\aleph_{0}}
by our hypothesis on
\lambda
, we can easily refine the covering
(X_{e})_{e\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(2^{\aleph_{0}})}}
of
p(A)
to a weak partition
(B_{e})_{e\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(2^{\aleph_{0}})}}
consisting of
\lambda
-Borel sets satisfying
B_{e}\subseteq X_{e}
. Then it is enough to set, for each
x\in p(A)
,
\varrho^{A}_{n}(x)=\vartheta^{A}_{e(n)}(x)
where
e\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(2^{\aleph_{0}})}
is unique such that
x\in B_{e}
.

(v)

Let
(\varrho^{A}_{n})_{n\in\omega}
be as in part
(iv)
and set

P_{n}=\mathrm{graph}(\varrho^{A}_{n})\setminus\bigcup_{m<n}\mathrm{graph}(\varrho^{A}_{m}).\qed

Recall that an equivalence relation
E
is called
countable
if all its equivalence classes have size at most
\aleph_{0}
. In particular, if
E
is a countable equivalence relation on a
\lambda
-Polish space, then all its
E
-equivalence classes are at most
F_{\sigma}
. It follows that if such a class
[x]_{E}
is also
G_{\delta}
, as it happens in
(3)
of Proposition
6.1.8
, then it is a transfinite countable difference of open subsets of the (classical!) Polish space
\mathrm{cl}([x]_{E})
by
[
28
, Theorem 22.27]
, and hence the same is true with respect to the whole space
X
.

Proposition 6.1.8
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
E
be a countable
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relation on a standard
\lambda
-Borel space
X
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

E
is potentially closed;

(2)

there is a
\lambda
-Polish topology on
X
generating its
\lambda
-Borel structure such that
all
E
equivalence classes are closed;

(3)

there is a
\lambda
-Polish topology on
X
generating its
\lambda
-Borel structure such that
all
E
equivalence classes are
G_{\delta}
;

(4)

E
is smooth;

(5)

E
has a
\lambda
-Borel selector (equivalently: a
\lambda
-Borel transversal).

In particular, if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and
E
is a countable
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relation on a standard
\lambda
-Borel space
X
such that all its equivalence classes are finite (and hence closed), then
E
has a
\lambda
-Borel selector.

Proof.

Conditions
(1)
and
(2)
are equivalent by Corollary
5.5.8
, while
(2)

\Rightarrow

(3)
is obvious. The implication
(3)

\Rightarrow

(4)
can be proved as in Proposition
5.5.9
, once we notice that we already know that the map
x\mapsto\mathrm{cl}([x]_{E})
is
\lambda
-Borel because we can apply Theorem
6.1.7
(ii)
with
A=E
. For the implication
(2)

\Rightarrow

(5)
, we use the same observation and compose the
\lambda
-Borel map
x\mapsto\mathrm{cl}([x]_{E})=[x]_{E}
with the
\lambda
-Borel function
\sigma^{X}_{0}\colon F(X)\to X
from Theorem
5.4.12
to get the desired
\lambda
-Borel selector. Finally,
(5)

\Rightarrow

(4)
is obvious, while
(4)

\Rightarrow

(1)
follows from the argument at the end of Subsection
5.5.2
.
∎

Another consequence of Theorem
6.1.7
(v)
is the analogue of the celebrated Feldman-Moore theorem. To deduce it from the mentioned result, we cannot simply adapt to our context the standard argument from e.g.
[
15
, Theorem 7.1.4]
, as our spaces have uncountable weight. To overcome this difficulty, we will exploit the following observation, which can be used as an alternative also in the classical setup
\lambda=\omega
.

Lemma 6.1.9
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
Q\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X\times X)
is the graph of an injective partial function
f
from
X
into itself, then
Q
can be written as
Q=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}R_{k}
, where the sets
R_{k}
are pairwise disjoint,
\lambda
-Borel, and such that the two projections
p(R_{k})
and
p_{2}(R_{k})
of
R_{k}
are either disjoint or equal.

Proof.

By Theorems
5.3.8
and
5.3.6
(together with Remark
5.3.7
), the function
f
is a
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism between the
\lambda
-Borel sets
p(Q)=\operatorname{dom}(f)
and
p_{2}(Q)=\operatorname{rng}(f)
.
Let
B_{0}=p(Q)
, and

B_{k+1}=\begin{cases}B_{k}\cap f(B_{k})&\text{if $k$ is even},\\
B_{k}\cap f^{-1}(B_{k})&\text{if $k$ is odd}.\end{cases}

Notice that
B_{k}\supseteq B_{k+1}
, for every
k\in\omega
.
Finally, let
B_{\infty}=\bigcap_{k\in\omega}B_{k}
. Using Theorem
5.3.8
again, it is easy to prove by induction on
k\in\omega
that each
B_{k}
is
\lambda
-Borel, so that
B_{\infty}\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
too. Let
R_{0}=Q\cap(B_{\infty}\times X)
and
R_{k+1}=Q\cap((B_{k}\setminus B_{k+1})\times X)
: we claim that the sets
R_{k}
, which are clearly
\lambda
-Borel, are as required.

Since the sets
B_{k}\setminus B_{k+1}
together with
B_{\infty}
form a weak partition of
B_{0}=p(Q)
, we get that the sets
R_{k}
are pairwise disjoint and that
Q=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}R_{k}
.
By construction,
p(R_{0})=B_{\infty}
and
p(R_{k+1})=B_{k}\setminus B_{k+1}
, and moreover
p_{2}(R_{k})=f(p(R_{k}))
for every
k\in\omega
.

It follows that, by construction,
p_{2}(R_{0})=B_{\infty}
, so that
p_{2}(R_{0})=p(R_{0})
. Indeed, assume first that
y\in B_{\infty}
. Then
y\in B_{k+1}
for every even
k\in\omega
, and so
y\in f(B_{k})
for every such
k
. In particular,
y\in\operatorname{rng}(f)
: let
x\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
be the only element such that
f(x)=y
. Then
x\in B_{k}
for every even
k\in\omega
, hence
x\in B_{\infty}
because the sets
B_{k}
are decreasing with respect to inclusion. Conversely, if
y\in p_{2}(R_{0})\subseteq\operatorname{rng}(f)
it means that the only
x
such that
f(x)=y
belongs to
p(R_{0})=B_{\infty}
, and in particular to
B_{k+1}
for every odd
k\in\omega
. This means that
y=f(x)\in B_{k}
for every such
k
, and hence
y\in B_{\infty}
.

Finally, fix any
k\in\omega
. In order to prove that
p(R_{k+1})\cap p_{2}(R_{k+1})=\emptyset
we need to check that
f(B_{k}\setminus B_{k+1})\cap(B_{k}\setminus B_{k+1})=\emptyset
.
Notice that
B_{k}\setminus B_{k+1}=B_{k}\setminus f(B_{k})
if
k
is even, and
B_{k}\setminus B_{k+1}=B_{k}\setminus f^{-1}(B_{k})
if
k
is odd. Therefore, if
k
is even we get
f(B_{k}\setminus f(B_{k}))\cap(B_{k}\setminus f(B_{k}))=\emptyset
because
f(B_{k}\setminus f(B_{k}))\subseteq f(B_{k})
; if
k
is odd, then
f(B_{k}\setminus f^{-1}(B_{k}))\cap(B_{k}\setminus f^{-1}(B_{k}))=\emptyset
because
f(B_{k}\setminus f^{-1}(B_{k}))\cap B_{k}=\emptyset
.
∎

Of course, the above lemma holds also in the context of standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces whenever we assume
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
We are now ready to prove the generalized Feldman-Moore theorem.

Theorem 6.1.10
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a standard
\lambda
-Borel space. Then
E
is a countable
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relation on
X
if and only if
E
is induced by a
\lambda
-Borel action
2
2

2

Indeed, our proof shows that we can even assume that
G
acts on
X
by
\lambda
-Borel automorphisms.

of a (discrete) countable group
G
on
X
.

Proof.

One direction is obvious, so let us assume that
E
is a countable
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relation on
X
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
X
is a
\lambda
-Polish space. By Theorem
6.1.7
(v)
applied with
A=E
, we get that
E=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}P_{n}
with
\{P_{n}\mid n\in\omega\}
a family of pairwise disjoint
\lambda
-Borel sets which are graphs of partial functions from
X
into itself. For every
n,m\in\omega
, consider the
\lambda
-Borel set
Q_{n,m}=P_{n}\cap P_{m}^{-1}
.
Then
E=\bigcup_{n,m\in\omega}Q_{n,m}
, and the sets
Q_{n,m}
are still pairwise disjoint graphs of partial function
f_{n,m}
from
X
into itself.
Moreover, each
f_{n,m}
is injective because
Q_{n,m}\subseteq P^{-1}_{m}
and the latter is the graph of a partial function.

Next apply Lemma
6.1.9
to each
Q_{n,m}
and write it as
Q_{n,m}=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}R_{n,m,k}
, for suitable
\lambda
-Borel sets
R_{n,m,k}
.
By Theorems
5.3.8
and
5.3.6
(together with Remark
5.3.7
), each
R_{n,m,k}
is the graph of a
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism
h_{n,m,k}
between the
\lambda
-Borel sets
A_{n,m,k}=p(R_{n,m,k})
and
B_{n,m,k}=p_{2}(R_{n,m,k})
, and either
A_{n,m,k}=B_{n,m,k}
or
A_{n,m,k}\cap B_{n,m,k}
. In the first case, we let
g_{n,m,k}\colon X\to X
be defined by setting
g_{n,m,k}(x)=h_{n,m,k}(x)
if
x\in A_{n,m,k}
and
g_{n,m,k}(x)=x
otherwise; in the second case, we instead let
g_{n,m,k}\colon X\to X
be defined by
g_{n,m,k}(x)=h_{n,m,k}(x)
if
A_{n,m,k}
,
g_{n,m,k}(x)=h_{n,m,k}^{-1}(x)
if
x\in B_{n,m,k}
, and
g_{n,m,k}(x)=x
otherwise. Notice that each
g_{n,m,k}
is a well-defined
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism between
X
and itself, and moreover

x\mathrel{E}y\iff g_{n,m,k}(x)=y\text{ for some $n,m,k\in\omega$}.

Let
G
be the set of
\lambda
-Borel automorphisms obtained by closing under composition the family
\{g_{n,m,k}\mid n,m,k\in\omega\}
and adding the identity map on
X
if necessary. Then
G
is a countable group with respect to the composition operation, and its natural action on
X
induces precisely the equivalence relation
E
.
∎

Among the many important consequences of the Feldman-Moore theorem, we would like to mention at least Slaman-Steel’s “marker lemma”, that can now be generalized as follows.

Lemma 6.1.11
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
E
be a countable
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relation on a standard
\lambda
-Borel space
X
. Let

B=\{x\in X\mid[x]_{E}\text{ is infinite}\}
.
Then there is a countable decreasing sequence
B\supseteq S_{0}\supseteq S_{1}\supseteq S_{2}\supseteq\dotsc
of
\lambda
-Borel sets such that
[S_{n}]_{E}=B
for every
n\in\omega
, yet
\bigcap_{n\in\omega}S_{n}=\emptyset
.

Sketch of the proof.

(See
[
15
, Lemma 7.1.5 and Exercise 7.1.4]
for more details.)
By Propositions
5.4.6
and
3.2.5
(together with Remark
3.2.6
), without loss of generality we can assume that
X
is a closed subset of
B(\lambda)
, and in particular a
\lambda
-Polish space.
By Theorem
6.1.10
there is a countable group
G=\{g_{n}\mid n\in\omega\}
and a
\lambda
-Borel action
(g,x)\to g\cdot x
of
G
on
X
inducing
E
.
Therefore the
E
-invariant set
B
is
\lambda
-Borel, as

x\in B\iff\forall n\exists m\forall k\leq n\,(g_{m}\cdot x\neq g_{k}\cdot x).

By Theorem
6.1.7
(ii)
, the map
x\mapsto\mathrm{cl}([x]_{E})
is
\lambda
-Borel, hence so is the map
f
sending
x\in X
to
\sigma_{0}^{X}(\mathrm{cl}([x]_{E}))
, where
\sigma_{0}^{X}
is as in Theorem
5.4.12
.
Consider the
E
-invariant
\lambda
-Borel set
A=\{x\in X\mid f(x)\in[x]_{E}\}
. Then
A\supseteq X\setminus B
, and
f
is a
\lambda
-Borel selector for the restriction of
E
to
A
. For every
n\in\omega
, let

x\in S_{n}\iff(x\in A\cap B\wedge\forall m<n\,(g_{m}\cdot x\neq f(x)))\vee(x\notin A\wedge x\restriction n=f(x)\restriction n).

Then the sets
S_{n}
are as required.
∎

Let us also mention yet another corollary of Theorem
6.1.7
.
A map
f\colon X\to Y
is called
countable-to-
1
if
f^{-1}(y)
is (at most) countable for every
y\in Y
.

Corollary 6.1.12
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X,Y
be standard
\lambda
-Borel spaces. If a
\lambda
-Borel map
f\colon X\to Y
is countable-to-
1
, then
\operatorname{rng}(f)
is
\lambda
-Borel and
f
admits a
\lambda
-Borel right-inverse.

Proof.

Apply Theorem
6.1.7
(i)
to the set
A=\{(y,x)\in Y\times X\mid f(x)=y\}
, which is
\lambda
-Borel by Theorem
5.3.6
and has countable vertical sections because
f
is countable-to-
1
.
∎

Along the same lines, Theorem
6.1.7
(ii)
also implies the following fact.

Corollary 6.1.13
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
,
X
be a standard
\lambda
-Borel space, and
Y
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Let
f\colon X\to F(Y)
be such that
|f(x)|\leq\aleph_{0}
for all
x\in X
. Then
f
is
\lambda
-Borel if and only if the set
F=\{(x,y)\in X\times Y\mid y\in f(x)\}
is
\lambda
-Borel.

6.1.4.
\lambda
-Borel uniformizations for sets with compact sections

We finally consider
\lambda
-Borel sets with compact vertical sections.
The following result is the analogue of
[
28
, Theorem 28.8]
. Our proof is again necessarily different, this time because non-separable (complete) metric spaces cannot have a metrizable compactification.
The argument we are going to present works also when
\lambda=\omega
, hence it gives an alternative proof of
[
28
, Theorem 28.8]
which does not pass through compactification.

Theorem 6.1.14
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Let
X
be a standard
\lambda
-Borel space, and
Y
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
A\subseteq X\times Y
is a
\lambda
-Borel set all of whose vertical sections
A_{x}
are compact, then the map
x\mapsto A_{x}
is
\lambda
-Borel as a map from
X
to
K(Y)\subseteq F(Y)
. Equivalently, a map
f\colon X\to K(Y)
is
\lambda
-Borel if and only if the set
F=\{(x,y)\in X\times Y\mid y\in f(x)\}
is
\lambda
-Borel.

Proof.

We first show that we can restrict the attention to the special case
Y=B(\lambda)
. Indeed, if
Y
is an arbitrary nonempty
\lambda
-Polish space, then by Proposition
3.2.11
there is a continuous surjection
f\colon B(\lambda)\to Y
with the property that the preimage of any compact set is still compact. It follows that

A^{\prime}=\{(x,y)\in X\times B(\lambda)\mid(x,f(y))\in A\}

is a
\lambda
-Borel set with compact sections. Suppose that
x\mapsto A^{\prime}_{x}
is
\lambda
-Borel as a map from
X
to
K(B(\lambda))
. Since
A^{\prime}_{x}=f^{-1}(A_{x})
and
f
is surjective, for any open
U\subseteq Y
we have
A_{x}\cap U\neq\emptyset
if and only if
A^{\prime}_{x}\cap f^{-1}(U)\neq\emptyset
, thus the function
x\mapsto A_{x}
is
\lambda
-Borel as well.

So assume
Y=B(\lambda)
from now on. For each
0\neq n\in\omega
,
let
(S^{n}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\lambda}
be an enumeration without repetitions of
all cofinite subsets of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{n}{\lambda}
, and for each
\alpha<\lambda
let

U^{n}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{s\in S^{n}_{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{N}_{s}
. We first claim that

\mathcal{U}=\{U^{n}_{\alpha}\mid 0\neq n\in\omega\wedge\alpha<\lambda\}
is a basis for

\mathcal{V}=\{B(\lambda)\setminus A_{x}\mid x\in X\}
. Indeed, given
x\in X

and
y\notin A_{x}
, there is
0\neq k\in\omega
such that

\boldsymbol{N}_{y\restriction k}\cap A_{x}=\emptyset
because
A_{x}
is closed. Let

S=\{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}\mid\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\cap A_{x}=\emptyset\}
: since
A_{x}
is
compact and
\boldsymbol{N}_{t}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{t^{\prime}}=\emptyset
if
\operatorname{lh}(t)=\operatorname{lh}(t^{\prime})
and

t\neq t^{\prime}
, it follows that
S
is cofinite and thus
S=S^{k}_{\alpha}
for some

\alpha<\lambda
. By the choice of
k
and
S
, it follows that
y\in U^{k}_{\alpha}\subseteq B(\lambda)\setminus A_{x}
,
as desired.

By Theorem
5.5.5
there are
\lambda
-Borel sets
B^{n}_{\alpha}\subseteq X
such that

(X\times B(\lambda))\setminus A=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\neq n\in\omega\\
\alpha<\lambda\end{subarray}}(B^{n}_{\alpha}\times U^{n}_{\alpha}).

Fix an arbitrary
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
. We claim that for all
x\in X

(6.1)

A_{x}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\neq\emptyset\iff\forall n\geq\operatorname{lh}(s)\,\exists m\geq n\,\exists t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{m}{\lambda}\,\Big[t\supseteq s\wedge\exists\alpha<\lambda\,(x\in B^{m}_{\alpha})\\
\wedge\forall k\leq m\,\forall\beta<\lambda\,(x\in B^{k}_{\beta}\Rightarrow t\restriction k\notin S^{k}_{\beta})\Big].

Since the right part of equation (
6.1
) clearly defines a
\lambda
-Borel set, the map
x\mapsto A_{x}
will then be
\lambda
-Borel, as desired.

Fix any
x\in X
. To prove the above equivalence, assume first that there is
y\in A_{x}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}
and fix any
n\geq\operatorname{lh}(s)
. Since
\boldsymbol{N}_{y\restriction n}
is closed but not compact, there is
z\in B(\lambda)\setminus A_{x}
such that
z\restriction n=y\restriction n
.
Let
0\neq m\in\omega
and
\alpha<\lambda
be such that
(x,z)\in B^{m}_{\alpha}\times U^{m}_{\alpha}
, so that
z\restriction m\in S^{m}_{\alpha}
, and set
t=y\restriction m
: we claim that
m
and
t
are as required. First of all, observe that
m>n
, as otherwise
y\in U^{m}_{\alpha}
(because
y\restriction m=z\restriction m
if
m\leq n
) and thus
(x,y)\in A\cap(B^{m}_{\alpha}\times U^{m}_{\alpha})
, a contradiction.
It follows that
t\supseteq s
because both sequences are initial segments of
y
and
\operatorname{lh}(t)=m>n\geq\operatorname{lh}(s)
. Moreover,
x\in B^{m}_{\alpha}
for the chosen
\alpha
, so also the second conjunct in the formula between the square brackets is satisfied. Finally, if
x\in B^{k}_{\beta}
and
t\restriction k\in S^{k}_{\beta}
for some
k\leq m
and
\beta<\lambda
, then by
y\supseteq t
we would have again
(x,y)\in A\cap(B^{k}_{\beta}\times U^{k}_{\beta})
, a contradiction. Thus
x\in B^{k}_{\beta}\Rightarrow t\restriction k\notin S^{k}_{\beta}
for all such
k
and
\beta
, as required.

Conversely, assume now that the right hand side of the equivalence (
6.1
) is satisfied. We call
t
a witness if the part in square brackets of the formula is satisfied with
m=\operatorname{lh}(t)
, that is,
t\supseteq s
,
x\in B^{\operatorname{lh}(t)}_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha<\lambda
, and
x\in B^{k}_{\beta}\Rightarrow t\restriction k\notin S^{k}_{\beta}
for all
k\leq\operatorname{lh}(t)
and
\beta<\lambda
. Let

T_{s}=\{t\restriction k\mid k\leq\operatorname{lh}(t)\wedge t\text{ is a witness}\}

be the tree generated by all witnesses. Clearly
T_{s}
is infinite because our condition requires that there are witnesses of arbitrarily high length.

Claim 6.1.14.1
.

Each level of
T_{s}
is finite.

Proof of the claim.

Fix any
0\neq n\in\omega
. Let
m\geq n
be smallest such that
x\in B^{m}_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha<\lambda
, which exists because there must be witnesses of arbitrarily high length.
Notice that if
t
is any witness of length at least
m
, then
t\restriction m\notin S^{m}_{\alpha}
by
x\in B^{m}_{\alpha}
. It follows that every
u\in T_{s}
of length
n
is of the form
t\restriction n
for some
t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{m}{\lambda}\setminus S^{m}_{\alpha}
, and since
S^{m}_{\alpha}
is cofinite there are only finitely many such sequences
u
.
∎

Since
T_{s}
is an infinite finitely branching
\omega
-tree, by König’s lemma there is an infinite branch
y\in[T_{s}]
. Clearly,
y\in\boldsymbol{N}_{s}
because all sequences in
T_{s}
are compatible with
s
because any witness extends
s
by definition: we claim that
(x,y)\in A
, so that
y
witnesses
A_{x}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\neq\emptyset
, as required. If not, there are
0\neq k\in\omega
and
\beta<\lambda
such that
(x,y)\in B^{k}_{\beta}\times U^{k}_{\beta}
. Since
y\in[T_{s}]
, there is a witness
t
of length at least
k
such that
y\restriction k=t\restriction k
. Since
x\in B^{k}_{\beta}
, by definition of “witness” it follows that
t\restriction k\notin S^{k}_{\beta}
. On the other hand,
y\in U^{k}_{\beta}
is equivalent to
y\restriction k\in S^{k}_{\beta}
, and this is then a contradiction.
∎

We are now ready to prove the analogue of Theorem
6.1.7
for
\lambda
-Borel sets
A
with compact vertical sections.
The statements and their proofs are quite similar to the previous ones, but with the following significant differences. For
x\in p(A)
:

•

A_{x}
is now automatically closed because it is compact;

•

A_{x}
can now be uncountable, but still we have
|A_{x}|\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
and
\operatorname{dens}(A_{x})\leq\aleph_{0}
because
A_{x}
is compact metrizable.

This in particular means that we will still be able to uniformly enumerate dense subsets of the
A_{x}
with countably many
\lambda
-Borel functions, but we will need continuum many of them to uniformly enumerate the whole sections.
Notice also that the analogue of Theorem
6.1.7
(ii)
is already proved in Theorem
6.1.14
, and will thus be omitted.

Theorem 6.1.15
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Let
X
be a standard
\lambda
-Borel space,
Y
be a
\lambda
-Polish space, and
A\subseteq X\times Y
be a
\lambda
-Borel set all of whose vertical sections are compact.

(i)

There is a
\lambda
-Borel uniformization of
A
, hence in particular
p(A)
is
\lambda
-Borel.

(ii)

There is a sequence
(\varsigma^{A}_{n})_{n\in\omega}
of
\lambda
-Borel functions
\varsigma^{A}_{n}\colon p(A)\to Y
such that for all
x\in p(A)
the set
\{\varsigma^{A}_{n}(x)\mid n\in\omega\}
is dense in
A_{x}
.

(iii)

There is a sequence
(\varrho^{A}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<2^{\aleph_{0}}}
of
\lambda
-Borel functions
\varrho^{A}_{\alpha}\colon p(A)\to Y
such that

A_{x}=\{\varrho_{\alpha}^{A}(x)\mid\alpha<2^{\aleph_{0}}\}

for all
x\in p(A)
.

(iv)

The set
A
can be written as
A=\bigcup_{\alpha<2^{\aleph_{0}}}P_{\alpha}
, where the sets
P_{\alpha}
are pairwise disjoint
\lambda
-Borel graphs of partial (necessarily
\lambda
-Borel) functions.

Proof.

(i)

The map
x\mapsto A_{x}
is
\lambda
-Borel by Theorem
6.1.14
. Consider a function
\sigma^{Y}_{0}\colon F(Y)\to Y
as in Theorem
5.4.12
. The map
f\colon X\to Y
sending
x
to
\sigma^{Y}_{0}(A_{x})
is a
\lambda
-Borel uniformizing function for
A
.

(ii)

Arguing as in the proof of Theorem
6.1.14
, one easily sees that it is enough to consider the case
Y=B(\lambda)
. Moreover, for
x\in p(A)
there are exactly
\aleph_{0}
-many
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
such that
A_{x}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\neq\emptyset
because
A_{x}
is compact. Thus we can repeat the argument from Theorem
6.1.7
(iii)
, using the fact that
A_{x}\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{\sigma^{-1}(\alpha)}
, being a closed subset of the compact set
A_{x}
, is compact itself.

(iii)

Follow the proof of Theorem
6.1.7
(iv)
until the construction of the functions
(\vartheta^{A}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<2^{\aleph_{0}}}
. (This is possible because each
A_{x}
, being compact, is also closed.) Since for all
x\in p(A)
we already have

A_{x}=\{\vartheta^{A}_{\alpha}(x)\mid\alpha<2^{\aleph_{0}}\},

we can simply set
\varrho^{A}_{\alpha}=\vartheta^{A}_{\alpha}
.

(iv)

Let
(\varrho^{A}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<2^{\aleph_{0}}}
be as in part
(iii)
, and set

P_{\alpha}=\mathrm{graph}(\varrho^{A}_{\alpha})\setminus\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}\mathrm{graph}(\varrho^{A}_{\beta}).\qed

Similarly to the case of
\lambda
-Borel sets with countable vertical sections, once we have Theorems
6.1.14
and
6.1.15

we can then derive a number of standard consequences, whose proofs are omitted because they are essentially the same of the corresponding results in Section
6.1.3
. First, as an easy application to
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relations we get:

Corollary 6.1.16
.

Assume
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. If
X
is
\lambda
-Polish and
E
is a
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relation on
X
all of whose equivalence classes are compact, then
E
has a
\lambda
-Borel selector (equivalently: a
\lambda
-Borel transversal). In particular,
E
is smooth.

Since
2^{\aleph_{0}}<\lambda
when
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, the following can be seen as a form of the Feldman-Moore theorem for
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relations with compact equivalence classes.

Theorem 6.1.17
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space, and let
E
be a
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relation on
X
with compact equivalence classes. Then
E
is induced by a
\lambda
-Borel action of a (discrete) continuum-sized group
G
on
X
.

There is an interesting phenomenon that arises when considering
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relations induced by a
\lambda
-Borel action of an
uncountable
(discrete) group. Given a cardinal
\kappa\leq\lambda
, say that an equivalence relation
E
on a standard
\lambda
-Borel space
X
is
hyper-
\kappa
-small
if
E
can be written as an increasing union
\bigcup_{\alpha<\kappa}E_{\alpha}
of
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relations on
X
that are
\kappa
-small
, that is: for each
\alpha<\kappa
there is
\mu<\kappa
such that all
E_{\alpha}
-equivalence classes have size at most
\mu
.
Notice that hyper-
\aleph_{0}
-smallness is a strengthening of what in classical descriptive set theory is called “hyperfiniteness”.

Lemma 6.1.18
.

Let
\omega<\kappa\leq\lambda
be a cardinal. If the equivalence relation
E
on the standard
\lambda
-Borel space
X
is induced by a
\lambda
-Borel action of a group
G
of size
\kappa
, then
E
is hyper-
\kappa
-small.

Proof.

Enumerate
G
as
G=\{g_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\kappa\}
. For every
\alpha<\kappa
, let
G_{\alpha}
be the subgroup of
G
generated by
\{g_{\beta}\mid\beta<\alpha\}
, and let
E_{\alpha}
be the equivalence relation on
X
induced by the restriction of the action of
G
to
G_{\alpha}
. Then the sequence
(E_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\kappa}
witnesses that
E
is hyper-
\kappa
-small.
∎

Combining this with Theorem
6.1.17
, we then get:

Corollary 6.1.19
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space, and let
E
be a
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relation on
X
with compact equivalence classes. Then
E
is hyper-
2^{\aleph_{0}}
-small.

In particular, if
\mathsf{CH}
holds, then every
E
as above can be written as an
\omega_{1}
-increasing union of countable
\lambda
-Borel equivalence relations.

Finally, for
X
a topological space call
a map
f\colon X\to Y
, where
X
is a topological space,
compact-to-
1
if
f^{-1}(y)
is compact for every
y\in Y
.

Corollary 6.1.20
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space, and let
Y
be a standard
\lambda
-Borel space. If a
\lambda
-Borel map
f\colon X\to Y
is compact-to-
1
, then
\operatorname{rng}(f)
is
\lambda
-Borel and
f
admits a
\lambda
-Borel right-inverse.

It is quite natural to conjecture that results along the lines of Theorems
6.1.7
,
6.1.14
, and
6.1.15
could be obtained for
\lambda
-Borel sets with sections of size at most
\lambda
. The natural adaptation of the classical techniques for the “small section” uniformization results from
[
28
, Sections 18.C and 35.H]
seems not to be of help here, mainly because we are still missing a Baire category theory for
\lambda
-Polish spaces matching well with the structure of
\lambda
-Borel sets. However, let us notice that the conjecture, if confirmed, would subsume all the results in Sections
6.1.3
and
6.1.4
(with the notable exception of Theorems
6.1.10
and
6.1.17
) because in our generalized context most of the “smallness notions” imply having small size: this includes e.g. the case of compact sections,
K_{\lambda}
sections (i.e. sections which are
\lambda
-sized unions of compact sets),
<\lambda
-Lindelöf sections (i.e. sections which are
\mu
-Lindelöf for some
\mu<\lambda
, where
\mu
possibly varies with the section), sections which are
\lambda
-sized unions of closed
\lambda
-Lindelöf sets (see Corollary
3.4.11
), and so on.
The above discussion shows that settling the conjecture is an important problem in the area, as it would yield one of the strongest possible uniformization result for
\lambda
-Borel sets.

6.2.
Boundedness theorems and ranks for
\lambda
-coanalytic sets

In this section we quickly review some important results related to the existence of
\lambda
-coanalytic ranks on
\lambda
-coanalytic sets, which roughly correspond to the material in Sections 34.A–34.C and 35.A–35.E of
[
28
]
. All proofs in the generalized setting are natural adaptations of the corresponding classical arguments and will thus be omitted for the sake of conciseness. The only notable exception to this is the proof of Theorem
6.2.4
, in which one needs to justify why the analogue of the classical argument can still be carried out despite the fact that the space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
of functions from
\lambda
into itself is not
\lambda
-Polish when equipped with the bounded topology.

First we notice that the proof of
[
28
, Theorem 31.1]
, due to Kunen, yields the following boundedness theorem for
\lambda
-analytic well-founded (strict) relations.

Theorem 6.2.1
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
\prec
is a
\lambda
-analytic well-founded (strict) relation on
X
, then
\rho(\prec)<\lambda^{+}
.

The same argument basically shows that the following generalized Kunen-Martin theorem holds if we assume
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
(see also
[
28
, Theorem 31.5]
).

Theorem 6.2.2
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space, and let
\kappa\geq\lambda
be a cardinal. If
\prec
is a
\kappa
-Souslin well-founded (strict) relation on
X
, then
\rho(\prec)<\kappa^{+}
.

In particular, under
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
there are no
\lambda
-analytic well-orders of
B(\lambda)
or of any
\lambda
-Polish space
X
with
|X|>\lambda
. Similarly, by Theorem
5.7.5
we get that under
{\sf AC}_{\lambda^{+}}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
there are no
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}
well-orderings of such spaces if the generalized continuum hypothesis fails at
\lambda
, i.e.
2^{\lambda}\neq\lambda^{+}
.
In contrast, we will show that, as in the classical case, in the constructible universe
L
there is a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}
well-ordering of any given
\lambda
-Polish space (Proposition
7.2.12
).

Arguing as in
[
28
, Theorem 31.2 and Exercise 31.3]
and using Propositions
5.1.4
and
5.1.5
, one then gets the following boundedness theorems for well-founded
\omega
-trees on
\lambda
and well-orders of
\lambda
.

Theorem 6.2.3
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. If
A\subseteq\mathrm{WF}_{\lambda}
is
\lambda
-analytic, then
\sup\{\rho(T_{x})\mid x\in A\}<\lambda^{+}
.

Similarly, if
A\subseteq\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
is
\lambda
-analytic, then
\sup\{\mathrm{ot}(\preceq_{x})\mid x\in A\}<\lambda^{+}
.

We now move to the notion of ranked (or normed, or prewellordered) classes. The notions involved are exactly those presented in
[
28
, Section 34.B]
, except that we work with classes of subsets of
\lambda
-Polish spaces rather than just (classical) Polish spaces.
We will just recall the basic definitions and facts, referring the reader to the mentioned monograph for more details.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
be a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass,
X
a
\lambda
-Polish space, and
A\subseteq X
. A
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
-rank
on
A
is a function
\varphi\colon A\to\mathrm{Ord}
for which there are relations
{\leq^{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\varphi}},{\leq^{\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}_{\varphi}}\subseteq X^{2}
in
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
and
\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
, respectively, such that for all
x\in X
and
y\in A

x\in A\wedge\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(y)\iff x\leq^{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\varphi}y\iff x\leq^{\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}_{\varphi}y.

If
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is also closed under finite unions and intersections, then one can prove that:

(6.2)

A map
\varphi\colon A\to\mathrm{Ord}
is a
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
-rank if and only if there exists
\leq^{\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}_{\varphi}
as above, together with a binary relation
{<^{\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}_{\varphi}}\subseteq X^{2}
in
\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
such that for all
x\in X
and
y\in A

x\in A\wedge\varphi(x)<\varphi(y)\iff x<^{\check{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}_{\varphi}y.

A
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
-rank is
regular
if its range is downward closed in
\mathrm{Ord}
.
Finally, we say that the boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is
ranked
if for every
\lambda
-Polish space
X
, each set in
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
admits a
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
-rank.

Theorem 6.2.4
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
Then the boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
is ranked (with ranks ranging in
\lambda^{+}
).

The proof is identical to that of
[
28
, Theorem 34.4]
. The only subtlety is due to the fact that we have to use the space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
, which would not be
\lambda
-Polish (nor standard
\lambda
-Borel) if, as common sense would suggest, we endow it with the bounded topology.

Proof.

(Compare with
[
28
, Theorem 34.4]
.)
By Proposition
5.1.5
, it is enough to show that the map
\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}\to\lambda^{+}
sending each
x\in\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
to its order type
\mathrm{ot}({\preceq_{x}})
is a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
-rank.
It is easy to see that the relations

\displaystyle x\leq^{\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}}y\iff\exists z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}\,[\forall\alpha,\beta<\lambda\,(\alpha\preceq_{x}\beta\iff z(\alpha)\preceq_{y}z(\beta))]

and

\displaystyle x<^{\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}}y\iff\exists\epsilon<\lambda\,\exists z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}\,[

\displaystyle\forall\alpha<\lambda\,(z(\alpha)\preceq_{y}\epsilon\wedge z(\alpha)\neq\epsilon)

\displaystyle\wedge\forall\alpha,\beta<\lambda\,(\alpha\preceq_{x}\beta\iff z(\alpha)\preceq_{y}z(\beta))]

are as in (
6.2
). Indeed, when
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
is equipped with the
\lambda
-Borel structure
\mathcal{B}=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda},\tau_{p})
induced by the
product topology
\tau_{p}
, it is a standard
\lambda
-Borel space (Example
5.4.5
), and in both formulas the parts in square brackets define sets which are
\lambda
-Borel in the (standard
\lambda
-Borel) product space
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda},\mathcal{B})\times(\mathrm{LO}_{\lambda})^{2}
. It follows that
\leq^{\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}}
and
<^{\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}}
are
\lambda
-analytic, as required.
∎

If
A\subseteq B(\lambda)
is
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(X)
, a natural way to define a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
-rank on it is the following (we are basically combining the proofs of Propositions
5.1.4
and
5.1.5
with that of Theorem
6.2.4
, see
[
28
, Exercise 34.7]
). Let
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\lambda\times\lambda)}
be any tree such that
B(\lambda)\setminus A=p[T]
, so that
x\in A\iff T(x)
is well-founded. Then the map
\varphi\colon A\to\lambda^{+}
defined by
\varphi(x)=\rho_{T(x)}(\emptyset)
is a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
-rank on
A
.

From Theorem
6.2.4
, the existence of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
-universal sets of
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}
and
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
, and their closure properties (Proposition
5.2.1
and Corollary
5.2.2
), one derives in the usual way some structural properties of
\lambda
-(co)analytic sets (see e.g. the proof of
[
28
, Theorem 35.1]
).

Theorem 6.2.5
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.

(i)

The class
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
has the reduction property but not the separation property, while
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}
has the separation property
3
3

3

This also follows from Theorems
5.3.1
and
5.3.5
.

but not the reduction property.

(ii)

The class
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
has the ordinal
\lambda
-uniformization property and the
\lambda
-generalized reduction property.

(iii)

The class
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}
has the
\lambda
-generalized separation property.

Remark 6.2.6
.

As in the classical setting, Theorem
6.2.5
(i)
implies that Theorem
5.3.1
cannot be extended to
\lambda
-coanalytic spaces (compare this with Remark
5.3.3
). Indeed, if
X
is
\lambda
-Polish and
B_{0},B_{1}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(X)
are disjoint sets that cannot be separated by a
\lambda
-Borel set, then
Y=B_{0}\cup B_{1}
is a
\lambda
-coanalytic space whose
\lambda
-analytic
4
4

4

Here we are naturally extending the notion of being
\lambda
-analytic to subsets of arbitrary subspaces
Y\subseteq X
of a
\lambda
-Polish space
X
as follows: A set
A\subseteq Y
is
\lambda
-analytic if and only if
A=A^{\prime}\cap Y
for some
A^{\prime}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
.

subsets
B_{0}
and
B_{1}
cannot be separated by a set in
\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(Y)
.

The reduction property for
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
implies that we can parametrize sets in
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}
in a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}
fashion using a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
set of codes (see the proof of
[
28
, Theorem 35.5]
).

Theorem 6.2.7
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then there is a
\lambda
-coanalytic set
D\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
and two sets
S\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\times X)
and
P\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\times X)
such that
S_{d}=P_{d}
for all
d\in D
, and
\{S_{d}\mid d\in D\}=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)
.

Other consequences of the fact that the boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
is ranked are the following higher analogues of
[
28
, Theorems 35.22 and 35.23]
, which yield in particular corresponding boundedness theorems for
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
-ranks.

Theorem 6.2.8
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
A\subseteq X
is
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
but not
\lambda
-Borel, then for every
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
-rank
\varphi\colon A\to\mathrm{Ord}
and every
\lambda
-analytic
B\subseteq A
there is
x_{0}\in A
with
\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(x_{0})
for all
x\in B
.

Theorem 6.2.9
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Let
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(X)
, and let
\varphi\colon A\to\mathrm{Ord}
be a regular
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
-rank. Then we have that
\varphi(A)=\sup\{\varphi(x)\mid x\in A\}\leq\lambda^{+}
, and that
A
is
\lambda
-Borel if and only if
\varphi(A)<\lambda^{+}
.

If
\psi\colon A\to\lambda^{+}
is any
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
-rank and
B\subseteq A
is
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}
, then
\sup\{\psi(x)\mid x\in B\}<\lambda^{+}
.

A standard consequence of this is that there is no
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}
set
A\subseteq\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
of size greater than
\lambda
with the property that
x
and
y
have different order types for all distinct
x,y\in A
.

Remark 6.2.10
.

By Remark
5.1.6
, all the rank theory as developed so far works only when
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
and
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Notably, boundedness theorems for
\lambda
-analytic sets along the lines of Theorem
6.2.9
have been obtained in
[
10
, Proposition 7.6 and Corollary 7.7]
for
arbitrary
strong limit singular cardinals. However, if
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)>\omega
one cannot longer use ranks ranging into ordinal numbers, but rather use their variant ranging in certain trees “ordered” by a suitable quasi-order.

Theorem
6.2.9
also provides an alternative proof of the fact that every
\lambda
-coanalytic subset
A
of a
\lambda
-Polish space
X
can be written as a well-ordered union of
\lambda^{+}
-many
\lambda
-Borel sets. Indeed, let
\varphi\colon A\to\lambda^{+}
be a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
-rank on
A
: then
A=\bigcup_{\xi<\lambda^{+}}A_{\xi}
, where
A_{\xi}
is the
\lambda
-Borel set
\{x\in A\mid\varphi(x)\leq\xi\}
.

Other standard consequences of the fact that
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
is ranked are the so-called reflection theorems. Let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
be a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass, and
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Let
\Phi
be a property for subsets of
X
, i.e.

\Phi\subseteq\mathscr{P}(X)
. Then
\Phi
is
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
on
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
if for any
\lambda
-Polish space
Y
and any
A\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(Y\times X)
the set of
y\in Y
such that
A_{y}

has property
\Phi
(that is,
A_{y}\in\Phi
) is in
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
.

Theorem 6.2.11

(First Reflection Theorem)
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
\Phi\subseteq\mathscr{P}(X)
is a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
on
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
property, then for every
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(X)
with property
\Phi
there exists
B\subseteq A
, still with property
\Phi
, such that
B\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
and
X
be as above. Let
\Psi
be a property for pair of subsets of
X
, i.e.
\Psi\subseteq\mathscr{P}(X)\times\mathscr{P}(X)
. We say again that
\Psi
is
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
on
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
if for any
\lambda
-Polish spaces
Y,Z
and any
A\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(Y\times X)
and
B\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(Z\times X)
, the set of
(y,z)\in Y\times Z
such that the corresponding pair
(A_{y},B_{z})
of vertical sections has property
\Psi
is in
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
. Moreover, we say that
\Psi
is
upward monotone
if for all pairs
(A,B)
with property
\Psi
and all
A\subseteq A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B\subseteq B^{\prime}\subseteq X
, the pair
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
has property
\Psi
as well. Finally,
\Psi
is
downward continuous in the second variable
if given
A,B_{n}\subseteq X
such that
B_{n}\supseteq B_{n+1}
for all
n\in\omega
, if each pair
(A,B_{n})
has property
\Psi
then
\big(A,\bigcap_{n\in\omega}B_{n}\big)
has property
\Psi
as well.

Theorem 6.2.12

(Second Reflection Theorem)
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
\Psi\subseteq\mathscr{P}(X)\times\mathscr{P}(X)
is a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
on
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
property which is both upward monotone and downward continuous in the second variable, then for every
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(X)
such that
(A,X\setminus A)
has property
\Psi
there exists
B\subseteq A
such that
(B,X\setminus B)
still has property
\Psi
and
B\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{1}(X)=\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
.

The proofs of these theorems are basically the same as those of
[
28
, Theorems 35.10 and 35.16]
, and as usual will thus be omitted. As in the classical setup, the reflection theorems admit a dual formulation and have a number of standard consequences which go through also in the generalized setting — we refer the interested reader to
[
28
, Section 35.C]
for more information on this.

Chapter 7
\lambda
-Perfect Set Property

The classical Perfect Set Property (briefly,
\mathrm{PSP}
) for a set
A\subseteq X
, where
X
is a Polish space, states that either
|A|\leq\omega
, or else there is an embedding of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}
into
A
. Since the Cantor space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}
is compact, in the latter case the range of the embedding is necessarily closed in
X
; moreover, to have such an embedding it is enough to construct a continuous injective function
f\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{2}\to A
.

In the generalized setting, all previous variants of the
\mathrm{PSP}
are not necessarily equivalent to each other (see e.g. the discussion in
[
3
, Section 5]
), so let us list the most relevant options in our setup.

Definition 7.0.1
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space.

(a)

A set
A\subseteq X
has the
\lambda
-Perfect Set Property
(briefly,
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
) if either
|A|\leq\lambda
, or
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
embeds into
A
as a closed-in-
X
set.

(b)

A set
A\subseteq X
has the
weak
\lambda
-Perfect Set Property
(briefly,
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{wPSP}
) if either
|A|\leq\lambda
, or there is continuous injection
f\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\to A
.

(c)

A set
A\subseteq X
has the
\lambda
-Borel
\lambda
-Perfect Set Property
(briefly,
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{BorPSP}
) if either
|A|\leq\lambda
, or there is
\lambda
-Borel injection
f\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\to A
.

We write
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(A)
to say that the set
A
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
, and similarly for
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{wPSP}
and
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{BorPSP}
. Analogously, if
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass we write
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
(respectively,
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{wPSP}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
or
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{BorPSP}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
) to say that all sets in
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
(respectively, the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{wPSP}
or the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{BorPSP})
, for every
\lambda
-Polish space
X
.

Since
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
is not
\lambda
-Polish if
2^{<\lambda}>\lambda
, it makes also sense to consider variants of the above properties where
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
is replaced with
B(\lambda)
or, equivalently, with
C(\lambda)
. When considering such variants, we will systematically add a
*
to the notation. For instance,
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}^{*}(A)
is the statement: Either
|A|\leq\lambda
, or else
B(\lambda)
(equivalently,
C(\lambda)
) embeds into
A
as a closed-in-
X
set.
(By Corollary
3.4.4
, if
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible then the second alternative is equivalent to requiring that
A
contains a
\lambda
-perfect subspace of the ambient
\lambda
-Polish space
X
.)
Clearly, such starred versions coincide with the original ones if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, as in that case
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\approx B(\lambda)
by Theorem
3.2.2
.

Trivially, if
X
is a
\lambda
-Polish space and
|X|\leq\lambda
then all its subsets have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
. When
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, using the axiom of choice
{\sf AC}
it is possible to build in any
\lambda
-Polish space of size greater than
\lambda
a subset without the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
. The proof is the same as the classical Bernstein’s proof (see
[
26
, Proposition 11.4(a)]
).

Proposition 7.0.2

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space such that
|X|>\lambda
. Then there exists
A\subseteq X
without the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
.

Sketch of the proof.

(See
[
26
, Proposition 11.4(a)]
for more details.)
There are
2^{\lambda}
-many
\lambda
-perfect subsets of
X
, and since by Corollary
3.4.4
in each one of those we can embed
B(\lambda)
, they all have cardinality
\lambda^{\omega}
. Since
\lambda^{\omega}=2^{\lambda}
because we assumed
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, we can follow the construction in
[
26
, Proposition 11.4(a)]
to build a set
A\subseteq X
that does not contain any
\lambda
-perfect set and has size
|A|=2^{\lambda}>\lambda
.
∎

7.1.
\lambda
-
\mathrm{PSP}
for
\lambda
-analytic sets

The proof of the next result is reminiscent of those of Theorem
3.4.3
and Theorem
3.4.5
, and is strictly related to
[
47
, Theorems 5 and 22]
(although the settings are slightly different and the arguments more involved). Notice also that Remark
3.4.10
shows that the cardinal assumption on
\lambda
cannot be removed: if
\lambda
is not
\omega
-inaccessible, then there are even closed subsets of
B(\lambda)
which do not have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}^{*}
(nor the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{wPSP}^{*}
).

Theorem 7.1.1
.

Let
\lambda
be
\omega
-inaccessible, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then every
\lambda
-analytic subset
A
of
X
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}^{*}
, and thus either
|A|\leq\lambda
or else
|A|=\lambda^{\omega}

In particular, if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
this applies to every
A\in\lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Bor}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
F\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
be a closed set such that
A=p(F)
,
and let
\mathcal{B}=\{U_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
be a basis for
X\times B(\lambda)
. Let
S=\{\alpha<\lambda\mid|p(U_{\alpha}\cap F)|\leq\lambda\}
and set
C=\bigcup_{\alpha\in S}U_{\alpha}
.
Set also
F^{\prime}=F\setminus C
and
A^{\prime}=p(F^{\prime})
. Notice that
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and
|A\setminus A^{\prime}|\leq\lambda
because

A\setminus A^{\prime}\subseteq p(C\cap F)=p\bigg(\bigcup_{\alpha\in S}(U_{\alpha}\cap F)\bigg)=\bigcup_{\alpha\in S}p(U_{\alpha}\cap S),

and that if
V\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
is open and
V\cap F^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, then
|p(V\cap F^{\prime})|\nleq\lambda
.

If
A^{\prime}=\emptyset
then
|A|=|A\setminus A^{\prime}|\leq\lambda
, and hence
A
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}^{*}
.
We can thus assume without loss of generality that
A^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and show that
C(\lambda)\approx B(\lambda)
embeds into
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
as a closed-in-
X
set. To this aim, fix compatible complete metrics
d,d^{\prime}\leq 1
on
X
and
F^{\prime}
,
respectively. (Here we use that
F^{\prime}
is
\lambda
-Polish because it is closed in
X\times B(\lambda)
.)

We will build
\lambda
-schemes
\{B_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}\}
and
\{B^{\prime}_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}\}
on
X
and
F^{\prime}
, respectively, together with
a family
\{r_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
of positive reals
such that for all
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
and distinct
\beta,\beta^{\prime}<\lambda
:

(1)

p(B^{\prime}_{s})\subseteq B_{s}
,
B^{\prime}_{\emptyset}=F^{\prime}
, and
B_{\emptyset}=X
;

(2)

\mathrm{cl}(B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})\subseteq B_{s}
and
\mathrm{cl}(B^{\prime}_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})\subseteq B^{\prime}_{s}
;

(3)

if
B^{\prime}_{s}\neq\emptyset
(so that also
B_{s}\neq\emptyset
by
(1)
), then
B^{\prime}_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
\beta<\lambda_{\operatorname{lh}(s)}
;

(4)

both
\mathrm{diam}(B_{s})
and
\mathrm{diam}(B^{\prime}_{s})
are at most
2^{-\operatorname{lh}(s)}
(where the two diameters refer to
d
and
d^{\prime}
, respectively);

(5)

B_{s}
is open in
X
and
B^{\prime}_{s}
is open in
F^{\prime}
;

(6)

B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}\cap B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta^{\prime}}=\emptyset
;

(7)

the distance between
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}
and
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta^{\prime}}
is at least
r_{s}
(if they are both nonempty).

Let
f\colon B(\lambda)\to X
and
f^{\prime}\colon B(\lambda)\to F^{\prime}
be the (partial) continuous functions induced by the
\lambda
-schemes
\{B_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}\}
and
\{B^{\prime}_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}\}
, respectively. By
(2)
and
(3)
we have that both
f
and
f^{\prime}
have domain
C(\lambda)
. By
(1)

we have
f=p\circ f^{\prime}
, hence in particular the range of
f
is contained in
p(F^{\prime})=A^{\prime}
. Conditions
(5)
and
(6)
ensure that
f
is an embedding, while
(7)
, together with
(2)
, ensures that its range is closed in
X
.

It remains to construct
B_{s}
,
B^{\prime}_{s}
, and
r_{s}
by recursion on
\mathrm{lh}(s)
.
Let
B_{\emptyset}=X
and
B^{\prime}_{\emptyset}=F^{\prime}
. Next assume that
B_{s}
and
B^{\prime}_{s}
have been defined so that the above conditions
(1)
–
(7)
are satisfied.
If
B^{\prime}_{s}=\emptyset
, it is enough to let
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}=B^{\prime}_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}=\emptyset
for all
\beta<\lambda
(and
r_{s}
be any real greater than
0
), hence let us consider the case
B^{\prime}_{s}\neq\emptyset
. For
\lambda_{\operatorname{lh}(s)}\leq\beta<\lambda
, let again
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}=B^{\prime}_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}=\emptyset
.
Next notice that
|p(B^{\prime}_{s})|\nleq\lambda
by definition of
F^{\prime}
, hence
\operatorname{dens}(p(B^{\prime}_{s}))=\lambda
by Lemma
2.4.4
applied to the metrizable space
p(B^{\prime}_{s})
.
Apply Lemma
2.4.1
to
p(B^{\prime}_{s})
with
\nu=\lambda_{\operatorname{lh}(s)}
, and enumerate the resulting
r
-spaced set (for some
r>0
) as
(x_{\beta})_{\beta<\operatorname{lh}(s)}
. Set
r_{s}=r/3
, and let
B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}
be an open ball centered in
x_{\beta}
with radius at most
\min\{2^{-(\operatorname{lh}(s)+2)},r_{s}\}
and small enough to ensure
\mathrm{cl}(B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})\subseteq B_{s}
. Now for each
\beta<\operatorname{lh}(s)
pick
y_{\beta}\in B(\lambda)
such that
(x_{\beta},y_{\beta})\in B^{\prime}_{s}
, and let
B^{\prime}_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta}
be an open ball (relatively to
F^{\prime}
) centered in
(x_{\beta},y_{\beta})
with radius at most
2^{-(\operatorname{lh}(s)+2)}
and such that
\mathrm{cl}(B^{\prime}_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})\subseteq B^{\prime}_{s}\cap p^{-1}(B_{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}\beta})
(This is possible because the latter is an open-in-
F^{\prime}
neighborhood of
(x_{\beta},y_{\beta})
.) This concludes the construction and the proof.
∎

Corollary 7.1.2
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. Then every
\lambda
-analytic subset
A
of
X
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
, and thus either
|A|\leq\lambda
or else
|A|=2^{\lambda}
.

In particular, the previous corollary applies to all
\lambda
-Borel subsets of
X
. Thus it improves Theorem
4.3.5
, which in the new terminology states that all
\lambda
-Borel subset of
X
satisfy an intermediate version of the property lying in between
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{wPSP}
and the full
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
(but still equivalent to them by Corollary
7.1.3
below).

A map
f\colon X\to Y
is called
\lambda
-to-
1
if
f^{-1}(y)
has size at most
\lambda
for every
y\in Y
.

Corollary 7.1.3
.

Let
\lambda
be
\omega
-inaccessible,
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space, and
A\subseteq X
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

B(\lambda)
embeds into
A
as a closed-in-
X
set;

(2)

there is a continuous injection from
B(\lambda)
into
A
;

(3)

there is a
\lambda
-to-
1
continuous function from
B(\lambda)
into
A
.

Moreover, if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
we can weaken “continuous” to “
\lambda
-Borel” in items
(2)
and
(3)
, and we can replace
B(\lambda)
with
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
in all occurrences.

Proof.

It is clear that each condition trivially implies the next one, so let
f\colon B(\lambda)\to A
be continuous and
\lambda
-to-
1
. The set
f(B(\lambda))\subseteq A
is
\lambda
-analytic, and since
|B(\lambda)|>\lambda
and
f
is
\lambda
-to-
1
, then
|f(B(\lambda))|\nleq\lambda
. Since
f(B(\lambda))
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}^{*}
by Theorem
7.1.1
, we have that
B(\lambda)
embeds into
f(B(\lambda))
(and therefore in
A
) as a closed-in-
X
set.

For the “moreover” part we can use the same argument, using also Proposition
5.1.2
and Theorem
3.2.2
when needed.
∎

Corollary
7.1.3
also shows that
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}^{*}
is equivalent to
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{wPSP}^{*}
when
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible. If moreover
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then all of
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}^{(*)}
,
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{wPSP}^{(*)}
, and
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{BorPSP}^{(*)}
are equivalent to each other, and no distinction among the variants of the
\lambda
-Perfect Set Property has to be made.

A further consequence of Corollary
7.1.3
is that the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
can be transferred from a single
\lambda
-Polish space of size greater than
\lambda
to all other
\lambda
-Polish spaces.

Corollary 7.1.4
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
be a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass closed under
\lambda
-Borel preimages. Suppose that there is some
\lambda
-Polish space
X
with
|X|>\lambda
such that
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(A)
for all
A\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
. Then
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
holds, that is, for every
\lambda
-Polish space
Y
and every
B\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(Y)
we have
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(B)
.

Proof.

If
|Y|\leq\lambda
there is nothing to prove, so assume
|Y|>\lambda
. Then by Theorem
5.3.10
there is a a
\lambda
-Borel isomorphism
f\colon X\to Y
. Let
B\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(Y)
. Then
A=f^{-1}(B)
is in
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
by the hypothesis on
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
, hence
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(A)
. If
|A|\leq\lambda
then
|B|\leq\lambda
as well because
f
is a bijection. If instead there is a continuous injection
g\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\to A
, then
f\circ g\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\to B
is a
\lambda
-Borel injection, hence we are done by Corollary
7.1.3
.
∎

7.2.
A
\lambda
-coanalytic sets without
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}

It is natural after Theorem
7.1.1
to ask whether
\lambda
-coanalytic sets need to have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
; or, more in general, if there is a
\lambda
-projective set without the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
, and how low it is in the
\lambda
-projective hierarchy. In the classical case, there is no conclusive answer, since there are models with two extremes: in the constructible universe
L
there is a coanalytic set without the
\mathrm{PSP}
(
[
26
, Theorem 13.12]
), while under

{\sf ZF}+{\sf AD}

all sets of reals have the
\mathrm{PSP}
(
[
26
, Theorem 27.9]
). We are going to prove now that a similar phenomenon arises also in the context of
\lambda
-Polish spaces (see Theorem
7.2.10
and Corollary
8.3.8
).

Suppose that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. We already observed in Section
5.1
that linear orders on
\lambda
can be coded by elements of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, and Proposition
5.1.5
shows that the set
\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
of codes of well-founded linear orders on
\lambda
is a complete
\lambda
-coanalytic set.
The same coding procedure can be applied to arbitrary binary relations on
\lambda
: every
E\subseteq\lambda\times\lambda
can be coded by
x_{E}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
setting
x_{E}(\alpha,\beta)=1\iff\alpha\mathrel{E}\beta
, and, conversely, every
x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
codes the binary relation
E_{x}=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\lambda\times\lambda\mid x(\alpha,\beta)=1\}
. In this section, structures with domain
\lambda
and a binary relation on it will be conceived as codes for
\lambda
-sized models of set theory, that is, structures of the form
(M,{\in})
for
M
a set of cardinality
\lambda
. For this reason, we will denote by
\in_{x}
(rather than
E_{x}
) the relation coded by
x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
. Also, the space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
will again be tacitly identified with
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
using the Gödel pairing function, and will thus be a
\lambda
-Polish space as soon as
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.

The following observation follows from
[
4
, Proposition 8.9(d)]
, once we recall that on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}\approx{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
the product topology is finer than the bounded topology.

Fact 7.2.1
.

If
\upvarphi(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})
is a first-order formula in the language of set theory
and
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in\lambda
, then the set

\{x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}\mid(\lambda,{\in_{x}})\models\upvarphi[\alpha_{1}/x_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}/x_{n}]\},

is a
\lambda
-Borel subset of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
.

Consider the following subsets of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\{x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}\mid{\in_{x}}\text{ is extensional}\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\{x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}\mid{\in_{x}}\text{ is well-founded}\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}_{\lambda}\cap\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}.

All three sets are cleary invariant under isomorphism, that is, if
x
belongs to one of the sets and
y\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
is such that
(\lambda,\in_{x})\cong(y,\in_{y})
, then
y
belongs to the same set as well.

Lemma 7.2.2
.

The set
\mathrm{E}_{\lambda}
is
\lambda
-Borel, while
\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}
and
\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
are complete
\lambda
-coanalytic sets.

Sketch of the proof.

An easy computation, involving Fact
7.2.1
when necessary, provides the upper bounds on the complexity of the three sets at hand. The completeness of
\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}
and
\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
for
\lambda
-coanalytic sets follows from the proof of Proposition
5.1.5
, once we notice that linear orders are necessarily extensional and
\mathrm{LO}_{\lambda}\cap\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}=\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
.
∎

Recall from Section
2.6
that every extensional, well-founded relation collapses to a unique isomorphic transitive model of set theory, its Mostowski collapse, via the Mostowski collapsing function. For
x\in\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
, we denote by
\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,\in_{x})
the Mostowski collapse of
(\lambda,{\in_{x}})
, and by
\pi_{x}
the corresponding collapsing function, which is an isomorphism between
(\lambda,{\in_{x}})
and
\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,\in_{x})
.
Conversely, every transitive model of set theory
(M,{\in})
with
|M|=\lambda
can be coded by some element of
\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
: just fix any bijection
\pi\colon\lambda\to M
, and define
x\in\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
by letting
x(\alpha,\beta)=1\iff\pi(\alpha)\in\pi(\beta)
. (Notice that for such an
x\in\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
, we have
\pi_{x}=\pi
.)
In this framework, the elements of
\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
must thus be conceived as codes for the transitive models of set theory of size
\lambda
. Of course, codes are not unique: there are
2^{\lambda}
-many codes (one for each bijection
\pi\colon\lambda\to M
) for any
(M,\in)
as above.

Remark 7.2.3
.

Note that the name “collapse”, in this case, is quite misleading: the “collapse” of
\alpha<\lambda
can be an ordinal greater than
\lambda
. For example, suppose that
x\in\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
and that
\alpha
is an ordinal in
(\lambda,\in_{x})
, i.e., that the set

\mathrm{Pred}_{x}(\alpha)=\{\beta<\lambda\mid\beta\in_{x}\alpha\}

of
\in_{x}
-predecessors of
\alpha
is linearly ordered. It is possible that

\mathrm{ot}(\mathrm{Pred}_{x}(\alpha),\in_{x})=\gamma>\lambda
, and therefore
\pi_{x}(\alpha)
will go up to
\gamma
. In fact,
\mathrm{Ord}^{\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,\in_{x})}
can be any ordinal between
\lambda
and
\lambda^{+}
. In a certain sense,
\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,\in_{x})
is curled inside
\lambda
, and the “collapse” unfurls it.

When
x\in\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
, we can recursively define the “
\in_{x}
-transitive closure”
\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha)
of any
\alpha\in\lambda
, but we will use the following equivalent definition that will make the future complexity analysis more transparent:

\displaystyle\beta\in\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha)\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\exists s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\,[

\displaystyle s(0)=\beta\wedge{s(\operatorname{lh}(s)-1)=\alpha}\wedge{}

\displaystyle\ \forall n<\operatorname{lh}(s)-1\,(s(n)\in_{x}s(n+1))].

Contrary to
\mathrm{Pred}_{x}(\alpha)
, it is possible that there is no element of
\lambda
that codes
\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha)
, i.e., that there is no
\beta<\lambda
such that
\forall\gamma<\lambda\,(\gamma\in_{x}\beta\iff\gamma\in\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha))
, as possibly
\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,\in_{x})
does not satisfy enough axioms of
{\sf ZFC}
for the transitive closure to live inside the model.
This would be an issue in the proof of Theorem
7.2.10
. Luckily, in the situation of interest we will have that
\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha)=\mathrm{Pred}_{x}(\alpha)
(see Remark
7.2.9
), and this will allow us to overcome the mentioned difficulty.

Recall from Section
2.6
that
H_{\lambda}
is the collection of all sets whose transitive closure has size smaller than
\lambda
, and that
|H_{\lambda}|=\lambda
under the assumption
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.

Lemma 7.2.4
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
b\in H_{\lambda}
and
\alpha<\lambda
. Then the set

P_{\alpha,b}=\{x\in\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}\mid\pi_{x}(\alpha)=b\}

is
\lambda
-analytic relatively to
\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Observe that
\pi_{x}(\alpha)=b
if and only if there is a function
f\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{(H_{\lambda})}
such that for every
\beta,\gamma<\lambda

(1)

f\restriction\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha)
is a bijection between
\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha)
and
\mathrm{trcl}(b)
;

(2)

if
\beta,\gamma\in\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha)
, then
\beta\in_{x}\gamma\iff f(\beta)\in f(\gamma)
;

(3)

if
\beta\notin\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha)
, then
f(\beta)=0
.

The three conditions above define a subset
C
of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{(H_{\lambda})}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
. Equip
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{(H_{\lambda})}
with the product of discrete topology on
H_{\lambda}
, so that it becomes a topological space homeomorphic to
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda},\tau_{p})
. By Example
5.4.5
, the space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{(H_{\lambda})}
is then standard
\lambda
-Borel, and
C
is easily seen to be
\lambda
-Borel in the product
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{(H_{\lambda})}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
. Since
P_{\alpha,b}=p(C)\cap\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
, the result easily follows.
∎

For technical reason, it would be desirable to have that the set
P_{\alpha,b}
from Lemma
7.2.4
is
\lambda
-Borel (relatively to the space of codes under consideration) rather then merely
\lambda
-analytic. This is what happens in the classical case
\lambda=\omega
, and the same would be true if
\lambda
were regular because we could then replace the function
f\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{(H_{\lambda})}
with a function in
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(H_{\lambda})}
(which is a set of cardinality
\lambda
, for regular cardinals
\lambda
satisfying
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
) doing essentially the same job. But in the singular case, this does not work for those
x\in\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
for which the set of
\in_{x}
-predecessors of
\alpha
is unbounded in
\lambda
, let alone with the fact that
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\lambda}{(H_{\lambda})}
would anyway be of cardinality larger than
\lambda
. To overcome these difficulties, we restrict the set of codes
\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
to a set of “good” codes
\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
.

Definition 7.2.5
.

We say that
x\in\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
has
bounded collapse
if for every
b\in H_{\lambda}
and
\alpha<\lambda
, if
\pi_{x}(\alpha)=b
then
\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha)
is bounded in
\lambda
.
We denote by
\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
the sets of all
x\in\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
that have bounded collapse.

Lemma 7.2.6
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.

(i)

\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
is a
\lambda
-coanalytic subset of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
.

(ii)

For every
b\in H_{\lambda}
and
\alpha<\lambda
, the set

P^{\prime}_{\alpha,b}=\{x\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}\mid\pi_{x}(\alpha)=b\}

is
\lambda
-Borel relatively to
\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
, and therefore it is
\lambda
-coanalytic in
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
.

Proof.

(i)

The definition of
\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
is:

x\in\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}\wedge\forall\alpha<\lambda\,\forall b\in H_{\lambda}\,[x\notin P_{\alpha,b}\vee\exists\gamma<\lambda\,\forall\delta<\lambda\,(\delta\in\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha)\Rightarrow\delta<\gamma)].

By Lemma
7.2.4
, the formula
x\notin P_{\alpha,b}
defines a
\lambda
-coanalytic set, while the part on the right of the disjunction defines a
\lambda
-Borel set. Therefore
\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
is the intersection of the
\lambda
-coanalytic set
\mathrm{EW}_{\lambda}
(Lemma
7.2.2
), and the intersection of
\lambda
-many
\lambda
-coanalytic sets, thus it is
\lambda
-coanalytic itself by Corollary
5.2.2
.

(ii)

Suppose that
x\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
. If
\pi_{x}(\alpha)=b
, then this can be witnessed by a function
f\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\delta}{(H_{\kappa})}
, for
\delta=\sup(\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\alpha))<\lambda
and
\kappa<\lambda
such that
b\in H_{\kappa}
, satisfying conditions
(1)
–
(3)
from the proof of Lemma
7.2.4
for all
\beta,\gamma<\delta
. Therefore the quantifier “
\exists f\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{(H_{\lambda})}
” in the proof of Lemma
7.2.4
can now be turned into “
\exists\delta<\lambda\,\exists\kappa<\lambda\,\exists f\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\delta}{(H_{\kappa})}
”, which easily corresponds to a union of size
\lambda
because, having assumed
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, we have that
|{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\delta}{(H_{\kappa})}|=|{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\delta}{(2^{<\kappa})}|<\lambda
. We thus conclude that
P^{\prime}_{\alpha,b}
is
\lambda
-Borel relatively to
\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
.
∎

Notice that the set
\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
is no longer closed under isomorphism: this is not an issue as long as we can still code all transitive models of set theory of size
\lambda
.

Lemma 7.2.7
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.
For any transitive model of set theory
(M,\in)
such that
|M|=\lambda
, there is
x\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
such that
\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,\in_{x})=(M,\in)
.

Proof.

By the discussion before Remark
7.2.3
, it is enough to find a bijection
\pi\colon\lambda\to M
such that for every
b\in M\cap H_{\lambda}
, the set
\{\pi^{-1}(c)\mid c\in\mathrm{trcl}(b)\}
is bounded in
\lambda
. For ease of exposition, we will in fact define the inverse of
\pi
, which will be denoted by
\varrho
.

First of all, note that
|M\cap H_{\lambda}|=\lambda
. If
M\subseteq H_{\lambda}
this is obvious. Otherwise, there is an
a\in M
such that
|\mathrm{trcl}(a)|=\lambda
. By the Axiom of Foundation, there must be an
a^{\prime}\in M
such that
|\mathrm{trcl}(a^{\prime})|=\lambda
, but for all
b\in a^{\prime}
, and therefore all
b\in\mathrm{trcl}(a^{\prime})
,
b\in H_{\lambda}
. By transitivity of
M
,
\mathrm{trcl}(a^{\prime})\subseteq M
, so
|M\cap H_{\lambda}|\geq|\mathrm{trcl}(a^{\prime})|=\lambda
again.
Moreover, we can suppose that also
|M\setminus H_{\lambda}|
has cardinality
\lambda
(otherwise the proof is even easier, and is left to the reader).

Since the sequence
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda_{i})_{i\in\omega}
is cofinal in
\lambda
, we have that
H_{\lambda}=\bigcup_{i\in\omega}H_{\lambda_{i}}
and
|H_{\lambda_{i}}|=2^{<\lambda_{i}}<\lambda
. (In particular,
M\cap(H_{\lambda_{i+1}}\setminus H_{\lambda_{i}})\neq\emptyset
because of the cardinality of
M\cap H_{\lambda}
.) We can thus find a bijection
\varrho_{1}
between
M\cap H_{\lambda}
and the set
A=\{\alpha<\lambda\mid\alpha\text{ is even}\}
such that for every
i\in\omega
, if
a\in M\cap H_{\lambda_{i}}
and
b\in M\cap(H_{\lambda}\setminus H_{\lambda_{i}})
then
\varrho_{1}(a)<\varrho_{1}(b)
. Then we let
\varrho_{2}
any bijection between
M\setminus H_{\lambda}
and
\lambda\setminus A
, and finally we let
\pi=\varrho^{-1}
for
\varrho=\varrho_{1}\cup\varrho_{2}
.

Given any
b\in M\cap H_{\lambda}
, there exists an
i\in\omega
such that
b\in H_{\lambda_{i}}
, so that also
c\in H_{\lambda_{i}}
for every
c\in\mathrm{trcl}(b)
. By choice of
\varrho_{1}
, the set
\{\pi^{-1}(c)\mid c\in b\}
is then bounded in
\lambda
by any
\varrho_{1}(d)
with
d\in M\cap(H_{\lambda_{i+1}}\setminus H_{\lambda_{i}})
.
∎

Before moving to the main result of the section, we need to compute the complexity of a few other sets. For
x,y\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\ \times\lambda}{2}
, we write
x\cong y
to abbreviate
(\lambda,\in_{x})\cong(\lambda,\in_{y})
.

Lemma 7.2.8
.

(i)

The isomorphism relation
\cong

is
\lambda
-analytic as a subset of
({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2})^{2}
.

(ii)

For every
\alpha<\lambda
, the set
\{(x,y)\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}\mid\pi_{x}(\alpha)=y\}
is
\lambda
-Borel relatively to
\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
.

(iii)

The set
\{(x,y)\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}\mid y\in\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,\in_{x})\}
is
\lambda
-Borel relatively to
\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
.

Proof.

(i)

This follows from the fact that
x\cong y
if and only if there is a bijective map
f\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
such that
\forall\alpha,\beta<\lambda\,(\alpha\in_{x}\beta\iff f(x)\in_{y}f(y))
, together with the fact that
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}
is a standard
\lambda
-Borel space when equipped with the
\lambda
-Borel structure induced by the product topology (Example
5.4.5
).

(ii)

Here we cannot directly use Lemma
7.2.4
because
y\notin H_{\lambda}
.
However,

\pi_{x}(\alpha)=y
if and only if

\forall\delta<\lambda\,[\delta\in_{x}\alpha\Rightarrow\exists\beta,\gamma<\lambda\,\exists i\in\{0,1\}\,(y(\beta,\gamma)=i\wedge\pi_{x}(\delta)=((\beta,\gamma),i))]\\
