\wedge\forall\beta,\gamma<\lambda\,\forall i\in\{0,1\}\,[y(\beta,\gamma)=i\Rightarrow\exists\delta<\lambda\,(\delta\in_{x}\alpha\wedge\pi_{x}(\delta)=((\beta,\gamma),i))].

Since each
((\beta,\gamma),i)
belongs to
H_{\lambda}
, the sets
P^{\prime}_{\delta,((\beta,\gamma),i)}
are
\lambda
-Borel relatively to
\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
by Lemma
7.2.6
(ii)
, and thus an easy computation along the previous formula yields the desired result.

(iii)

Use part
(ii)
and the fact that for
y\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
, we have

y\in\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,\in_{x})\iff\exists\alpha<\lambda\,(\pi_{x}(\alpha)=y).\qed

Recall from Proposition
2.7.2
the sentence
\upsigma_{0}
and the formula
\upvarphi_{0}(u,v)
. The following observation will be used in the proof of Theorem
7.2.10
.

Remark 7.2.9
.

Let
\lambda\leq\delta<\lambda^{+}
, and let
x\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
be such that
\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,{\in_{x}})=L_{\delta}
. (Such an
x
exists by Lemma
7.2.7
and the fact that
|L_{\delta}|=|\delta|=\lambda
.)
Let
\gamma<\delta
be a limit ordinal.
Since
L_{\gamma}\in L_{\delta}
, there is
\beta<\lambda
such that
\pi_{x}(\beta)=L_{\gamma}
.
It follows that
\pi_{x}
maps
\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\beta)
onto
L_{\gamma}
, and
since
L_{\gamma}
is transitive and
\pi_{x}
is an isomorphism, we get that
\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\beta)=\mathrm{Pred}_{x}(\beta)
.
Denote by
(\lambda,{\in_{x}})\restriction\beta
the substructure of
(\lambda,\in_{x})
with domain
\mathrm{Pred}_{x}(\beta)
.
By the previous discussion,
(\lambda,\in_{x})\restriction\beta
is isomorphic to
L_{\gamma}
via
\pi_{x}
, so that in particular
(\lambda,\in_{x})\restriction\beta\models\upsigma_{0}
.
Conversely, if
\beta<\lambda
is such that
(\lambda,{\in_{x}})\restriction\beta\models\upsigma_{0}
, where
(\lambda,{\in_{x}})\restriction\beta
is as above, then by choice of
\upsigma_{0}

the image of
\mathrm{Pred}_{x}(\beta)
under
\pi_{x}
is
L_{\gamma}
, for some limit ordinal
\gamma
. Since
L_{\gamma}
is transitive and
\pi_{x}
is an isomorphism, this implies that
\mathrm{Pred}_{x}(\beta)=\mathrm{trcl}_{x}(\beta)
, and thus
\pi_{x}(\beta)=L_{\gamma}
. Therefore
\gamma<\delta
because
\pi_{x}(\beta)\in\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,{\in_{x}})=L_{\delta}
.
Summing up: for a given
\beta<\lambda
, we have that
\pi_{x}(\beta)=L_{\gamma}
for some limit ordinal
\gamma<\delta
if and only if
(\lambda,{\in_{x}})\restriction\beta\models\upsigma_{0}
.

Notice also that for every
\beta<\lambda
, the condition “
(\lambda,\in_{x})\restriction\beta\models\upsigma_{0}
” is
\lambda
-Borel. Indeed, let
\upsigma^{\prime}_{0}(w)
be obtained by bounding every quantifier in
\upsigma_{0}
with a fresh variable
w
, that is, every quantification of the form “
\exists v
” appearing in
\upsigma_{0}
is replaced by “
\exists v\in w
”, and similarly for the universal quantifications. Then for every
x\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
we have
(\lambda,{\in_{x}})\restriction\beta\models\upsigma_{0}\iff(\lambda,{\in_{x}})\models\upsigma^{\prime}_{0}[\beta/w]
, and the latter is a
\lambda
-Borel condition by Fact
7.2.1
.

We can now prove that, under some conditions, there exists a
\lambda
-coanalytic set without the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
.

Theorem 7.2.10
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
and
(\lambda^{+})^{L}=\lambda^{+}
. Then there is a
\lambda
-coanalytic set
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
without the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
.

The idea behind the proof of Theorem
7.2.10
, an adaptation of
[
26
, Theorem 13.12]
, is the following.
For every ordinal
\lambda\leq\alpha<\lambda^{+}
, let
1
1

1

Here we use the fact that we can find a code for
\alpha
inside
L
because we assumed
(\lambda^{+})^{L}=\lambda^{+}
.

x^{(\alpha)}\in\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}\cap L
be
<_{L}
-least
such that
\mathrm{ot}(\preceq_{x^{(\alpha)}})=\alpha
, and let

A=\{x^{(\alpha)}\mid\lambda\leq\alpha<\lambda^{+}\}.

The set
A
is designed to be a copy of
\{\alpha\in\mathrm{Ord}\mid\lambda\leq\alpha<\lambda^{+}\}
inside
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
, in the sense that the map
\alpha\mapsto x^{(\alpha)}
is a (canonical) bijection between the two sets.
Thus
|A|\nleq\lambda
and, if
A
were
\lambda
-coanalytic, then by an easy argument involving the Boundedness Theorem
6.2.9
the space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
could not be embedded into
A
. Unfortunately, the
<_{L}
-minimality of
x^{(\alpha)}
in its definition cannot be rendered in a
\lambda
-coanalytic way.
The best we can do is to observe that this can be checked by looking at a model of the form
L_{\delta}
, for some limit
\lambda\leq\delta<\lambda^{+}
. Indeed,
x^{(\alpha)}
can be identified with a subset of
\lambda
, hence there is some limit ordinal
\lambda\leq\delta<\lambda^{+}
such that
x^{(\alpha)}\in L_{\delta}
(see e.g. the proof of
[
25
, Theorem 13.20]
). By Proposition
2.7.2
, for every
y
we have

y<_{L}x^{(\alpha)}\iff y\in L_{\delta}\wedge(L_{\delta},\in)\models\upvarphi_{0}[y/u,x^{(\alpha)}/v].

Thus since all codes for
\alpha
are obviously “isomorphic” to each other,
x^{(\alpha)}
is the
<_{L}
-minimal code for
\alpha
if and only if there is
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
such that

(7.1)

z\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}\wedge(\lambda,{\in_{z}})\models\upsigma_{0}\wedge\exists\beta<\lambda\,(\pi_{z}(\beta)=x^{(\alpha)}\wedge\\
\forall\gamma<\lambda\,\forall y\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}\,((\lambda,{\in_{z}})\models\upvarphi_{0}[\gamma/u,\beta/v]\wedge\pi_{z}(\gamma)=y\Rightarrow y\not\cong x^{(\alpha)}).

Let us call any
z
as in (
7.1
) a witness for the
<_{L}
-minimality of
x^{(\alpha)}
.
Using the results discussed in this section, one can easily see that the definition in (
7.1
) is
\lambda
-coanalytic, and thus we end up with a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}
-condition because of the existential quantification on
z
preceding it. (Indeed, if we replace
x^{(\alpha)}
with
x
in (
7.1
) and add the requirement that
x\in\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
, we get a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}
-definition of
A
itself.)

To get rid of this extra quantification, one can make the choice of
z
canonical by first minimizing the ordinal
\delta
(that is, we only consider the smallest limit ordinal
\lambda\leq\delta_{1}<\lambda^{+}
for which
x^{(\alpha)}\in L_{\delta_{1}}
), and then taking the
<_{L}
-minimal
z^{(\alpha)}_{1}\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
such that
\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,{\in_{z^{(\alpha)}_{1}}})=L_{\delta_{1}}
.
But now we have only shifted our problem: how can we certify that
z_{1}
is the
<_{L}
-least with the required properties? Repeating the above argument, we may look at witnesses
z\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
coding some large enough
L_{\delta}
for which
z^{(\alpha)}_{1}\in L_{\delta}
. Letting
\lambda\leq\delta_{2}<\lambda^{+}
be the smallest such
\delta
, and
z^{(\alpha)}_{2}
be the
<_{L}
-least
z\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}
such that
\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,{\in_{z}})=L_{\delta_{2}}
, we get a canonical witness for the
<_{L}
-minimality of
z^{(\alpha)}_{1}
, and we have moved one step further.

Iterating the process and setting
z^{(\alpha)}_{0}=x^{(\alpha)}
, we end up with a sequence
(z^{(\alpha)}_{n})_{n\in\omega}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2})}
such that
z^{(\alpha)}_{0}
is the
<_{L}
-least code for
\alpha
, and each
z^{(\alpha)}_{n+1}
is the “canonical witness” for the
<_{L}
-minimality of
z^{(\alpha)}_{n}
.
Redefining
A
as

A=\{(z^{(\alpha)}_{n})_{n\in\omega}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2})}\mid\lambda\leq\alpha<\lambda^{+}\},

we finally get a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
-set such that for each
\lambda\leq\alpha<\lambda^{+}
there is exactly one element of
A
representing
\alpha
(in the sense that its first coordinate codes precisely
\alpha
), and thus
A
cannot have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
by the initial argument applied to this new
A
.

Proof of Theorem
7.2.10
.

By Corollary
7.1.4
, we can clearly work with the
\lambda
-Polish space
Z={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2})}
instead of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, as they are
\lambda
-Borel isomorphic by Theorem
5.3.10
.

Let
A\subseteq Z
be the set of all
(z_{n})_{n\in\omega}
such that for every
n\in\omega
:

(1)

z_{0}\in\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
;

(2)

z_{n+1}\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}\wedge(\lambda,{\in_{z_{n+1}}})\models\upsigma_{0}
;

(3)

z_{n}\in\mathrm{tr}(\lambda,{\in_{z_{n+1}}})
;

(4)

\forall\beta<\lambda\,[(\lambda,{\in_{z_{n+1}}})\restriction\beta\models\upsigma_{0}\Rightarrow\neg\exists\gamma<\lambda\,(\gamma\in_{z_{n+1}}\beta\wedge\pi_{z_{n+1}}(\gamma)=z_{n})]
;

(5)

for every
y\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
, if

\exists\beta,\gamma<\lambda\,(\pi_{z_{n+1}}(\beta)=y\wedge\pi_{z_{n+1}}(\gamma)=z_{n}\wedge(\lambda,{\in_{z_{n+1}}})\models\upvarphi_{0}[\beta/u,\gamma/v]),

then
y\not\cong z_{n}
.

Condition
(1)
says that that
z_{0}
codes some ordinal
\omega\leq\alpha<\lambda^{+}
, and is
\lambda
-coanalytic by Proposition
5.1.5
.

Condition
(2)
says that each
z_{n+1}
has bounded collapse (so that
(z_{n})_{n\in\omega}\in\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda})}
) and codes
L_{\delta_{n+1}}
(necessarily for some
\lambda\leq\delta_{n+1}<\lambda^{+}
, since
(\lambda,\in_{z_{n+1}})
is a structure of size
\lambda
),
and is
\lambda
-coanalytic by Lemma
7.2.6
(i)
and Fact
7.2.1
.

Condition
(3)
says that
z_{n}\in L_{\delta_{n+1}}
, and is
\lambda
-Borel relatively to
\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda})}
by Lemma
7.2.8
(iii)
.

Condition
(4)
says that
\delta_{n+1}
is the smallest ordinal
\lambda\leq\delta<\lambda^{+}
with
z_{n}\in L_{\delta}
, and is
\lambda
-Borel relatively to
\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda})}
by Remark
7.2.9
and Lemma
7.2.8
(ii)
.

Finally, Condition
(5)
says that
z_{n}
is the
<_{L}
-least code for
\alpha
(if
n=0
) or for
L_{\delta_{n}}
(if
n>1
), and is
\lambda
-coanalytic by Fact
7.2.1
and parts
(i)
and
(ii)
of Lemma
7.2.8
.

This shows that
A
is
\lambda
-coanalytic. Moreover, for every ordinal
\lambda\leq\alpha<\lambda^{+}
there is exactly one
(z_{n})_{n\in\omega}
such that
\mathrm{ot}(\preceq_{z_{0}})=\alpha
. The existence part follows from the discussion before this proof. Uniqueness follows from the fact that if
(z_{n})_{n\in\omega},(z^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\omega}\in A
, then
z_{n}=z^{\prime}_{n}
implies
z_{n+1}=z^{\prime}_{n+1}
for every
n\in\omega
by definition of
A
. Since if
\mathrm{ot}(\preceq_{z_{0}})=\mathrm{ot}(\preceq_{z^{\prime}_{0}})=\alpha
then
z_{0}=z^{\prime}_{0}
by definition of
A
again, the result easily follows.

Finally, we show that
A
does not have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
. First of all,
|A|\nleq\lambda
because the map
g
sending each
\lambda\leq\alpha<\lambda^{+}
to the unique
(z_{n})_{n\in\omega}\in A
with
\mathrm{ot}(\preceq_{z_{0}})=\alpha
is a well-defined bijection between
\{\alpha\in\mathrm{Ord}\mid\lambda\leq\alpha<\lambda^{+}\}
and
A
. (No choice is needed here, as there is only one bijection with the described property.)
Suppose towards a contradiction that there is an embedding
f\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}\to A
, and let

B=\{z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}\mid z=z_{0}\text{ for some }(z_{n})_{n\in\omega}\in\operatorname{rng}(f)\}.

Being the range of the composition of
f
with the projection from
Z
onto its first coordinate,
B\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2})
. Moreover,
B\subseteq\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
by construction. Recall from (the proof of) Theorem
6.2.4
that the map
\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}\to\lambda^{+}
sending each
x\in\mathrm{WO}_{\lambda}
to its order type
\mathrm{ot}(\preceq_{x})
is a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
-rank, and therefore

C=\{\mathrm{ot}(\preceq_{z})\mid z\in B\}

is bounded in
\lambda^{+}
by Theorem
6.2.9
, and therefore
|C|\leq\lambda
. But by construction,
C
is the range of the injective map
g^{-1}\circ f
, and hence we should have
|C|\geq|{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}|>\lambda
, a contradiction.
∎

The hypothesis
(\lambda^{+})^{L}=\lambda^{+}
in Theorem
7.2.10
is not trivial, as it is an anti-large cardinal hypothesis, i.e., a hypothesis that is inconsistent with large enough cardinals. In fact, whether
(\lambda^{+})^{L}=\lambda^{+}
or not depends on the large cardinal object
0^{\sharp}
: if
0^{\sharp}
exists, then by
[
26
, Theorem 9.17(c)]
any uncountable cardinal
\kappa
is inaccessible in
L
, and therefore
(\kappa^{+})^{L}\leq(2^{\kappa})^{L}<\kappa^{+}
. On the other hand, if
0^{\sharp}
does not exist, then by Jensen’s Covering Theorem
(\lambda^{+})^{L}=\lambda^{+}
holds for all singular cardinals
\lambda
(see
[
25
, Corollary 18.32]
).

Passing to the contrapositive, if
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1})
holds for some cardinal
\lambda
satisfying
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda>{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda)=\omega
, then
(\lambda^{+})^{L}\neq\lambda^{+}
, and hence
0^{\sharp}
exists. We can derive two remarks from this.

On the one hand, whether
0^{\sharp}
exists or not is independent of the chosen cardinal
\lambda
,
hence we can have the existence of
\lambda
-coanalytic sets without the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
for all strong limit cardinals, simultaneously.

Corollary 7.2.11
.

Suppose
0^{\sharp}
does not exist. Then for all strong limit cardinals
\lambda>\omega
of countable cofinality there
A\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2})
without the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
.

The second remark is a negative result. Since
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1})
implies a large cardinal assumption, it is not provable in
{\sf ZFC}
. This means that if we look for pointclasses
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
in the projective hierarchy such that
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
, the best result that we can obtain in
{\sf ZFC}
alone is
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}
, and to have more we need large cardinals.

We further notice that it is easy to relativize the previous argument to
L[a]
for any
a\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, so that if
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1})
, then
a^{\sharp}
exists for all
a\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
.

We end this section by noticing that, as in the classical case, in
L
there is a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}
-well-ordering of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
. This can be proved using the same methods developed for the proof of Theorem
7.2.10
.

Proposition 7.2.12

(
\mathsf{V=L}
)
.

For every limit ordinal
\lambda
with countable cofinality and every
\lambda
-Polish space
X
, there is a
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}
well-ordering of
X
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.3.10
, without loss of generality we may assume
X={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
. Then for every
x,y\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
we have that
x<_{L}y
if and only if

\exists z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}\,[z\in\mathrm{BC}_{\lambda}\wedge(\lambda,{\in_{z}})\models\upsigma_{0}\wedge\exists\alpha,\beta<\lambda\,(\pi_{z}(\alpha)=x\\
\wedge\pi_{z}(\beta)=y\wedge(\lambda,{\in_{z}})\models\upvarphi_{0}[\alpha/u,\beta/v].

Using Fact
7.2.1
and Lemma
7.2.8
(after having identified
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda\times\lambda}{2}
and
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
), one easily sees that the above condition is
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}
, hence we are done.
∎

7.3.
Games for the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}

There is a game-theoretic formulation of
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
similar
2
2

2

Indeed, when
\lambda=\omega
our game
\omega
-
G^{*}(A)
is game-theoretically equivalent to the game
G^{*}(A)
from
[
28
, Section 21.A]
: each of the players has a winning strategy in one of the games if and only if (s)he has a winning strategy in the other one.

to the
*
-game
G^{*}(A)
game in
[
28
, Section 21.A]
. Recall that we fixed a strictly increasing sequence
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda_{k})_{k\in\omega}
of cardinals cofinal in
\lambda
, and that without loss of generality we can assume that each
\lambda_{k}
is regular whenever
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
.

Definition 7.3.1
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space and fix a base
\mathcal{B}=\{U_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
for it with
U_{\alpha}\neq\emptyset
for all
\alpha<\lambda
.
Given
A\subseteq X
, consider the following infinite two-players zero-sum perfect information game
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(\alpha^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}}&(\alpha^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}}&\dotsc&(\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}&\dotsc\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}&\dotsc&i_{k}&\dotsc\end{array}

The game is played with
\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{II}
moving alternatively, with
\mathbf{I}
moving first. At each round
k\in\omega
:

(a)

\mathbf{I}
chooses a sequence
(\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
of ordinals smaller than
\lambda
such that

•

(\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
is bounded in
\lambda
, i.e. there is
\gamma<\lambda
such that
\alpha^{k}_{i}\leq\gamma
for all
i<\lambda_{k}
;

•

U_{\alpha^{k}_{i}}\cap U_{\alpha^{k}_{j}}=\emptyset
if
i,j<\lambda_{k}
are distinct;

•

\mathrm{diam}(U^{k}_{i})\leq 2^{-k}
for all
i<\lambda_{k}
, where the diameter is computed with respect to any compatible complete metric on
X
fixed in advance;

•

\mathrm{cl}(U_{\alpha^{k}_{i}})\subseteq U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
for all
i<\lambda_{k}
, where if
k=0
we set
U_{\alpha^{-1}_{i_{-1}}}=X
.

(b)

After
\mathbf{I}
’s move,
\mathbf{II}
replies by choosing some
i_{k}<\lambda_{k}
.

The game ends after
\omega
-many rounds, or when player
\mathbf{I}
is not able to make his/her next move without violating the rules above. In the latter case
\mathbf{II}
immediately wins. In the former case,
\bigcap_{k\in\omega}U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}=\{x\}
for some
x\in X
, and
\mathbf{I}
wins if and only if
x\in A
.

The interested reader can easily verify that
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
can be formalized as a Gale-Stewart game with rules (in the sense discussed at the end of Section
4.5
).

Remark 7.3.2
.

If
\lambda
is
\omega
-inaccessible and the space
X
is
\lambda
-perfect, then
no play of the game
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
will terminate at a finite stage because

\mathbf{I}
will always be able to make the next move. To see this, use the fact that in such a case each nonempty open subset of
X
has density character
\lambda
, and this guarantees that
\mathbf{I}
can make one more legal move thanks to Lemma
2.4.1
(see the proof of Theorem
7.3.3
(i)
for more details).

Theorem 7.3.3
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
,
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space, and
A\subseteq X
.

(i)

\mathbf{I}
has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
if and only if there is a continuous injection from
C(\lambda)
into
A
.

(ii)

\mathbf{II}
has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
if and only if
A
is well-orderable and
|A|\leq\lambda
.

Thus the game
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
is determined if and only if
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(A)
.

Proof.

The additional part follows from

C(\lambda)\approx B(\lambda)
and Corollary
7.1.3
.

(i)

Suppose that
\mathbf{I}
has a winning strategy
\sigma
in
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
. For
x\in C(\lambda)
, consider the run of the game where
\mathbf{I}
follows
\sigma
and
\mathbf{II}
plays
x(k)
at each round
k\in\omega
, and let
f(x)
be the unique element in the resulting set
\bigcap_{k\in\omega}U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}
. It is not hard to see that
f\colon C(\lambda)\to A
is continuous and injective.

Conversely, assume that there is a continuous injection from
C(\lambda)
into
A
. By Corollary
7.1.3
, there is a closed-in-
X
set
C\subseteq A
which is homeomorphic to
B(\lambda)
. We will describe a legal strategy
\sigma
for
\mathbf{I}
such that
\mathbf{I}
can always make the next move and with the additional property that
U_{\alpha^{k}_{i}}\cap C\neq\emptyset
for all
k\in\omega
and
i<\lambda_{k}
. Such a strategy
\sigma
will then be winning for
\mathbf{I}
because by construction the unique point in
\bigcap_{k\in\omega}U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}
will be the limit of points
x_{k}\in U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}\cap C
, and thus will be in
C\subseteq A
because
C
is assumed to be closed. To define
\sigma
, for each
k\in\omega
and
\alpha<\lambda
such that
U_{\alpha}\cap C\neq\emptyset
fix an
r_{\alpha,k}
-spaced set
C_{\alpha,k}\subseteq U_{\alpha}\cap C
with
|C_{\alpha,k}|=\lambda_{k}
, for a suitable positive real
r_{\alpha,k}
. (The existence of such a set is granted by Lemma
2.4.1
applied with
\nu=\lambda_{k}
and the fact that
U_{\alpha}\cap C
is a metrizable space of density character
\lambda
because it is a relatively open subspace of a homeomorphic copy of
B(\lambda)
.) Suppose that we are at turn
k\in\omega
, so that
\mathbf{II}
just selected the ordinal
i_{k-1}<\lambda_{k-1}
, and that by inductive hypothesis
U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}\cap C\neq\emptyset
. Let
\{x_{i}\mid i<\lambda_{k}\}
be an enumeration without repetitions of
C_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}},k}
and for each
i<\lambda_{k}
let
\beta_{i}<\lambda
be such that
x_{i}\in U_{\beta_{i}}
(so that
U_{\beta_{i}}\cap C\neq\emptyset
),
\mathrm{diam}(U_{\beta_{i}})<\min\left\{2^{-k},\frac{1}{3}r_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}},k}\right\}
, and
\mathrm{cl}(U_{\beta_{i}})\subseteq U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
. (This is possible because
X
is metrizable.) The sequence
(\beta_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
might be unbounded in
\lambda
, however, since
\lambda_{k}
is regular the set
B_{n}=\{\beta_{i}\mid i<\lambda_{k}\wedge\beta_{i}<\lambda_{n}\}
must be of size
\lambda_{k}
for at least one
n\in\omega
. Letting
(\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
be an enumeration without repetitions of such a
B_{n}
, we clearly get a legal move for
\mathbf{I}
which moreover satisfies
U_{\alpha^{k}_{i}}\cap C\neq\emptyset
for all
i<\lambda_{k}
, as required.

(ii)

Suppose that
\mathbf{II}
has a winning strategy
\tau
in
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
. Let
P
be the set of all legal positions in
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
in which
\mathbf{II}
followed
\tau
and
\mathbf{I}
has to move next. Formally,
P
is a collection of finite sequences of even length over the set

\bigcup_{k\in\omega}\bigg(\lambda_{k}\cup\bigcup_{n\in\omega}{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda_{k}}{(\lambda_{n})}\bigg).

Notice that since we assumed
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, such a set is well-orderable and has size
\lambda
, so that
|P|\leq\lambda
as well. Let
p\in P
be of length
2k
for some
k\in\omega
. A point
x\in X
is called
bad for
p
if
3
3

3

Recall that if
k=0
we set
U_{\alpha^{-1}_{i_{-1}}}=X
.

x\in U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
but for all
(\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
, if it is a legal move for
\mathbf{I}
in the next turn, then the reply
i_{k}
of
\mathbf{II}
following
\tau
against such move of
\mathbf{I}
is such that
x\notin U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}
. Let also

A_{p}=\{x\in X\mid x\text{ is bad for }p\}.

(Notice that, in particular,
A_{p}\subseteq U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
, and that
A_{p}=U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
if
\mathbf{I}
cannot make any further legal move beyond
p
.)

Claim 7.3.3.1
.

For every
p\in P
of length
2k
the set
A_{p}
is well-orderable and

|A_{p}|\leq(\lambda_{k})^{\omega},

and thus in particular
|A_{p}|<\lambda
.

Proof of the claim.

If not, then
\operatorname{dens}(A_{p})>\lambda_{k}
by Fact
2.4.3
and the fact that weight and density character coincide on the metrizable space
A_{p}
. By Lemma
2.4.1
applied with
\nu=\lambda_{k}
, there is some
r
-spaced set
B=\{x_{i}\mid i<\lambda_{k}\}\subseteq A_{p}\subseteq U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
(for some
r>0
) such that
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
for all distinct
i,j<\lambda_{k}
. Arguing as in the backward direction of part
(i)
above, from the points
x_{i}
we can then obtain a legal move
(\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
for
\mathbf{I}
such that
U_{\alpha^{k}_{i}}\cap B\neq\emptyset
for all
i<\lambda_{k}
. Let
i_{k}
be
\mathbf{II}
’s reply to such move, and consider any point
x\in U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}\cap B
: then
x
is not bad for
p
, contradicting
x\in B\subseteq A_{p}
.
∎

On the other hand, it easy to see that for every
x\in A
there is
p\in P
such that
x\in A_{p}
. Otherwise, one could recursively construct
4
4

4

Notice that no choice beyond the one granted by
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
is needed here.

a run
p=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}p_{k}
in
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
where each
p_{k}\in P
has length
2k
and is such that
x
belongs to the last basic open set
U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
selected by
\mathbf{II}
in the partial run
p_{k}
. Indeed, since
x
is not bad for
p_{k-1}
, player
\mathbf{I}
has a legal move
(\alpha^{k-1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k-1}}
such that if
i_{k-1}
is the reply by
\mathbf{II}
following
\tau
, then we still have
x\in U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
. This contradicts the fact that
\tau
is winning for
\mathbf{II}
because the run
p=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}p_{k}
does not stop at a finite stage,
\bigcap_{k\in\omega}U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}=\{x\}
, but
x\in A
. Thus
A\subseteq\bigcup_{p\in P}A_{p}
, and hence
|A|\leq\lambda
by
|P|\leq\lambda
and Claim
7.3.3.1
.

Conversely, suppose that
|A|\leq\lambda
, and enumerate it as
(x_{i})_{i<\lambda}
(possibly with repetitions). We define a strategy for
\mathbf{II}
as follows. In the first turn, (s)he plays any ordinal smaller than
\lambda_{0}
, e.g.
i_{0}=0
. Suppose now that at round
k\geq 1
player
\mathbf{I}
played
(\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
. Since the open sets
U_{\alpha^{k}_{i}}
are pairwise disjoint and
\lambda_{k}>\lambda_{k-1}
, there is
i<\lambda_{k}
such that
x_{j}\notin U_{\alpha^{k}_{i}}
for all
j<\lambda_{k-1}
: let
\mathbf{II}
play the least such
i
as his/her next move
i_{k}
. If the corresponding run of the game
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
does not stop at a finite stage, by construction
\bigcap_{k\in\omega}U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}\cap A=\emptyset
, hence
\mathbf{II}
won such a run. It follows that the strategy we just described is winning for
\mathbf{II}
, as required.
∎

Remark 7.3.4
.

In the proof of Theorem
7.3.3
(i)
we heavily used Corollary
7.1.3
, which in turn relies on the fact that, under our assumptions on
\lambda
, all
\lambda
-analytic sets have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
(Corollary
7.1.2
). This can be avoided by modifying the rules of
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
and asking that each
\mathbf{I}
’s move
(\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
is such that there is
r>0
for which all pairs
(U_{\alpha^{k}_{i}},U_{\alpha^{k}_{j}})
have distance greater than
r
if
i\neq j
(this is obviously stronger than just requiring
U_{\alpha^{k}_{i}}\cap U_{\alpha^{k}_{j}}=\emptyset
). The very same proof would then show that, had we used this variant of
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
, then
5
5

5

The sketched equivalence between each of the two games and the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
for their payoff set proves that such games are indeed equivalent to each other in the game-theoretic sense.

\mathbf{I}
would have a winning strategy if and only if
A
contains a
closed-in-
X
homeomorphic copy of
C(\lambda)
; notably, however, the argument would now be direct and would not require passing through Corollary
7.1.3
.

The classic
*
-game
G^{*}(A)
is often used to show that if a certain class of sets is determined, then all sets in that class have the
\mathrm{PSP}
. For example, all Borel sets have the
\mathrm{PSP}
because of Martin’s Borel determinacy (Theorem
4.5.3
), and the same is true for projective sets if we assume projective determinacy.
In contrast, in our generalized setting we lack determinacy results beyond closed and open sets (Section
4.5
), so Theorem
7.3.3
might seem useless. Nevertheless, we can reverse the approach and use that results to obtain that every time we prove a theorem of the form
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
for a certain boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
, then we automatically get the determinacy of an entire class of games associated to it, namely, all games of the form
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
for
A\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
(notice that the topological complexity of
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
is approximately the same of
A
itself); in view of Proposition
4.5.6
, such a consequence is highly nontrivial.

We next move to the unfolded version of the generalized
*
-game
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
.

Definition 7.3.5
.

Let
X
and
\mathcal{B}
be as in Definition
7.3.1
. Given
C\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
, consider the variant
\lambda
-
G^{*}_{u}(C)

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(\alpha^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0)&(\alpha^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1)&\dotsc&(\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}},y(k)&\dotsc\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}&\dotsc&i_{k}&\dotsc\end{array}

of the generalized
*
-game from Definition
7.3.1

in which at each turn
\mathbf{I}
additionally plays
y(k)<\lambda
, and
\mathbf{I}
wins if and only if (s)he can move (legally) for
\omega
-many rounds and
(x,y)\in C
, where
x
is the unique point in
\bigcap_{k\in\omega}U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}
and
y=(y(k))_{k\in\omega}
.

Theorem 7.3.6
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
,
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space, and
C\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
.

(i)

If
\mathbf{I}
has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
G_{u}^{*}(C)
, then there is a continuous injection of
C(\lambda)
into
p(C)
.

(ii)

If
\mathbf{II}
has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
G_{u}^{*}(A)
, then
p(C)
is well-orderable and
|p(C)|\leq\lambda
.

Thus if the game
\lambda
-
G_{u}^{*}(C)
is determined, then
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(p(C))
.

Proof.

(i)

It is easy to see that if
\mathbf{I}
has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
G^{*}_{u}(C)
, then (s)he has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
G^{*}(p(C))
as well, namely, the strategy obtained by forgetting the ordinal
y(k)
in each of them. Thus the conclusion follows from Theorem
7.3.3
(i)
.

(ii)

The proof is very similar to the proof of the akin statement in Theorem
7.3.3
(ii)
, we just need to check that the presence of the ordinals
y(k)
does not significantly change the cardinality of the objects involved. Let again
P
be the set of all legal positions in
\lambda
-
G^{*}_{u}(C)
in which
\mathbf{II}
followed his/her winning strategy
\tau
and
\mathbf{I}
has to move next. Since there are only
\lambda
-many possible values for each
y(k)
, arguing as in the proof of Theorem
7.3.3
(ii)
it is easy to check that
|P|\leq\lambda
because we assumed
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Next, given
\gamma<\lambda
,
p\in P
of length
2k
, and
x\in U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
(where
U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
is the last basic open set selected by
\mathbf{II}
along
p
), we say that
x
is
bad for
(p,\gamma)
if
x\in U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
and the following holds: For every sequence
(\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
, if the pair
((\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}},\gamma)
is a legal move for
\mathbf{I}
and
i_{k}<\lambda_{k}
is
\mathbf{II}
’s reply following
\tau
, then
x\notin U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}
. Let

A_{p,\gamma}=\{x\in X\mid x\text{ is bad for }(p,\gamma)\}.

Notice that
A_{p,\gamma}\subseteq U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
by definition, and that if
\mathbf{I}
cannot make any further legal move after
p
then
A_{p,\gamma}=U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
. Arguing as in Claim
7.3.3.1
, one can then check that
|A_{p,\gamma}|\leq(\lambda_{k})^{\omega}<\lambda
. (Otherwise
\mathrm{dens}(A_{p,\gamma})>\lambda_{k}
, thus there would be an
r
-spaced set
B\subseteq A_{p,\gamma}
from which one could then extract a legal move
((\alpha^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}},\gamma)
for
\mathbf{I}
such that
U_{\alpha^{k}_{i}}\cap B\neq\emptyset
for all
i<\lambda_{k}
. Such a move would witness that the points in
U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}\cap B
, where
i_{k}
is
\mathbf{II}
’s reply according to
\tau
, are not in
A_{p,\gamma}
, a contradiction.) Finally, we claim that
p(C)\subseteq\bigcup\{A_{p,\gamma}\mid p\in P\wedge\gamma<\lambda\}
: this concludes the proof because then
|p(C)|\leq\lambda
by the fact that
|P|\leq\lambda
and
|A_{p,\gamma}|<\lambda
. Assume towards a contradiction that
x\in p(C)\setminus\bigcup\{A_{p,\gamma}\mid p\in P\wedge\gamma<\lambda\}
. Let
y\in B(\lambda)
be such that
(x,y)\in C
. Then using the inductive hypothesis
x\notin A_{p_{k},y(k)}
, one can recursively construct an increasing sequence of legal positions
(p_{k})_{k\in\omega}
such that
p_{k}\in P
has length
2k
and if
k>0

•

the last move of
\mathbf{I}
appearing in
p_{k}
is of the form
((\alpha^{k-1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k-1}},y(k-1))
(that is,
y
determines the second component of the pair), and

•

x\in U_{\alpha^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
, where
i_{k-1}<\lambda_{k-1}
is the last move of
\mathbf{II}
in
p_{k}
(which is played according to
\tau
because
p\in P
).

Since by construction
x\in\bigcap_{k\in\omega}U_{\alpha^{k}_{i_{k}}}
and
(x,y)\in C
, it then follows that
\mathbf{I}
wins the run
\bigcup_{k\in\omega}p_{k}
, contradicting the fact that
\tau
was a winning strategy for
\mathbf{II}
.
∎

Remark 7.3.7
.

Arguing as in Remark
7.3.4
, it is easy to define a variant of
\lambda
-
G^{*}_{u}(C)
in which the existence of a winning strategy for
\mathbf{I}
directly implies that
p(C)
contains a closed-in-
X
homeomorphic copy of
C(\lambda)
(without passing through Corollary
7.1.3
). In this way we obtain an alternative purely game-theoretic proof of Corollary
7.1.2
. Indeed, if
A\subseteq X
is
\lambda
-analytic, then there is a closed set
C\subseteq X\times B(\lambda)
such that
A=p(C)
by Proposition
5.1.2
. This implies that the game
\lambda
-
G^{*}_{u}(C)
, once coded as a Gale-Stewart game (with rules) on
\lambda
, has a closed payoff set, and thus it is determined by Proposition
4.5.1
. By the analogue of Theorem
7.3.6
for the modified game, we thus get that either
A=p(C)
contains a closed-in-
X
homeomorphic copy of
C(\lambda)\approx{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
(if
\mathbf{I}
has a winning strategy), or else
|A|\leq\lambda
(if
\mathbf{II}
has a winning strategy).

When
\mathcal{B}
is a tree-basis in the sense of Definition
3.2.7
,
it is convenient to introduce further variants of

\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
and
\lambda
-
G^{*}_{u}(A)
that better fit the combinatorial structure of the index tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
of
\mathcal{B}
.

Definition 7.3.8
.

Let
X
be
\lambda
-Polish, and let
\mathcal{B}=\{U_{s}\mid s\in T\}
be a tree-basis for
X
with index tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
. Given
A\subseteq X
consider the following game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}}&(s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}}&\dotsc&(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}&\dotsc\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}&\dotsc&i_{k}&\dotsc\end{array}

where:

(a)

\mathbf{I}
chooses a sequence
(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
of length
\lambda_{k}
of elements of
T
so that

•

there are
0\neq j_{k}\in\omega
and a cardinal
\mu_{k}<\lambda
such that
s^{k}_{i}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{j_{k}}{\mu_{k}}
for all
i<\lambda_{k}
;

•

U_{s^{k}_{i}}\cap U_{s^{k}_{j}}=\emptyset
if
i,j<\lambda_{k}
are distinct;

•

s^{k}_{i}
is a proper extension of
s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}
for all
i<\lambda_{k}
(where
s^{-1}_{i_{-1}}=\emptyset
).

(b)

After
\mathbf{I}
’s move,
\mathbf{II}
replies choosing some
i_{k}<\lambda_{k}
.

(Notice that the rules above impose that
j_{k}\geq k+1
, so that
\mathrm{diam}(U_{s^{k}_{i}})\leq 2^{-(k+1)}
for all
k\in\omega
and
i<\lambda_{k}
.)
The game ends if at some finite stage
\mathbf{I}
cannot make any further legal move, in which case
\mathbf{II}
wins, or after
\omega
-many rounds, in which case

\mathbf{I}
wins if and only if
6
6

6

Recall that
f_{\mathcal{B}}\colon[T]\to X
is the canonical continuous open surjection sending
y\in[T]
to the unique point in
\bigcap_{k\in\omega}U_{y\restriction k}
.

f_{\mathcal{B}}(\bigcup_{k\in\omega}s^{k}_{i_{k}})\in A
.

We are going to show that
if
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, then
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)
is game-theoretically equivalent to
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
. This will follow from the existence of a bijection

(7.2)

\phi\colon{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\to\lambda

such that

•

for all
\alpha<\lambda
there are
j\in\omega
and
\mu<\lambda
such that
\phi^{-1}(\beta)\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{j}{\mu}
for all
\beta<\alpha
and, conversely,

•

for every
j\in\omega
and
\mu<\lambda
the set
\{\phi(s)\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{j}{\mu}\}
is bounded in
\lambda
.

(For example,
\phi
can be defined as the union over
k\in\omega
of bijections between
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\leq k+1}{\lambda_{k}}\setminus{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\leq k}{\lambda_{k-1}}
and
\lambda_{k}\setminus\lambda_{k-1}
, where
\lambda_{-1}=\emptyset
.)

Theorem 7.3.9
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
,
\mathcal{B}
, and
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
be as in Definition
7.3.8
. Let
A\subseteq X
.

(i)

\mathbf{I}
has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)
if and only if there is a continuous injection from
C(\lambda)
into
A
.

(ii)

\mathbf{II}
has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)
if and only if
|A|\leq\lambda
.

In particular, the game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)
is game-theoretically equivalent to
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
.

Proof.

For the additional part, it is enough to combine the present result with Theorem
7.3.3
.

(i)

Suppose that
\mathbf{I}
has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)
. By identifying each of his/her legal moves
(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda}
with the sequence of ordinals
(\phi(s^{k}_{i}))_{i<\lambda}
, where
\phi
is as in (
7.2
), it is easy to check that such a strategy is also a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
once we enumerate
\mathcal{B}
according to
\phi
, hence there is a continuous injection from
C(\lambda)
into
A
by Theorem
7.3.3
(i)
.

For the backward direction we argue as in the corresponding part of the proof of Theorem
7.3.3
(i)
. Suppose that there is a closed-in-
X
set
C\subseteq A
such that
C\approx B(\lambda)
, whose existence is granted by Corollary
7.1.3
. We can again recursively construct a legal strategy
\sigma
for
\mathbf{I}
such that
U_{s^{k}_{i}}\cap C\neq\emptyset
for all
k\in\omega
and
i<\lambda_{k}
, so that
\sigma
is automatically winning because
C
is closed. Indeed, once we know that
U_{s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}\cap C\neq\emptyset
, then by considering a suitable
r
-spaced subset of it we can find a
j_{k}>\operatorname{lh}(s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}})
and a sequence
(t^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
of elements of
T
such that the open sets
U_{t^{k}_{i}}
are pairwise disjoint, they still intersect
C
, and moreover
\operatorname{lh}(t^{k}_{i})=j_{k}
and
t^{k}_{i}\supseteq s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}
for every
i<\lambda_{k}
. For each
n\in\omega
, consider the set
S_{n}
of those
t^{k}_{i}
which belong to
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{j_{k}}{\lambda_{n}}
. Since
\lambda_{k}
is regular, there is
n\in\omega
such that
|S_{n}|=\lambda_{k}
. Let
\bar{n}
be smallest with such property, set
\mu_{k}=\lambda_{\bar{n}}
, and let
(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
be an enumeration of
S_{\bar{n}}
. Clearly, the move
(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
is then as required.

(ii)

Assume that
\mathbf{II}
has a winning strategy
\tau
in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)
. We basically follow the corresponding argument from Theorem
7.3.3
(ii)
, although technically we need to be a bit more careful. Let
P
be the set of all legal positions
p
in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)
in which
\mathbf{II}
followed
\tau
and
\mathbf{I}
has to move next, so that
p
has even length
2k
. If
k>0
, the last moves of
\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{II}
in
p
are of the form, respectively,
(s^{k-1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k-1}}
and
i_{k-1}
, so that
\mathbf{II}
has chosen
s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}
. We extend this to the case
k=0
by setting
s^{-1}_{i_{-1}}=\emptyset
.
Call
\tilde{x}\in[T]

bad for
p
if it extends
s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}
but for every legal move
(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
for
\mathbf{I}
in the next turn, the reply
i_{k}
of
\mathbf{II}
following
\tau
against
\mathbf{I}
’s move is such that
\tilde{x}
does not extend
s^{k}_{i_{k}}
. We let

\widetilde{A}_{p}=\{\tilde{x}\in[T]\mid\tilde{x}\text{ is bad for }p\}

and

A_{p}=\{x\in X\mid\exists\tilde{x}\in\widetilde{A}_{p}\,(f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})=x)\}=f_{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{A}_{p}).

It is not hard to see that each
\widetilde{A}_{p}
is closed. Indeed, if
\tilde{x}\in[T]\setminus\widetilde{A}_{p}
, it means that there is a legal move
(s_{i}^{k})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
for
\mathbf{I}
in the next turn such that
\tilde{x}\supseteq s^{k}_{i_{k}}
, where
i_{k}
is
\mathbf{II}
’s reply according to
\tau
. It follows that the same move
(s_{i}^{k})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
witnesses that
\tilde{y}\notin\widetilde{A}_{p}
for every
\tilde{y}\in[T]\cap\boldsymbol{N}_{s^{k}_{i_{k}}}
, so that
\boldsymbol{N}_{s^{k}_{i_{k}}}
is an open neighborhood of
\tilde{x}
disjoint from
\widetilde{A}_{p}
.
Moreover,
A_{p}\subseteq U_{s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
, and
A_{p}=U_{s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
if
\mathbf{I}
cannot make any legal move beyond
p
.

Claim 7.3.9.1
.

For every
p\in P
of length
2k

|A_{p}|\leq(\lambda_{k})^{\omega},

and thus
|A_{p}|<\lambda
.

Proof of the claim.

Assume towards a contradiction that
|A_{p}|>(\lambda_{k})^{\omega}
, so that
\operatorname{dens}(A_{p})>\lambda_{k}
. Pick an
r
-spaced set
B\subseteq A_{p}
of size
\lambda_{k}
(for some real
r>0
). For each
x\in B\subseteq A_{p}
,
f^{-1}_{\mathcal{B}}(x)
is closed because
f_{\mathcal{B}}
is continuous, hence so is
f^{-1}_{\mathcal{B}}(x)\cap\widetilde{A}_{p}
: let
\tilde{x}\in\widetilde{A}_{p}
be the leftmost branch of the pruned
\omega
-tree
T_{x}
such that
f^{-1}_{\mathcal{B}}(x)\cap\widetilde{A}_{p}=[T_{x}]
, so that
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})=x
and
s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}\subseteq\tilde{x}
(because
\tilde{x}\in\widetilde{A}_{p}
) for each such
\tilde{x}
. Let
j_{k}>j_{k-1}=\operatorname{lh}(s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}})
be such that
2^{-{j_{k}}}<\frac{r}{2}
, so that
|\{\tilde{x}\restriction j_{k}\mid x\in B\}|=\lambda_{k}
because
B
is
r
-spaced.
Since
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{j_{k}}{\lambda}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{j_{k}}{\lambda_{n}}
and
\lambda_{k}
is regular, there is (a minimal)
\bar{n}\in\omega
such that
|S_{\bar{n}}|=\lambda_{k}
where
S_{\bar{n}}=\{\tilde{x}\restriction j_{k}\mid x\in B\wedge{\tilde{x}_{i}\restriction j_{k}}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{j_{k}}{\lambda_{\bar{n}}}\}
. Let
(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
be an enumeration without repetitions of
S_{\bar{n}}
, so that
(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
becomes a legal move for
\mathbf{I}
extending
p
in the game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)
(as witnessed by
j_{k}
and
\mu_{k}=\lambda_{\bar{n}}
). Let also
x_{i}\in B\subseteq A_{p}
be such that
\tilde{x}_{i}\restriction j_{k}=s^{k}_{i}
, for any
i<\lambda_{k}
. If
i_{k}
is the reply of
\mathbf{II}
to the above move of
\mathbf{I}
, then
\tilde{x}_{i_{k}}\supseteq s^{k}_{i_{k}}
, contradicting
\tilde{x}_{i_{k}}\in\widetilde{A}_{p}
.
∎

Claim 7.3.9.2
.

A\subseteq\bigcup\{A_{p}\mid p\in P\}
.

Proof of the claim.

Assume towards a contradiction that
x\in A\setminus\bigcup\{A_{p}\mid p\in P\}
, and let
\tilde{x}\in[T]
be such that
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})=x
. We are going to construct an increasing sequence
(p_{k})_{k\in\omega}
of positions in
P
of length
2k
such that
s^{k}_{i_{k}}\subseteq\tilde{x}
for all
k\in\omega
: it follows that the run corresponding to the union of the positions
p_{k}
is won by
\mathbf{I}
, contradicting the fact that
\mathbf{II}
is following the winning strategy
\tau
.

The construction of the positions
p_{k}
is by recursion on
k
, starting from the empty position
p_{0}=\emptyset
. For the inductive step, assume that
p_{k}
is as required, that is, that
s^{\ell}_{i_{\ell}}\subseteq s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}\subseteq\tilde{x}
for all
\ell\leq k-1
.
Since
x\notin A_{p_{k}}
, it follows that

\tilde{x}^{\prime}\notin\widetilde{A}_{p_{k}}
for all
\tilde{x}^{\prime}\in[T]
with
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x}^{\prime})=x
: in particular, this holds for our
\tilde{x}
. Thus by definition of
\widetilde{A}_{p_{k}}
there is
(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
which is a legal move for
\mathbf{I}
such that
\tilde{x}
still extends
s^{k}_{i_{k}}
, where
i_{k}
is the reply of
\mathbf{II}
according to
\tau
. Letting
p_{k+1}
be the prolongation of
p_{k}
by such moves of
\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{II}
, we get that
p_{k+1}\in P
is as required.
∎

Because of the size of
P
and each
A_{p}
, we finally get
|A|\leq\lambda
, as required.

Conversely, assume that
|A|\leq\lambda
. By Theorem
7.3.3
(ii)
there is a winning strategy
\tau
for
\mathbf{II}
in
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
. Using again the fact that every legal move for
\mathbf{I}
in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)
can be turned via
\phi
into a legal move for
\mathbf{I}
in
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
, we then get that
\tau
can be straightforwardly transformed into a winning strategy for
\mathbf{II}
in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)
.
∎

The game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(A)
admits an obvious unfolded version, where the payoff set
C
is a subset of
X\times B(\lambda)
, and
\mathbf{I}
additionally plays an ordinal
y(k)<\lambda
at each turn
k\in\omega
. Assuming
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, one can mix the ideas from Theorems
7.3.6
and
7.3.9
and prove that if
\mathbf{I}
has a winning strategy in such a game, then there is a continuous injection from
C(\lambda)
into
p(C)
, while if
\mathbf{II}
has a winning strategy, then
|p(C)|\leq\lambda
; in particular, if the game is determined then
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(p(C))
.
However, for technical reasons (see Section
7.4
, and in particular the proof of Theorem
7.4.12
) we need to further generalize
such a game by considering a version of it in which the payoff set
\widetilde{C}
is rather a subset of
B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda)
, and the determinacy of the new game implies that
f_{\mathcal{B}}(p(\widetilde{C}))
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
. (It is not hard to see that the simpler version of the unfolded game considered at the beginning of this paragraph corresponds then to the special case where
\widetilde{C}=(f_{\mathcal{B}}\times\operatorname{id})^{-1}(C)
, so that
f_{\mathcal{B}}(p(\widetilde{C}))=p(C)
.)

Definition 7.3.10
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space, and let
\mathcal{B}=\{U_{s}\mid s\in T\}
be a tree-basis for
X
with index tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\kappa}
. Given
\widetilde{C}\subseteq B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda)
, the game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\widetilde{C})

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0)&(s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1)&\dotsc&(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}},y(k)&\dotsc\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}&\dotsc&i_{k}&\dotsc\end{array}

is played as in Definition
7.3.8
except that
at each turn
\mathbf{I}
additionally plays
y(k)<\lambda
. Player
\mathbf{I}
wins if and only if (s)he can move (legally) for
\omega
-many rounds and
(\tilde{x},y)\in\widetilde{C}
, where
\tilde{x}=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}s^{k}_{i_{k}}
and
y=(y(k))_{k\in\omega}
.

Theorem 7.3.11
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
,
\mathcal{B}
, and
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
be as in Definition
7.3.10
. Let
\widetilde{C}\subseteq B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda)
.

(i)

If
\mathbf{I}
has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}_{u}^{*}(\widetilde{C})
, then there is a continuous injection from
C(\lambda)
into
f_{\mathcal{B}}(p(\widetilde{C}))
.

(ii)

If
\mathbf{II}
has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}_{u}^{*}(\widetilde{C})
, then
f_{\mathcal{B}}(p(\widetilde{C}))
is well-orderable and
|f_{\mathcal{B}}(p(\widetilde{C}))|\leq\lambda
.

Thus if the game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}_{u}^{*}(\widetilde{C})
is determined, then
f_{\mathcal{B}}(p(\widetilde{C}))
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
.

Proof.

(i)

If
\mathbf{I}
has a winning strategy in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\widetilde{C})
, then (s)he can also win
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(f_{\mathcal{B}}(p(\widetilde{C})))
by forgetting the additional moves
y(k)
played by
\mathbf{I}
in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}_{u}^{*}(\widetilde{C})
, hence there is a continuous injection from
C(\lambda)
into
f_{\mathcal{B}}(p(\widetilde{C}))
by Theorem
7.3.9
.

(ii)

Let
\tau
be a winning strategy for
\mathbf{II}
in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\widetilde{C})
.
Let
P
the set of legal positions in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\widetilde{C})
of even length where
\mathbf{II}
followed
\tau
. If
p
has length
2k
, the last moves of
\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{II}
in
p
are of the form
((s^{k-1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k-1}},y(k-1))
and
i_{k-1}
, respectively. (If
k=0
we set
s^{-1}_{i_{-1}}=\emptyset
and
i_{-1}=0
.) Given
\gamma<\lambda
, we call
\tilde{x}\in[T]

bad for
(p,\gamma)
if
\tilde{x}\supseteq s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}
and for every
(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
, if the pair
((s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}},\gamma)
is a legal move for
\mathbf{I}
after
p
, then
\tilde{x}\not\supseteq s^{k}_{i_{k}}
for
i_{k}
the reply by
\mathbf{II}
according to
\tau
. We then set

\widetilde{A}_{p,\gamma}=\{\tilde{x}\in[T]\mid\tilde{x}\text{ is bad for }(p,\gamma)\}

and

A_{p,\gamma}=\{x\in X\mid\exists\tilde{x}\in\widetilde{A}_{p,\gamma}\,(f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})=x)\}=f_{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{A}_{p,\gamma}).

Notice in particular that
A_{p,\gamma}\subseteq U_{s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
, and that
A_{p,\gamma}=U_{s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}}
if
\mathbf{I}
cannot make any legal move after
p
.

Arguing as in Claim
7.3.9.1
, we get
|A_{p,\gamma}|\leq(\lambda_{k})^{\omega}<\lambda
. Moreover,
p(\widetilde{C})\subseteq\bigcup\{\widetilde{A}_{p,\gamma}\mid p\in P\wedge\gamma<\lambda\}
: if
\tilde{x}\in p(\widetilde{C})\setminus\{\widetilde{A}_{p,\gamma}\mid p\in P\wedge\gamma<\lambda\}
, picking any
y\in B(\lambda)
such that
(\tilde{x},y)\in\widetilde{C}
we could then construct an increasing sequence of positions
p_{k}\in P
of length
2k
so that each
p_{k}
is “compatible” with
\widetilde{x}
and
y
, in the sense that those are the elements determined by the run
\bigcup_{k\in\omega}p_{k}
; this contradicts the fact that
\tau
was winning for
\mathbf{II}
.
It follows that

f_{\mathcal{B}}(p(\widetilde{C}))\subseteq f_{\mathcal{B}}(\bigcup\{\widetilde{A}_{p,\gamma}\mid p\in P\wedge\gamma<\lambda\})=\bigcup\{A_{p,\gamma}\mid p\in P\wedge\gamma<\lambda\},

and since
|P|\leq\lambda
we conclude that
|f_{\mathcal{B}}(p(\widetilde{C}))|\leq\lambda
, as desired.
∎

Theorems
7.3.9
and
7.3.11
become particularly significant when
X
is a closed subset of
B(\lambda)
(including the extreme case
X=B(\lambda)
) and the chosen tree-basis is the canonical one, that is,
\{\boldsymbol{N}_{s}(X)\mid s\in T\}
for
T=\{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\mid\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\cap X\neq\emptyset\}
. In such cases,
f_{\mathcal{B}}
is the identity map and the game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\cdot)
from Definition
7.3.10
is nothing else than the natural unfolding of the game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(\cdot)
from Definition
7.3.8
.

7.4.
Representable sets and
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}

If we could have that all games of the form
\lambda
-
G^{*}(A)
are determined, we would conclude that all subsets of any
\lambda
-Polish space
X
have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
. Unfortunately, as discussed in Section
4.5
, full determinacy is out of reach in the generalized context. But the existence of such a game-theoretic equivalent of
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
suggests a new development. Martin’s proof that all
\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
sets of reals are determined if there exists a measurable cardinal (see
[
26
, Theorem 31.1]
) can be divided in two parts: in the first part, it is proven that if there exists a measurable cardinal, then all
\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
sets have a certain tree-structure; such sets were called afterwards “homogeneously Souslin sets”. In the second part, it is proven that if the payoff of a game is a homogeneously Souslin set, then it is possible to define an unfolded version of the game, i.e. a closed (and therefore determined, under
{\sf AC}
) game where:

•

one of the players has to play additional moves, which are designed to witness that if a run in the unfolded game goes on for
\omega
-many moves, then the play in the original game obtained by removing those additional moves is indeed winning for such player;

•

every winning strategy in the unfolded game for any of the two player yields a winning strategy in the original game for the same player.

The projection of a homogeneously Souslin set is called “weakly homogeneously Souslin” (see Definition
8.1.1
). Such sets have been crucial in the proof of the consistency of
{\sf AD}
from the existence of large cardinals, and they enjoy the same regularity properties of determined set, even if they are not necessarily determined. The standard proof is the following: regularity properties like the
\mathrm{PSP}
have a game-theoretic equivalent, and the unfolded version of such game for weakly homogeneously Souslin sets is closed, therefore determined (under
{\sf AC}
). Then it is shown that
the determinacy of the unfolded game implies the determinacy of a “partially unfolded” version of the game, and that the latter suffices to prove that the set enjoys the given regularity property. (See
[
26
, Chapter 32]
for more information.)

We are going to define a generalization of weakly homogeneously Souslin sets in
\lambda
-Polish spaces, called representable sets, and prove that representability implies the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
.
Our strategy for proving this will follow closely the one described in the previous paragraph in the context of weakly homogeneously Souslin sets. Indeed,
the game-theoretic equivalent
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(\cdot)
of the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
and its “partially unfolded” version
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\cdot)
have been already introduced in Definitions
7.3.8
and
7.3.10
, respectively; the “fully” unfolded version of
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(\cdot)
, to be used with representable sets and denoted by
G_{\pi,F}
, will instead appear in the proof of Theorem
7.4.12
.

Our definition of representability is inspired by the notion of “
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable set” under
\mathsf{I0}
coming from
[
50
, Definition 110]
, and we will see in Chapter
8
that our definition is indeed a direct generalization of both concepts.

Definition 7.4.1
.

A family of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}
is
orderly
if there is a nonempty set
K
, called
support of
\mathbb{U}
, such that every
\mathcal{U}\in\mathbb{U}
concentrates on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}
for some
k\in\omega
, that is
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}\in\mathcal{U}
; the natural number
k
is then called
level of
\mathcal{U}
, and is denoted by
\operatorname{lev}(\mathcal{U})
.
An orderly family of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}
is
nonprincipal
if there is at least one
\mathcal{U}\in\mathbb{U}
which is nonprincipal.

When
\mathbb{U}
is an orderly families of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}
, we always denote by
K
its support. Notice that we can clearly assume that every
\mathcal{U}\in\mathbb{U}
is actually an ultrafilter on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\operatorname{lev}(\mathcal{U})}{K}
.

Definition 7.4.2
.

Let
\mathbb{U}
be an orderly family of ultrafilters.
A
tower of ultrafilters
(
in
\mathbb{U}
) is a countable sequence
(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
of ultrafilters from
\mathbb{U}
such that for all
k\in\omega
and
\ell<k

(a)

\operatorname{lev}(\mathcal{U}_{k})=k
;

(b)

\mathcal{U}_{k}
projects onto
\mathcal{U}_{\ell}
, that is, for every
A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\ell}{K}
we have

A\in\mathcal{U}_{\ell}\iff A^{\ell\to k}=\{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}\mid s\restriction\ell\in A\}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}.

A tower of ultrafilters
(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
is
well-founded
if for every sequence of sets
(A_{k})_{k\in\omega}
with
A_{k}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
there is
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K}
such that
z\restriction k\in A_{k}
for all
k\in\omega
.

Remark 7.4.3
.

The term “well-founded” comes from the analysis by Gaifman and Powell on such towers. If
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
are ultrafilters and
\mathcal{U}
projects onto
\mathcal{V}
, then there exists an elementary embedding
j_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U}}:\operatorname{Ult}(V,\mathcal{V})\to\operatorname{Ult}(V,\mathcal{U})
, where
\operatorname{Ult}(V,\mathcal{V})
and
\operatorname{Ult}(V,\mathcal{U})
are the ultrapowers of the universe of sets
V
by
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{U}
, respectively. Therefore a tower of ultrafilters
(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
induces a directed system
(\operatorname{Ult}(V,(\mathcal{U}_{k})),j_{\mathcal{U}_{k},\mathcal{U}_{m}})_{m\geq k}
, and the membership relation on the direct limit of such system is well-founded if the tower of ultrafilters is well-founded in the sense of Definition
7.4.2
(or, in their terminology, countably complete; see
[
14
]
and
[
42
]
).

Definition 7.4.4
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
,
\mathbb{U}
be an orderly family of ultrafilters, and

\mathcal{B}=\{U_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
be a tree-basis
7
7

7

Recall that by Proposition
3.2.8
we can always assume without loss of generality that the index tree of the chosen tree-basis
\mathcal{B}
is the whole
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
.

for a nonempty
\lambda
-Polish space
X
.
A
\mathbb{U}
-representation
for a set
Z\subseteq X
(with respect to
\mathcal{B}
) is a function

\pi\colon\bigcup_{k\in\omega}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda})\to\mathbb{U}

such that

(a)

for all
k\in\omega
and all
s,t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}
,
\operatorname{lev}(\pi(s,t))=k
;

(b)

for all
\tilde{x},y\in B(\lambda)
, the family
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}=(\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k))_{k\in\omega}
is a tower of ultrafilters such that
\pi({\tilde{x}\restriction k},{y\restriction k})
is (at least)
\lambda_{k}^{+}
-complete;

(c)

for every
\tilde{x}\in B(\lambda)
,
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})\in Z
if and only if there is
y\in B(\lambda)
such that
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded.

A set
Z\subseteq X
will be called
\mathbb{U}
-representable
if there is a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for
Z
(with respect to some tree-basis for
X
).

The reference to
\mathbb{U}
may be omitted when it is clear or irrelevant.
Clearly, we can always take
\mathbb{U}=\operatorname{rng}(\pi)
, so that
\pi
is surjective: this will always be tacitly assumed when speaking of
\mathbb{U}
-representable sets, unless otherwise stated.

Remark 7.4.5
.

Condition
(c)
in Definition
7.4.4
is subtle, as it entails that for every
x\in X
, the fact that
x\in Z
implies that
for every
\tilde{x}\in f^{-1}_{\mathcal{B}}(x)
there is
y\in B(\lambda)
such that
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded, while in order to prove that
x\in Z
it is enough to show that
there is some
\tilde{x}\in f^{-1}_{\mathcal{B}}(x)
such that
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded, for a suitable
y\in B(\lambda)
. In other words,

\displaystyle x\in Z

\displaystyle\iff\forall\tilde{x}\in f^{-1}_{\mathcal{B}}(x)\,\exists y\in B(\lambda)\,(T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}\text{ is well-founded})

\displaystyle\iff\exists\tilde{x}\in f^{-1}_{\mathcal{B}}(x)\,\exists y\in B(\lambda)\,(T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}\text{ is well-founded}).

In particular,
Z\subseteq X
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}
if and only if
f^{-1}_{\mathcal{B}}(Z)
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to the canonical basis
\{\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
on
B(\lambda)
.

Interestingly enough, all results except Proposition
8.3.6
would continue to work if condition
(c)
in Definition
7.4.4
is weakened to

(c
′
)

for every
x\in X
,
x\in Z
if and only if there are
\tilde{x},y\in B(\lambda)
such that
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})=x
and
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded.

Proposition 7.4.6
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
\pi
be a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for a set
Z\subseteq X
, for some
\lambda
-Polish space
X
and some orderly family of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}=\operatorname{rng}(\pi)
. Then every ultrafilter in
\mathbb{U}
is at least
\lambda^{+}
-complete.
If moreover
\mathbb{U}
is nonprincipal, then there is a measurable cardinal
\kappa>\lambda
such that every ultrafilter in
\mathbb{U}
is at least
\kappa
-complete.

In particular, if there is a
\mathbb{U}
-representable set with
\mathbb{U}
nonprincipal, then there is a measurable cardinal above
\lambda
.

Proof.

This essentially follows from the fact that it is not possible to project an ultrafilter to another ultrafilter with less completeness. Indeed, fix any infinite cardinal
\nu
and two natural numbers
k>\ell
. Suppose that
\mathcal{U}_{\ell}
is an ultrafilter on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\ell}{K}
that is not
\nu^{+}
-complete, and that
\mathcal{U}_{k}
is a
\nu^{+}
-complete ultrafilter on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}
that projects onto
\mathcal{U}_{\ell}
. Since
\mathcal{U}_{\ell}
is not
\nu^{+}
-complete, there is a sequence
(A_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\nu}
of sets in
\mathcal{U}_{\ell}
such that
A=\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}A_{\alpha}\notin\mathcal{U}_{\ell}
.
Setting
B_{\alpha}=A_{\alpha}\setminus A
, we get a sequence
(B_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\nu}
of sets in
\mathcal{U}_{\ell}
such that

\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}B_{\alpha}=\emptyset
. But then
(B_{\alpha}^{\ell\to k})_{\alpha<\nu}
is a sequence of sets in
\mathcal{U}_{k}
such that
\bigcap_{\alpha<\nu}B_{\alpha}^{\ell\to k}=\emptyset
, against the
\nu^{+}
-completeness of
\mathcal{U}_{k}
.

Consider now any
\tilde{x},y\in B(\lambda)
and the corresponding tower of ultrafilters
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
from Definition
7.4.4
(b)
. The previous paragraph shows that each
\mathcal{U}\in T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is at least
\lambda_{k}^{+}
-complete,
for every
k\in\omega
. It follows that every ultrafilter in
\mathbb{U}=\operatorname{rng}(\pi)=\bigcup_{\tilde{x},y\in B(\lambda)}T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is
\lambda
-complete, and hence also
\lambda^{+}
-complete because
\lambda
is singular.

Assume now that
\mathbb{U}
is nonprincipal. Since the completeness number of a nonprincipal ultrafilter is always a measurable cardinal (see Section
2.8
), the smallest measurable cardinal
\kappa
which is the completeness number of some nonprincipal
\mathcal{U}\in\mathbb{U}
is as required.
∎

Remark 7.4.7
.

It follows that in item
(b)
of Definition
7.4.4
we can equivalently require each of the ultrafilters
\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k)
to be (at least)
\kappa
-complete, for some fixed
\kappa>\lambda
.
This reformulation of Definition
7.4.4
makes sense also when
\lambda=\omega
, and indeed we will show that in such case it becomes equivalent to the classical notion of
\kappa
-weakly homogeneous Souslin set
(see Section
8.1
).

Remark 7.4.8
.

Proposition
7.4.6
raises the question of which subsets
Z\subseteq X
of a
\lambda
-Polish space
X
are
\mathbb{U}
-representable if
\mathbb{U}
only contains principal ultrafilters. It is easy to see that this is the case for
Z=X
. Conversely, we are now going to show that
if
\pi
is a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for a set
Z\subseteq X
and every ultrafilter in
\operatorname{rng}(\pi)
is principal, then necessarily
Z=X
. Indeed, fix any
x\in X
. Let
\tilde{x}\in B(\lambda)
be such that
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})=x
, and pick an arbitrary
y\in B(\lambda)
. For any
k\in\omega
there is
z_{k}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}
such that
\{z_{k}\}\in\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k)
because
\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k)
is principal.
But then for every
\ell<k
we have that
\{z_{\ell}\}^{\ell\to k}\cap\{z_{k}\}\neq\emptyset
because
\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k)
projects onto
\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction\ell,y\restriction\ell)
, therefore
z_{\ell}\subseteq z_{k}
. It follows that
z=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}z_{k}
witnesses that the tower
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded, and hence
x\in Z
.

One desirable property of weakly homogeneously Souslin subsets of the classical Baire space
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
is that, in particular, they are Souslin, i.e. they are the projection of a tree. For our definition of representable set, we will see that this is automatically true under
{\sf AC}
(this follows from Proposition
7.4.11
and the comment after it); however, it is not necessarily true in choiceless settings. For this reason, we define a stronger version of representability that does the job even if
{\sf AC}
is not assumed.

Definition 7.4.9
.

Let
\lambda
,
\mathbb{U}
,
X
, and
\mathcal{B}
be as in Definition
7.4.4
. A
\mathbb{U}
-representation
\pi
for a set
Z\subseteq X
has the
tower condition
if there is
F\colon\operatorname{rng}(\pi)\to\bigcup\mathbb{U}
such that

(a)

F(\mathcal{U})\in\mathcal{U}
for all
\mathcal{U}\in\operatorname{rng}(\pi)
;

(b)

for all
\tilde{x},y\in B(\lambda)
, the tower
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded if and only if there is
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K}
(where
K
is the support of
\mathbb{U}
) such that
z\restriction k\in F(\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k))
for all
k\in\omega
.

If a set
Z\subseteq X
admits a
\mathbb{U}
-representation with the tower condition, we will simply say that
Z
is
TC-representable
.

Remark 7.4.10
.

The terms “representable” and “tower condition” are taken from
[
50
]
. Woodin introduces these terms as a generalization of weakly homogeneously Souslin sets in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
, under the strong large cardinal assumption
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
. Such definitions are a particular case of our definition: we are going to discuss this in detail in Section
8.2
.

Proposition 7.4.11

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space. If
Z\subseteq X
is
\mathbb{U}
representable for some orderly family of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}
, then it is also TC-representable.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for
Z
(with respect to some tree-basis
\mathcal{B}
for
X
). If every
\mathcal{U}\in\operatorname{rng}(\pi)
is principal, it is enough to let
F(\mathcal{U})
be the set generating
\mathcal{U}
. Otherwise, by Proposition
7.4.6
there is a measurable cardinal
\kappa>\lambda
such that every
\mathcal{U}\in\operatorname{rng}(\pi)
is at least
\kappa
-complete. Since we are assuming
{\sf AC}
, the cardinal
\kappa
is also strong limit, and thus
\kappa>2^{\lambda}
.

Let
K
be the support of
\mathbb{U}
.
Given any
\tilde{x},y\in B(\lambda)
, consider the tower of ultrafilters
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
. If it is not well-founded, then there is a sequence
(A^{\tilde{x},y}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
with
A^{\tilde{x},y}_{k}\in\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k)
witnessing this, i.e. such that there is no
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K}
such that
\forall k\in\omega\,(z\restriction k\in A^{\tilde{x},y}_{k})
. If instead
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded, let
A^{\tilde{x},y}_{k}={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}
for all
k\in\omega
. For every
k\in\omega
and
s,t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}
, let

F(\pi(s,t))=\bigcap\{A^{\tilde{x},y}_{k}\mid(\tilde{x},y)\in\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\times\boldsymbol{N}_{t}\}.

We claim that the function
F\colon\operatorname{rng}(\pi)\to\bigcup\mathbb{U}
we just defined witnesses that
\pi
has the tower condition. Condition
(a)
of Definition
7.4.9
is satisfied by choice of
A^{\tilde{x},y}_{k}
and the fact that
\pi(s,t)
is a
\kappa
-complete ultrafilter for
\kappa>2^{\lambda}
. As for condition
(b)
of the same definition, if
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded then, by definition, there is
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K}
such that
z\restriction k\in F(\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k))
for all
k\in\omega
simply because
F(\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k))\in\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k)
.
If instead
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is not well-founded, then there cannot be
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K}
as above by choice of
A^{\tilde{x},y}_{k}
and the fact that
F(\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k))\subseteq A^{\tilde{x},y}_{k}
.
∎

Thus the tower condition and TC-representability are more significant when we work in choiceless settings. Even in such situations, though,
every set
Z\subseteq B(\lambda)
which is TC-representable with respect to the canonical basis
\mathcal{B}=\{\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
for
B(\lambda)
, so that
f_{\mathcal{B}}
is the identity map, can be written as
Z=p[T]
for some pruned tree
T
over
\lambda\times Y
, where
Y=\lambda\times K
. Indeed, let

T=\{(s,(t,u))\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{(\lambda\times Y)}\mid k\in\omega\wedge u\in F(\pi(s,t))\}.

Then
x\in Z
if and only if there is
y\in B(\lambda)
such that
T^{\pi}_{x,y}
is well-founded, if and only if there is
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K}
such that
(x,(y,z))\in[T]
.

The main goal of this section is to prove the following general result.

Theorem 7.4.12
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
. Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space, and let
Z\subseteq X
be a TC-representable set. Then either there is a continuous injection from
C(\lambda)
into
Z
, or else
Z
is well-orderable and
|Z|\leq\lambda
. In particular,
Z
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
.

The proof of Theorem
7.4.12
is reminiscent of the proof that all
\kappa
-weakly homogeneously Souslin sets have the
\mathrm{PSP}
(see
[
26
, Theorem 32.7]
).

Proof.

Fix a
\mathbb{U}
-representation
\pi
for
Z\subseteq X

with the tower condition, and let
F
be a witness of the latter. We denote by
K
the support of
\mathbb{U}
.
Consider the following infinite two-players zero-sum game
8
8

8

The game
G_{\pi,F}
is clearly reminiscent of the game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\cdot)
, except that now
\mathbf{I}
has to additionally play elements
z(k)\in K
from the second turn on.

with perfect information
G_{\pi,F}
:

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0)&z(0),(s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1)&\dotsc&z(k-1),(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}},y(k)&\dotsc\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}&\dotsc&i_{k}&\dotsc\end{array}

The game is played alternatively with
\mathbf{I}
playing first. At each round
k\in\omega
:

(a)

\mathbf{I}
chooses a sequence
(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
and an ordinal
y(k)<\lambda
as in the game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\cdot)
from Definition
7.3.10
, the only difference being that now the index tree
T
of the tree-basis
\mathcal{B}
of
X
is assumed to be the entire
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
.

(b)

If
k>0
, then
\mathbf{I}
additionally chooses some
z(k-1)\in K
, ensuring that

(z(j))_{j<k}\in F(\pi(s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}\restriction k,(y(j))_{j<k})).

(Notice that the rules in
(a)
entail that
\operatorname{lh}(s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}})\geq k
for all
k\geq 1
, so the requirement makes sense.)

(c)

As in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\cdot)
, after
\mathbf{I}
’s move player
\mathbf{II}
replies by choosing some
i_{k}<\lambda_{k}
.

The game ends after
\omega
-many rounds or when player
\mathbf{I}
is no longer able to move according to the rules above; in the latter case
\mathbf{II}
wins, otherwise (i.e. if
\mathbf{I}
can survive for infinitely many rounds)
\mathbf{I}
wins.

The game
G_{\pi,F}
is the “fully unfolded” version of the game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(Z)
. Indeed, if
\mathbf{I}
wins a run of
G_{\pi,F}
, then the sequence
z=(z(k))_{k\in\omega}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K}
is a witness, through the tower condition witnessed by
F
, of the well-foundedness of the tower
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
, where
\tilde{x}=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}s^{k}_{i_{k}}
and
y=(y(k))_{k\in\omega}
, and therefore
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})\in Z
.

The game
G_{\pi,F}
is closed for
\mathbf{I}
, hence it is quasidetermined by Proposition
4.5.1
.
If
\mathbf{I}
has a winning quasistrategy in
G_{\pi,F}
, then (s)he also has a winning quasistrategy in the game
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(Z)
— simply “forget” the
z
and
y
part, and play only the sequences
s_{i}^{k}
. By
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, the set of all possible moves in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(Z)
is well-orderable (in fact, it has size
\lambda
), and hence the winning quasistrategy of
\mathbf{I}
in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(Z)
can be refined to a winning strategy. Therefore
C(\lambda)
embeds into
Z
by Theorem
7.3.9
.

Suppose now that
\mathbf{II}
has a winning quasistrategy in
G_{\pi,F}
. Player
\mathbf{II}
’s set of moves in
G_{\pi,F}
is the cardinal
\lambda
, hence (s)he also has a winning strategy in
G_{\pi,F}
: we want to show that this implies

|Z|\leq\lambda
. One could be tempted to do as for
\mathbf{I}
, i.e. to just ignore
y
and
z
, but there is a crucial obstacle. Suppose that
\tau
is a winning strategy for
\mathbf{II}
in the game
G_{\pi,F}
, and suppose that there are two different legal moves
p
and
q
for
\mathbf{I}
with the same
(s_{i}^{k})_{i<\lambda_{k}}
but different additional parts
y(k)
and
z(k)
. Then it is possible that
\tau(p)\neq\tau(q)
, while the strategy for
\mathbf{II}
in
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(Z)
must choose just one
i_{k}
, independently of the forgotten part of
\mathbf{I}
’s moves. We now prove that we can indeed make a single choice for all possible values of
z(k)
, so that such part of
\mathbf{I}
’s moves can indeed be discarded: this leaves us with a winning strategy for
\mathbf{II}
in a game of the form
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\widetilde{C})
for a certain set
\widetilde{C}\subseteq B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda)
, which by Theorem
7.3.11
(ii)
will be enough to ensure
|Z|\leq\lambda
.

Let us consider the following variant of the game
G_{\pi,F}
, which does not depend on
F
and is denoted by
G_{\pi}
:

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0)&(s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1)&\dotsc&(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}},y(k)&\dotsc\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}&\dotsc&i_{k}&\dotsc\end{array}

The rules are exactly those of
G_{\pi,F}
, except that now
\mathbf{I}
does not have to play
z(k)\in K
in any of the rounds. The main difference is in the winning condition:
\mathbf{I}
wins if (s)he can survive for
\omega
-many rounds and the tower
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded, where
\tilde{x}=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}s^{k}_{i_{k}}
and
y=(y(k))_{k\in\omega}
are obtained from the run as usual. This is a kind of “intermediate unfolding” of
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(Z)
, but, in contrast to the case of
G_{\pi,F}
, the complexity of the payoff set of the game
G_{\pi}
is now quite high and strictly depends on the complexity of
\pi
(and thus of the represented set
Z
), so it is not clear
a priori
that the game
G_{\pi}
is (quasi)determined. However, if
\mathbf{I}
has a winning (quasi)strategy in
G_{\pi,F}
then (s)he can turn it into a winning (quasi)strategy in
G_{\pi}
by forgetting the additional moves
z(k)
. We are now going to show that a similar result holds for
\mathbf{II}
.

Claim 7.4.12.1
.

If
\mathbf{II}
has a winning strategy in
G_{\pi,F}
, then (s)he also has a winning strategy in
G_{\pi}
.

Proof of the claim.

We show how to transform any winning strategy
\tau
for
\mathbf{II}
in
G_{\pi,F}
into a winning strategy for
\mathbf{II}
in
G_{\pi}
, by simulating a given run of
G_{\pi}
in the game
G_{\pi,F}
.

The first move in
G_{\pi,F}
and
G_{\pi}
are of the same form, so
\mathbf{II}
can just use
\tau
to reply to
\mathbf{I}
’s first move
((s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0))
and play some
i_{0}<\lambda_{0}
.

Let
p_{0}
be the position reached after this first round, and let

((s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1))

be
\mathbf{I}
’s next move in
G_{\pi}
. Simulate this move in the game
G_{\pi,F}
, letting
\mathbf{I}
randomly choose which
a\in K
to play as
z(0)
to complete his/her move. More precisely, for each
a\in K
let
i_{a}<\lambda_{1}
be
\mathbf{II}
’s reply in
G_{\pi,F}
according to
\tau
when
\mathbf{I}
plays

(a,(s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1))

in the second turn. Set
\mathcal{U}_{1}=\pi(s^{0}_{i_{0}}\restriction 1,(y(0)))
and
A^{(1)}=F(\mathcal{U}_{1})
.
For each
i<\lambda_{1}
, let

A^{(1)}_{i}=\{a\in A^{(1)}\mid i_{a}=i\}.

Since the sets
A^{(1)}_{i}
form a weak partition of
A^{(1)}=F(\mathcal{U}_{1})\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{1}
is at least
\lambda_{1}^{+}
-complete, there is
i_{1}<\lambda_{1}
such that
A^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
: let
\mathbf{II}
play such
i_{1}
in the second turn of
G_{\pi}
, and denote by
p_{1}
the position reached in this way. Notice that by construction
A^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\subseteq F(\mathcal{U}_{1})
, hence for each
\langle a\rangle\in A^{(1)}_{i_{1}}
the position

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c}\mathbf{I}&(s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0)&a,(s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1)\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}\end{array}

is legal in
G_{\pi,F}
and such that
\mathbf{II}
has followed
\tau
in each round.

Now let

((s^{2}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(2))

be
\mathbf{I}
’s next move in
G_{\pi}
after
p_{1}
. Consider the ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}_{2}=\pi(s^{1}_{i_{1}}\restriction 2,(y(0),y(1)))
, and notice that

(A^{(1)}_{i_{1}})^{1\to 2}=\{t\in K^{2}\mid t\restriction 1\in A^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}

because
s^{1}_{i_{1}}\supseteq s^{0}_{i_{0}}
by the rules of
G_{\pi}
, and hence the ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}_{2}
projects onto
\mathcal{U}_{1}
by definition of representation. Set

A^{(2)}=(A^{(1)}_{i_{1}})^{1\to 2}\cap F(\mathcal{U}_{2}),

so that
A^{(2)}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
as well. For each
t\in A^{(2)}
let
i_{t}
be
\mathbf{II}
’s reply according to
\tau
in the partial run in
G_{\pi,F}
played as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0)&t(0),(s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1)&t(1),(s^{2}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{2}},y(2)\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}&i_{t}\end{array}

(Notice that in this way
\mathbf{II}
has so far played according to
\tau
by definition of
A^{(2)}
.) For each
i<\lambda_{2}
let

A^{(2)}_{i}=\{t\in A^{(2)}\mid i_{t}=i\}.

Since the sets
A^{(2)}_{i}
for a weak partition of
A^{(2)}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
is at least
\lambda^{+}_{2}
-complete, there is
i_{2}<\lambda_{2}
such that
A^{(2)}_{i_{2}}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
: let
\mathbf{II}
play such
i_{2}
in the next turn in
G_{\pi}
, and denote by
p_{2}
the position reached in this way. Notice that still
A^{(2)}_{i_{2}}\subseteq F(\mathcal{U}_{2})
, so that
for each
t\in A^{(2)}_{i_{2}}
the position

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0)&t(0),(s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1)&t(1),(s^{2}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{2}},y(2)\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}&i_{2}\end{array}

is legal in
G_{\pi,F}
and such that
\mathbf{II}
has played according to
\tau
so far.

Continuing in this way, after round
k>0
we will have reached a position
p_{k}
in
G_{\pi}
of the form

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0)&(s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1)&\dotsc&(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}},y(k)\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}&\dotsc&i_{k}\end{array}

and, simultaneously, constructed a set

A^{(k)}_{i_{k}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}=\pi(s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}\restriction k,(y(j))_{j<k})

such that
A^{(k)}_{i_{k}}\subseteq A^{(k)}=(A^{(k-1)}_{i_{k-1}})^{k-1\to k}\cap F(\mathcal{U}_{k})
, and for every
t\in A^{(k)}_{i_{k}}
the position

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0)&t(0),(s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1)&\dotsc&t(k-1),(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}},y(k)\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}&\dotsc&i_{k}\end{array}

is legal in
G_{\pi,F}
and such that
\mathbf{II}
has played according to
\tau
(in particular, the moves of
\mathbf{II}
according to
\tau
in such a partial run are actually independent of the specific choice of
t\in A^{(k)}_{i_{k}}
by construction).

Suppose that
((s^{k+1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k+1}},y(k+1))
is the next move of
\mathbf{I}
after
p_{k}
in the game
G_{\pi}
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{k+1}=\pi(s^{k}_{i_{k}}\restriction(k+1),(y(j))_{j<k+1})
, and notice that

(A^{(k)}_{i_{k}})^{k\to k+1}=\{t\in K^{k+1}\mid t\restriction k\in A^{(k)}_{i_{k}}\}\in\mathcal{U}_{k+1}

by definition of representation. (Here we again use the fact that, by the rules of
G_{\pi}
, the sequence
s^{k}_{i_{k}}
extends
s^{k-1}_{i_{k-1}}
and thus
\mathcal{U}_{k+1}
projects onto
\mathcal{U}_{k}
). Let

A^{(k+1)}=(A^{(k)}_{i_{k}})^{k\to k+1}\cap F(\mathcal{U}_{k+1}),

so that
A^{(k+1)}\in\mathcal{U}_{k+1}
.
For each
t\in A^{(k+1)}
, let
i_{t}
be
\mathbf{II}
’s reply according to
\tau
in the partial run in
G_{\pi,F}
played as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0)&\dotsc&t(k-1),(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}},y(k)&t(k),(s^{k+1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k+1}},y(k+1)\\
