\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&\dotsc&i_{k}&i_{t}\end{array}

(Notice that in this way
\mathbf{II}
has always played according to
\tau
by the properties of
A^{(k)}_{i_{k}}
.) For each
i<\lambda_{k+1}
, let

A^{(k+1)}_{i}=\{t\in A^{(k+1)}\mid i_{t}=i\}.

Since the sets
A^{(k+1)}_{i}
form a weak partition of
A^{(k+1)}\in\mathcal{U}_{k+1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{k+1}
is at least
\lambda^{+}_{k+1}
-complete, there is
i_{k+1}<\lambda_{k+1}
such that
A^{(k+1)}_{i_{k+1}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k+1}
: let
\mathbf{II}
play such
i_{k+1}
in the next turn in
G_{\pi}
, and denote by
p_{k+1}
the position reached in this way. It is clear that all the properties described in the previous paragraph are preserved after this new step.

The above procedure describes a strategy for
\mathbf{II}
in
G_{\pi}
: we claim that it is a winning one. Suppose not, i.e. that
\mathbf{I}
has been able to go on for
\omega
-many rounds in such a way that
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}=(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is well-founded, where
\tilde{x}=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}s^{k}_{i_{k}}
and
y=(y(k))_{k\in\omega}
. Since for each
k\geq 1
we have
A^{(k)}_{i_{k}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
, it follows that there is
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K}
such that
z\restriction k\in A^{(k)}_{i_{k}}
for all
k\geq 1
. But then

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c|c}\mathbf{I}&(s^{0}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{0}},y(0)&z(0),(s^{1}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{1}},y(1)&\dotsc&z(k-1),(s^{k}_{i})_{i<\lambda_{k}},y(k)&\dotsc\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{II}&i_{0}&i_{1}&\dotsc&i_{k}&\dotsc\end{array}

is an infinite legal run in
G_{\pi,F}
(because by
A^{(k)}_{i_{k}}\subseteq F(\mathcal{U}_{k})
the condition on the additional moves
z(k)
is verified) in which
\mathbf{II}
followed
\tau
: this means that
\mathbf{I}
has won such run, contradicting the fact that
\tau
was winning for
\mathbf{II}
.
∎

By Claim
7.4.12.1
, we now know that
\mathbf{II}
has a winning strategy in
G_{\pi}
.
To complete the proof, just observe that
G_{\pi}
is of the form
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\widetilde{C})
, where

\widetilde{C}=\{(\tilde{x},y)\in B(\lambda)\times B(\lambda)\mid T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}\text{ is well-founded}\},

and that
f_{\mathcal{B}}(p(\widetilde{C}))=Z
by definition of
\mathbb{U}
-representation. Thus
|Z|\leq\lambda
by Theorem
7.3.11
.
∎

The proof of Theorem
7.4.12
shows that, indeed, the game
G_{\pi}=\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}_{u}(\widetilde{C})
is determined, but gives no direct information on the games
\lambda
-
G^{*}(Z)
and
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(Z)
. But because of Theorems
7.3.3
and
7.3.9
, we can
a posteriori
conclude that since
Z
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
, then also
\lambda
-
G^{*}(Z)
and
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(Z)
are determined.

Corollary 7.4.13
.

Assume that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space.
If
Z\subseteq X
is TC-representable, then
\lambda
-
G^{*}(Z)
is determined, and the same is true for its variant
\lambda
-
\widetilde{G}^{*}(Z)
.

Chapter 8
More on representability

In this chapter we will show that our concept of
\mathbb{U}
-representability is a generalization of both weakly homogeneously Souslin-ness (for subsets of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
) and
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representability (for subsets of
V_{\lambda+1}
, where
j
witnesses that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds).
As a consequence, we wil argue that under
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, Theorem
7.4.12
yields that all definable subsets of any
\lambda
-Polish space, and in particular all its
\lambda
-projective sets, have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
.

8.1.
Representability and weakly homogeneously Souslin sets

The goal of this section is to show that if we set
\lambda=\omega
in Definition
7.4.4
(see Remark
7.4.7
), then we obtain an equivalent reformulation of the following classical notion (see e.g.
[
26
, pp. 453]
).

Definition 8.1.1
.

Let
\kappa>\omega
.
A set
Z\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
is
\kappa
-weakly homogeneously Souslin
if and only if there exist a set
Y
and a tree
T
on
\omega\times Y
such that:

(a)

T(s)=\{u\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{Y}\mid(s,u)\in T\}\neq\emptyset
for every
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\omega}
;

(b)

for every
k\in\omega
and every
s,t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\omega}
there is a
\kappa
-complete ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}_{s,t}
over
T(s)
such that
\mathcal{U}_{s,t}
projects onto
\mathcal{U}_{s\restriction\ell,t\restriction\ell}
for all
\ell<k
;

(c)

x\in Z
if and only if there is a
y\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
such that the tower of ultrafilters
(\mathcal{U}_{x\restriction k,y\restriction k})_{k\in\omega}
is well-founded.

We say that
Z\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
is
weakly homogeneously Souslin
if it is
\kappa
-weakly homogeneously Souslin for some
\kappa>\omega
.

Remark 8.1.2
.

As for Definition
7.4.4
, if at least one ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}_{s,t}
in item
(b)
of Definition
8.1.1
is nonprincipal, then we can again assume without loss of generality that the cardinal
\kappa
appearing in Definition
8.1.1
is actually a measurable cardinal. In particular, the existence of a weakly homogeneous set with such a property implies that there is a measurable cardinal.

Proposition 8.1.3
.

A set
Z\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}=B(\omega)
is weakly homogeneously Souslin if and only if it is
\mathbb{U}
-representable (in the sense of Definition
7.4.4
applied with
\mathcal{B}=\{\boldsymbol{N}_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\omega}\}
the canonical basis for
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
and
\lambda=\omega
; see Remark
7.4.7
).

Proof.

Suppose first that
Z\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
is
\kappa
-weakly homogeneously Souslin, for some
\kappa>\omega
, as witnessed by the tree
T\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{(\omega\times Y)}
.
For each
k\in\omega
and
s,t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\omega}
, let

\pi(s,t)=\{A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{Y}\mid H\subseteq A\text{ for some }H\in\mathcal{U}_{s,t}\},

and let
\mathbb{U}=\operatorname{rng}(\pi)
.
It is easy to see that
\mathbb{U}
is an orderly family of
\kappa
-complete ultrafilters with support
K=Y
, and that
\pi
is a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for
Z
with respect to the canonical basis
\mathcal{B}
of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
(in which case
f_{\mathcal{B}}
is the identity map).

On the other hand, let
\mathbb{U}
be an orderly family of
\kappa
-complete ultrafilters (for some
\kappa>\omega
) with support
K
, and let
\pi
be a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for
Z
(with respect to the canonical basis
\mathcal{B}
). Let
Y=K
, and let
T={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\omega\times Y}
be the complete tree on
\omega\times Y
. Finally, for every
k\in\omega
and
s,t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{Y}
, let
\mathcal{U}_{s,t}=\pi(s,t)
. It is easy to see that the tree
T
and the ultrafilters
\mathcal{U}_{s,t}
witness that
Z
is
\kappa
-weakly homogeneously Souslin.
∎

As for
\mathbb{U}
-representability, in the definition of weakly homogeneously Souslin set principal ultrafilters are allowed. However, arguing as in Remark
7.4.8
one can easily see that the existence of a proper weakly homogeneously Souslin subset
Z
of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
implies that there is a measurable cardinal
\kappa
.
Indeed, if
Z\neq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
is weakly homogeneously Souslin, then some of the ultrafilters
\mathcal{U}_{s,t}
witnessing this need to be nonprincipal, and hence their completeness number is a measurable cardinal. Since under
{\sf AC}
any measurable cardinal
\kappa
satisfies
\kappa>2^{\aleph_{0}}
, this entails that the tower condition from Definition
7.4.9
is automatic when
\lambda=\omega
(see the proof of Proposition
7.4.11
), and this explains why such a condition does not explicitly appear in the literature on weakly homogeneously Souslin sets, where one usually works in
{\sf ZFC}
.
It must also be noted that the equivalence among the different definitions of weakly homogeneously Souslin sets that can be found in the literature can break down when there is no measurable cardinal: for example, according to
[
25
, Definition 33.32]
there are no weakly homogeneously Souslin sets if no measurable cardinal exists, while according to
[
29
, Definition 1.3.4]
, in such a situation the only weakly homogeneously Souslin set is the empty set.

The existence of a measurable cardinal unlocks the possibility of having nontrivial weakly homogeneously Souslin subsets of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
. Indeed, it is known that if there exists a measurable cardinal, then all
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}
sets are weakly homogeneously Souslin (see
[
26
, Exercises 32.1 and 31.3]
). Moreover, if larger cardinal assumptions are made, then more and more subsets of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
become weakly homogeneously Souslin. For example, Woodin showed that if there are
\omega
-many Woodin cardinals with a measurable cardinal above them, then all sets in
L(\mathbb{R})
are weakly homogeneously Souslin (see
[
26
, Theorem 32.13]
).

8.2.
Representability and
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable sets under
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)

In his seminal work
[
50
]
, Woodin recognized that under
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
there are strong similarities between the theory of
L(V_{\lambda+1})
, and the theory of
L(\mathbb{R})
under
{\sf AD}
.
For example, if
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds, then
L(V_{\lambda+1})\not\models{\sf AC}
but
L(V_{\lambda+1})\models{\sf DC}_{\lambda}
, and this is the analogue of the fact that under
{\sf AD}
we have
L(\mathbb{R})\not\models{\sf AC}
but
L(\mathbb{R})\models{\sf DC}_{\omega}
. Furthermore, under the mentioned assumptions we have that
\lambda^{+}
is measurable in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
, just as
\omega_{1}
is measurable in
L(\mathbb{R})
; a suitable version of Moschovakis’ Coding Lemma holds in both
L(V_{\lambda+1})
and
L(\mathbb{R})
; and so on — all of this is well described in
[
50
]
. To further his case, Woodin isolates in
[
50
, Definitions 103-111]
an analogue of weakly homogeneously Souslin sets in the
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
context, namely,
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable sets. The definition of
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representability is slightly more involved than our definition of
\mathbb{U}
-representability, and needs some understanding of the model
L(V_{\lambda+1})
.

Since under
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
the set
V_{\lambda+1}
is not well-orderable in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
, in analogy with
L(\mathbb{R})
we let

\Theta^{L(V_{\lambda+1})}=\sup\{\alpha\in\mathrm{Ord}\mid L(V_{\lambda+1})\models\text{``there is a surjection }\pi\colon V_{\lambda+1}\to\alpha\text{''}\}.

This is a way to define the smallest cardinal “larger” than
|V_{\lambda+1}|
in the case when the cardinality of
V_{\lambda+1}
, as computed in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
, is not a cardinal number (otherwise
\Theta
is just
|V_{\lambda+1}|^{+}
).

For the definition of
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representability, it is important to recall that if
j
is an elementary embedding that witnesses
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, then
j
is iterable (see
[
50
]
just before Definition 15 for the definition of an iterate, and Lemma 17 ibidem for the proof). We denote by
j^{n}
the
n
-th iterate of
j
.

The collection of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}(j)
is defined as a set of
L(V_{\lambda+1})
-ultrafilters that have the following three properties: they have size comparable to
V_{\lambda+1}
, they are fixed points for coboundedly many iterates of
j
, and they concentrate on elements that are fixed points for coboundedly many iterates of
j
(see
[
50
, Definition 103]
).

Definition 8.2.1
.

Given an elementary embedding
j
witnessing
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, let
\mathbb{U}(j)
be the collection of all
\mathcal{U}\in L(V_{\lambda+1})
such that in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
the following hold:

(a)

\mathcal{U}
is a
\lambda^{+}
-complete ultrafilter on some set
K_{\mathcal{U}}
satisfying
K_{\mathcal{U}}\in L_{\xi}(V_{\lambda+1})
, for some
\xi<\Theta^{L(V_{\lambda+1})}
;

(b)

for some
\gamma<\Theta^{L(V_{\lambda+1})}
,
\mathcal{U}
is generated by
\mathcal{U}\cap L_{\gamma}(V_{\lambda+1})
;

(c)

j^{n}(\mathcal{U})=\mathcal{U}
for all sufficiently large
n\in\omega
;

(d)

there is
A\in L(V_{\lambda+1})
such that
A\subseteq K_{\mathcal{U}}
and
\{a\in A\mid j^{n}(a)=a\}\in\mathcal{U}
.

Woodin then defines a subfamily of
\mathbb{U}(j)
, denoted by
\mathbb{U}(j,\kappa,(a_{i})_{i\in\omega})
, which is parametrized by a cardinal
\kappa<\Theta^{L(V_{\lambda+1})}
and a sequence
(a_{i})_{i\in\omega}
of elements of
L_{\kappa}(V_{\lambda+1})
. Its exact definition goes beyond our purposes, and will thus be omitted: indeed, we mention the families
\mathbb{U}(j,\kappa,(a_{i})_{i\in\omega})
only to faithfully report
[
50
, Definitions 110-111]
in the definition below, but for the results of this section it is enough to keep in mind that
\mathbb{U}(j,\kappa,(a_{i})_{i\in\omega})\subseteq\mathbb{U}(j)
.

Definition 8.2.2
.

Let
j
be a witness for
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
,
\kappa<\Theta^{L(V_{\lambda+1})}
, and
(a_{i})_{i\in\omega}
be a sequence of elements of
L_{\kappa}(V_{\lambda+1})
.
A set
Z\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
belonging to
L(V_{\lambda+1})
is
\mathbb{U}(j,\kappa,(a_{i})_{i\in\omega})
-representable
if there exists a strictly increasing sequence of infinite cardinals
(\lambda_{k})_{k\in\omega}
cofinal in
\lambda
and a function

\pi\colon\bigcup\{V_{\lambda_{k}+1}\times V_{\lambda_{k}+1}\times\{k\}\mid k\in\omega\}\to\mathbb{U}(j,\kappa,(a_{i})_{i\in\omega})

such that the following conditions hold:

(a)

for all
(a,b,k)\in\operatorname{dom}(\pi)
there exists a set
A_{a,b,k}\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{(L(V_{\lambda+1}))}
such that
A_{a,b,k}\in\pi(a,b,k)
;

(b)

for all
(a,b,k)\in\operatorname{dom}(\pi)
and
\ell<k
we have that
\pi(a,b,k)
projects onto
\pi(a\cap V_{\lambda_{\ell}},b\cap V_{\lambda_{\ell}},\ell)
;

(c)

for all
x\in V_{\lambda+1}
,
x\in Z
if and only if there exists
y\in V_{\lambda+1}
such that

•

(x\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},y\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},k)\in\operatorname{dom}(\pi)
for all
k\in\omega
, and

•

the tower of ultrafilters
(\pi(x\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},y\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},k))_{k\in\omega}
is well-founded.

A set
Z\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
belonging to
L(V_{\lambda+1})
is
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable
if and only if
it is
\mathbb{U}(j,\kappa,(a_{i})_{i\in\omega})
-representable for some
\kappa
and
(a_{i})_{i\in\omega}
as above.

We are going to show that our notion of
\mathbb{U}
-representability is aligned with Woodin’s Definition
8.2.2
, and since we will often work inside
L(V_{\lambda+1})
under
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, we will also make sure to avoid any use of
{\sf AC}
.
Notice that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
implies that
|V_{\lambda}|=\lambda
, and that any bijection witnessing this belongs to
V_{\lambda+1}
; therefore
V_{\lambda+1}
is a
\lambda
-Polish space homeomorphic to
B(\lambda)
in both
V
and
L(V_{\lambda+1})
(see Theorem
3.2.2
(iii)
), and it makes sense to consider
\mathbb{U}
-representable subsets of it.

First recall that for
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
ultrafilters on the sets
X
and
Y
, respectively, their
tensor product

\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{V}
is the ultrafilter on
X\times Y
defined by

A\in\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{V}\iff\{x\in X\mid A_{x}\in\mathcal{V}\}\in\mathcal{U},

where as usual
A_{x}
denotes the vertical section of
A
at
x
.
If
X={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}
and
Y={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\ell}{K}
for some set
K
and natural numbers
k,\ell\in\omega
, we let
\mathcal{U}{}^{\smallfrown}{}\mathcal{V}
be the ultrafilter on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k+\ell}{K}
defined by stipulating that
B\in\mathcal{U}{}^{\smallfrown}{}\mathcal{V}
if and only if
B
is of the form

B=\{s{}^{\smallfrown}{}t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k+\ell}{K}\mid(s,t)\in A\}

for some
A\in\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{V}
.
It is easy to verify that for every
\nu\in\mathrm{Card}
, the ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}{}^{\smallfrown}{}\mathcal{V}
is
\nu
-complete if so are
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
, that
\mathcal{U}{}^{\smallfrown}{}\mathcal{V}
projects onto
\mathcal{U}
, and that if
\mathcal{V}
projects onto
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
then
\mathcal{U}{}^{\smallfrown}{}\mathcal{V}
projects onto
\mathcal{U}{}^{\smallfrown}{}\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.
In particular, if
(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
and
(\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
are towers of ultrafilters in the same orderly family
\mathcal{U}
, then for every
n\in\omega
the family
(\mathcal{W}^{(n)}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
defined by

\mathcal{W}^{(n)}_{k}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{U}_{k}&\text{if }k\leq n\\
\mathcal{U}_{n}{}^{\smallfrown}{}\mathcal{V}_{k-(n+1)}&\text{if }k>n\end{cases}

is a tower of ultrafilters in some orderly family
\mathbb{U}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathbb{U}
with the same support
K
.

Proposition 8.2.3
.

Let
j
be a witness for
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, and let
Z\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
be a set in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
.
If
Z
is
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable, then
there is some orderly family of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}
such that

Z
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable (with respect to a suitable tree-basis
\mathcal{B}
for
V_{\lambda+1}
).

Proof.

Let
(\lambda_{k})_{k\in\omega}
and
\pi^{\prime}
witness that
Z
is
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable.
Recall from the proof of Theorem
3.2.2
(iii)
the sets
V^{-}_{\lambda_{k}+1}=\mathscr{P}(V_{\lambda_{k}}\setminus V_{\lambda_{k-1}})
, and for every
k\in\omega

fix a surjection
\sigma_{k}\colon\lambda\to V^{-}_{\lambda_{k}+1}
.
Recall also the basic open sets
O_{(a,\alpha)}
generating the Woodin’s topology on
V_{\lambda+1}
(see Example
3.1.2
(e)
), which are clearly clopen sets.
Let
U_{\emptyset}=V_{\lambda+1}
, and for every
k>0
and
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}
let
U_{s}=O_{(a(s),\alpha(s))}
, where
\alpha(s)=\lambda_{k-1}
and
a(s)=\bigcup_{\ell<k}\sigma_{\ell}(s(\ell))
.
Then
\mathcal{B}=\{U_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
is a tree-basis for
V_{\lambda+1}
, and clearly
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})=\bigcup_{k\in\omega}\sigma_{k}(\tilde{x}(k))
for every
\tilde{x}\in B(\lambda)
.

For each
(a,b,k)\in\operatorname{dom}(\pi^{\prime})
, let
K_{a,b,k}=\{u(\ell)\mid u\in A_{a,b,k}\wedge\ell<k\}
, where
A_{a,b,k}
is as in condition
(a)
of Definition
8.2.2
. Let
K=\bigcup_{(a,b,k)\in\operatorname{dom}(\pi^{\prime})}K_{a,b,k}
, and fix any element
p\in K
. Let
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
be the only proper ultrafilter on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{0}{K}=\{\emptyset\}
,
\overline{\mathcal{V}}=\{B\subseteq K\mid p\in B\}
be the principal ultrafilter on
K
generated by
p
, and for each
(a,b,k)\in\operatorname{dom}(\pi^{\prime})
, let
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,k}=\{B\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}\mid B\cap A_{a,b,k}\in\pi^{\prime}(a,b,k)\}
.
Finally, let

\mathbb{U}=\{\overline{\mathcal{U}},\overline{\mathcal{V}}\}\cup\{\overline{\mathcal{V}}{}^{\smallfrown}{}\mathcal{U}_{a,b,k}\mid(a,b,k)\in\operatorname{dom}(\pi^{\prime})\}.

By construction,
\mathbb{U}
is an orderly family with support
K
, and moreover

\operatorname{lev}(\overline{\mathcal{U}})=0
,
\operatorname{lev}(\overline{\mathcal{V}})=1
, and

\operatorname{lev}(\overline{\mathcal{V}}{}^{\smallfrown}{}\mathcal{U}_{a,b,k})=k+1
for every
(a,b,k)\in\operatorname{dom}(\pi^{\prime})
.
Now define

\pi\colon\bigcup_{k\in\omega}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}\lambda\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}\lambda)\to\mathbb{U}

by letting for every
k\in\omega
and
s,t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}
,
\pi(s,t)=\overline{\mathcal{U}}
if
k=0
,
\pi(s,t)=\overline{\mathcal{V}}
if
k=1
, and
\pi(s,t)=\overline{\mathcal{V}}{}^{\smallfrown}{}\mathcal{U}_{a(s),a(t),k-1}
if
k>1
.
We claim that
\pi
is a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for
Z
with respect to the tree-basis
\mathcal{B}
defined above, i.e. that conditions
(a)
–
(c)
from Definition
7.4.4
are satisfied for our choice of
\mathbb{U}
,
\mathcal{B}
, and
\pi
.

Condition
(a)
holds by construction. Notice also that all ultrafilters in
\mathbb{U}
are
\lambda^{+}
-complete, because so are
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
(being principal) and each
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,k}
(because
\pi(a,b,k)
, being a member of
\mathbb{U}(j)
, is
\lambda^{+}
-complete). In order to prove that
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}=(\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k))_{k\in\omega}
is a tower of ultrafilters for any
\tilde{x},y\in B(\lambda)
, it is enough to show that for every
1<\ell<k
, the ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,k-1}
projects onto
\mathcal{U}_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime},\ell-1}
, where
a=a(\tilde{x}\restriction k)
,
b=y\restriction k
,
a^{\prime}=a(\tilde{x}\restriction\ell)
, and
b^{\prime}=y\restriction\ell
. This follows from the fact that by condition
(b)
in Definition
8.2.2
,
\pi^{\prime}(a,b,k-1)
projects onto
\pi^{\prime}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},\ell-1)
because by construction
a^{\prime}=a\cap V_{\ell-1}
and
b^{\prime}=b\cap V_{\ell-1}
. This shows that condition
(b)
from Definition
7.4.4
holds as well. Finally, we check that also condition
(c)
from the same definition is satisfied.
Let
\tilde{x}\in B(\lambda)
, and let
x=f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})
. Suppose first that
x\in Z
. Let
y\in V_{\lambda+1}
be as in Definition
8.2.2
(c)
, and let
\tilde{y}\in B(\lambda)
be such that
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{y})=y
. By construction, for every
k\in\omega
we have that
x\cap V_{\lambda_{k}}=a(\tilde{x}\restriction k+1)
and
y\cap V_{\lambda_{k}}=a(\tilde{y}\restriction k+1)
. Since by choice of
y
the tower of ultrafilters
(\pi^{\prime}(x\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},y\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},k))_{k\in\omega}
is well-founded, so is the tower
(\mathcal{U}_{a(\tilde{x}\restriction k+1),a(\tilde{y}\restriction k+1),k})_{k\in\omega}
, which in turn implies that
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}
is well-founded. This proves the forward implication of Definition
7.4.4
(c)
. For the backward implication, we basically reverse the argument. If
\tilde{y}\in B(\lambda)
is such that
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}
is well-founded, then both
(\mathcal{U}_{a(\tilde{x}\restriction k+1),a(\tilde{y}\restriction k+1),k})_{k\in\omega}
and
(\pi^{\prime}(x\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},y\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},k))_{k\in\omega}
are well-founded, where again
y=f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{y})
; therefore
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})=x\in Z
by Definition
8.2.2
(c)
.
∎

Arguing as in Proposition
7.4.6
, it is easy to see that if there is some
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable
Z\subsetneq V_{\lambda+1}
, so that
Z
is also
\mathbb{U}
-representable for a suitable (necessarily nonprincipal) orderly family of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}
by Proposition
8.2.3
, then there is a measurable cardinal
\kappa>\lambda
, and one can assume that all ultrafilters appearing in the range of both the
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representation
\pi^{\prime}
and the
\mathbb{U}
-representation
\pi
for
Z
are at least
\kappa
-complete.
If
{\sf AC}
is assumed, then
\pi
would automatically have the tower condition (in the sense of our Definition
7.4.9
, see Proposition
7.4.11
), and hence every
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable set would be TC-representable. However,
L(V_{\lambda+1})
does not satisfy
{\sf AC}
if
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds, and that is why Woodin introduced his own version of the tower condition in
[
50
, Definition 121]
.

Definition 8.2.4
.

Let
j
be a witness for
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
. Let
\mathbb{V}\subseteq\mathbb{U}(j)
be such that
\mathbb{V}\in L(V_{\lambda+1})
and
|\mathbb{V}|\leq\lambda
. Then the
tower condition holds for
\mathbb{V}
if and only if there is a function
F\colon\mathbb{V}\to L(V_{\lambda+1})
such that

(a)

F(\mathcal{U})\in\mathcal{U}
for each
\mathcal{U}\in A
;
1
1

1

This requirement actually is not in the original definition in
[
50
]
, but it is in
[
7
, Definition 6.5]
, where the crucial Theorem
8.2.6
is proved.

(b)

for each tower of ultrafilters
2
2

2

As one can expect, this means that for some set
K
, each
\mathcal{U}_{k}
concentrates on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}
, and for every
k>\ell
the ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}_{k}
projects onto
\mathcal{U}_{\ell}
.

(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
in
\mathbb{V}
, the tower is well-founded in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
if and only if there exists
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{L(V_{\lambda+1})}
such that
z\restriction k\in F(\mathcal{U}_{k})
for every
k\in\omega
.

We are now going to show that, once again, Woodin’s Definition
8.2.4
aligns with our version of the tower condition from Definition
7.4.9
. Notice that if
\pi
witnesses that a set is
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable, then
|\operatorname{rng}(\pi)|\leq\lambda
,
and hence it makes sense to ask if Woodin’s tower condition holds for
\mathbb{V}=\operatorname{rng}(\pi)
.
As for Proposition
8.2.3
, also the next result is developed without assuming
{\sf AC}
, and can thus be applied e.g. in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
.

Proposition 8.2.5
.

Let
j
be a witness for
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, and let
Z\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
be a set in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
. If
\pi^{\prime}
witnesses that
Z
is
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable and the tower condition (in the sense of Definition
8.2.4
) holds for
\mathbb{V}=\operatorname{rng}(\pi^{\prime})
, then
Z
is TC-representable.

Proof.

We use the same notation as in the proof of Proposition
8.2.3
.
Let
F^{\prime}\colon\operatorname{rng}(\pi^{\prime})\to L(V_{\lambda+1})
witness the fact that the tower condition holds for
\operatorname{rng}(\pi^{\prime})
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
F^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime}(a,b,k))\in\mathcal{U}_{a,b,k}
: if not, just systematically replace
F^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime}(a,b,k))
with
F^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime}(a,b,k))\cap A_{a,b,k}
.
Let
\pi
be the
\mathbb{U}
-representation of
Z
obtained from
\pi^{\prime}
as in the proof of Proposition
8.2.3
. Set
F(\pi(\emptyset,\emptyset))=\{\emptyset\}
,
F(\pi(s,t))=\{p\}
for every
s,t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{1}{\lambda}
, and for every
k>1
and
s,t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}
let

F(\pi(s,t))=p{}^{\smallfrown}{}F^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime}(a(s),a(t),k-1)),

where for
B\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k-1}{K}
we let
p{}^{\smallfrown}{}B=\{p{}^{\smallfrown}{}u\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}\mid u\in B\}
. Notice that in all cases,
F(\pi(s,t))\in\pi(s,t)
by construction.
Recalling that for every
x,y\in V_{\lambda+1}
and every
\tilde{x},\tilde{y}\in B(\lambda)
such that
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})=x
and
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{y})=y
, the tower of ultrafilters
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}
is well-founded if and only if so is
(\pi^{\prime}(x\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},y\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},k))_{k\in\omega}
, it is easy to see that
F
satisfies also condition
(b)
of Definition
7.4.9
.
Indeed, suppose that
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K}
is such that

z\restriction k\in F(\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,\tilde{y}\restriction k))

for all
k\in\omega
.
Then
z(0)=p
by choice of
F
, and thus there is
z^{\prime}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K}
such that
z=p{}^{\smallfrown}{}z^{\prime}
. But then by construction

z^{\prime}\restriction k\in F^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime}(x\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},y\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},k))

for all
k\in\omega
.
It follows that
(\pi^{\prime}(x\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},y\cap V_{\lambda_{k}},k))_{k\in\omega}
is well-founded, and hence so is
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}
.
∎

The above discussion naturally raises the question of which
\mathbb{V}\subseteq\mathbb{U}(j)
satisfy Woodin’s tower condition. Immediately after
[
50
, Definition 121]
, it is observed that, assuming
{\sf DC}_{\lambda}
, this is always true if
|\mathbb{V}|<\lambda
, and from this Woodin derived a rather weak form of the
\lambda
-Perfect Set Property for all
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable sets (see Lemma
8.3.1
).
Later on, Scott Cramer conclusively answered the above question by proving that, indeed, all families
\mathbb{V}
as in Definition
8.2.4
satisfy the tower condition
[
7
, Theorem 6.8]
.

Theorem 8.2.6

(
{\sf DC}_{\lambda}
)
.

Let
j
be a witness for
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, and work in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
.
3
3

3

This means that we only consider sets
Z\in L(V_{\lambda+1})
, and that both the
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representability and the
\mathbb{U}
-representability have to be verified in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
.

Then Woodin’s tower condition holds for every
\mathbb{V}\subseteq\mathbb{U}(j)
such that
|\mathbb{V}|\leq\lambda
.

Since if
\pi
witnesses that a set
X\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
is
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable then
\mathbb{V}=\operatorname{rng}(\pi)
has size at most
\lambda
,
combining Proposition
8.2.3
and Theorem
8.2.6
we obtain:

Corollary 8.2.7

(
{\sf DC}_{\lambda}
)
.

Let
j
be a witness for
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, and work in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
. If
Z\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
is
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable, then
Z
is also TC-representable.

8.3.
Which sets are
\mathbb{U}
-representable?

The base theory for both weakly homogeneously Souslin-ness and
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representability is
{\sf ZFC}
, and for this reason
we will assume
{\sf AC}
throughout this section and the next one, unless otherwise specified.

In classical descriptive set theory, regularity properties like the Perfect Set Property tend to exhibit the following pattern:

(i)

All analytic sets enjoy the given regularity property (see
[
26
, Theorem 12.2]
).

(ii)

If
\mathsf{V=L}
, then there is a low-level projective set without the regularity property (see
[
26
, Corollary 13.10 and Theorem 13.12]
).
For example, if the regularity property is the Perfect Set Property, then in the constructible universe
L
we have a coanalytic counterexample, while if we consider e.g. the Baire Property or Lebesgue Measurability, then we have counterexamples within the pointclass
\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{1}_{2}
.

(iii)

If we assume sufficiently strong large cardinal assumptions, then all definable sets have the regularity property at hand. For example,
if there are
\omega
-many Woodin cardinals and a measurable above, then
L(\mathbb{R})\models{\sf AD}
, and therefore all the subsets of
\mathbb{R}
in
L(\mathbb{R})
, projective sets included, enjoy the regularity property (see e.g.
[
41
, Theorem 8.24]
; the proof that determined sets enjoy all the main regularity properties can be found e.g. in
[
28
, Chapter 21]
).

One of the most interesting proof of
(iii)
factors through the notion of weakly homogeneously Souslin set briefly discussed in Section
8.1
. Indeed, weakly homogeneously Souslin sets themselves follow a pattern very similar to the one above:

(I)

If there exists a measurable cardinal, then all analytic sets are weakly homogeneously Souslin; in fact, all
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{2}
sets are (see e.g.
[
26
, Exercises 32.1 and 32.3]
).

(II)

If
\mathsf{V=L}
, no nontrivial set is weakly homogeneously Souslin: this is because in
L
there are no measurable cardinals, and hence all ultrafilters appearing in the definition of weakly homogeneusly Souslin-ness are necessarily principal.

(III)

If there are
\omega
-many Woodin cardinals and a measurable above, all sets in
L(\mathbb{R})
are weakly homogeneously Souslin (see e.g.
[
26
, Theorem 32.13]
).

Since every weakly homogeneously Souslin set enjoy all the main regularity properties (
[
26
, Theorem 32.7]
), we can think of weakly homogeneous Souslin-ness as a sort of “super” regularity property.
Note that the large cardinal hypothesis for
(III)
is the same implying
L(\mathbb{R})\models{\sf AD}
in
(iii)
above.

The goal of this section is to observe that, remarkably, we have a very similar situation when moving to the generalized context, as long as we consider the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
as our regularity property, and
\mathbb{U}
-representable and TC-representable sets as higher analogues of weakly homogeneously Souslin sets (see Proposition
8.1.3
and the discussion following it). Indeed, we already proved that all
\lambda
-analytic sets have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
(Theorem
7.1.1
), and that there are
\lambda
-coanalytics sets without the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
if
\mathsf{V=L}
(Corollary
7.2.11
);
these results are the exact counterparts of
(i)
and
(ii)
above.
As for
(iii)
, there has been a lot of work aiming at showing that we can get analogues of it in the generalized context if we assume
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
and we replace the inner model
L(\mathbb{R})
with its higher analogue
L(V_{\lambda+1})
.
The first result of this kind is due to Woodin and appears in
[
50
, Lemma 122]
.

Lemma 8.3.1

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Let
j
be a witness for
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, and let
Z\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
be
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
. If
|Z|>\lambda
, then there is a continuous injective function from
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}2
into
Z
.

The proof of Lemma
8.3.1
exploits the representability structure and the fact that the tower condition in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
holds for every
\mathbb{V}\subseteq\mathbb{U}(j)
with
|\mathbb{V}|<\lambda
. Notice that the property described in the conclusion of the theorem is much weaker than the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
discussed in this paper, yet it was the first indication that
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable sets have interesting regularity properties. Another attempt was made by Shi in
[
45
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Theorem 8.3.2

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Assume that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds. Let
Z\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
be a set in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
that is definable in
V_{\lambda+1}
(possibly with parameters in
V_{\lambda+1}
). If
|Z|>\lambda
, then there is a continuous injective function from
C(\lambda)
into
Z
.

In view of Theorem
3.2.2
and Corollary
7.1.3
, Shi’s theorem essentially states that all
\lambda
-projective sets satisfy the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
. The proof uses generic absoluteness for
\mathsf{I0}
, a deep result about the structure of
L(V_{\lambda+1})\cap V_{\lambda+2}
, and the generic extensions of its iterations. Shi comments that the same proof can be used for a larger class of subsets of
V_{\lambda+1}
: if
\gamma
is closed enough and every
A\in L_{\gamma}(V_{\lambda+1})
is
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable, then all the subsets of
V_{\lambda+1}
belonging to
L_{\gamma}(V_{\lambda+1})
have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
. Finally, the conclusive theorem is obtained by Cramer in
[
7
, Theorem 5.1]
.

Theorem 8.3.3

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Assume that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds. Let
Z\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
be any set in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
. If
|Z|>\lambda
, then there is a continuous injective function from
B(\lambda)
into
Z
.

Using again Theorem
3.2.2
and Corollary
7.1.3
, this gives the ultimate result in this direction and an exact analogue of
(iii)
in the generalized context (under different large cardinal assumptions, of course).
Notably, Theorem
8.3.3
has no
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representability assumption on the set
Z
: its proof heavily uses the elementary embedding
j
that witnesses
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, and its manipulations via inverse limits.

Remark 8.3.4
.

When dealing with
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, there are two models of reference, namely, the universe of sets
V
, in which we assume
{\sf ZFC}
, and the inner model
L(V_{\lambda+1})
, which instead only satisfies
{\sf ZF}+{\sf DC}_{\lambda}
. Therefore, in principle we should be careful when considering the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
, and always specify in which of the two models we intend it to be considered.
However, if we restrict the attention to subsets of, say,
4
4

4

Similar observations hold if we replace
B(\lambda)
with one of the spaces
C(\lambda)
,
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, or
V_{\lambda+1}
.

the generalized Baire space
B(\lambda)
, the situation is rather indifferent: if
A\subseteq B(\lambda)
belongs to
V_{\lambda+1}
, then
A
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
in
V_{\lambda+1}
if and only if it has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
in
V
.

To see this, the first thing to notice is that
every function
\phi\colon\lambda\to\lambda
belongs to
V_{\lambda+1}
, as its elements are pair of ordinals in
\lambda\times\lambda
, which thus belong to
V_{\lambda}
. It readily follows that

B(\lambda)^{L(V_{\lambda+1})}=B(\lambda)^{V}
. Also, since Theorem
3.2.2
is true in both
V
and
L(V_{\lambda+1})
, it is enough to consider the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}^{*}
instead of the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
(see the discussion after Definition
7.0.1
). Now it is easy to check that the witnesses of the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
(in whatever model we are considering it) can be canonically coded as functions from
\lambda
into itself. Indeed, if
f\colon B(\lambda)\to B(\lambda)
is an embedding of
B(\lambda)
into
A
, then it is coded by the function
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\to{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
sending each
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
into the longest sequence
t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
such that
\operatorname{lh}(t)\leq\operatorname{lh}(s)
and
f(\boldsymbol{N}_{s})\subseteq\boldsymbol{N}_{t}
, which in turn can be converted into a function
\phi\colon\lambda\to\lambda
by identifying
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
with
\lambda
in the usual way.
If instead
|A|\leq\lambda
, then
A
can be identified with a sequence
(x_{\beta})_{\beta<\lambda}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{(B(\lambda))}={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda})}
, which can then be canonically turned into a function
\phi\colon\lambda\to\lambda
using a suitable bijection between
\lambda\times\omega
and
\lambda
. Our claim easily follows from the fact that, in any case,
\phi\in L(V_{\lambda+1})
.

Combining this observation with Corollary
7.1.4
, one sees that when
X
is a
\lambda
-Polish space belonging to
L(V_{\lambda+1})
and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass closed under
\lambda
-Borel preimages and such that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)\subseteq L(V_{\lambda+1})
, we can unambiguously say that all sets in
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(X)
have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
, without having to specify whether the truth of such assertion must be computed in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
or in
V
.

The tools developed in this paper allow us to see that, in analogy with what happens in the classical case, also Theorem
8.3.3
has a proof that factors through representability. Indeed, we already showed in Theorem
7.4.12
that all TC-representable sets have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
, so it becomes crucial to understand which sets are representable, in one sense or another. Notice that the first part of the next result does not need any strong choice assumption, and thus it works also in inner models like
L(V_{\lambda+1})
.

Proposition 8.3.5
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and assume that there is a measurable cardinal
\kappa>\lambda
. Let
X
be any
\lambda
-Polish space. Then there is an orderly family of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}
such that all
\lambda
-analytic subsets of
X
are
\mathbb{U}
-representable (with respect to any tree-basis for
X
). If moreover we assume
{\sf AC}
, then every
Z\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
is TC-representable.

Proof.

The additional part concerning TC-representability follows from the first one and Proposition
7.4.11
.

The following construction is somewhat implicit in Martin’s proof that the existence of a measurable cardinal implies that every coanalytic set is determined (see
[
26
, Theorem 31.1]
). Let
\mathcal{U}
be a normal ultrafilter on
\kappa
. Recall that for every set
H
and every
k\in\omega
, the symbol
[H]^{k}
denotes the collection of all subsets of
H
of size
k
. When
H\subseteq\kappa
, we also let

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\uparrow k}{H}

\displaystyle=\{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{H}\mid\forall i<j<k\,(s(i)<s(k))\},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\downarrow k}{H}

\displaystyle=\{s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{H}\mid\forall i<j<k\,(s(i)>s(k))\}

be the sets of all sequences in
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{H}
which are strinctly increasing and strictly decreasing, respectively. Clearly,
[H]^{k}
can be naturally identified with both
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\uparrow k}{H}
and
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\downarrow k}{H}
.
By Rowbottom’s Theorem (see
[
26
, Theorem 7.17]
), both

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{k}

\displaystyle=\{A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\kappa}\mid{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\uparrow k}{H}\subseteq A\text{ for some }H\in\mathcal{U}\},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{k}

\displaystyle=\{A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\kappa}\mid{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\downarrow k}{H}\subseteq A\text{ for some }H\in\mathcal{U}\}

are
\kappa
-complete ultrafilters on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\kappa}
, and it is easy to see that if
k>\ell
then
\mathcal{U}_{k}
projects onto
\mathcal{U}_{\ell}
and, similarly,
\mathcal{V}_{k}
projects onto
\mathcal{V}_{\ell}
. This shows that both
(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
and
(\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
are towers of
\kappa
-complete ultrafilters, the difference being that the former is well-founded (because
\mathcal{U}
is
\kappa
-complete and
\kappa>\omega
), while the latter is not (because there are no decreasing infinite sequences of ordinals).

Recall from the paragraph preceding Proposition
8.2.3
the “concatenation” operation
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V})\mapsto\mathcal{U}{}^{\smallfrown}{}\mathcal{V}
for ultrafilters based on the tensor product, and the “mixed” towers of
\kappa
-complete ultrafilters
(\mathcal{W}^{(n)}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
obtained from the two towers
(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
and
(\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
, which in our cases are always non-well-founded because so is
(\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
.
Let
\mathbb{U}=\{\mathcal{W}^{(n)}_{k}\mid n\leq k\}
,
and notice that the support of the orderly family
\mathbb{U}
is
K=\kappa
.
We claim that every
Z\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to any tree-basis
\mathcal{B}
for
X
.

Let
Z^{\prime}=f_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(Z)
. Then
Z^{\prime}\in\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(B(\lambda))
, hence there is an
\omega
-tree
T
on
\lambda\times\lambda
such that
Z^{\prime}=p[T]
.
For each
k\in\omega
and
s,t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}
, let
\pi(s,t)=\mathcal{W}^{(n)}_{k}
, where
n\leq k
is largest such that
(s\restriction n,t\restriction n)\in T
. We claim that
\pi
is a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for
Z
.

Consider any
\tilde{x}\in B(\lambda)
. If
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})\in Z
, then
\tilde{x}\in Z^{\prime}
and hence there is
y\in B(\lambda)
such that
(\tilde{x},y)\in[T]
. Then
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}=(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
is well-founded.
If instead
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})\notin Z
, then
\tilde{x}\notin Z^{\prime}
, hence for every
y\in B(\lambda)
we must have
(\tilde{x},y)\notin[T]
. Therefore
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}=(\mathcal{W}^{(n)}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
, where
n\in\omega
is largest such that
(\tilde{x}\restriction n,y\restriction n)\in T
, and so
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is not well-founded.
∎

Next we show that, under the same hypotheses, representable sets give rise to a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass whose closure properties are only slightly weaker than those of
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}
(see Proposition
5.2.1
).
For comparison, we mention that in
[
50
, Lemmas 114 and 115]
it has been shown that some of these closure properties are enjoyed also by the collection of
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable subsets of
V_{\lambda+1}
.

Proposition 8.3.6
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
, and assume that there is a measurable cardinal
\kappa>\lambda
. For any
\lambda
-Polish space
X
, let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}(X)
be the collection of all
Z\subseteq X
which are
\mathbb{U}
-representable (with respect to
some
tree-basis
\mathcal{B}
for
X
), where
\mathbb{U}
varies over all orderly families of ultrafilters. Then:

(i)

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}
is a boldface
\lambda
-poinclass containing
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}
, in the sense that
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)\subseteq\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}(X)
for every
\lambda
-Polish space
X
.

(ii)

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}
is closed under projections, well-ordered unions of length at most
\lambda
, and finite intersections.

(iii)

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}
is closed under
\lambda
-Borel images and preimages, in the following strong sense.
Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a
\lambda
-Borel function between the
\lambda
-Polish spaces
X
and
Y
. Suppose that
\mathcal{B}_{X}
and
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
are tree-bases for
X
and
Y
, respectively.

•

If
Z\subseteq X
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X}
, then
f(Z)
is
\mathbb{V}
-respresentable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
, for some orderly family of ultrafilters
\mathbb{V}
.

•

Conversely, if
W\subseteq Y
is
\mathbb{V}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
, then
f^{-1}(W)
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
, for some orderly family of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}
.

If moreover we assume
{\sf AC}
,
5
5

5

Notice that
{\sf AC}
is needed only to show the inclusion
\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)\subseteq\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}(X)
(which follows from Proposition
8.3.5
), which in turn is used to show that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{TC}}
is closed under
\lambda
-Borel images and preimages. However, our proof shows that even in a choiceless setting,
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{TC}}
is closed under projections, unions of size
\lambda
, and finite intersections. Moreover, a different argument allows one to check that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{TC}}
is at least closed under continuous preimages, i.e. it is a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass.
then the same holds for the pointclass
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{TC}}
obtained by letting
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{TC}}(X)
be the collection of all TC-representable sets
Z\subseteq X
.

In particular, by Proposition
8.3.6
(iii)
the property of being representable is independent of the chosen tree-basis on the space.

Proof.

The Gödel pairing function
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon\lambda\times\lambda\to\lambda
canonical induces bijections
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}\to{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\lambda}
and
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}\to{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}
, for every
k\in\omega
, which will still be denoted by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
; their inverses will be denoted by
(\cdot)_{0}
and
(\cdot)_{1}
, respectively, so that
\langle(x)_{0},(x)_{1}\rangle=x
for all elements
x
of interest.

We first prove that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}
is closed under projections. Given two tree-bases
\mathcal{B}_{X}=\{U_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
and
\mathcal{B}_{Y}=\{V_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
for the
\lambda
-Polish spaces
X
and
Y
, respectively, we let
\mathcal{B}_{X\times Y}=\{W_{s}\mid s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}\}
be the tree-basis
6
6

6

To have that
\mathcal{B}_{X\times Y}
satisfies condition
(d)
of Definition
3.2.7
, the product
X\times Y
has to be equipped with the sup metric.

for their product
X\times Y
canonically induced by
\mathcal{B}_{X}
and
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
, namely,
W_{s}=U_{(s)_{0}}\times V_{(s)_{1}}
for every
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{<\omega}{\lambda}
.
Notice that for every
\tilde{x}\in B(\lambda)
, we have
f_{\mathcal{B}_{X\times Y}}(\tilde{x})=(f_{\mathcal{B}_{X}((\tilde{x})_{0})},f_{\mathcal{B}_{Y}}((\tilde{x})_{1}))
.

Claim 8.3.6.1
.

If
C\subseteq X\times Y
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X\times Y}
, then
p(C)
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X}
and, similarly, the projection
p_{2}(C)
of
C
onto
Y
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
.

Proof of the claim.

Let
\pi^{\prime}
be a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for
C
with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X\times Y}
. Define
\pi\colon\bigcup_{k\in\omega}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda})\to\mathbb{U}
by setting

\pi(s,t)=\pi^{\prime}(\langle s,(t)_{0}\rangle,(t)_{1}).

Then
\operatorname{rng}(\pi)=\operatorname{rng}(\pi^{\prime})=\mathbb{U}
, and
since for every
\tilde{x},y\in B(\lambda)
we have
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}=T^{\pi^{\prime}}_{\langle\tilde{x},(y)_{0}\rangle,(y)_{1}}
and
T^{\pi^{\prime}}_{\tilde{x},y}=T^{\pi}_{(\tilde{x})_{0},\langle(\tilde{x})_{1},y\rangle}
, one can easily check that
\pi
is a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for
p(C)
.
The case of
p_{2}(C)
is similar, the only difference being that we have to set
\pi(s,t)=\pi^{\prime}(\langle(t)_{0},s\rangle,(t)_{1})
.
∎

Conversely, we have the following result.

Claim 8.3.6.2
.

If
C\subseteq X
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X}
, then
C\times Y
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X\times Y}
, where
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
is any tree-basis for
Y
.

Proof of the claim.

Let
\pi^{\prime}
be a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for
C
with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X}
.
Let
\pi\colon\bigcup_{k\in\omega}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda})\to\mathbb{U}
be defined by

\pi(s,t)=\pi^{\prime}((s)_{0},t).

Then
\operatorname{rng}(\pi)=\operatorname{rng}(\pi^{\prime})=\mathbb{U}
, and
\pi
is a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for
C\times Y
with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X\times Y}
.
∎

Of course, by a similar argument we have that if
D\subseteq Y
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
, then
X\times D
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X\times Y}
, where
\mathcal{B}_{X}
is any tree-basis for
X
.

Next we show that each
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}
is closed under finite intersections.
Given two orderly family of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}
and
\mathbb{V}
with support
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
, respectively, we can consider their tensor product

\mathbb{U}\otimes\mathbb{V}=\{\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{V}\mid{\mathcal{U}\in\mathbb{U}}\wedge{\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}}\wedge{\operatorname{lev}(\mathcal{U})=\operatorname{lev}(\mathcal{V})}\}.

As written,
\mathbb{U}\otimes\mathbb{V}
is not an orderly family of ultrafilters, as if
\operatorname{lev}(\mathcal{U})=\operatorname{lev}(\mathcal{V})=k
then
\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{V}
is an ultrafilter on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K_{0}}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K_{1}}
. However, such an ultrafilter can be straightforwardly identified with an ultrafilter on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{(K_{0}\times K_{1})}
, still denoted by
\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{V}
: under this identification,
\mathbb{U}\otimes\mathbb{V}
becomes an orderly family of ultrafilters with support
K_{0}\times K_{1}
, and
\operatorname{lev}(\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{V})=\operatorname{lev}(\mathcal{U})=\operatorname{lev}(\mathcal{V})
.

In the proof of the following claim we will tacitly use the above identification, and freely switch from one presentation (and notation) to the other one when convenient.
We will also need the following general facts concerning tensor products.
First of all, for any cardinal
\kappa
we have that
\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{V}
is
\kappa
-complete if and only if so are
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
projects onto
\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{V}
whenever
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
projects onto
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
projects onto
\mathcal{V}
. In particular, if
(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
is a tower of ultrafilters in
\mathbb{U}
and
(\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
is a tower of ultrafilters in
\mathbb{V}
, then
(\mathcal{U}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
is a tower of ultrafilters in
\mathbb{U}\otimes\mathbb{V}
. Finally, the tower
(\mathcal{U}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
is well-founded if and only if so are
(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
and
(\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
.
To see this, suppose that both
(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
and
(\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
are well-founded, and
pick any sequence of sets
(A_{k})_{k\in\omega}
with
A_{k}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{k}
. For each
k\in\omega
, let
B_{k}^{(0)}=\{b\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K_{0}}\mid(A_{k})_{b}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}\}
, so that
B^{(0)}_{k}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
by choice of
A_{k}
. Since
(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
is well-founded, there is
z_{0}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K_{0}}
such that
z_{0}\restriction k\in B^{(0)}_{k}
for every
k\in\omega
.
Let
B^{(1)}_{k}=(A_{k})_{z_{0}\restriction k}
, so that
B^{(1)}_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
. Since
(\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
is well-founded, there is
z_{1}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K_{1}}
such that
z_{1}\restriction k\in B^{(1)}_{k}
for every
k\in\omega
.
Let
z=(z_{0}(k),z_{1}(k))_{k\in\omega}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(K_{0}\times K_{1})}
.
Then
z\restriction k\in A_{k}
for every
k\in\omega
, and since
(A_{k})_{k\in\omega}
was arbitrary, this shows that
(\mathcal{U}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
is well-founded too. Conversely, pick any
(A^{(0)}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
and
(A^{(1)}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
such that
A^{(0)}_{k}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
and
A^{(1)}_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
for every
k\in\omega
. Then
A_{k}=A^{(0)}_{k}\times A^{(1)}_{k}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{k}
.
If
(\mathcal{U}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
is well-founded, there is
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(K_{0}\times K_{1})}
such that
z\restriction k\in A_{k}
for every
k\in\omega
. Let
z_{0}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K_{0}}
and
z_{1}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K_{1}}
be such that
z(k)=(z_{0}(k),z_{1}(k))
for every
k\in\omega
. Then
z_{0}\restriction k\in A^{(0)}_{k}
and
z_{1}\restriction k\in A^{(1)}_{k}
by choice of
A_{k}
. Since
(A^{(0)}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
and
(A^{(1)}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
were arbitrary, this shows that both
(\mathcal{U}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
and
(\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k\in\omega}
are well-founded.

Claim 8.3.6.3
.

If
C\subseteq X
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to some tree-basis
\mathcal{B}
for
X
and
D\subseteq X
is
\mathbb{V}
-representable with respect to the same
\mathcal{B}
, then
C\cap D
is
\mathbb{U}\otimes\mathbb{V}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof of the claim.

Let
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
be the supports of
\mathbb{U}
and
\mathbb{V}
, respectively.
Given a
\mathbb{U}
-representation
\pi_{C}
for
C
with
\operatorname{rng}(\pi_{C})=\mathbb{U}
and a
\mathbb{V}
-representation
\pi_{D}
for
D
with
\operatorname{rng}(\pi_{D})=\mathbb{V}
, let
\pi\colon\bigcup_{k\in\omega}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda})\to\mathbb{U}\otimes\mathbb{V}
be defined by

(8.1)

\pi(s,t)=\pi_{C}(s,(t)_{0})\otimes\pi_{D}(s,(t)_{1}).

Then
\operatorname{rng}(\pi)\subseteq\mathbb{U}\otimes\mathbb{V}
, and we claim that
\pi
is a
\mathbb{U}\otimes\mathbb{V}
-representation for
C\cap D
.
Fix any
\tilde{x},y\in B(\lambda)
. Since

(8.2)

T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}=(\pi_{C}(\tilde{x}\restriction k,(y)_{0}\restriction k)\otimes\pi_{D}(\tilde{x}\restriction k,(y)_{1}\restriction k))_{k\in\omega},

we get that
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is a tower of ultrafilters because so are
T^{\pi_{C}}_{\tilde{x},(y)_{0}}
and
T^{\pi_{D}}_{\tilde{x},(y)_{1}}
.
Moreover, each
\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k)
is at least
\lambda_{k}^{+}
-complete because so are
\pi_{C}(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k)
and
\pi_{D}(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k)
.

Consider any
\tilde{x}\in B(\lambda)
. If
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})\in C\cap D
, then there are
y_{0},y_{1}\in B(\lambda)
such that both
T^{\pi_{C}}_{\tilde{x},y_{0}}
and
T^{\pi_{D}}_{\tilde{x},y_{1}}
are well-founded.
It follows that there is also
y\in B(\lambda)
such that
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded: by (
8.2
), it is enough to set
y=\langle y_{0},y_{1}\rangle
.
Conversely, suppose that
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded for some
y\in B(\lambda)
.
Then
T^{\pi_{C}}_{\tilde{x},(y)_{0}}
and
T^{\pi_{D}}_{\tilde{x},(y)_{1}}
are well-founded (because of (
8.2
) again), and hence
(y)_{0}
witnesses
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})\in C
, while
(y)_{1}
witness
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})\in D
. Therefore
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})\in C\cap D
, and the proof is complete.
∎

We are now ready to show that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}
is closed under
\lambda
-Borel images and preimages, in the sense of
(iii)
. First suppose that
Z\subseteq X
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to a given tree-basis
\mathcal{B}_{X}
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
be a tree-basis for the
\lambda
-Polish space
Y
, and let
f\colon X\to Y
be a
\lambda
-Borel function, so that its graph
D=\mathrm{graph}(f)
is
\lambda
-Borel in
X\times Y
by Proposition
4.1.3
.
By Claim
8.3.6.2
, the set
C=Z\times Y
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X\times Y}
, while by Proposition
8.3.5
there is an orderly family of ultrafilters
\mathbb{U}^{\prime}
such that
D
is
\mathbb{U}^{\prime}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X,Y}
.
Then by Claim
8.3.6.3
the set
C\cap D
is
\mathbb{V}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X\times Y}
, where
\mathbb{V}=\mathbb{U}\otimes\mathbb{U}^{\prime}
. Then
f(Z)=p_{2}(C\cap D)
is
\mathbb{V}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
by Claim
8.3.6.1
. The case of preimages under
f
is similar. If
W\subseteq Y
is
\mathbb{V}
representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
, then
(X\times W)\cap D
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X\times Y}
for
\mathbb{U}=\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{U}^{\prime}
, and hence
f^{-1}(W)=p((X\times W)\cap D)
is
\mathbb{U}
-representable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{X}
.

Closure under
\lambda
-Borel preimages entails that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}
is a boldface
\lambda
-poinclass. Together with the fact that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}(X)\supseteq\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(X)
by Proposition
8.3.5
, this proves
(i)
.

As for
(ii)
, we already proved closure under projections and finite intersections in Claim
8.3.6.1
and Claim
8.3.6.3
, respectively, so we only need to show that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}(X)
is closed under well-ordered unions of size
\lambda
. In what follows, all representations refer to a tree-basis
\mathcal{B}
for
X
fixed in advance..
Suppose that for every
\alpha<\lambda
we are given a set
Z_{\alpha}\subseteq X
and a
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}
-representation
\pi_{\alpha}
for
Z_{\alpha}
, where
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}=\operatorname{rng}(\pi_{\alpha})
is an orderly family of ultrafilters with support
K_{\alpha}
. Let
K=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}K_{\alpha}
.
For each
\alpha<\lambda
, let
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}^{+}=\{\mathcal{U}^{+}\mid\mathcal{U}\in\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}\}
, where if
\operatorname{lev}(\mathcal{U})=k
we let
\mathcal{U}^{+}=\{A\subseteq{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K}\mid A\cap{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{K_{\alpha}}\in\mathcal{U}\}
. Then
\mathbb{U}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}\mathbb{U}^{+}_{\alpha}
is an orderly family of ultrafilters with support
K
.
For each
\alpha<\lambda
, the map
\pi_{\alpha}^{+}\colon\bigcup_{k\in\omega}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda})\to\mathbb{U}^{+}_{\alpha}
defined by
\pi_{\alpha}^{+}(s,t)=(\pi_{\alpha}(s,t))^{+}
is a
\mathbb{U}^{+}_{\alpha}
-representation for
Z_{\alpha}
with
\operatorname{rng}(\pi^{+}_{\alpha})=\mathbb{U}^{+}_{\alpha}
. Define
\pi\colon\bigcup_{k\in\omega}({\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}\times{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda})\to\mathbb{U}
by letting
\pi(\emptyset,\emptyset)
be the only proper ultrafilter on
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{0}{K}=\{\emptyset\}
, and

(8.3)

\pi(s,t)=\pi^{+}_{(t(0))_{0}}(s,(t)_{1})

for every
s,t\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{k}{\lambda}
with
k>0
.
Then
\operatorname{rng}(\pi)=\mathbb{U}
, and
we claim that
\pi
is a
\mathbb{U}
-representation for
Z=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}Z_{\alpha}
.

Fix any
\tilde{x}\in B(\lambda)
.
Suppose first that
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})\in Z_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha<\lambda
. Let
y\in B(\lambda)
be such that
T^{\pi^{+}_{\alpha}}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded, and let
y^{\prime}\in B(\lambda)
be such that
(y^{\prime})_{1}=y
and
(y^{\prime}(0))_{0}=\alpha
. Then
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y^{\prime}}=T^{\pi^{+}_{\alpha}}_{\tilde{x},y}
, and thus
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y^{\prime}}
is well-founded too.
Conversely, suppose that there is
y\in B(\lambda)
such that
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
is well-founded, and let
\alpha=(y(0))_{0}
. Since
T^{\pi^{+}_{\alpha}}_{\tilde{x},(y)_{1}}=T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
, the tower
T^{\pi^{+}_{\alpha}}_{\tilde{x},(y)_{1}}
is well-founded as well, so
f_{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{x})\in Z_{\alpha}\subseteq Z
.

This concludes the proof for what concerns
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}
. The case of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{TC}}
can be treated similarly, the only difference being that we have to additionally check that the tower condition is preserved along the various transformations of the orderly families of ultrafilters involved. This is trivial for Claim
8.3.6.1
and Claim
8.3.6.2
, as the family remain the same.
Moving to Claim
8.3.6.3
, let
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
on
\mathbb{U}
and
\mathbb{V}
witnessing that
\pi_{C}
and
\pi_{D}
have the tower condition, and let
\pi
be obtained from
\pi_{C}
and
\pi_{D}
as in (
8.1
). Define
F
on
\mathbb{U}\otimes\mathbb{V}
setting
F(\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{V})=F_{0}(\mathcal{U})\times F_{1}(\mathcal{V})
: we claim that the restriction of
F
to
\operatorname{rng}(\pi)
witnesses that
\pi
has the tower condition.
Suppose to have a tower
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
such that there is
z\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{(K_{0}\times K_{1})}
satisfying
z\restriction k\in F(\pi(\tilde{x}\restriction k,y\restriction k))
for every
k\in\omega
. Let
z_{0}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K_{0}}
and
z_{1}\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{K_{1}}
be such that
z(k)=(z_{0}(k),z_{1}(k))
for each
k\in\omega
. Then
z_{0}\restriction k\in F_{0}(\pi_{C}(\tilde{x}\restriction k,(y)_{0}\restriction k))
and
z_{1}\restriction k\in F_{1}(\pi_{C}(\tilde{x}\restriction k,(y)_{1}\restriction k))
for all
k\in\omega
, hence
T^{\pi_{C}}_{\tilde{x},(y)_{0}}
and
T^{\pi_{D}}_{\tilde{x},(y)_{1}}
are well-founded, and thus so is
T^{\pi}_{\tilde{x},y}
by (
8.2
).

The last case that requires attention is closure under unions of size
\lambda
. Suppose that for each
\alpha<\lambda
there is a function
F_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}
witnessing that
\pi_{\alpha}
has the tower condition. Since
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{+}
for every
\mathcal{U}\in\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}
, we can lift
F_{\alpha}
to a function
F^{+}_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{U}^{+}_{\alpha}
by setting
F^{+}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{U}^{+})=F_{\alpha}(\mathcal{U})
. The map
F^{+}_{\alpha}
is well-defined because the operation
\mathcal{U}\mapsto\mathcal{U}^{+}
is injective on
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}
, and it witnesses that
\pi^{+}_{\alpha}
has the tower condition, too.
Moreover, without loss of generality we can suppose that the supports
K_{\alpha}
were pairwise disjoint, so that the families
\mathbb{U}^{+}_{\alpha}
are pairwise disjoint as well. This means that the map
F=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}F_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{U}
is well-defined, and it is easy to check that it witnesses that the representation
\pi
from (
8.3
) has the tower condition.
∎

What is missing in Proposition
8.3.6
is the closure under complements of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{repr}}(X)
and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{TC}}(X)
: this is unavoidable because such closure property might fail, even in presence of a measurable cardinal
\kappa>\lambda
.
In fact, one measurable cardinal is enough to prove that the
\lambda
-analytic sets have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
(Proposition
8.3.5
), but work in
[
5
]
built on the results of Section
7.2
shows that the consistency strength of the statement “all the
\lambda
-coanalytic sets have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
” is much higher. Therefore, in a model of
{\sf ZFC}
plus the existence of a measurable cardinal above
\lambda
plus the negation of the consistency of larger cardinals, all the
\lambda
-analytic sets have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
and there is a
\lambda
-coanalytic set (i.e., a complement of a
\lambda
-analytic set) without the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
, so that it cannot be
\mathbb{U}
-representable or TC-representable by Proposition
7.4.11
and Theorem
7.4.12
.

Proposition
8.3.5
is the exact counterpart in the generalized setting of item
(I)
in the discussion on which subsets of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\omega}{\omega}
are weakly homogeneously Souslin at the beginning of this section, and if we assume
{\sf AC}
it implies Theorem
7.1.1
(under stronger hypotheses) via Theorem
7.4.12
. The analogue of item
(II)
holds as well, and for the same reason: by Proposition
7.4.6
and Remark
7.4.8
, if there is no measurable cardinal above
\lambda
, and in particular if
\mathsf{V=L}
, then the only
\mathbb{U}
-representable subset of a
\lambda
-Polish space
X
is the whole space itself.

As for the generalized version of item
(III)
, the natural move is to see what happens under
\mathsf{I0}
. In Corollary
8.2.7
we observed that if we are concerned with subset of
V_{\lambda+1}
living in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
, then TC-representability is implied by
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representability, so it becomes crucial to understand when a set as above is
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable.
The following result come from
[
7
, Corollary 6.11]
.

Theorem 8.3.7

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Let
j
be a witness for
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, let
\kappa=\lambda^{+}
, and let
\eta=\sup\{(\kappa^{+})^{L[A]}\mid A\subseteq\lambda\}
. Then every subset of
V_{\lambda+1}
belonging to
L_{\eta}(V_{\lambda+1})
is
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
.

Since all
\lambda
-projective subsets of
V_{\lambda+1}
already appear in
L_{1}(V_{\lambda+1})
, combining the above result with Corollary
8.2.7
, Theorem
7.4.12
, and Corollary
7.1.4
(taking into account also Remark
8.3.4
), we obtain:

Corollary 8.3.8

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Assume
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
. Then all
\lambda
-projective sets have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
, that is,
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(\lambda\text{-}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1}_{n})
holds for every natural number
n\geq 1
.

Note that the same result holds for any boldface
\lambda
-pointclass
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
closed under
\lambda
-Borel preimages such that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(V_{\lambda+1})\subseteq L_{\eta}(V_{\lambda+1})
.

Theorem
8.3.7
has been extended by Cramer himself to encompass all sets in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
. The following statement appears in
[
8
, Theorem 84]
; unfortunately, although its proof has been widely circulated among experts, it has not been published yet, and so we cannot give a better reference.

Theorem 8.3.9

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Let
j
be a witness for
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, and work in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
. Then every
Z\subseteq V_{\lambda+1}
is
\mathbb{U}(j)
-representable.

Together with Corollary
8.2.7
, this provides the desired analogue of
(III)
: If
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds, then all subsets of
V_{\lambda+1}
in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
are TC-representable (in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
). Combining this with our results linking TC-representability to the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
(and taking into account Remark
8.3.4
), we obtain another proof of Cramer’s Theorem
8.3.3
, which can now be stated in full generality.

Corollary 8.3.10

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Assume that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds. Let
X
be a
\lambda
-Polish space in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
.
7
7

7

We can e.g. let
X
be one of the spaces
B(\lambda)
,
C(\lambda)
,
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{2}
, or
V_{\lambda+1}
, among the others.

Then all
Z\subseteq X
belonging to
L(V_{\lambda+1})
have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
.

In particular, combining Corollary
8.3.10
with Corollary
7.1.4
we obtain:

Corollary 8.3.11

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Assume that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds. If
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is a boldface
\lambda
-pointclass closed under
\lambda
-Borel preimages and such that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(V_{\lambda+1})\subseteq L(V_{\lambda+1})
, then
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
.

The added value of this alternative proof is that it comes directly from the representability structure, and not from the embedding structure (as it was the case in Cramer’s original proof). This actually reinforces the analogy between
\mathsf{I0}
and (large cardinals implying)
{\sf AD}
, as it shows that not only there are parallels between single concepts, but also in the way they interact among each other. Moreover, it opens the possibility of finding new analogues structures, different than
{\sf AD}
and
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, by looking for
\lambda
-Polish spaces on which many sets are
\mathbb{U}
-representable.

8.4.
Further results

Recall that we are currently working in
{\sf ZFC}
.
By Corollary
8.3.10
and Remark
8.3.4
, under
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
we have that
L(V_{\lambda+1})
satisfies

“All subsets of an arbitrary
\lambda
-Polish space have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
.”

One may ask whether
L(V_{\lambda+1})
is the only model of set theory in which this happens. More generally, one can ask which (generalized) descriptive set theoretic consequences of
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
can hold also in models where
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
fails. It turns out that any property about
V_{\lambda+1}
that can be expressed in a simple way can be reflected downwards, and therefore it is true also in a model in which
\mathsf{I0}
does not hold. The key theorem is the following deep result by Woodin, which is part of
[
50
, Theorem 137]
.
8
8

8

To recover Theorem
8.4.1
from
[
50
, Theorem 137]
, set
A_{0}=\lambda_{0}
, so that
A=\lambda
: with this notation, Theorem
8.4.1
appears towards the end of the proof.

Theorem 8.4.1

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Let
j
be a
proper
elementary embedding witnessing
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, and let
\lambda_{0}=\operatorname{crt}(j)<\lambda
. Then there exists a club
C
in
\lambda_{0}
such that for all
\lambda^{\prime}\in C
with
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda^{\prime})=\omega
and for all
\alpha<\lambda^{\prime}
,

(L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda^{\prime}+1}),{\in},\lambda^{\prime})\equiv(L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1}),{\in},\lambda),

where as usual
\equiv
denotes elementary equivalence.

Corollary 8.4.2

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Let
j
be a
proper
elementary embedding witnessing
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, and let
\lambda_{0}=\operatorname{crt}(j)<\lambda
. Let
\alpha<\lambda_{0}
, and let
\upvarphi(u)
be a first-order formula in the language of set theory such that
(L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1}),{\in})\models\upvarphi[\lambda/u]
. Then there is
\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda
such that
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda^{\prime})=\omega
,
\alpha<\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda_{0}
,
2^{<\lambda^{\prime}}=\lambda^{\prime}
, and
(L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda^{\prime}+1}),{\in})\models\upvarphi[\lambda^{\prime}/u]
.

Proof.

The only part that is not immediately covered by Theorem
8.4.1
is
2^{<\lambda^{\prime}}=\lambda^{\prime}
, but this holds as it is expressible in
(L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1}),{\in},\lambda)
by a first-order formula using
\lambda
as a parameter.∎

In order to apply Corollary
8.4.2
in our context, we first need to understand how the property “
Z
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
” can be expressed in first-order logic, and for which ordinals
\alpha
such property is true in
L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
. (Exceptionally for the results in this section, the next proposition does not require
{\sf AC}
.)

Proposition 8.4.3
.

Let
\lambda
be such that
2^{<\lambda}=\lambda
,

\alpha<\Theta^{L(V_{\lambda+1})}
be an ordinal different from
0
, and
Z\subseteq B(\lambda)
be a set in
L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
. Then there is a first-order formula
\uppsi(u,v)
in the language of set theory such that
Z
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
(as a subset of
B(\lambda)
) if and only if
(L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1}),{\in})\models\uppsi[\lambda/u,Z/v]
.

Sketch of the proof.

By the argument in Remark
8.3.4
, if
Z\in L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
, then the witnesses of this are definable from a corresponding function
\phi\colon\lambda\to\lambda
, which necessarily belongs to
V_{\lambda+1}
; therefore they belong to
L_{1}(V_{\lambda+1})\subseteq L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
, and witness that
Z
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
also in
L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
. We then get that
Z
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
(in
V
or, equivalently, in
L(V_{\lambda+1})
) if and only if
L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
satisfies the sentence

“
\exists\phi\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\lambda}{\lambda}\,[
\phi
codes either a continuous embedding from
B(\lambda)
into
Z
,

or a sequence
(x_{\beta})_{\beta<\lambda}
enumerating
Z]
”.

The latter can be easily formalized by a first-order formula in the language of set theory using
\lambda
and
Z
as parameters, hence we are done.
∎

Corollary 8.4.4

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Assume that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds. Then there is
\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda
such that
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda^{\prime})=\omega
,
2^{<\lambda^{\prime}}=\lambda^{\prime}
, and all the
\lambda^{\prime}
-projective sets in any
\lambda^{\prime}
-Polish space have the
\lambda^{\prime}
-
\mathrm{PSP}
.

Proof.

If
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds, then there is a
proper
elementary embedding
j
witnessing this (see the proof of
[
50
, Lemma 5]
or
[
9
, Theorem 5.12 and Definition 5.13]
). This enables us to use, in what follows, Corollary
8.4.2
with
\alpha=1
, so that
\alpha<\operatorname{crt}(j)
is satisfied (independently of the choice of
j
).

Let
\uppsi(u,v)
be as in Proposition
8.4.3
, and let

\upvarphi(u)
be the formalization of

“
\forall v\,[\,
if every element of
v
is a function from
\omega
to
u
, then
\uppsi(u,v)]
”,

Since every
Z\subseteq B(\lambda)
that belongs to
L_{1}(V_{\lambda+1})
has the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
(Corollary
8.3.10
), we have
(L_{1}(V_{\lambda+1}),{\in})\models\upvarphi[\lambda/u]
by Proposition
8.4.3
.
Then by Corollary
8.4.2
there is

1<\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda
such that
{\operatorname{cf}}(\lambda^{\prime})=\omega
,
2^{<\lambda^{\prime}}=\lambda^{\prime}
, and
(L_{1}(V_{\lambda^{\prime}+1}),{\in})\models\upvarphi[\lambda^{\prime}/u]
. Hence all the subsets of
B(\lambda^{\prime})
belonging to
L_{1}(V_{\lambda^{\prime}+1})
, including the
\lambda^{\prime}
-projective ones, have the
\lambda^{\prime}
-
\mathrm{PSP}
by Proposition
8.4.3
again. An application of Corollary
7.1.4
allows us to transfer the
\lambda^{\prime}
-
\mathrm{PSP}
from
B(\lambda^{\prime})
to any
\lambda^{\prime}
-Polish space, hence we are done.
∎

Corollary 8.4.5

(
{\sf AC}
)
.

Assume that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds. Then there is a
\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda
such that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda^{\prime})
does not hold, yet all
\lambda^{\prime}
-projective subsets of any
\lambda^{\prime}
-Polish space have the
\lambda^{\prime}
-
\mathrm{PSP}
.

Proof.

Let
\bar{\lambda}
be smallest such that
\mathsf{I0}(\bar{\lambda})
holds, so that in particular
\bar{\lambda}\leq\lambda
. Let
\lambda^{\prime}<\bar{\lambda}
be as in Corollary
8.4.4
applied to
\bar{\lambda}
. Then all
\lambda^{\prime}
-projective subset of any
\lambda^{\prime}
-Polish space have the
\lambda^{\prime}
-
\mathrm{PSP}
, but
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda^{\prime})
does not hold by choice of
\bar{\lambda}
.
∎

In particular, for a given
\lambda
the assertion

(
\star
)

“All
\lambda
-projective subsets of an arbitrary
\lambda
-Polish space have the
\lambda\text{-}\mathrm{PSP}
”

is not equivalent to
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
in
{\sf ZFC}
alone.
Nevertheless, the construction leading to Corollary
8.4.5
assumes
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
, so equiconsistency is not ruled out. In fact, the analysis in
[
31
]
shows that even the equivalence with very large cardinal properties is not ruled out.
Indeed, if
j
is an elementary embedding witnessing
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
and
\lambda_{0}=\operatorname{crt}(j)
, then for every
\alpha<\lambda_{0}
there is an elementary embedding
j_{\alpha}\colon L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})\to L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})

(see e.g.
[
31
, Theorem 3(ii)]
for a much stronger proposition). The existence of such elementary embeddings
j_{\alpha}
may be seen as a weak (but still high in the consistency hierarchy) form of
\mathsf{I0}
. We want to show that such property is retained at the level of the cardinal
\lambda^{\prime}
appearing in the proof of Corollary
8.4.5
: to this aim,
let us go once again over such argument.

In order to simplify the notation, suppose that
\lambda
itself is smallest such that
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda)
holds, as witnessed by the elementary embedding
j
. Let also
\lambda_{0}=\operatorname{crt}(j)
, and let
j_{\alpha}\colon L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})\to L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
, for
\alpha<\lambda_{0}
, be the elementary embeddings isolated by Laver. By
[
31
, Theorem 2(v)]
, each
j_{\alpha}
is defined from
j\restriction V_{\lambda}
: since the latter belongs to
V_{\lambda+1}
, we have that
j_{\alpha}\in L_{\alpha+1}(V_{\lambda+1})
and that

L_{\alpha+1}(V_{\lambda+1})\models\text{``there is an elementary embedding }j_{\alpha}\colon L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})\to L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})\text{''}.

Since
\mathrm{Ord}^{L_{\alpha+1}(V_{\lambda+1})}=\lambda+1+\alpha+1
, the ordinal
\alpha
is definable in
L_{\alpha+1}(V_{\lambda+1})
from
\lambda
. Also,
V_{\lambda+1}
is easily definable inside
L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
.
Moreover, we can build
L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
as computed inside
L_{\alpha+1}(V_{\lambda+1})
, and by absoluteness the resulting set will be
L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
itself. Therefore
L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
is definable in
L_{\alpha+1}(V_{\lambda+1})
from
\lambda
, and thus the first-order sentence formalizing

“there is an elementary embedding
j_{\alpha}\colon L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})\to L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1})
”

uses only
\lambda
as a parameter.
Recall that the
\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda
in Corollary
8.4.5
comes from an application of Theorem
8.4.1
. Therefore
\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda_{0}
, and
(L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda^{\prime}+1}),{\in},\lambda^{\prime})\equiv(L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda+1}),{\in},\lambda)
for all
\alpha<\lambda^{\prime}
. It follows that for all
\alpha<\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda_{0}

L_{\alpha+1}(V_{\lambda^{\prime}+1})\models\text{``there is an elementary embedding }j_{\alpha}\colon L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda^{\prime}+1})\to L_{\alpha}(V_{\lambda^{\prime}+1})\text{''}.

So while
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda^{\prime})
does not hold, still the fragment of
\mathsf{I0}(\lambda^{\prime})
corresponding to the existence of the elementary embeddings
j_{\alpha}
still holds.
This means that, as claimed, the proof of Corollary
8.4.5
cannot rule out that the statement (

⋆

‣
8.4
) is equivalent to weak forms of
\mathsf{I0}
.

Index
